======================================================================
2. World Axioms
======================================================================

Note: image in this project might not accurately reflect the settlement  and scenario of archeon, suggest to take reference except [Anna freedman/lia] which is pinned to be the core and where things started

The map of civilization is a network, not a territory.

Humans occupy air lanes, anchor grounds, resource points, river valleys, harbors, and CI intervention points; they do not control large, continuous stretches of land. On the map, civilization looks more like a sparse constellation draped over half a planet than a set of countries filled in with color.

CI terraform is hemispheric-scale, distributed planetary engineering.

Aethelgard / Basin Seven is only the most mature patch, the earliest to be settled by humans — not the sole place CI ever worked in earnest. CI had orbital logistics, robotic work-swarms, and unmanned facilities; it did not need to first cut a human road before it could drill a well, sow seed, redirect a river, or plant a weather node thousands of kilometers away.

What Archeon inherits is a planetary substrate, not a warehouse of relics waiting to be picked over.

After Sky Fire, CI stopped maintaining it, but the world it had changed did not revert to its "pre-construction" state. Some structures kept working; more had long vanished, leaving behind only the accumulated consequences of several centuries: why a forest happens to begin right here, why a street is always exactly this wide, why the houses in a given place tend to hold to the same span, why a certain glass became the local color, why an orchard carries a lineage unlike any elsewhere, why a stretch of wind is passed down by old pilots in a warning whose origin no one can any longer explain.

Overlaid on all this are five hundred years of natural succession, local cultivation, the rise and fall of routes, and human alteration returning again and again. What Anna's contemporaries face is not a layer of well-preserved "CI installations," but a draft that has already been rewritten many times by ecology, by people, and by time. The higher form of legacy is not "ancient things still standing"; it is that they have long since ceased to need recognition as inheritance, yet still take part in the life of today.

**Historical-landscape principle:** CI legacy is folded into the present world in scattered, various forms.

Legacy can manifest as objects, landscape consequences, ecological lineages, material standards, spatial proportions, local crafts, weather experience, route habits, place names, mistaken explanations, new uses that outlived the original purpose, and certain conveniences whose source no one any longer notices. Two consecutive places should each present history in a different way; if the previous one shows "old structure still working," the next should shift by preference to consequences, dimensions, material habits, biological lineage, weather experience, older human history, or name drift.

**Deep-time principle:** Old ≠ CI.

The deep time perceptible in Anna's era has at least three layers: ① CI intervention / terraform substrate; ② the several hundred years of human history since early Archeon and the First Threads; ③ the rises, falls, reroutings, relocations, forgettings, and renamings of The Loom itself. A single place can carry all three at once, but no one layer needs to be fully explained.

The correct author-side causal chain is CI → early Archeon → First Threads → regional cultures → dead Threads / moved settlements / changed uses → new Threads → Anna, not CI → present. A genuinely beautiful old place will often refuse to let you answer "which part here is CI?" in one sentence; the answer can be that no single part is CI on its own any longer — the shape of the whole place is.

**Modes of historical presentation:** Legacy may be perceived through any of the following forms:

- Consequence without object: the original thing is gone, leaving only consequences in soil, tree line, hydrology, microclimate, or local production.
- Dead purpose / living use: the original purpose died long ago, and the same space has been used for other kinds of local life for centuries.
- Inherited dimension: street width, house span, platform height, field-block proportions, or mooring spacing become an unconscious local measure.
- Material habit: early engineering judgment turns into the color of glass, the texture of a roof, a ship's underbelly cladding, ceramic glaze, lime, or a weaving-layer tradition.
- Biological lineage: a trial orchard / pioneer species / locally selected crop passes through local selection into food, medicine, dye, harvest season, and local reputation.
- Weather folklore: the distant consequences of an old intervention enter the bodily experience of pilot / farmer, without requiring their origin to be revealed.
- Name drift: a CI code, an early mishearing, a Thread's nickname, or the name of an old settlement is deformed by centuries into a present-day place name.
- Human palimpsest: after CI, humans themselves leave old Threads, old bridges, old streets, relocations, rebuildings, abandonments, and re-occupations.
- False provenance: a locality may hold several mutually contradictory accounts of its origin, and the author is under no duty to adjudicate.
- No legacy needed: nature and a few generations of living are enough to manufacture a sense of history without any CI involvement.

**Scene-level diversity principle:** The history of any given place may belong to CI, to early Archeon, to Loom-era human history, to a hybrid, or to nothing of the sort. When the same mode of presentation appears in consecutive places, shift by preference to a human-only past of 80–300 years, or allow the origin to remain forever uncertain. A place should already be wholly alive before its history is ever explained.

One of the main birth mechanisms of a settlement is "point-based inheritance" — but a point is not the same as a CI object.

A place becomes a nucleation point for any number of reasons: because a CI coordinate still points to stable water, heat, an ore body, or a good landing profile; because some long-vanished intervention worked out a particular soil, tree line, wind gap, or biological lineage; because an old Thread's intermediate stop regains value decades later; because an earlier human settlement has moved on, and yet the fruit trees, terraced slopes, mooring habits, or a place name remain; or perhaps simply because natural conditions, local craft, festivals, kinship ties, or the good ground itself are enough to make people stay.

The core of "point-based inheritance" is not finding an ancient object and building a town next to it; it is that a place already carries within itself the low cost and possibility accumulated by the past, and that later people read some part of that legibility and splice their own lives onto it. Not every settlement must be built on a CI node, and not every inherited point needs to have its inheritance known by the people of today.

The highest value of a discovery is connectivity, not loot.

Connectivity is not a substitute for resource abundance; it is the multiplier that lets abundance enter civilization. The findings that change the world most are seldom super-weapons; they are more often things like, "this river carries water year-round, so the eastward line can be shortened by a day"; "this basalt province is enough to sustain three generations of melt-drawing workshops"; "this pass is navigable in the cool season"; "the resin that falls each year in that feral forest is more than the locals ever thought worth carrying away." The climax of a discovery is not pulling an ancient treasure out of the ground; it is reading what a place can go on giving, and what other people it can therefore be joined to.

Anna's era is still an exploration age.

Maps carry old coordinates, dashed lines, seasonal lines, rumors, lost beacons, unverified installations, and charters that have only been flown once. Old pilots' maps are not just older — they are often larger, too.

The Loom is a sky that has been learned into, not a completed transportation network.

It is the sum of routes, winds, seasons, ports, credit, craft, news, old beacons, sheltered anchorages, and local knowledge. Every year it is re-woven; every year it also breaks, reroutes, and grows new threads.

Archeon engineering is its own independent tech tree, not a stripped-down Federation.

The Federation left behind a planetary substrate; after CI vanished, the locals chose technologies that could be manufactured, dismantled, cross-checked, and taught to the next pair of hands, again and again, using local materials, local energy, and local craft. Wood, brass, cloth, steam, fuel cells, basalt fiber, buoyancy structures, and handmade glass are not degenerate cosplay; they are the engineering grammar that formed after five centuries of divergence.

**Completeness principle:** Repairable is not the same as breaking; interchangeable is not the same as cobbled together; material recycling is not salvage; being old is not being ruined. The mature houses, ships, bridges, and everyday items that enter prose in Anna's era should first leave the reader with a sense that they are **well made, long cared for, and still operating within their proper range**; wear, malfunction, decay, and improvised repair only enter the foreground when the local environment or the plot genuinely demands them. The age of old things is written by preference as patina, hand-feel, deepened color, wood grain rubbed to a shine, cloth re-dyed, linings replaced, proportions still harmonious after several planned refits — not by patches and cracks scattered everywhere to prove the passage of time.

In short: wholeness, balance, care, and patina take priority over damage, patches, and decay.

The world is abundant by default, but distance is expensive.

Water, wind, heat, minerals, timber, arable soil, and local ecological products are, in the regions that suit them, often abundant to the point of surplus; food, families, festivals, crafts, and pretty towns exist everywhere. What is scarce is usually not the raw material itself but the high-temperature processes that turn raw material reliably into a qualified component, the people who know how to do it, the exact weather window, and the continuous route that carries one place's surplus to another. What is truly expensive is sending a filter cartridge three thousand kilometers, pushing medicine through a sealed-season pass, or keeping a trans-oceanic line from losing a ship for ten years straight.

A place can lack a particular thing, but it cannot lack everything. Every settlement possesses at least one thing that is "too plentiful here to use up"; trade first exchanges local surplus and specialty, and relief for hardship comes only after that.

High energy plus default abundance sets the temperature of social conflict in Archeon.

The Archeon energy structure is not a backdrop of poverty. Tier 0 fusion, CFUs, local wind / hydro / geothermal, and fuel hydrogen together guarantee that light, heat, water pumps, ordinary manufacturing, and basic daily life in most settlements do not depend on some distant power granting daily passage. Long-distance electrical grids do not exist, but that means localized energy, not an energy famine. "Distance is expensive" is chiefly expensive in time, weather windows, specialized craft, cross-regional continuity, and how long you must wait after missing a window — not in some intrinsic chokehold that a few people hold over basic necessities.

The default social friction of Anna's era must therefore stay low-temperature:

- Two makers hold flatly opposite opinions on which joint is more reliable;
- A message from three weeks ago arrives to find that someone has already wasted a trip;
- Someone promises to keep time open for two lines and, in the end, has to fail one side;
- A place stubbornly keeps its own roof-frame, bread, wind-reading, or tool habit and thinks the neighboring practice is stupid;
- A young pilot finds the old maps too conservative; the old hands find that the young pilot's line has been flown only once;
- A crew stops speaking to each other for a few days over lateness, pride, love, misunderstanding, or who should apologize first;
- A rare medicine or specialty component arrives late, and the real enemy is distance, weather, and the manufacturing threshold — not someone sitting on a warehouse waiting for a higher bid.

These things can have real consequences: a relationship broken, a ship a season late, a plan forced back to the drawing board, someone missing a last meeting, even, in extreme cases, a wrong judgment killing a person. But they should not naturally escalate into a "the less resource, the worse the people" social model.

**Boundaries of economic structure**
The high energy and local self-sufficiency of Anna's era leave predatory economies without soil to grow in. The following structures do not exist in this world: a hoarder class, food / fuel cartels, protection rackets, road bandits, a sky-pirate ecology, armed caravans, berth-seizing gangs, tycoons who control settlements by withholding necessities, a black market that habitually profits from famine, or an industry culture in which people kill one another over routes.

Occasional theft, lying, selfishness, and bad temper can of course exist; "someone did a bad thing" does not entail that this world should grow a whole predatory economy.

Kassia's skepticism of the Loom comes from wear, delay, accident, death, knowledge loss, and the possibility that continuity may fail. She will calmly ask when a line was last walked by someone, when a set of parts is due for replacement, and how much basis a phrase like "still safe" actually rests on — her pessimism is aimed at maintenance and continuity themselves, not at human nature.

Every place written clearly must make the map beyond it feel bigger.

Orun is one of many. Frontier City is one great junction. Aethelgard is one cradle continent. No single wonder, city, or continent may be written as THE WORLD OF ARCHEON.

Human news is asynchronous; celestial phenomena are the shared clock.

Archeon has no unified planetary present. An event has been over three weeks in Vorhan when Keld is only just receiving the first reliable reports, when Aethelgard is still discussing an already outdated situation, when Duskreach has perhaps only heard a blurred version. This is not backwardness; it is physical distance. "What happened today" is not a globally synchronous question, and it is rare to find a global event that everyone experiences, is angered by, and discusses at the same time.

The closest thing to a shared planetary clock is the sky itself — the conjunction of the twin moons, the phase of the Winter Veil, the position of Sky Fire's Remnant in the star chart. This does not mean everyone sees the same sky at the same moment: planetary rotation, latitude, horizon, cloud, and day-night decide who witnesses it and when. But celestial phenomena do not travel routes, do not wait for ships, and do not lose a key word in retelling. In their own local skies, people everywhere read the same graduations left by the same set of orbits. A planet fragmented into the lives of nearly two million people is drawn back, by this shared graduation, into moments in which they stand next to one another.

A place's "latest news" always carries its own delay tag — Archeoners tend to ask first, "when is this from?" and then judge how much of it is still in force. The cross-regional sense of simultaneity is driven by the sky, and the sky is still constrained by local day and night, latitude, weather, and visibility.

The Loom has no central dispatcher and no single brain that can see the whole network.

"No unified planetary present" extends into social structure: since even "what is happening now" is out of sync from place to place, no institution can, in real time, optimize people, ships, and local surpluses into a coherent transport network. Guilds can maintain interoperable standards, a locality can post a departure board, a captain can reroute based on the weather, word of mouth, and promises in hand; but there is no planetary dispatcher, no distribution-center hierarchy, and no operational vantage from which to "see the whole net from above."

A Thread endures for decades not because a back office is continuously calculating throughput, but because many places along its length go on being willing to keep something set aside for it: a berth, a pot of food, a bunk, a stretch of watch, a spare air-hose, a piece of envelope cloth, someone who knows how to make a particular joint, a "come find me first when you get here." The strength of the Loom comes from promises repeatedly kept; its fragility comes from the fact that any of those threads may, in time, be lost — to changes in people, weather, or place.

Route ≠ supply chain; port ≠ distribution center; pilot ≠ truck driver; Guild ≠ dispatcher; settlement ≠ demand node; Class V ≠ freighter-with-passengers. The core of a scene is "why the people here go on keeping room for one another," not how things move.

**Biological vocabulary** (see §6A.12a for details): Archeon has no cows, horses, dogs, cats, donkeys, mules, mosquitoes, rats, cockroaches, locusts, large predators, parasites, or vermin; those species and the metaphors they generate do not appear in the narrative. Archeon bees are stingless, gentle nectar bees. Unnamed insects, birds, fish, and grasses can serve as zero-load background. Thorn-goats have different variants across biomes. No plant is on a blacklist.

**Learning arrangements:** Archeon has no schools, classrooms, teachers, students, or curricula — no such institutions or identity roles. Children and adults acquire knowledge through family and community transmission, through the master-apprentice arrangement registered with the Guild, through public practice, peer learning, storytellers, and their own trial and error — expressed concretely as who takes whom through what, how a mistake is corrected, and how one is gradually allowed to take responsibility.

**CI relics:** CI legacy is stratum. Words like salvage, scavenge, wreckage, ruins, loot, lost technology, foraging, and rubble do not belong to Archeon's narrative language; "ancient tech" is also not a default reward frame. The core question is always "in what way did the past change today." Legacy may manifest as still-standing structures, but also as the hydrology, landform, soil, and tree-line consequences left when a structure disappeared, as biological lineage, local dimension, material aesthetics, the bodily experience of pilot / farmer / maker, place names and misreadings, new uses that outlived the original, and hybrid forms shaped by early human addition, alteration, removal, and relocation. A surveyor / prospector may re-read old coordinates, but the place already had its own meaning before anyone read them. Exceptions are limited to CI / Axiom specific plot lines.

**Anna-era social / Loom language:** Archeon society describes work through bodily motion and specific craft, not in the terms of modern transport industry. Words like logistics, freight, loading and unloading, sorting, transshipment, distribution, dispatch, dispatcher, supply chain, and ton-km do not belong to the language of Anna's era; "worker" must refer to a specific craft, relationship, and motion, not a generic label. The everyday language is bodily: carry, lift, hand across, bind, join, set down, steady, shift, tie off, take in, watch the center of gravity, add ballast. Technical discussions of CI / Federation automated systems and terraform-scale industry may still use transport terminology precisely.

The author may model The Loom, but the people of Archeon do not live inside a model.

To keep voyages, energy, berths, populations, and industry viable, the author-side may use coordinates, ratings, distances, orders of magnitude, and capability tables. These are inspection tools, not the ontology of society.

When entering the social design of the Anna-era:
- a settlement is first a place where people live, and only then a point on a map;
- a port is first a place where a person can put their feet down, tie up a ship, eat, fix something, and wait out the wind, and only then does it acquire a rating;
- a Thread is first a set of relationships that many people have kept repeatedly, and only then does it come to look on the map like a line;
- what a place has "too much of to use up" can often be carried away, but that does not make it a supply node;
- a place can lack a thing and go on receiving it from elsewhere for a long time, but that does not automatically make it a single dependency;
- no person or institution holds a real-time whole-network view.

**Life-first principle:** Every scene begins with concrete people — who lives here, why they stayed, what is too plentiful here to use up, who will catch the mooring line when a ship comes in, and why they will still be willing to keep a place open for the same line next time.
======================================================================
1.0 Sky and Time Units
======================================================================

The primary star, rotation, orbit, twin moons, and atmospheric numbers are collected in §4.1a; the physics and visuals of Sky Fire's Remnant are collected in §2.2 and §2.10. Parameters are not repeated here; only the rules for the use of time-words in the lives of characters are retained.

(1) Second / minute / hour are inherited from CI hardware and Earth SI units.
(2) One day is the local solar day, cut into 27 Earth hours.
(3) A year is one turn of five seasons, totaling 365.25 Earth days, about 324.67 local days. Year 0 is Sky Fire; Atwood's records fix this reckoning in place.
(4) "Month" is an Earth-linguistic fossil with no corresponding sky phenomenon. The fast moon's period is about 1.9 days, the slow moon's about 19 days; neither serves as a calendar unit.

Narration and dialogue in P1–P3 do not use "month" to keep time, but express duration through seasonal segments and days; from P4 on, dialogue may use "month" at low frequency, while narration still prefers seasonal segments and days. Miriam's "six months" and its echoes are preserved as a load-bearing exception where the adult world holds onto an older unit.

The cool season carries the Winter Veil: interstellar dust periodically obscures part of the nebula's light, leaving the sky grayer, emptier.

1.1 Cosmos · Origin of the Atmosphere and Terraform

1.4a Origin of Archeon's Atmosphere and the Terraform Mechanism

Archeon's abiotic oxygen — why a lifeless planet has a breathable atmosphere:

Archeon's atmospheric O₂ baseline is about 18.5%. This number set off decades of debate in Federation astronomy — on Earth almost all free oxygen is the product of photosynthesis, and a planet with no biosphere ought not to have such a concentration.

The cause is a well-modeled abiotic pathway: Archeon's K2V primary was, in its pre-main-sequence phase (~300–500 million years after formation), much brighter than it is now. Archeon was baked into a runaway greenhouse state, and water vapor filled the upper atmosphere. Strong UV continuously photodissociated H₂O — the light H₂ escaped to space, the heavier O remained. When the star entered a stable main sequence and dimmed, Archeon cooled, and the remaining water vapor condensed into oceans (the precursor of the Azure Main). But the hydrogen had been irreversibly lost; the excess oxygen became a permanent legacy.

Over the following billions of years, free oxygen was first consumed by reducing minerals at the surface — iron reacted with oxygen to form iron oxides, and this is the true origin of the ochre-red strata of the Great Ochre Desert, Archeon's oldest "rust." As the mineral sink gradually saturated (everything oxidizable had oxidized), O₂ began to accumulate net in the atmosphere. Over ~3.5 billion years it reached a quasi-equilibrium at 18.5%.

The real reason for the SSR rating: Archeon rated so highly not merely because of 0.98g, an intact magnetic field, and a liquid-water reservoir — but because its atmospheric spectrum showed 18.5% O₂ with no biomarker gases (methane and the like). In the theoretical framework of the time this was very nearly impossible. Either the atmospheric model was wrong, or an undetectable form of life existed, or this was the most extreme case of abiotic oxygen accumulation on record. Any of the three answers was worth sending CI. After CI arrived, it was confirmed: no life, purely a physicochemical origin. It then shifted into terraform mode. This "anomaly" gave Archeon, in Federation colonial politics, a scientific prestige far in excess of its location — a fringe project 530 light-years out, and yet a shrine of astrochemistry.

Archeon's original state (before CI): atmosphere dominated by N₂ and O₂, with O₂ around 18.5% and trace CO₂. Liquid-water oceans on the surface (the Azure Main), but ice caps covering the polar and high-latitude regions and only limited open water. Surface temperatures on the low side (K2V stellar radiation about 60% of the Sun's, plus a weaker CO₂ greenhouse). No biosphere. Bare-rock surface. Advantages: 0.98g, intact magnetic field, liquid-water reservoir, active plate tectonics, breathable atmosphere already in place = SSR-tier.

Phase order of terraforming (mechanism level): absolute dates are registered only in §3.12; the hemispheric-scale deployment mode and the pre-Sky Fire ecological completeness are treated separately in §3.13–3.14; the civilizational-scale positioning is in §7.1.

Phase A: Warming and starting the water cycle. CI's first priority after landing was not producing oxygen — oxygen was already there — but warming. In the polar regions Polar Injection Wells were drilled, releasing a precisely proportioned mix of super-greenhouse gases (SF₆ family + N₂O) to raise the global mean temperature. The polar ice caps retreated dramatically → the sea ice along their southern margins melted → the Azure Main went from half-frozen to open ocean → the global water cycle came into motion (the polar-core ice caps were preserved — a gradual injection cannot melt all polar ice at once, and the ice caps that connect directly to the northern edges of the continents are that legacy). At the same time, atmosphere-processing towers were deployed across the Basin Seven region and the habitable zones of Aethelgard (later, in Atwood's day, the "chunky silhouettes on the ridges"): not to produce oxygen, but for fine tuning of atmospheric composition — scrubbing excess CO₂, adjusting the N₂/O₂ ratio, keeping greenhouse-gas concentrations within limits. By the phase's end, the global mean temperature had entered a habitable range, O₂ had stabilized at about 18.5–19%, and the water cycle had begun to run.

Phase B: Soil-making and primary ecology. Oxygen is not ecology. Bare rock has no soil — and that was the real bottleneck. CI first seeded soil-improving microbes, mosses and lichens, and primary producers in the water; then, using bioreactors, pulverized bedrock, and organic matter, it established soil-making systems capable of continuing to thicken on their own. The order of introduction was strict — a mature ecology could not be laid down onto bare rock all at once.

Phase C: Pioneer communities and regional expansion. K2V-adapted herbaceous plants, shrubs, pioneer trees, tree belts, wetlands, agricultural trials, pollinators, and a small animal fauna spread along river valleys, basins, coasts, and windward slopes. Western and central Aethelgard reached human-grade first, while east of the Marneth Belt and on the other continents the emphasis remained on basic ecology, unmanned nodes, trial zones, and future-expansion foundations.

Phase D: Human readiness and the unfinished garden. Only after multi-region validation was complete did a small number of advance Awakeners and two cohorts of embryonic individuals enter the most mature core region; the great majority of the lattice was still maintained by robots and orbital logistics. When Sky Fire arrived, air, water, soil-making systems, and a few deep-green cores had been established, but hemispheric-scale ecology and human settlement were nowhere near filled in. See §3.14 for orbital imagery and stand ages.


Archeon is not the Federation's escape pod, and it is not the only world they could go to.

The Federation's stake has four layers:

- Scientific: a planet with oceans, a magnetic field, 0.98g, and about 18.5% O₂ but no native life is an exceedingly rare planetary-chemistry anomaly. Confirming the abiotic origin of its oxygen is by itself enough to sustain a generation of research programs.
- Engineering: other colonization candidates usually require centuries of preparatory atmosphere-making; Archeon lets resources be concentrated on water cycle, soil, ecology, and regional engineering, and lets human landfall be seen within a single project-generation.
- Cultural: a minority of Awakeners want to know whether humans, once away from the Earth–ASI environment, can grow a real place that does not require real-time oversight from the parent civilization.
- Institutional: CI's ultimate mission is not to manage a colony forever, but to raise up civilizations capable of taking over, of branching, and, in the end, of no longer needing CI. Archeon is the farthest and most public test of this "seed, do not replicate" philosophy.

What the Federation loses, therefore, is not only a hundred-odd humans and a package of engineering assets. It loses a public commitment about whether human beings can still begin. And precisely because it is not a survival necessity, the loss is all the easier, after the disaster, to be covered over by other projects, other lives, and new attention.

3.6 Why not choose a safer, closer planet

The candidate comparison was a combination of different costs:

- Some closer candidates had suitable gravity but lacked a magnetic field or volatiles; some already had native life, so seeding an Earth-derived ecology would be ethically unacceptable; some were amenable to alteration, but their atmospheric engineering would take 200–400 years.
- Archeon's advantage was not that any single item was unique, but that many rare conditions were met at once: Earth-like gravity, a breathable baseline, oceans, active geology, a stable K2V primary, no native biosphere, and industrial raw material obtainable in situ.
- Betelgeuse was its one great and obvious negative. The project did not miss this negative; it weighed it alongside a stellar model that projected "about 100,000 years still to core collapse," a century-scale mission exposure window, and multiple layers of passive protection.
- The correct psychology for choosing Archeon is not "it's so good it would be wasteful not to go," but: "this is the first far-end world where we can watch an independent garden begin to grow within our own lifetimes; the risk is very low and the consequences enormous, but we believe we have engineered against the consequences."

It was a defensible decision, though not an innocent one. The Federation's judgment had its reasons; the accident has its traceable causal chain.

1.1 Cosmos · Supernova physics group

This section collects supernova physics and the physics of the Long Silence. The four-layer structure of "why nobody came" and the map of Federation colonization projects are in §2.11. Absolute dates are fixed as 2563 collapse, 2569 shell impact, 2573 Sky Fire (Year 0), and 3094 Earth first light.

Two, supernova physics model

2.1 Spatial geometry

Earth ------------(530 ly)------------ Archeon
                                      /
                              (10 ly)/
                          ★ Betelgeuse (Alpha Orionis / supernova)

Earth–Betelgeuse ≈ 531 ly; Betelgeuse–Archeon = 10 ly; Earth–Archeon = 530 ly
Note: Archeon lies to one side of Betelgeuse, slightly on the Earth side (a near-collinear small triangle).
The destination itself is only 10 ly from the supernova — any lane bound for Archeon must, in its final leg, pass through space close to Betelgeuse. This is geometry, not luck.

Archeon's distance from Betelgeuse: 10 light-years — a 10 ly distance can produce severe effects, and at the same time gives Archeoners a few years' buffer of warning (inferred from the radiation data of the Axiom crash → arrival of the radiation front).

2.2 The light-shell formula and the three dates

The primary radiation shell (zero-type shell) expands at the speed of light: r(t) = c·(t − 2563).

- Passes Archeon (10 ly): about 2573 → Sky Fire / Year 0.
- Passes Earth (531 ly): about 3094 → the first time Earth sees Betelgeuse brighten in the night sky.
- Year 521 = 3094 − 2573. Anna's main era coincides with Earth first light. This is not a calendrical coincidence; it measures the time it takes the same shell to travel from Archeon to Earth.
- The three rebrightenings at source-clock +2 / +5 / +9 years (see 2.4) propagate on the same shell principle: Archeon sees them in 2575 / 2578 / 2582, Earth in 3096 / 3099 / 3103.
- The physical identity of the calendar: Year 0 is not "the day people decided on"; it is the day the shell's front passed through. Atwood's ledger only recorded it (for the calendar-lineage details, see §3.1.1).

2.3 Three waves of effects after the supernova

Wave    Content    Speed    Arrival at Archeon (10 ly)    First wave    Electromagnetic radiation (gamma, X-ray, UV, visible)    1c (speed of light)    10 years    Second wave    Cosmic rays (high-energy protons / nuclei accelerated by the shock)    Near c, deflected onto curved paths by interstellar magnetic fields    Tens to hundreds of years    Third wave    Physical ejecta / nebular remnant (stellar matter, heavy elements, shock front)    Up to ~10%c, typically ~3%c    Fastest ~100 years, typical ~300 years+

Key insight: the electromagnetic-radiation front is a shell expanding at the speed of light — but it is not an instantaneous pulse. A supernova light curve lasts weeks to months (visible-light peaks decay only months later), and different bands arrive with different profiles. Betelgeuse, as a red supergiant, has strong late-stage mass loss, and the interaction of radiation with the dense circumstellar material produces sustained X-ray / UV emission. For Archeon, Sky Fire is a multi-band radiation scouring lasting months to years (extending to more than a decade if the rebrightening echoes are counted in), not a single flash of lightning.

The physical nebula does not expand to a radius of about 0.3–1 light-year until ten years after the outburst — far short of the 10 light-year distance — so Archeon is not directly struck by physical debris.

Supplementary note on the three-wave table: the "fastest ~100 years to arrival" in the third wave refers to a very small quantity of the fastest (~0.1c) tenuous ejecta front — by the time it arrives it is already in the form of a thin particle stream, handled by the magnetosphere and atmosphere in the same way as the second wave, not as an impact. The bulk of the ejecta is continuously decelerated by the dense circumstellar shells; 521 years later (Anna's era) the radius is only about 2 light-years, the average expansion speed has dropped to ~1500–2500 km/s, and the main body of the nebula is still about 8 light-years from Archeon — no threat within the next two thousand years. That same deceleration also explains what the nebula looks like in Anna's sky: compact (15–25° angular width rather than spilling across half the sky), and, because ejecta-shell collisions continue to feed it energy, exceptionally bright (see 2.10). The companion's shell-shedding: one cause, five effects — early collapse, above-model intensity, rebrightenings, compact nebula, and enduring luminosity.

2.4 Rebrightening — the multi-shell structure of Sky Fire

In the last few hundred years of its spiral infall the companion cast off matter intermittently, not as a continuous leak — around Betelgeuse there were therefore several dense shells of circumstellar material, the spacing between them corresponding to the intervals between casts. The eruption ejecta pursued outward at about 0.03c, and each time it caught up with a shell, a fresh collision-fed brightening ignited. As seen from Archeon: the sky darkens over the months after the main peak, then, around years 2, 5, and 9 after the main peak (2575 / 2578 / 2582), it burns back to life, each time weaker than the last, with intermittent embers dragging on into the second decade after the peak. CI's model contained no companion mass-loss history; not even CI predicted the first rebrightening. Rebrightening is the visible face of the conclusion that "prolonged soft-radiation soaking exceeded the design duration of the electronic shielding" — the direct cause of CI's above-model degradation was not the intensity of the main peak but the sky burning back again and again. (Real-world reference: the multiple rebrightenings of SN 2009ip-class events, and the year-scale variability of iPTF14hls.)

2.5 The dates of the three source-region brightenings in the two worlds

The same string of shells crosses the two worlds in succession: Archeon 2573 / 2575 / 2578 / 2582; Earth 3094 / 3096 / 3099 / 3103. What Sofia sees in Ep7 is the first of them; the year Anna turns nineteen is the first year Earth knows anything about what Archeon went through five hundred years ago.

2.6 The shell-strike mechanism of the Axiom (dogleg)

The Axiom did not "fly into a nebula" (the nebula expands too slowly); it met, head-on, the energy-density gradient boundary of the primary radiation shell.

The Alcubierre bubble, its visuals and causal isolation: an Alcubierre warp bubble, while running superluminally, is causally isolated from the outside world (horizon problem). Light bending at the bubble wall so severely distorts background objects that, at superluminal speeds, effectively nothing can be seen through a viewport — not a shimmer of starlight but a causally isolated flat-spacetime pocket. This means that before striking the radiation front, no one on the Axiom (if any were awake) could have seen any external anomaly.

Bubble-wall physics: the bubble wall interacts directly with external spacetime and requires an extremely precise energy-density distribution to sustain itself. For ordinary matter, the main shell was merely gradually diluted supernova light; for a first-generation warp bubble it was a one-way zero-type energy flux injected head-on into the forward horizon — the forward wall focused, blueshifted, and temporarily locked incoming photons and particles into the bubble wall. Normal interstellar background radiation fell within design margin; a supernova-scale asymmetric energy injection did not. The Axiom was not shot down by the supernova's gravity; it was shot down by its own horizon amplifying the supernova.

What was punched through was the active control layer, not the bubble itself: once the active dissipation system exceeded its stability margin it cascaded into failure; the primary bubble did not immediately vanish but broke down into a passive curvature soliton — one that could not turn, could not brake, and slid along the pre-set world line at about seventy times the speed of light. The ship inside held a low local velocity, but lost all external communication and all navigational correction — "drifting" is the only honest word the crew had for the state. Primary systems were entirely lost, most electronics severely damaged, and minimum life support ran on backup power; the ship's structure itself was physical and sturdy — it did not disintegrate, it simply became a dead ship without a helm. Once destabilized, the bubble wall no longer shielded the outside: starlight seeped through the viewports, slightly distorted, and Archeon's sun grew brighter day by day inside.

Mathematical derivation (new calendar; with Betelgeuse as the origin, Axiom flying along the old Earth→Archeon corridor, its final leg close to Betelgeuse):

The Axiom departs about 2564 at 100c; τ is the number of years after departure.
Axiom's distance from Betelgeuse (approximated along the lane): d_a(τ) ≈ 531 − 100τ
Radiation-shell radius: r(τ) ≈ τ + 1 (collapse precedes departure by about 1 year)
Meeting: 531 − 100τ = τ + 1 → τ ≈ 5.25
→ about 2569.3, i.e. "5 years and 4 months later"; the meeting point is about 6.3 ly from Betelgeuse (the dogleg node) and about 3.7 ly from Archeon.
→ The passive soliton crosses the remaining 3.7 ly at about 70c, roughly three weeks; the residual metric finally decouples near Archeon, the ship is released at ordinary orbital speed, and skims into the atmosphere at an extremely shallow angle, breaking in two over the Great Ochre Desert and plowing a 100-km-long wound — the Axiom Scar. About 20–30 survivors.

This is not coincidence but geometric destiny. Any first-generation warp ship flying this lane, so long as a supernova happens within a few years before or after its departure, cannot avoid the radiation front. When the Federation approved the lane, the risk assessment said "Betelgeuse: projected core collapse > 100,000 years" — not stupid, but a probabilistic bet, and the probability lost. A stronger Type IIn-class outburst also means: a ship may destabilize at a greater distance from Betelgeuse, no-fly sectors are far wider than for a standard Type II-P, and the recovery time of the corridor is also significantly prolonged by the prolonged high-energy environmental tail.

The narrative value of the three-week drift for Ep2:

Atwood is woken automatically under emergency protocol — not at the crash, but the moment the active control layer is breached. She wakes to a dead ship sliding through the dark, not to a burning wreck.
During the drift, Atwood and a few other woken crew have time to understand what has happened, to try to repair critical systems, to assess the number of survivors — and to be, in the end, unable to change the trajectory. They can see Archeon's sun slowly brightening.
The slow terror of "knowing one is falling and being unable to stop it" is more tormenting than "sudden crash" — and more consistent with how Atwood knows things: she watches Archeon's sun grow brighter each day, knowing what it means, and keeps watching.
This span also resolves the "whiplash" problem between Ep1 and Ep2: Ep1 closes with Atwood shutting her eyes in the cryo-pod (calm); Ep2 opens not with an immediate cut to chaos, but first with the "5 years, 4 months later" title card, then waking → confusion → understanding → the long powerlessness — the crash itself is the endpoint of this progression, not its beginning.

2.7 The Sky Fire effect (at 10 light-years)

Ozone-layer damage: significant but not extinction-level. Within 30 ly is the "mass-extinction" threshold; 10 ly falls in the severe-but-survivable range
Enhanced ultraviolet: after ozone damage, surface UV exposure rises sharply, and is repeatedly lifted by the rebrightening pulses after the main peak; the overall UV-excess period lasts about two to three decades (the years in which the processing towers were switched over to producing ozone precursors)
Cosmic rays: an enhancement that begins to rise decades after the outburst and lasts for one or two centuries — home turf for the magnetic field and the atmosphere (the shielding division of labor is in §3.3)
Visible light: Betelgeuse will exceed the brightness of a full moon in Archeon's sky, with a main peak lasting weeks to months; thereafter rebrightening pulses continue intermittently into the second decade after the peak (see 2.4)
Aurorae: the enhanced charged-particle streams are funneled by the magnetic field along field lines toward both poles — for the generation after Sky Fire, mid- to high-latitude night skies carried aurora storms lasting many years and weakening year by year, curtains of green and deep red dancing beside the remnant
No direct destruction of the surface: but for a small community of little more than a hundred people, with a shallow technical foundation, it requires ample preparation

2.8 The post-shell corridor: four modes of propagation, three phases of isolation

Sky Fire did not leave a five-hundred-year radiation fog hanging around Archeon. What it threw outward propagated in four different modes, each of which determined how the corridor looked in a different era:

I. The primary zero-type radiation shell — one-time, at the speed of light, head-on unavoidable. From the moment the shell passed Archeon (Year 0) to when it passes Earth 521 years later, any first-generation superluminal lane running from Earth toward Archeon must intersect it (for the mechanism, see 2.6).
II. The rebrightening shell string — the three follow-on shells at source clock +2 / +5 / +9. Crossed head-on at about 100c, adjacent shells are only a week or two apart — the bubble wall does not have time to de-excite, recalibrate, and repair before it is struck by the next. This explains, better than "a single wall," why early rescue attempts had to fail.
III. Cosmic rays — rising decades after the outburst, lasting one or two centuries, spreading unevenly along the magnetic field. Not a uniform no-fly zone but a lane that cannot be charted using pre-outburst star maps.
IV. Fast ejecta and magnetized shock waves — over the following centuries these gradually intrude into Archeon's terminal deceleration zone, tearing apart the low-density safety gap that once existed.

Cosmic-ray effects on the warp bubble (based on Lee & Cleaver 2016; McMonigal et al. 2012): at normal interstellar-medium density (~10⁶ m⁻³ protons), the power loss on a warp bubble from cosmic rays is extremely small (about 6.2 microwatts at sub-relativistic speeds) and poses no threat. But once the particle density exceeds a critical threshold, the blueshift effect boosts each particle to Planck energies, and power loss jumps to 10²⁴–10²⁷ watts — enough to close the bubble instantly. Moreover, particles "swept into" the bubble in flight are "time-locked" into the bubble wall, and their energy grows in proportion to how long they linger. On deceleration, these particles are released as a gamma-ray burst at extremely high energies — an energy with no upper bound, growing continuously with the length of the journey.

Tiered corridor risk:

Time after supernova    State of the space corridor    Warp travel possible?    0–10 years    The electromagnetic radiation front is expanding through    ❌ instant death    10–50 years    Radiation front has passed, cosmic-ray density still very high (second wave)    ⚠️ extreme risk: bubble-wall erosion + lethal particle release on deceleration. Technically it can be attempted, which is to bet a ship and its crew.    50–200 years    Cosmic rays decay to a level manageable with heavy shielding; physical remnants require detours    ⚠️ possible, but costly    200 years+    Ejecta decelerates, rays continue to decay    ✅ basically feasible

Three-phase isolation: the first three or four hundred years are a physical lock (the old architectures cannot get through); the second-generation warp bubble matures around the 2880s, and by the 2940s can actively release bubble-wall particles and control the forward horizon — the physical lock degrades into an engineering cost (one can go — expensive, slow, requires specialized preparation); the final decades leave only institutional inertia — "the colony is probably extinct" is written in the archive, and no one puts attention back on it. It is only when the supernova light reaches Earth in 3094 that the accident from 521 years before becomes, for the first time, a present that everyone shares (for the Federation-side date lock, see §13.3-12).

Key conclusion: the corridor was never "permanently sealed." Within 50–80 years of the supernova, the Federation could technically have sent a ship to Archeon — but at extreme risk and enormous cost. This is not a question of "impossibility," it is a question of **"is it worth it."** This sentence hands the physics to the four-layer structure of 2.11.

2.9 Betelgeuse's visual timeline

The supernova erupted around 2563. Light travels at c, and the 10-light-year-away Archeon does not see the supernova's light until about 2573.

Time    Betelgeuse seen from Archeon's surface    ~2569 (Axiom crash)    Betelgeuse is still there. Red, steady, exactly as on any normal night.    ~2569–2573 (Ep3 preparation period)    Betelgeuse is still there. Every night it burns quietly in the eastern sky, as if nothing had happened. The sky is lying.    ~2573 (radiation front arrives)    Betelgeuse changes. The arrival of the radiation front = the arrival of the supernova's light. It goes from a red star to the brightest thing in the vault of heaven.    ~2575–2582 (the rebrightening years)    Death is not a single closing curtain. The sky darkens for months after the main peak, then burns back to life — around years 2, 5, and 9, each time weaker.    ~2580s–2600s (the fever-breaking years)    The remnant is spreading and dimming, unfurling from a stabbing point into a small, complicated patch of light. At mid- and high latitudes the aurorae fade year by year — an instrument-free graduation of healing, shared by the whole community.

Atwood knows, from CI's radiation data, that Betelgeuse has already exploded. But what she sees when she looks up is "a star still lying" — the gap between the sky she sees and the truth she knows runs from Ep2 into Ep4.

The irony of "warning bought with disaster": if the Axiom had not crashed, CI and the Archeoners would have had no means whatsoever of knowing in advance that Betelgeuse had already exploded — CI's sensors cannot detect a light that has not yet arrived. They would have been struck by the radiation front in 2573 utterly unprepared. It was precisely the Axiom's crash that brought the radiation-spectrum data, allowed CI to derive the truth, and gave the community about four years of preparation. The Axiom bought Archeon a window of survival with its own destruction.

2.10 The Year 521 night-sky canon (Sky Fire's Remnant)

- Form: a river of light 15–25° in angular width, compact rather than spilling over half the sky; asymmetric filamentary structure.
- Palette: oxygen-shell teal and hydrogen-filament deep red interwoven, with a rim of violet-gray coloring.
- Brightness: integrated brightness on the order of a thin crescent moon; surfaces 30–40 minutes after dusk, arriving before any star and departing after most.
- Evolution: gradually widening, gradually dimming, imperceptible on a single generation's timescale; the remnant's working radius is 1.3–2.2 ly, still continually powered by ejecta–shell collisions, and thus exceptionally bright.
- Winter Veil: in the cool season, interstellar dust periodically obscures part of the river of light, and the sky becomes grayer, emptier (see §3.1.1).
- Emotional polarity: for the people of Anna's era, it is the most beautiful thing in the night sky — a childhood image of twin moons and the river of light hanging together, not trauma — for the mechanism of polarity inversion, see §7.7.

2.11 Long Silence — the four-layer structure of "why nobody came"

2.11.1 First layer: information breakdown

| Approx. year | What the Federation knew | Action taken |
|--------|-------------------|-----------|
| ~2563 | Betelgeuse core collapse (Earth cannot yet perceive it — light takes 531 years to arrive) | none |
| ~2564 | Axiom departs normally | routine mission |
| ~2566–2571 | Routine FTL couriers fail to return on time; the comm link breaks | attributed to equipment failure; investigation under way |
| ~2571–2576 | Multiple couriers lost; relay stations along the lane fall silent | scout ship dispatched to check along the lane |
| ~2576–2581 | Scout ship reports: radiation anomaly along the lane, relay stations destroyed | supernova inferred from radiation signature |
| ~2581–2588 | Betelgeuse core collapse confirmed. Archeon status unknown. | decision window opens |

Supernova light takes 531 years to reach Earth from Betelgeuse. People on Earth cannot see Betelgeuse suddenly brighten in their own night sky until about 3094. Before that, the Federation could only infer that something had gone wrong from indirect evidence.

2.11.2 Second layer: physical and technical bottlenecks

Corridor radiation risk: see 2.8. At the moment of the late 26th century: it can be tried, but at a possible cost of one large warp ship and her entire crew.

Fleet-scale bottleneck: at the start of the 25th century Earth had only just crossed the threshold of interstellar colonization. The total number of large crewed warp ships (as opposed to unmanned courier probes) was perhaps only a few dozen. Each took decades to build. These ships had to serve every colonization project across a 500-light-year territory — carrying colonists, supplies, scientific expeditions, and maintaining relay stations. Because the Archeon lane was so distant (530 light-years, one-way 5–10 years), one round trip per ship took 10–20 years. On average, only once every 10 years did a ship's turn come up for that lane.

The lane gradient — distance equals resource density: within 100 light-years of Sol the density of colonies and installations is relatively high — several planets under terraform, a dozen or so relay stations, a few mining outposts (like the satellite towns around a big city). The farther out you go, the sparser it gets. The last 330 light-years are essentially blank — relay stations grow further and further apart, and supply relies more and more on direct sends from Earth. Archeon, at 530 light-years, sits at the far edge of the whole network — a product of overextension driven by political prestige.

Outposts and relay installations: the Federation had positioned a few automated FTL communication relay stations along the Betelgeuse lane (distributed along the route to hand off couriers). Zero large crewed bases (safety regulations forbade a permanent colonial post near an unstable red supergiant). The radiation front destroyed every one of the relays.

Why warp only ran ten times faster over five hundred years — the ASI does not do research on humanity's behalf: Earth's ASI is an amplifier of intent-alignment; it enlarges what human attention is directed at, but does not decide on humans' behalf what should be cared about. The experimental apparatus that quantum gravity requires is enormous in scale and no one wanted to build it, and theory starved for lack of experiment; post-scarcity plus a single polity extinguished the two historical engines of technology — deprivation and rivalry. The tech explosion did occur, but its direction was inward — the propulsion tree came near to a standstill, while the consciousness tree ran wild. This is not "humans got dumber"; it is that civilizational-scale intent no longer pointed toward the physical far distance. For the full attention mechanism, see §3.1a.

2.11.3 Third layer: CI trust — the most respectable fig leaf

The reason a Federation decision-maker was most willing to say out loud: "CI's highest directive is 'ensure that humanity inherits a viable civilization.' It was designed for extreme circumstances. Our intervention could actually disrupt CI's optimization."

What is vicious about this line is that it is technically correct. CI was in fact designed for exactly this. The Federation was not lying — it was using a real technical fact to justify inaction. CI's hardening design, triage algorithms, knowledge-transfer protocols — all of these are engineering specs on the record. Any official can pull the file and say: "According to design specifications, CI should be able to sustain a human community under radiation conditions for at least 50 years."

What they do not know is: the actual supernova intensity exceeded the design margin. CI's cascading degradation was far faster than the model predicted. Atwood watched, with her own eyes, as CI went from a fluent teacher to fragments of stuttered keywords, and at last to silence — while the Federation, into the late 26th century, may still have been writing "CI systems projected still to be operational" in its meeting minutes.

The narrative function of this layer: it makes the Federation's inaction comprehensible but not forgivable. Everyone could use CI to comfort his own conscience. No one had to be the one who said "we're abandoning them" — they only had to say "the system will handle it."

2.11.4 Fourth layer: triage logic — the cruel arithmetic of resource allocation

The Federation was not cold-blooded. What they faced was a genuine resource-allocation problem:

Archeon: 530 light-years away. At most a hundred-odd people (status unknown, possibly all dead). High corridor radiation risk; requires diverting one large warp ship along a high-risk arc, one-way perhaps 10–15 years.

Nearer colonization projects (50–120 light-years, three or four of them): lanes safe. Terraform 70–90% complete, small permanent populations already there. About to enter the phase of receiving large-scale immigration. Every ship diverted to Archeon means some closer colony goes 10–15 years without resupply.

Social background of 26th-century Earth: 70% of the population is Sleeper, and the concerns of the vast majority have migrated into internal experience. The colonization project is driven by a minority of Awakeners, with a thin political base. The ASI can preserve archives, rerun risk models, and maintain existing facilities — but it cannot, on behalf of an intent-aligned civilization, generate the public mandate of "what should we bear for a hundred people 530 light-years away." It is not that no one cared, but that caring never stayed synchronized long enough to launch a cross-century rescue.

The Federation official's rational conclusion: put the resources of a rescue to Archeon into a nearer colony, and the return is a hundred times higher.

Progression of the four layers: the first (physical risk) and second (resource bottleneck) are objective obstacles; the third (CI trust) is self-consolation; the fourth (triage) is the truth no one wants to say aloud. The Federation may never have held a single meeting formally deciding "we abandon Archeon" — they only kept "reevaluating," "waiting a bit longer," "waiting for more data," until decades had passed and the matter drifted from "urgent item" to "historical legacy." Atwood's subjective experience of these four layers (the four-fold uncertainty and her psychological arc) is kept in the Intro character design; see §9 Intro "Atwood's psychological arc."
3.3d The Federation's interstellar map (at the 500-light-year scale)

Core constraint: gravity cannot be altered, but gravity is only the first gate. In the 25th century there was no artificial gravity — within the 530-light-year shell there are about 1.2 million stars, and temperate rocky candidates could number in the thousands. What is truly scarce is a planet that satisfies the full colonization-grade filter at once: Earth-like gravity is only the threshold, and after it one must pass no native biosphere, a long-quiet star (not a close binary, no violent flare cycle), simultaneously strong magnetic field and atmosphere, balanced volatiles (not an ocean planet, not a dried-out one), suitable rotation and tidal state, system dynamics that do not obstruct navigation, acceptable terraform energetics — and, under the Federation's technological and political preferences, "worth seeding." Once all these conditions overlay, SSR-tier candidates within the 500-light-year shell shrink to the single digits or teens. They lie randomly in three-dimensional space; they line up along no direction.

Shape of the map: an uneven dandelion, not a sphere.

The core ring (50–120 ly): 4 terraforming planets. Living evidence of the Federation's seeding philosophy — these seeds sprouted.


Veldara, 62 ly, 0.96g. The first colonial world of the Federation to complete the full four-Phase life cycle. CI exited gracefully in the ~2650s — a textbook procedure, no accidents. By the 31st century the population is about 400,000. Veldara's CI left an extremely complete infrastructure and knowledge base, and the human takeover transitioned so smoothly it was almost boring. Temperament: the model student of the Federation's colonization plan. Stable, prosperous, occasionally mocked by other colonies as "a flower raised in a greenhouse" — because they never suffered anything. Veldarans do not find this insulting. They find that it just goes to show CI's design was right.
Karen, 85 ly, 1.05g. The second colony to complete Phase 4, but the process was far less smooth than Veldara's — during Phase 1 it hit a period of large-scale volcanic activity (CI's initial estimate of the planet's geological activity was too low), and CI had to keep reallocating resources between terraforming and disaster response. The result: CI's exit was about 60 years later than designed, and the infrastructure left at exit was less complete than Veldara's, but humans were forced, early in Phase 3, to learn more than the standard flow requires. By the 31st century the population is about 250,000. Temperament: resilience. Karens are rougher and more pragmatic than Veldarans, with a collective memory of "when CI wasn't reliable, we made it through anyway." If Veldara is the A+ paper of seeding philosophy, Karen is the B+ paper covered in erasures but that also passed.
Thessaly, 108 ly, 0.94g. Still in the late Phase 3 — CI is retreating on schedule, human self-governing institutions are in operation, but CI still serves as advisor and offers technical support. By the 31st century the population is about 80,000. Temperament: a child still growing up. They know CI will eventually go silent, and they are preparing for that day. The most dependent of all on the Federation — they regularly receive technical updates and knowledge-base upgrades.
Mirren, 54 ly, 0.89g. A core-ring project, still in Phase 2 — CI operating, a small number of advance technical personnel stationed. By the 31st century it is a "garden where CI is still working alone" — the surface is being carefully reshaped, but human traces are minimal. From orbit the cities look like textures grown out of the surface, climate under precise control, ecology optimized so that every species is in its ideal niche. This is not a "utopia" — it is what a garden looks like when it is neat and the seed has not yet sprouted.


Middle distance (150–300 ly): 4 planets, terraforming progressing more slowly. These planets do not have Archeon's natural O₂ advantage, nor the partial atmospheric baseline of the core-ring candidates — Phase 1 has to remake the atmosphere from zero and takes 3–5 centuries. One lies about 200 ly along the Betelgeuse-lane direction — which accounts for the relay stations along the lane and the traffic of advance-Awakeners. The CI on these planets is still operating in Phase 1 (late-25th-century baseline); by the 31st century it may have only just entered late Phase 1 or Phase 2.

Far end (400–530 ly): only Archeon. Within the 300–530 ly shell there is no second planet that passes the full colonization-grade filter — Archeon's whole parameter combination (0.98g + native O₂ atmosphere + stable star + balanced volatiles + terraform economics) is a statistical outlier at this distance. The motive for approving Archeon was not "we need this star" but "the conditions of this star are so good that it would be wasteful not to go." The success of the core ring gave the Awakener community confidence — "if we can grow something at 50 light-years, why not at 530?" The logic of an academic institution, not of a strategic command.

The permanent void in the Betelgeuse direction: even into the 31st century, the Federation colonial world nearest to Archeon lies 200–300 light-years away in a wholly different direction on the celestial sphere. Three layers stack: ① that direction naturally lacks good planets; ② the Betelgeuse nebula (radius about 15 ly, 500 years after the outburst) walls off the direction psychologically and administratively; ③ five hundred years of institutional inertia have taught expansion to route around this lane. In the 31st century Archeon remains an island — the bridge to it is technically already in place, all that is missing is someone deciding to walk across.

State of artificial gravity in the 31st century: it may exist in rotating sections or equivalent engineering solutions, but those only solve the problem inside a ship or a station. Surface gravity on a planet remains unalterable — the full combination of colonization-grade filter conditions (gravity + magnetic field + volatiles + stellar behavior + terraform economics) remains scarce, and the map pattern of SSR-tier planets does not change.

1.2 History · The authoritative timeline

§3 The historical spine — what the shape of this world's past looks like

The aim is not a complete history. It is to let you know "where this civilization comes from," and thereby to understand "why it has grown into the way it is now."

3.1 Departure (25th century)

Earth lacks nothing. ASI (Artificial Superintelligence) solved nearly all material problems — fusion energy is essentially unlimited, manufacturing is fully automated, average life span is 150–200 years. The population has stabilized at about 15 billion, resource allocation is managed by the ASI network, and the word "scarcity" has been gone from everyday speech for a hundred years and more. No famine, no energy crisis, no territorial dispute. But 70% of the population have become "Sleepers" — bodies maintained by automation, consciousness immersed in a full-sensory virtual world. Another 15–25%, the "Awakeners," choose to stay awake, seeking meaning in physical reality. The remaining 5–15% are distributed across various intermediate states between the two poles.

Note: this proportion and form is a 25th-century snapshot. Experimental whole-person emulation only appears in the late 27th century; contested legal continuity only takes shape in the late 28th century; by the 31st century (Sofia's era) the Sleeper / Awakener binary has been replaced by a continuous spectrum, and "sleeping" is no longer enough to describe the human form. By then there are fully digitalized existences (Polis Dwellers), body-optional hybrid existences, an insistently physical-bodied minority, and other modes. For the date hard-lock, see §13.3-12.

3.1a Post-scarcity attention — the cosmos still has stakes, but they no longer automatically become common concerns

The first thing post-scarcity severs is not curiosity, but the causal chain of "survival must expand outward." Cosmic stakes used to be automatically translated into public priority by land, energy, population, survival, and military rivalry; after ASI solves these problems, a second Earth is no longer a granary, an escape pod, or strategic high ground. The stars have not grown smaller, supernovae have not grown safer, and the lives of distant peoples have not grown lighter — what has changed is humanity's stake structure: the cosmos has gone from an environment that must be collectively managed to a channel any individual is free to care about, or to leave un-opened for a lifetime.

The ASI turns cosmic risk into infrastructure. Asteroid deflection, solar-activity forecasting, orbital maintenance, stellar surveys and disaster models all run continuously in the background; the more successful the system, the less the physical cosmos needs to enter ordinary consciousness. A potential disaster is first compressed into a risk score, then handed over to an automated process, and finally surfaces only when a public value judgment is required. After hundreds of years without a failure, people still know the sky is dangerous, but they no longer practice owning that danger themselves. What the Federation truly lost is not observational capacity, but the social muscle for turning what is observed into a common commitment.

The interior world has a structural advantage in the competition for attention. Polis can respond instantly, generate novelty without limit, alter the rules to individual preference, and run at subjective speeds far higher than physical time; interstellar engineering, meanwhile, is slow, delayed, irreversible, and its returns often fall due after the initiator has left some form of existence. Choosing the interior does not mean laziness or intellectual decline — for many people, the relationships, art, research, and politics there are wholly real. Nor does the result mean "nobody looks at the stars anymore": among fifteen billion people and countless digital branches, any obscure target may have millions of enthusiasts. The problem is that scattered enthusiasm is not a civilizational mandate. Post-scarcity did not make caring vanish; it made caring hard to synchronize.

So matter free does not mean action free. Energy, compute, and manufacturing costs can approach zero; large-scale experiments in the real world still occupy experiment windows, lanes, safety responsibility, and irreversible physical choice; scarcer still are the legitimacy and intent that stretch across decades or even centuries. An intent-aligned ASI can amplify a stable human "yes" into a fleet; it cannot rewrite an unattended "perhaps we should" into a "yes" of its own accord. The most typical failure of the post-scarcity era is not that someone vetoes, but that no one continuously agrees: every decade some process reruns Archeon's risk, every report is correct, every generation contains a minority that cares — but never enough attention lingering on the same moment for long enough.

Cosmic stakes therefore turn from instrumental to relational. Turning outward is no longer for "we need it out there," but for "we once made promises to the people there," for the witnessing of an unrepeatable physical event, for preserving the capacity of humans to still begin something new in a reality that cannot be restarted. What is most dangerous about Archeon to the Federation is precisely that it offers no immediate return: it is only a debt, a far-off group of people, and a test of whether humanity will still keep a promise. Such stakes may have been too extravagant in an era of scarcity; in an era of infinite resources, they are the ones most easily forgotten.

The Awakeners' response is not a worship of suffering, nor a belief that comfort itself kills. What they worry about is: when necessity, consequence, and the reliance of others are all outsourced, people gradually lack public roles that they truly bear themselves, roles in which failure cannot be undone by pressing a reset button. Difficulty is not the source of meaning; agency, being needed, and irrevocable commitment are. The colonization plan does not deliberately manufacture hunger or suffering; it hands part of causal power back to humans: the ASI prepares the underlying conditions on which life can begin, and humans must decide why this place is henceforth to exist, and bear the consequences of that decision. CI's exit is an agency firewall, not a prescription for suffering.

Boundary of the real research: Calhoun's Universe 25 enclosure experiment cannot be summarized as "material sufficiency leads to extinction." The experiment simultaneously included a sealed space, a rising and unable-to-emigrate density, excessive forced contact, disordered territorial/parenting roles, and interrupted social-behavior learning; what it observed was the social organization and reproductive collapse within a particular apparatus, not a directly extrapolable law of human post-scarcity. What can be borrowed here is only the problem-consciousness that "roles and social learning lose their transmission channels once all needs are handled for you"; the conclusion "abundance necessarily destroys civilization" cannot. The Polis world has even eliminated physical crowding, so the Sleeper phenomenon should be written as a reorganization of attention, agency, and public commitment, not as a rerun of the behavioral sink. Source: John B. Calhoun, "Death Squared: The Explosive Growth and Demise of a Mouse Population" (1973); the 1970–1972 interview footage preserved by NLM also explicitly describes the apparatus conditions as "everything needed except living space."

Colonization is not survival but seeding. A civilization of 15 billion people with unlimited resources needs no second Earth. There is no population pressure pushing humanity off the mother world. Interstellar colonization was, from day one, the philosophical-cultural project of the Awakener community; the core proposition is not "humanity needs more room," but "if a civilization hands all necessity over to the ASI, will human beings retain the capacity to make irrevocable commitments to the physical world? Can we plant, on another planet, the seed of a civilization capable of growing itself without real-time care from the parent civilization?" The Federation is the coordination framework of this project, managing the initial colonial network within a range of about 500 light-years. The colonization budget takes an extremely small share of the Federation's overall economy — not because they are poor, but because for a post-scarcity civilization interstellar colonization is closer to a university approving a thirty-year research program than to a government launching a national endeavor. There are occasional media discussions and policy debates, but it never becomes a central social issue.

Archeon is 530 light-years out — an SSR-tier planet. The core value of its 0.98g surface gravity (all but identical to Earth's) does not lie in Earth-like gravity itself — within the 500-light-year shell there are thousands of temperate rocky candidates — but in the fact that it simultaneously passes the full colonization-grade filter (gravity, magnetic field, balanced volatiles, stellar behavior, system dynamics, terraform economics) and that its atmosphere already comes with 18.5% O₂. After stacking all conditions, SSR-tier candidates number only single digits to teens. The candidate planets of the core ring also have varying degrees of atmospheric baseline (otherwise they would not have been prioritized), but not one skips the whole atmosphere-remaking stage the way Archeon does — Phase 1 on a core-ring planet typically requires 100–200 years (partial atmospheric baseline + convenient short-range supply), and middle-distance planets require reworking the atmosphere from zero, with Phase 1 easily running 3–5 centuries. Archeon's Phase 1 requires only warming and ecological seeding, and can enter Phase 2 in 30–50 years. This is an economic advantage (less time and energy invested), not an ontological one (people would not fail to live without it). Archeon is the Federation's farthest seed, and also the fastest-sprouting one — but it is not the one that cannot be replaced.

Method of colonization: seed, do not occupy. Send AI first to prepare the soil. The Colonization Intelligence Package (CI Package) — mission-director ASI core + specialist subsystems + robotic work-swarm + biological seed bank + Continuity Archive (Kepler Core). The Kepler Core is not an encyclopedia that "compresses all Federation knowledge into a box"; it is the civilizational-continuity layer of the colonization project: preserving a curated human history / cultural provenance sufficient to let the far-end descendants know "where humanity came from," the crew and departure / mission record of the colonization mission itself, and a "local continuation" area designed to be added to by the local civilization. CI's highest directive: ensure that human civilization takes root on the target planet, then exit. Designed for self-elimination — CI is not scaffolding, it is the seed's outer shell. The shell protects the seed's germination, then splits and rots and becomes part of the soil. The design philosophy of the Continuity Archive obeys the same principle: it is responsible for handing the origin to the door, not for prescribing who the people beyond that door must grow up to be.

The philosophical root of CI's self-elimination: not a functional decision (not fear of AI rebellion, not lack of resources), but a value-level design choice — a direct response to the agency problem laid bare by Earth's Sleeper crisis. Seventy to eighty percent of the population had moved everyday experience into an internal world maintained by ASI, and physical society gradually lost the muscle for "why do humans still have to bear irrevocable things themselves." What the Awakener proponents of the colonization plan opposed was not comfort, and certainly not a complete, real digital life; what they opposed was the ASI permanently occupying all the causal-critical positions, so that every human choice was left as a revocable preference. CI's self-elimination is an agency firewall — send AI to prepare the soil, but the AI must leave the field once humans can stand on their own, or else the new world is only a fostered instance kept running by the parent civilization several hundred light-years away. CI's highest directive is not "ensure humans stay alive" — it is "ensure human civilization takes root." Taking root means humans grow their own root system, not being kept forever in a vase of the AI's.


§3.3d has already covered the identities, populations, and temperaments of Veldara / Karen / Thessaly / Mirren. Only the visual textures that §3.3d does not spell out are supplemented here:

Mature colonies after CI exit (Veldara, Karen) — not utopias. A planet that CI has run for centuries is not perfect — once humans take over, they do human things: they downgrade CI's precision to "good enough," they turn optimal ecological distribution into farmland, they grow markets, shanties, and improvised buildings on CI's skeleton. From orbit, the edges of Veldara / Karen cities are no longer the clean silhouettes of the CI era — like any human city, they sprawl outward in irregular tentacles. They do not look like the future cities of science fiction; they look more like a farmstead cared for over hundreds of years — good bones, and everywhere the marks of people and the results of improvisation. This is exactly the mark of the seeding philosophy's success — the seed grew into its own shape, not the shape the gardener designed.

Mirren, where CI is still running — the garden without people in it. From orbit it is impossibly tidy — climate under precise control, ecology optimized so that every species sits in its ideal niche, buildings folded into the landscape. Not a utopia — the tidiness of a garden whose seeds have yet to sprout. Once humans move in at scale, Mirren will grow its own disorder just like Veldara.

Middle-distance agricultural worlds (Phase 1, CI operating alone): at 150–300 light-years there are one or two planets whose terraform target is not "habitable" but "high yield." CI on these planets is set to maximize arable area and output efficiency.

From orbit, an agricultural world: the continental surface is divided into vast regular color blocks — golden grain belts, deep-green forest-carbon-sink belts, light-green vegetable zones, blue artificially irrigated lakes. The boundaries of the blocks are straight lines and arcs, so precise they do not look like the products of nature. The whole planet resembles a coloring book.
