On the ground: endless wheatfields extending to the horizon. No fences — no animals that need to be fenced in. Unmanned harvester swarms move through the waves of wheat like schools of fish, keeping precise spacing between each machine. The air smells of dry grain and sun-warmed soil — a smell very like the one in the fields of Archeon's Basin Seven, but Archeon's version has an added layer of the scent of people and thorn-goats; the agricultural world's version is unrealistically clean.

The sole "city" is the harvest processing center — enormous silos and processing facilities, run by a few resident hybrid-existence administrators and a great many unmanned systems. Next to the center is a small residential zone, perhaps 200–500 people, the only place on the whole planet with "streets" and "windows." Whether these agricultural worlds, after completing Phase 1, will move on to the standard seeding process (embryo activation, CI graceful retreat) or remain as unmanned agricultural bases, is still, in the 31st-century Federation, a matter of internal debate.

The Federation's standard colonization life cycle (four-Phase model):


Phase 1: CI stand-alone period (standard about 30–50 years; Archeon's actual about 100, see below). CI Package arrives, begins terraform. No humans present. CI has full authority — warming the planet, starting the water cycle, building infrastructure, making soil, seeding a biosphere. (Archeon's peculiarity: atmospheric O₂ was already in place, and the emphasis of Phase 1 was warming and ecological construction rather than atmospheric remake, with less time pressure than a standard terraform project.)
Phase 2: Advance-assessment period (~5–10 years). A transport ship brings a dozen or so technical personnel for on-site assessment. CI still manages everything, but teaching begins — "every time a human learns to do something, that is one thing less for CI to do."
Phase 3: Colony-ship arrival + CI graceful retreat (~50–100 years). Colonists arrive at scale. CI hands off functions on a schedule — city planning to human committees, agricultural management to agronomists, education to human teachers. With each handoff, CI degrades in that domain. The trigger is not time, but capability milestones — "when humans can independently do X, CI stops doing X." Finally CI enters advisor mode.
Phase 4: CI silence + human self-governance (permanent). CI exits completely. The colony becomes a self-governing society.


The Federation is not an empire but a coordination framework. It does not send governors to run colonies. It does three things: maintain the FTL communications relay network, coordinate colony-ship scheduling, and provide technical standards and knowledge-base updates. In late Phase 3 the colony elects self-governing institutions; the Federation retains communication and trade relations but does not interfere in internal affairs — closer to the Commonwealth than to a colonial empire.

The AI configuration of a mature colony: it can receive technical updates, knowledge-base upgrades, and narrow specialist AIs (medical AI, engineering AI) from the Federation, but no more ASI-level "administrators." The premise of the whole colonization philosophy is: the ASI is the seed's shell, not the plant itself. Once the shell splits, human society runs on its own, makes its own mistakes, and makes its own choices.

Archeon's anomaly has two layers. The first is intentional: Archeon's Phase 1 was stretched to about a century (2472–2573), of which about 78 years were fully unmanned — this was not a process delay, it was the joint result of the Betelgeuse gate and the engineering choice of "lay the substrate across half the planet first, then bring people in" (for details see 3.12–3.13). Humans were therefore not put into a basin, but into the most mature corner of a lattice already laid across half the planet. The second is accidental: humans had been activated for only just over a decade, and Phase 2 had barely begun, when Sky Fire arrived. The "sustained technical input" that the standard process assumes was cut off entirely, and decades of graceful retreat were compressed into a cascading collapse. Yet CI's design was, under extreme stress-testing, unexpectedly validated twice — it taught them enough, and then the lights went out; and the water, soil, forest, wells, and coordinates it laid down went on working in its place for five hundred years after its death. Five hundred and twenty-one years later, they built Frontier City, The Loom, and the canal system of Riverbend. CI's designers likely never imagined their work would be verified this way.

CI arrives early; humans arrive very late. The seed fleet first confirms the abiotic oxygen, then, using orbital survey, seed foundry, energy, mining, water cycle, soil-making, and seeding, lays down the planetary substrate; a small number of advance Awakeners and embryonic individuals enter only in the later stages of engineering, in the most mature core. When the Axiom survivors arrive, what they fall into is a lattice already laid across half the planet, and yet with only a very few places truly inhabited; four years later Sky Fire cuts off sustained input just as Phase 2 has begun. For absolute years, cohort ages, and population working values see §3.12; for pre-Sky Fire ecological state, see §3.14.


3.2 The Crash (around 2569)

Why "one hundred thousand years" became zero: Betelgeuse's hidden companion

Betelgeuse has a low-mass companion (~0.5–0.8 M☉, lighter and dimmer than the Siwarha confirmed in the real world in 2025). In the 21st century, when human astronomy was active, the companion's orbital radius was about 10 AU — well outside Betelgeuse's photosphere (~3.5 AU), with no atmospheric interaction, no detectable wake or spectral anomaly. Eight to nine magnitudes dimmer than Betelgeuse (luminosity only 1/1500–1/4000 of Betelgeuse) — invisible to human telescopes. Betelgeuse's own convective noise (surface velocity variations of 5–20 km/s) completely drowned out the sub-km/s radial-velocity wobble the companion induced.

In the 22nd–23rd centuries, tidal decay pulled the orbit in to about 7 AU, and the companion began to scrape into the edge of Betelgeuse's expanding outer atmosphere. But at that time human society was going through a transition period of ASI management + Sleeper culture — the priority of basic astronomical monitoring was far below that of colonial engineering and social governance. Even if an automated sky-survey system noticed a new long-period photometric mode appearing in Betelgeuse, it was archived as "normal-evolution parameters of a red supergiant," and no one pursued it.

In the 24th–25th centuries, gas drag drove orbital decay into positive feedback — the deeper into the atmosphere, the greater the drag, and the faster the decay. In the several hundreds to thousands of years before collapse, the companion cast off large amounts of dense hydrogen circumstellar shell material, providing the supernova with a "fuel" not foreseen by the standard model.

The merger completes around 2563, the core evolution is stirred and accelerated, and it collapses.

Two layers of "unexpected" stacked:


Timing — the double failure of double safety confirmation. The Federation approved the Archeon project (~2450s) on the basis of a single-star evolutionary model that gave a >100,000-year margin to core collapse. After CI arrived in Archeon's orbit in ~2470s, it did a spectral reconfirmation — neutrino flux at Betelgeuse's core, elemental abundance ratios, nucleosynthesis signatures all showed the star still in late helium burning or on the eve of carbon burning, at least several thousand years from core collapse. Not calculation but direct observation of the nucleosynthesis clock. Theoretical + on-site observational double sign-off. The acceleration of carbon-neon-oxygen-silicon burning stages caused by the companion merger played out over the following decades, and the compressed signature lay in the observational noise band of "normal red-supergiant late-stage fluctuations," entirely unrecognizable. The Federation failed not by miscalculating, but because the era's observational precision was insufficient to see the signal.
Intensity — the mismatch in orders of magnitude between engineering margin and hazard level. The Axiom's lane passed 5 ly from Betelgeuse — by the theoretical safety margin of standard Type II-P at 4 ly × 2 + CI-confirmed millennial clock, this was textbook conservative engineering. Supporting constraint: the low-ISM-density clean corridor along the Earth→Archeon direction just happens to pass near Betelgeuse; a detour through a dirtier region would in fact have been more dangerous. But in ~150 years of Federation warp flight history there had been zero actual SN encounters — all hardening metrics were pure theoretical calculation. The external-medium + radiation environment of the Type IIn mechanism pushed the most critical high-energy parameters of the warp safety model (bubble-wall stability boundary, safe route geometry) an order of magnitude above the standard Type II-P baseline in effective hazard. A 2× theoretical margin and a millennial evolutionary clock both went to zero in the face of that effective hazard.


The round-trip information blackout: the Archeon lane is 530 light-years, one-way at 100c is 5.3 years, and a full round-trip cycle is about 12–15 years. The previous routine round-trip report — "all is normal" — was made around the 2550s (the advance ship's return leg), and the Axiom departed around 2564. Betelgeuse collapsed around 2563 — falling perfectly into the information blind spot between two round trips. Earth cannot know (light takes 531 years to arrive), the Axiom is causally isolated inside its warp bubble and cannot receive any external information; perhaps some earlier colonies already knew, but the warp-blockade problem and the earlier attempts at rescue both showed the difficulty of an anomalous supernova outburst.

The Axiom (a 900-meter supply ship, carrying 50–80 people including Eleanor Atwood) departs Earth around 2564. Around 2569.3 it meets the primary radiation shell head-on at the old-corridor dogleg node about 6.3 ly from Betelgeuse and about 3.7 ly from Archeon — the bubble's active control layer is breached, and it becomes a dead ship sliding along the pre-set world line. After drifting about three weeks, it skims into Archeon's atmosphere at an extremely shallow angle, breaks in two over the Great Ochre Desert, and plows a 100-km-long wound — the Axiom Scar. About 20–30 survivors, including Atwood.

The ironic gift: the Axiom's crash lets CI deduce, from the residual radiation spectrum, that Betelgeuse has already exploded — giving about four years of warning. Without the Axiom, when the radiation front arrived in 2573 they would have been utterly unprepared. The Axiom bought Archeon a window of survival with its own destruction.

The Archeon-side emergency courier ships: CI's original configuration included a small number of FTL courier probes. One of the design intentions was: if CI detected an anomaly in Betelgeuse's neutrino spectrum (hinting at accelerated core evolution), it would immediately launch a courier along a bypass arc toward the Earth-direction lane as warning. But CI, at Archeon, could only see the Betelgeuse of 10 years ago — the speed-of-light delay rendered the failsafe useless in this event. These probes are used by Atwood in Ep3B to send Archeon's final call for help to Earth.

3.3 Sky Fire and the Withering of CI (2573–about 2673)

Around 2573, the radiation front arrives — Sky Fire, Year 0. Betelgeuse turns from a red star into the brightest thing in the vault of the sky — ozone-layer damage, greatly increased UV exposure. Severe but not extinction-level. And not one-off: the sky darkens for a few months after the main peak, then re-brightens — around years 2, 5, and 9 after the main peak (2575 / 2578 / 2582) burning back each time, each time weaker than the last (for the multi-shell mechanism see 1.1 Supernova Physics §2.4). The first rebrightening occurred just as people were beginning to repair the surface; CI failed to forecast it. Each rebrightening ate away another layer of margin in CI's electronic systems — the direct cause of CI's above-model degradation was not the main peak, but the total duration of the soaking.

Ecological shock: the surge in UV exposure caused leaf scorching and a collapse of photosynthetic efficiency in shallow-rooted herbs and exposed shrubs. The radiation-resistant genes of the CI-modified species played a critical role: bee-swarm survival was far higher than expected, the DNA repair mechanism of the modified wheat kept the fields from a total loss; mosses and lichens were nearly unaffected. The atmosphere-processing towers' electronics were damaged by the radiation pulses, but their physical structures remained — CI prioritized keeping the towers running, and their core function shifted from fine tuning of the atmosphere to catalyzing ozone-layer repair by injecting ozone precursors into the stratosphere. Archeon's own magnetic field (about 2–3 times Earth's, from a metal-rich dense core) was entirely ineffective against Phase 1 EM radiation — electromagnetic waves are not bound by the magnetic field, and light, UV, and X-rays all passed straight through. But in the decades to a century or two of sustained cosmic-ray cascade of Phase 2, the magnetic field and the atmosphere form a shield with a clearly divided labor: the magnetic field is the first sieve — a stronger magnetic moment lifts the cutoff rigidity, deflecting away the low- to mid-energy charged particles that make up most of the flux, and greatly reducing atmospheric ionization, NOx production (the ozone's secondary killer), and surface-dose elevation; the truly high-energy end no magnetic field can stop, and is absorbed hard by an air column of about one atmosphere (equivalent to the shielding thickness of ten meters of water). The deflected particles are funneled along magnetic field lines toward both poles — for a generation after Sky Fire, mid- to high-latitude night skies carried aurora storms lasting many years and weakening year by year, curtains of green and deep red dancing beside the remnant; people watched the aurora fade year by year and treated it as the sky's thermometer showing the fever coming down. Sky Fire was got through by engineering and by the modified species; the Long Silence was got through by geology and by the atmosphere. O₂ itself is not the crisis (the planetary abiotic baseline of 18.5% is sufficient for breathing), but the true fragility of vegetation cover (~25%) is in food chain and soil stability — if vegetation dies over a large area, the thin soil layer accumulated over 30 years will be eroded to zero, and ecology building must start over.

Then comes CI's withering. It is not killed with a single hammer blow — it is a matter of turning off the lights one room at a time (2582–2623 fragmentation; 2623–2673 the will-and-testament period and regional silence, out of sync from region to region, no single global date). Supply chains break, precision hardware cannot be replaced. Language capability degrades from fluent sentences to short lines, keywords, pure data output. Atwood becomes the key translator. In its final days CI engraves knowledge onto metal plates and stone slabs — it knows that digital storage will also decay; only stone and metal can last five hundred years. And at last, silence.

The second half of CI's will: a map. What people most often remember CI carving into a metal plate is technology — reactor operating sequences, alloy formulas, hydraulic data. But CI, in triage, gave equal or higher priority to another class of thing: coordinates, well positions, assay records, river-modification reaches, tree-belt lineages, and the purpose and last known state of every secondary station. It had reasoned one thing through: what it built would outlive it, and humans, for decades to come, would not have the capacity to visit most of it. So it took "where what is" to be an inheritance more worth leaving than "how to build."

This set of coordinates uses CI's own planetary grid (today MAP-G0's 10° parent cells are its direct descendants), and it became one of the ancestors of the first layer of public geographical memory: CI grid → Atwood ledger → First Thread hand-drawn charts → local revisions → private maps. But by Anna's era the map has long since grown far larger than CI's will. Some dashed lines do begin with a line of metal-plate coordinates; others come from later-discovered wind gaps, ore beds, seasonal water, marriage lines, old settlements, an accidentally successful detour flight, an abandoned mooring, or a route first flown through in its entirety only decades ago.

The map is not CI metadata cashed out item by item over five hundred years. CI provided only the first draft; the following five centuries have gone on manufacturing new geographical facts, and have also gone on invalidating old ones. So the climax of a survey cannot always be "it turns out CI wrote this here long ago"; it can also be "CI did not write of this at all; the world went on changing after it fell silent." Those who re-read old coordinates are called surveyors / prospectors, not scavengers; but reading coordinates is not a precondition for a place to acquire meaning.

3.4 Branching: from the planetary substrate to Archeon engineering (Year 0 to about 110, 2573 to about 2683)

Archeon's technological change is not "a slide down from Federation high tech into cobbling scraps together," but a branching. What the Federation left behind is a planetary substrate; after CI's death, what the locals lose is a planet-sized industrial stack, not knowledge, materials, or electricity. What they face is a blueprint that requires a whole industrial city to be honored, and so they chose the branch that can be endlessly repaired using local ore, local furnace, and local hands. What grew out over five hundred years is called Archeon engineering, not a stripped-down Federation.

Branching is a decision, not entropy. What was kept and went on growing is knowledge that could be cross-checked with local materials, instruments, and repeated practice: pressure and thermodynamics, buoyancy as weight management, metallurgy and alloy batches, hydraulics and agronomy, meteorology and wind field, vacuum-tube-grade radio, glass, ceramics, basalt fiber. What was let go of is everything above the semiconductor — the whole information-processing layer — not because of ignorance, but because on the ground no process could reproduce it (the mechanism is registered only once in §6A.2 "Electronic-capability freezing line" and is not repeated here). For the first generations, this choice was not a single meeting; it was countless everyday judgments — "this one is still fixable, that one is not" — that accumulated into a default. A pressure-and-power-system craftsman can hear a resonance anomaly in a fusion reactor no worse than a sensor array. Apprenticeship replaces digital education; knowledge is moved from systems back into people's brains and hands (Atwood's ledger, on-site teaching, error records, straight-line lettering). Humans have grown an organic life-layer of their own upon CI's substrate. The Loom, as a crystallization of social structure, and the airship network as the vessels of the civilization — but that comes after Year 50; for the causal chain see §7.3.

Ecological ceiling, not famine. After CI went silent, the atmosphere-processing towers gradually lost automated control, and by around Year 50 were fully shut down. O₂ itself is not the issue — the planet's abiotic baseline of 18.5% is enough to breathe, and this floor depends on no artificial system or biology. The true constraint is the young ecological base: the towers shutting down means the interruption of ozone-repair catalysis (natural recovery of the Sky Fire-damaged ozone layer takes centuries) → UV exposure stays high → vegetation expansion is capped in speed → soil formation slows below erosion → in each generation, the distance one can push arable land outward has a ceiling. At the same time, the greenhouse-gas balance the towers used to fine-tune has slipped from control, and local temperature swings and extreme weather have increased. In this window (about Year 0–130), Archeon is neither suffocating nor starving — it is hitting a ceiling: population can grow, but each generation can only grow to the point the local halo will bear, and the surplus has to go to some other halo. The engineering philosophy of "break the giants, grow them sideways" is not for making oxygen; it is for making soil, farmland, and the next patch of arable land. Every generation pushes vegetation a little farther out — not planting oxygen, but planting tomorrow's bread. The world is abundant by default; what is scarce is the routes that carry surplus elsewhere.

Halo islands, not blue circles. Every generation pushes tree and grass seed outward. Wherever an irrigation channel reaches, that is where trees are planted. Small scrubber towers' ozone-repair halos overlay with the ecological-stability halos of vegetation, gradually joining into patches ("halos overlapped") — this is the fundamental mechanism for why Threads are dense here, and the cause of the mature air-route ring in central-western Aethelgard. But it explains one patch, not a whole planet. The correct picture is not a Main Habitable Zone shaded outward from a center, but dozens to hundreds of isolated halo islands: each grown outward around a surviving patch of CI substrate, with tens to hundreds of kilometers of empty ground between islands. A byproduct of vegetation expansion is a slow rise in global O₂: from the abiotic baseline of 18.5% up to the 19.5–20.5% common in populated areas of Anna's era — that percentage point or two is the five hundred years of biospheric photosynthetic contribution, but it does not lock the whole planet to a single number (§4.1a). The blanks between halo islands are precisely the precondition for §7.3, where airships had to appear.

Greenhouse-gas decay and slow cooling (narrative dormancy) — the long-term pressure lever: the warming mixtures injected in Phase A are decaying stepwise on their physical timescales: N₂O with a century-scale lifetime (essentially exhausted by Anna's era), the SF₆ family with a millennial one (still holding, but on the way down). After the towers shut down, no one is topping them up. The consequence is a ~500-year-scale global cooling — ice is quietly creeping south, and the polar ice caps are reclaiming, inch by inch, the territory Phase A had taken from them. This is the cleanest sort of long-term pressure in the world-view: a clock without a villain. Thematically it aligns tightly with the CI arc — the planet's warmth is itself a legacy decaying on its designed lifetime.
In Arc 1 the cooling does not appear overtly, at most as a deniable aside — an old man saying that in his grandfather's stories the Winter Veil seemed shorter. The felt change in Anna's era is imperceptible on a single-generation scale — and that is precisely what is terrifying about it. Only in a later arc does this lever enter the foreground.

Robotic exit — the physical starting point of the branching. CI's silence is not the starting point of the branching; the last working robot stopping is. The robotic work-swarm degrades about one phase behind CI's core: after CI's fragmentation it retreats into local autonomous mode, repeating the last task list received; after CI's silence it runs a purely local loop, with each unit stopping one at a time as mechanical wear takes its toll, with no high-end spares to be had; from about Year 50–140 (2623–2713) most are stopped, and at the very end a few of the structurally simplest water pumps and conveyors linger on, some cannibalized by humans for parts; thereafter, manual labor returns across the board (for detail, see §9 Intro 4.2). From that day forward humans rely entirely on themselves — and from that day forward, too, they truly begin to own their own technology.

Year 110 state: humans know several thousand coordinates but have only the population and transport capacity to use a dozen or so. Halo islands are separated by hundreds of kilometers, and on the ground there is no inherited road network to inherit — CI never needed roads. This is the starting point of §7.3: the first lines. The rest of the five-hundred-year causal prose (the first lines, the knots in the sky, the loom becoming a civilization, the second reaching-out, the age of exploration) all hang from §7 and are not repeated here.


3.12 Absolute Timeline

This section is the sole AD-year absolute date index for the whole text. Other chapters reference individual events or register their own narrative windows; the relative calendar of civilizational phases uniformly obeys §7.

About 2450s: long-distance spectroscopy lists Archeon as an anomaly candidate; abiotic oxygen and no-life conclusions are still under dispute.

About 2460s: unmanned probes confirm 0.98g, oceans, magnetic field, active geology, ~18.5% O₂, and no native biosphere; Betelgeuse risk enters the formal safety case.

About 2472: CI seed fleet arrives; full-planet orbital survey first, first batch of restricted seed foundries deployed.

2472–2484: orbital layer, energy, mining, materials trials, polar and ocean survey stations; humans not yet activated.

2482–2502: temperature, water cycle, salinity, river channels, and regional atmospheric conditioning; machine infrastructure begins to form a discontinuous point-array across half the planet.

2490–2528: microbes, soil bioreactors, pulverized bedrock, organic matter, and marine primary producers; the soil-making systems are set up first so that they will go on thickening on their own.

2505–2545: pioneer herbaceous plants, shrubs, tree belts, wetlands, agricultural trials, pollinators, and a small animal fauna; Primary / Secondary nodes expand rapidly.

2538–2550: multi-region human-readiness validation; the Betelgeuse gate is passed formally for the last time.

About 2550: 10–15 advance Awakeners arrive in a few core regions, responsible for on-site validation and human-care duties; this does not mark the start of large-scale migration.

About 2556: the first batch of about 40 embryonic individuals is activated.

About 2562: the second batch of about 40 embryonic individuals is activated.

About 2563: Betelgeuse core collapse. Neither Earth nor Archeon has yet received the optical news.

About 2564: the Axiom departs Earth normally, carrying 50–80 people, precision spares, knowledge updates, and Phase 2 expansion equipment; Atwood is about 30.

About 2569: the Axiom, along the old corridor about 6.3 ly from Betelgeuse, meets the primary shell; the active bubble fails, and about three weeks later it crashes onto Archeon. About 20–30 survivors. At this point the two embryo cohorts are about 13 and 7 years old, and Atwood's "witnessing children without an Earth memory grow up" still has real subjects.

About 2573: the primary shell arrives at Archeon. Sky Fire / Year 0. Surface population working value about 115–135.

2573–about 2623: CI damaged, sheltering, ecological reseeding, and knowledge triage; not an overnight shutdown but a progressive sacrifice of capability layers.

About 2623–2673: the working window in which CI's last high-order voice disappears; regions may not sync, no single global date is locked.

About 3094: Year 521. Anna's main era at 19; in the same year, the light of the 2563 outburst reaches Earth for the first time. The two are a synchronization produced by the geometry of the same light-shell, not a coincidence.

3.13 How CI laid down a hemispheric-scale substrate in about a century

"CI installations across half the planet" does not mean CI shipped thousands of finished products from Earth.

Deployment mechanism:

- The seed fleet carried a small number of high-precision cores, robots, reactors, orbital survey, and 8–12 initial seed foundries.
- The foundries mined iron, copper, silicon, sulfur, carbonates, and basalt on the spot, producing structural steel, glass, ceramics, basalt fiber, piping, standard motors, storage tanks, and low-tier control components.
- The high-end compute substrates, radiation-hardened sensors, precision optics, and ASI cores cannot be fully replicated at ordinary nodes and still depend on the initial inventory and a few precision fabs. This boundary later becomes the load-bearing reason for CI's degradation.
- Replication is a tiered, signed, quota-bound industrial expansion, not an unconstrained nanobot / grey-goo. Each generation of foundry can only manufacture machines within its licensed tier, and CI logs materials and lineage.
- Surface transport does not depend on first building roads: orbital drop, automated heavy-lift airships, short-range ground-effect transport, and on-site manufacturing scatter nodes across different continents.

Recommended orders of magnitude are revised to:

- Primary Nodes: about 120–300; large hydrology, energy, biological, regional-materials, and climate facilities.
- Secondary Stations: about 3,000–8,000; meteorology, soil, seed, drilling, assay, landing / beacon, and local processing stations.
- Passive Interventions: tens of thousands to over one hundred thousand; microbial communities, tree belts, wetlands, wells, slopes, river channels, coasts, and marine seedings. Many, in the end, leave only ecological consequences, no buildings.

This is "point-array coverage," not "half a planet paved with cities and forests."

3.13a Terraform Industry

- Total arrival mass of the initial seed fleet, working value about 0.9M tonnes; most of it is reactors, mining / smelting equipment, orbital tugs, robots, storage tanks, and ten seed foundries — not completed city components.
- The ten initial foundries expand to 24 regional works in the first twenty years, hitting a hard cap of 36 by about 2517. Only six are precision fabs with high-end sensor / optics / compute substrate; the rest can only replicate licensed structural pieces, motors, piping, glass, ceramics, and basalt fiber.
- Precision non-replicable inventory is about 60k tonnes, sealed by mission lineage and lifetime; it contains radiation-hardened compute substrate, optics, calibrators, and ASI-core material. The "many installations on the ground, high-order capability dead" of the post-Sky Fire world rests on this bottleneck.
- Peak available power in the late-engineering period is about 80–100 GW: about 40% fission, about 30% geothermal, and the rest from orbital / surface solar, wind, and hydropower. The energy is used for mining, pumping, materials, greenhouses, and node logistics; it does not act as a giant thermostat that reconditions an entire planet's temperature.
- Century-total local structural materials, about 250–450M tonnes; the bulk is earthworks, piping, water storage, wells, road foundations, and low-precision components. The hemispheric reach comes from a great many small interventions and coordinate drops, not from covering half the planet in machinery.
- Quantity lock: about 180 Primary Nodes, 5,200 Secondary Stations, and about 80,000 loggable Passive Interventions; ±20% tolerance is allowed, but the order-of-magnitude relationships among the three are fixed.
- Orbital drop is only responsible for high-value cores and early trans-oceanic seeding; once the regional works are built, over 90% of the structural mass comes from local sources. This preserves the seed fleet's interstellar capability and avoids infinite downmass.

3.14 Ecological completeness before Sky Fire

Three kinds of area must be understood separately:

- Infrastructure reach: CI's coordinates, surveys, passive drops, and installation points span about half a global scale.
- Managed ecological corridors: about 15–25% of global land — discontinuous river valleys, coasts, basins, windward slopes, and transport / hydrology corridors — has received actual ecological intervention.
- Human-grade mosaic: about 3–8% of global land, capable, under sustained management, of growing grain, sustaining life, and obtaining a reliable water supply; concentrated mainly along the Aethelgard Thread, with only isolated patches on other continents.

Thirty years can make farmland, tree belts, greenhouse valleys, and a core zone that looks very green, but not a mature, self-stabilizing, hemispheric ecology. About 80–100 years allow the first soils several decades of organic-matter accumulation and the first forest stands 50–70 years of structure, and rivers and coastal systems to go through several rounds of adjustment, which makes it more credible; it is still not a completed Earth.

The correct orbital image of 2573: a few deep-green cores, light-green corridors spread along rivers and windward slopes, many experimental patches, and much larger tracts of land still bare, still herbaceous, or having received only passive treatment.

The vegetation of 3094 cannot be produced by simply coloring the 2573 map green. Five hundred years have included Sky Fire filtering, CI shutdown, species escapes, fires, river migrations, local breeding, and completely unmanaged succession. Every biome of Anna's era must answer three questions at once: "what did the original climate allow?" "what did CI sow at the time?" and "what has it become on its own, after five hundred years of neglect or care?"


======================================================================
4. Beginning with a whole planet: Planet Archeon
======================================================================

4.1 The provisionally stable hard base

The following continues to serve as a working base; the precise formation history of the 18.5% abiotic oxygen still requires a dedicated planetary-chemistry audit, but this does not obstruct this round of historical reconstruction:

- Gravity about 0.98g.
- Before CI arrived, about 18.5% O₂, liquid water, magnetic field, and active plates were already in place, but no natural biosphere.
- The K2V primary bestows on the world a warm-tinted daylight spectrum; the twin moons make a complicated tidal rhythm.
- CI's principal task is not "to make Earth out of Mars," but to warm the planet, start the water cycle, make soil, seed an ecology, tune regional climates, and prepare landing points for humans.
- When Sky Fire occurred, this engineering was not yet complete.

What CI faced was an SSR-tier world with excellent conditions, air, and ocean already in place, and so its installation deployment covered half a planet rather than concentrating in a single 800 × 500 km basin.

4.1a Planetary parameters lock

- Diameter: 9,500 km; mean radius: 4,750 km.
- Equatorial circumference: about 29,845 km; 1° of latitude is always about 82.90 km.
- Surface area: about 2.835 × 10^8 km², about 55.6% that of Earth.
- Sea–land ratio: ocean 68% / land 32%; ocean about 1.928 × 10^8 km², land about 9.073 × 10^7 km². The higher ocean fraction is responsible for heat storage, moisture supply, and manufacturing genuine trans-oceanic distances; the land is still enough to accommodate four large continents, the Keld archipelago, and many polar island chains.
- Surface gravity: 0.98g. From radius and gravity, mass is about 0.545 M⊕ (3.25 × 10^24 kg), mean density about 7.25 g/cm³, escape velocity about 9.56 km/s.
- Internal explanation: Archeon is a metal-rich rocky planet with a high core fraction; the working value for the iron-nickel core mass fraction is about 42–47%, above Earth's; the active magnetic field, iron-rich basalts, ochre-red oxidation strata, and heavy-element ore provinces are all explained by the same formation history. The core fraction is not a prose number; it is only physics.
- Small planets usually cool faster; Archeon still maintains active plate tectonics, which must be jointly borne by three factors: a silicate mantle enriched in radioactive parent elements, the long-term tidal dissipation of the twin moons, and a hydrous asthenosphere that lowers the plate-motion threshold. The twin moons are one factor among many; they do not, on their own, explain active plates.
- Rotation, orbit, and axial tilt lock: 27-hour solar day, 365.25 Earth-day orbital year (≈ 324.67 local days), 13° axial tilt. 365.25 is the sole version (no more ±10%), and it puts Ep7 of the year 3094 on strictly the same day as Earth first light (see §3.7a). At 27 hours, this is still Earth-style fast rotation and does not produce a Venus-style single mega Hadley cell.
- Sea-level atmosphere working value: about 1.10 bar. Pre-CI O₂ volume fraction about 18.5% (pO₂ about 0.204 bar, near Earth breathable level); in Anna's era, under the influence of five hundred years of biosphere, populated areas commonly see about 19.5–20.5%, but no single number is locked for the whole planet.
- The precise K2V luminosity, orbital radius, and greenhouse-gas parameters still require a dedicated planetary-chemistry audit; before that audit is done, maps and climate lock relative structure only, and no decimal-precision global mean temperature is given.

4.2 Zoom order

Spatial hierarchy of the world bible:

planet
→ terraform hemisphere
→ oceans / continents / climate systems
→ CI lattice
→ human threads
→ regional weave (places where mature lines run together)
→ settlements
→ Atheria
→ Anna's front door

The map contracts inward from the whole planet, and behind the horizon there is always a real few thousand kilometers. The edge of a story is not the edge of the world.

4.3 Continents, seas, and coordinates: the P0 map lock

Four principal continents, one trans-oceanic archipelago system, polar island chains, and many small landmasses. In Anna's era, stable or seasonal human activity still amounts to about "three and a half": Aethelgard, Keld, and Vorhan have grown lines; Duskreach has only a discontinuous half; and Far Continent has no permanent human settlement. Precise coastlines can be fine-tuned within ASCII 10° cells; the relative positions of continents, their tectonic parent structures, climatic windward faces, and the wonders' provincial assignments are fixed.

A. Aethelgard — Cradle Continent

- Identity: the earliest, most densely populated, most maturely trafficked cradle continent — not the world.
- Locked envelope: about 58°W–34°E, 1°N–58°N; main body about 5,800–6,400 km east-west, about 4,300 km north-south, land-area working value about 18M km².
- Frontier, Atheria, Iris Hollow, Orun, Marneth, Skyleaf, and other existing sites occupy only a central-western / central patch of its mature-route ring.
- The Main Basin is an ancient high-density core, but it is only one "Thread" on Aethelgard.
- The continent's northern reaches, deep east, southern coast, and inland uplands retain large low-density zones, old nodes, seasonal lines, and unmanned country.
- Its maturity does not mean its resources have run out. The Orun basalt province, Marneth meltwater and highland ecology, western-coast marine products, northern-forest timber, and the vast agricultural-pastoral mosaic together support several distinct producing regions; a Thread is only the earliest to have woven these surpluses into stable trade.

B. Vorhan — Eastern Reach

- Locked envelope: about 55°E–158°E, 12°S–56°N, land-area working value about 17M km²; the belt around central 105°E is split by an active rift valley into eastern and western tectonic domains, but culturally the whole is still called Vorhan.
- The "CI never touched" setting is retracted. CI once left behind hydrology networks, geothermal well groups, mineral assay stations, forested corridors, seed banks, and large landing fields.
- Humans have only re-established stable contact within the last century or two, and settlements have formed along the old points as a young string of pearls.
- Accents, breads, ship types, wind knowledge, and festivals distinct from Aethelgard's have already appeared, but need not immediately develop into national conflict.
- The young string of pearls is not a chain of outposts dependent on Aethelgard for its blood. The rift geothermal, volcanic minerals, plateau water systems, wetlands, and eastern basalt province have already raised local shipyards, thin-air airship craft, and regional exchange; what Aethelgard brings is mutual-recognition standards and new relationships, not everything the civilization needs.

C. Keld Archipelago — Blue Crossing

- Locked envelope: about 18°E–64°E, 18°S–22°N; islands and shallow-sea platforms total about 4M km² of land. It straddles the Blue Crossing between Aethelgard and Vorhan; it is not a decorative archipelago crumbled into a corner of the map.
- Dozens to hundreds of islands; volcanic islands, coral / mineral islands, strong monsoon, and twin-moon tides.
- CI marine-ecology stations, tidal stations, island seed banks, and old skyports form a set of stepping stones.
- Local airships are more like sailing airships: variable wing surfaces, sail, light structure, treating the atmosphere as sea proper.
- What is exchanged between islands is not a uniform ration, but each island's surplus of salt, intertidal harvest, carbonate materials, volcanic minerals, seeds, and sail-making craft. The Blue Crossing is therefore a productive sea, not a chain of barely-surviving fuel stops.

D. Duskreach — Southern Warm Lands

- Locked envelope: about 15°E–125°E, 67°S–7°S, land-area working value about 15M km²; the northern coast is warm and wet, the eastern coast a volcanic arc, and the southern edge enters a subpolar zone where glaciers and geothermal fields sit side by side.
- No continuous colonization; settlements grow only along a few ancient CI infrastructure chains. A two-thousand-kilometer lane with only six reliable stops can exist.
- The difficulty here is the storm season, hot-and-humid corrosion, and intermittent lanes — not a post-apocalyptic poisoned earth.
- The Smoke Wall river system, Cloud Forest Steps country, the volcanic arc, and the southern geothermal province all hold water, wood, fibers, medicinal ecology, heat, and minerals far in excess of local per-capita demand. The difficulty is in preservation, corrosion resistance, cross-season transport, and getting the producing regions plugged into the Loom — not in barren land.

E. FAR-5 / Unnamed Far Continent (OPEN BY DESIGN)

- Locked envelope: about 180°W–70°W, 38°S–58°N, land-area working value about 24M km².
- In Anna's era, still almost no long-term human presence — not for wickedness, radiation, mysterious curse, or known barrenness; only because it is too far, its weather too unstable, no confirmed pull factor able to support repeated lanes, and no continuous supply points. `SURVEY INCOMPLETE` means, at once, unknown danger and unknown abundance.
- `FAR-5` is only a CI / cartographer coordinate name, not a local cultural one; internal terrain is uniformly written `SURVEY INCOMPLETE` and left blank.

F. Polar / subpolar island chains (still partly blank in places)

- Together with scattered small landmasses, land-area working value about 13M km². They connect ice-cap margins, oceanic weather, and long-range wind corridors.
- The Arctic is dominated by seasonal sea ice, fjord island chains, and small ice caps; the Antarctic, because Duskreach's southern edge and the island arcs approach the polar circle, holds a more continuous land ice and shelf ice. The two poles are not mirror images.
- May serve as the working area of the few great-range survey crews; no rush to turn it into a main-plot location.

4.3.1 Coordinate constitution

- Projection: the global ASCII uses equirectangular / plate carrée, only for indexing and spatial relations, and does not pretend that high-latitude areas are undistorted.
- Latitude: planetocentric latitude, north positive, south negative, range −90°…+90°.
- Longitude: east positive, range [−180°, +180°); 180°E and 180°W are the same line.
- Prime meridian: passes through the old CI Primary Node optical fiducial at Frontier City; Frontier City is locked at 26.0°N, 0.0°E. It is the zero line of cartography, not a political center.
- Global grid `G1[RxxCyy]`: each cell is 10°×10°. R00=70–80°N, rows go south; R15=80–70°S; C00=180–170°W, columns go east; C35=170–180°E. Cell centers are only used for ASCII placement; the decimal coordinates in the place tables take priority.
- Row-number calculation: `row = ceil((80 - latitude) / 10) - 1`; column-number calculation: `col = max(0, ceil((longitude + 180) / 10) - 1)`. This is equivalent to a general point taking its containing cell, and a point that falls exactly on a 10° grid line is uniformly assigned to the northern / western cell, avoiding one point being split into different cells by two AIs; ±180° is first normalized to −180°. A latitude whose absolute value ≥80° is recorded separately as `PN` / `PS` polar cap, not stuffed into a rectangular cell.
- Distance: north-south, every 1° is about 82.90 km; east-west, every 1° is about `82.90 × cos(latitude)` km. Formal voyages use great-circle, not ASCII character counts measured directly.
- Coasts may move within their 10° cell; if a mountain system, rift, gulf, wonder, or settlement crosses a cell, wind belts, ocean currents, rain shadows, voyages, and neighboring coordinates must be re-checked in sync.

4.3.2 MAP-G0 · Global terrain / wonders index (north up, east right)

Token grammar: `~~` ocean; `A.` Aethelgard; `V.` Vorhan; `K*` Keld island / shallow-sea platform; `D.` Duskreach; `F?` FAR-5 survey incomplete; `Ii` polar island / land ice; `01`–`15` are VISTA anchors, precise coordinates in the table. Each row is 36 tokens; columns start centered at −175° and go east 10° per cell.

4.3.6 Aethelgard story-region authoritative settlements register

Registration format: `settlement | coordinates | great-circle distance to Frontier | resident population | settlement type (§4.9.2 nine types or their terrain variant) · node tier | built-up area km² | farmland / production hinterland km²`; nearest T3 / Class V position, first plot use, and status are added in row-notes after the table, not as a separate column.
The following table registers only the main sites in Aethelgard that have entered story design at this stage; it is not all the settlements of the continent. Distances are computed on a diameter of 9,500 km (1° ≈ 82.90 km) and normal rounding errors are allowed. Built-up area and farmland are given as working values per the derivation rules of §4.9.3; settlement population includes the core streets in a continuous life-circle, the directly attached village groups, farmsteads, and the port-side living belt — it does not mean everyone is crammed into the "built-up area" figure in the table.

**Population design lock for this round**
- Aethelgard total population is still locked at about 1.23M, no downward revision.
- The 17 currently main-named settlements together have a resident population of about 950k.
- Frontier drops to 250k; Atheria is locked at 45k.
- Aside from Frontier, most main towns fall in the 30–60k range.
- Population does not automatically confer hub status, T3/T4, or a Class V berth.

| Settlement | Coordinates | Distance to Frontier | Resident population | Settlement type | Built-up area | Farmland / production hinterland |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---:|---:|
| Frontier | 26.0°N, 0.0°E | 0 km | 250k | Multi-port great city grown on a CI substrate (T4; largest Great Junction, but not a capital) | ~16–17 km² | ~620–680 km² + vertical farming walls (Basin Seven basin farmland, biome coefficient 0.7); the surroundings are enough for basic daily life, and grain, fruit, and wine from elsewhere entering the market are enrichment rather than lifeline |
| Crestline | 27.0°N, 0.3°E | 86 km | 40k | Cliff-gate gateway town (small member of the Falling Plateaus · T2) | ~5 km² (both sides of the cliff gate + north terrace and river-bank living belt) | ~120–150 km² terrace / river-bank farmsteads + river-fish drying yards |
| Atheria | 27.8°N, 0.5°E | 154 km | 45k | Terrace city (T2; mature ordinary town, not a secondary capital) | Three-tier ribbon about 12 km long, built-up area about 9–10 km² (three-tier ribbon dispersed-type calibration, core density lower than a general town tier) | 290–370 km²; clearly above basic grain needs, so grain, beans, and stepwine are a long-standing surplus |
| Coppergate | 18.0°N, 3.0°W | 702 km | 50k | Southern mature-lines junction great town (T3): Orun bypass edge, Saltmarch–Axiom, Glass Plains and many directions meet here; famous for copperwork | ~6 km² | ~160–190 km² Thread southern-edge farmstead belt |

- The 17 named settlements above have a combined resident population of 950k.
- Aethelgard's remaining ~280k is not a batch of "unnamed population buffer" but genuinely dispersed life: about 35–55 secondary route towns / valley towns / port towns combined ~125k; about 700–1,000 villages, farmsteads, river-bank settlements, mill villages, orchard villages, forest communities, and maker hamlets combined ~105k; about 250–450 permanently staffed small stations / small communities combined ~40k; river traffic, seasonal camps, and rotating personnel about 10k. The numbers are an author-side working distribution and do not add a unified settlement classification.
- Nearest T3 / Class V positions (§6A.9's 30k+ directional check, working values; mileage roughly by great-circle from the coordinates of §4.3.6): Crestline / Atheria → Frontier (~90 / 150 km); Cliffwatch / Windbreak → Coppergate (~460 / 580 km); Riverbend → Coppergate (~940 km, via the bypass edge); Saltmarch → Coppergate (~1,200 km); Millhaven → Frontier or Iris Hollow (~1,140 / 1,460 km, per destination); Canopy → Frontier (~1,420 km; the northern line still has no nearer T3); Stormperch → Thornfield (~540 km); Ironvein → Thornfield / Amber Reach (~740 / 810 km); Skyleaf → Cinderlode (~840 km, around the northeast rim of Marneth, not crossing the crown) or Thornfield (~1,050 km, via Kestrel's Gate) — in a sealed season both sides go down at once. Coppergate, Thornfield, Cinderlode, Amber Reach, and Iris Hollow each have their own T3; Frontier is T4.

**CI-origin lock for the resident small-stations layer** — this constraint applies only to this layer, not to all settlements.
This round's 250–450 "resident small stations / small communities" default to starting from some inherited CI low-cost point: an old well / hot spot / assay point / weather point / river-channel modification reach / stable foundation / good soil or tree line produced by seed-work, and the like. "CI origin" here does not mean a CI object can still be seen there today, still less that residents live off the relics.

Before entering prose, at least the following are met:
1. First write what is too plentiful, especially well made, or especially convenient here five hundred years later;
2. Then write how people have lived their way into the old draft as a place of their own: houses, stoves, fruit trees, kitchen plots, drying racks, eaves, local glass / woodwork / lime, and a set of dimensions whose origin no one asks about any more;
3. CI may exist only as consequence / dimension / biology / weather habit; avoid "old machine still standing" at every station;
4. A small station should be whole, cared for, and marked by time;
5. Water, heat, soil, forest, or a local material should count as an abundance in at least one form here, avoiding "far station = short of everything."

"Thousands of station points" is still permitted, but most are unmanned beacons, passive anchors, seasonal watch points, or positions maintained by a few people; the 250–450 with permanent residents are one layer among them.

- This table shares a source with the story-region anchors of §4.3.4; on numerical conflicts, this table takes precedence.

4.3.6b Non-Aethelgard first-batch named settlements

The first-batch named settlements on the other three continents preferentially fall at the positions the §4.6.6 vista-binding table requires ANCHOR — this way each name answers, at the same time, "how do the people nearby live by the wonder." Non-Aethelgard nodes do not take Frontier distance as identity (§6.8); this table gives the nearest regional junction or the vista it is bound to. The six names below come from an Aethelgard-release batch; regional junctions on any continent not listed here remain OPEN awaiting the story's entry.

| Settlement | Continent | Coordinates | Population | Settlement type / function | Built-up area | Farmland | Vista binding |
|---|---|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|
| Gearstead | Vorhan | 30.0°N, 80.0°E | 60k | The great city at the foot of the High Inland Sea; people going up to the plateau often change to ships fit for thin air here, while local life turns around geothermal water, foothill valley land, and titanium work. The city is not a port fanned outward into a circle: old streets stretch toward the mountain's foot along three warm-water channels; along the water are one- to three-story stone-based long houses, laundry platforms, bathhouses, and greenhouses; only farther from the water do you see the tall wood houses, furnace houses, and ship-change sheds. Walls are mostly pale gray volcanic stone / geopolymer, window frames and overhanging eaves in deep brown heartwood; the plateau dust wears the windward walls pale. The first people up in the morning are usually those who open the greenhouse sluices and let out the bath water, not the airport. Titanium second source, geothermal chemistry: see §5.11. | ~6 km² | ~180 km² foothill valley + plateau ship-change belt | V06 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| Driftmoor | Vorhan | 20.0°N, 108.0°E | 15k | Return-leg plateau station on the eastern side of the Rift (T2), also a low, laterally spread dryland town. Houses lie against wind-break stone walls in a few arcs, not chasing continuity along a street front; most are only one or two stories, roofs pressed low, doors and windows opening on the leeward side. Between residences are hardened-earth yards for drying tubers, water tanks, geothermal baking houses, and low greenhouses; the airport is only a bit of open ground outside town. People coming back on The Draft eat here, change ships, and let the body adjust back to lower altitude; but when no ship is coming, the town still runs by the days of sowing, drying, mending water channels, and geothermal baths. | ~2 km² | ~45 km² highland dryland | V05 【NAMED BINDING · return station】 |
| Shellharbor | Keld | 2.0°S, 54.0°E | 45k | The multi-port city on the edge of the Karst Sea. It is not a "harbor" on a flat piece of ground: dwellings, eateries, net-drying platforms, small glass workshops, and pilot families grow up on the habitable platforms of dozens of tower karst, connected at the lower level by hanging platforms and small water craft, at the higher level by narrow bridges, rope ladders, and short lift cages. Walls tend to pale limestone / basalt gray, roofs steep for the heavy rain, wet wood, sea salt, and deep-green vines push the city's color down; only on a clear day do you see the patchy blue-green flash of window glass. Ships entering the tower-water area usually seek a local pilot first — a matter of long relations and local knowledge, not a central entrance. Sail-type airship shipyards, optical second source: see §5.11. | ~5 km² tower top + hanging platform | ~120 km² tower-top planting + wetland harvest + fishing | V04 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| Tidemark | Keld | 11.0°N, 47.0°E | 30k | Twin-Moon tidal-bay town (T3). The town spreads in a long arc along both banks of the river mouth; the ring closest to the water is not the residential main street but the stone steps, boat sheds, laundry platforms, salt pools, and wet-goods houses whose use shifts with the tide; the houses of long residence are raised half a story to a story, their bases mostly stone, their upper parts in tide-resistant heartwood and dark tile. At low tide children and tide-chase harvesters walk from the front door all the way out to where the water normally stands; at high tide the same row of front doors leaves only mooring bollards above water. The people from here whose craft is the dual-chart every day read the river water and the two moons at once; mornings and evenings are not divided by "when the workshop opens" but by "the first stretch of water it is truly workable on today." | ~3.5 km² | ~90 km² along-coast + intertidal aquaculture | V08 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| Hearthstone | Duskreach | 47.0°S, 71.0°E | 15k | White Stair plateau-outer-edge bath town (seasonal T2). The town center is not workshops or moorings, but several groups of public bathhouses and warm-water channels always giving off white steam; residences grow around them, commonly a lower story of pale-gray stone / geopolymer against damp, an upper story of deep-brown timber, eaves wide, windows small and bright. Beside the bathhouses are long guest houses, geothermal kitchens, laundry corridors, and greenhouses still glowing orange all night; when outsiders come in the cool season many families temporarily give up their upper floor or side rooms to guests. The air always smells of wet stone, boiled tubers, and freshly washed cloth. The most audible morning sound is not the hammer but the stone sluice opening, the hot water filling the channels of a few streets again. | ~2 km² | ~40 km² greenhouse + highland | V12 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| Verdance | Duskreach | 33.0°S, 104.0°E | 20k | The Cloud Forest Steps first / second-level step port (T2). Here the "street" is itself an upper- and lower-level step surface: one house's roof may be the drying deck or a shortcut for the person on the level above; dwellings are mostly narrow and deep two- to three-story mixed wood-stone, backs against the wet slope, fronts opening wide eaves toward the misty forest. Orchards, herb gardens, public bake ovens, rain-water troughs, cable bridges, and small woodwork shops are scattered across different heights, with no single center. Timber and food go down; people, paper, salt, and finished small goods go up; but even with no outsider ships arriving, residents still spend a whole day between canopies, orchards, mist water, and slope. | ~2.5 km² tiered | ~60 km² step surface | V11 【NAMED BINDING · first level】 |

- Population distribution: Vorhan named 75k / 230k; Keld named 75k / 190k; Duskreach named 35k / 110k. The rest is the regional junctions of each continent (not yet named, OPEN), route towns, villages, and outposts; unnamed places are also inhabited.
- Vorhan's Organ Country geothermal grand settlement / high-temperature-craft city, Keld's grand mooring at the Roll's seasonal window, and Duskreach's Smoke Wall upstream lake-district port and southern geothermal grand settlement remain OPEN, to be named in Act 2 and later per the story's entry order.
- Every name must, before entering prose, pass the five thresholds of §4.6.6: a vista slice from the front door, the local surplus and specialty, the nearest T2+ hydrogen / repair point, "what is too plentiful here to use up" and what the outer lines bring back, and a stretch of country untamed.

4.3.7 Global settlement rollout lock

- Aethelgard's 17 currently named main settlements cover about 950k / 1.23M; this means the story has named the most mature places on the continent, and the remaining 280k are dispersed across many unnamed living communities. The first names on the other three continents are vista anchors, and each continent has its own life-network.
- Before a new continent enters the main text for the first time, at least the following must be registered: one mature mooring capable of receiving a Class V; two to four settlements whose life-centers are clearly different; one internal long-line the locals have walked over and over; one outward relationship, sustained by real interpersonal traffic, that connects to elsewhere; three kinds of local surplus; at least one local reason for life that would continue to exist without any air lane.
- New settlement registration in author-side priority order: terrain and water | who lives here / who comes seasonally | why this place goes on being inhabited | what is too plentiful here to use up | which specific craft is famous here | how the locals usually catch a ship when it comes in | why they will still be willing to leave a place for the same line next time | the nearest mature line / regularly-visited great mooring (direction-check only) | mooring capacity / hydrogen / deep repair (second-tier physical check).
- The main 30–60k settlements, when their populations grow, will express it in more neighbourhoods, along-canal / along-slope / forest-set life-belts, attached villages, everyday small mooring positions, and a fuller local occupational spectrum; formal berths, tower counts, and Class V capability are still determined by lane function.
- Unnamed 1–8k secondary route towns and 100–800 person life-communities carry a great deal of "very small on the map but perfectly livable for a lifetime" texture, each with a life fully its own.
- The Aethelgard resident-small-station layer may take a CI inherited point as its nucleation point; the core is "the past lowered the cost of staying here," not the presence of old equipment, a relic-discovery, or a reward-style reveal.
- Every settlement can lack a certain thing, but it should have its own basis for standing rather than relying only on distant input. Cross-continental exchange carries high-value differences, specialty components, seeds, medicine, culture, and seasonal gaps; it does not treat everyday staple grains and ordinary building materials as a default lifeline.
- Names grow in the story's entry order. Unnamed route towns, port villages, island towns, along-canal street villages, mill and kiln settlements, orchard communities, seasonal residences, and truly productive belts are always there in the background.

4.4 CI's hemispheric terraform lattice

What CI built was not a future city, but an unfinished planetary substrate.

The first-round orders of magnitude and production mechanism are in 3.13; only the map-layer summary is kept here:

- Primary Nodes: about 120–300. Large hydrology, climate, energy, biological, mineral-processing, and regional-control installations.
- Secondary Stations: about 3,000–8,000. Weather stations, seed trial plots, soil-improvement stations, small landing fields, communication stations, drilling, and assay stations.
- Passive Interventions: tens of thousands to over one hundred thousand. Microbial drops, plant belts, groundwater wells, river-channel modifications, artificial wetlands, slope stabilization, marine-ecology drops, wind barriers, and erosion-control forests.

The present state of a node is a continuous spectrum:

- Utterly gone, leaving only altered terrain.
- The above-ground structure is broken, the below-ground still stable.
- The machines are dead, but the wells, canals, soil, or forest go on working.
- Briefly awakened every few years by some condition.
- Used by locals for centuries and long ceased to be regarded as an ancient installation.
- Never visited by humans, still only old coordinates or rumor.

The right way to write CI legacy:

- An artificial water channel used for four hundred years that the local children only know as The Long Cut, and where they fish.
- A forest that shouldn't be there, taken at first for natural geography, until decades later a surveyor discovers it is a precipitation test zone.
- A settlement's hot spring flowing from a five-hundred-year-old geothermal bore, though the local bathhouse is older than the concept of "relic."
- Unnatural straight lines beneath a ridge are not a treasure map, but the foundation of a solar array eaten by grass.

4.4.2 MAP-E5 · 3094 biome + human-thread layer

Same grid as MAP-G0. First token position is dominant ecological potential: `O` ocean, `I` ice / polar, `?` unknown, `B` boreal / fjord, `T` temperate forest-farm mosaic, `H` highland, `D` desert, `K` Keld maritime islands, `S` steppe / savanna, `R` rain / cloud forest. Second position is Year 521 human intensity: `0` no regular activity, `1` survey / seasonal / extremely sparse line, `2` stable Threads and settlements, `3` Thread high-density cultivation. It is still a mosaic-level index and does not paint a whole cell with one vegetation type.

4.4.3 Global climate skeleton

- A 27-hour rotation is only about 12.5% slower than Earth's, so it preserves a three-cell large-scale circulation. Hadley-cell working boundaries are about 0–32°; 32–62° is dominated by mid-latitude westerlies and moving fronts; beyond 62° is polar easterlies and the storm track along the ice margin. The boundaries wobble; they are not walls painted in the sky.
- A low 13° tilt means small annual solar-elevation shifts; the seasonal temperature swing at a given site is weaker than at Earth's same latitude. Truly strong seasons come from continental heat capacity, monsoon reversal, sea-ice margins, and high-plateau snowpack, not from a uniform four-season color change.
- Low-latitude trades run east to west, and southern Keld and Vorhan therefore develop sailed sailing-airships; mid-latitude westerlies run west to east, and Aethelgard's northern line and Vorhan's northern line have fast eastward windows; Duskreach's southern edge and the circum-southern ocean form the world's most reliable and coldest long wind corridor.
- Main sea basins: the Azure Main lies between FAR-5 and Aethelgard; the Blue Main between Aethelgard, Keld, and Vorhan; the Southern Girdle at about 45–65°S, largely continuous. Northern-hemisphere subtropical gyres are clockwise, southern-hemisphere counterclockwise; western coasts of continents are dominated by cold currents, fog, and upwelling, eastern coasts by warm currents, monsoonal moisture, and strong storms.
- Aethelgard's western-coast cold current makes the fog, marine food chain, and stable low clouds around Iris Hollow; Keld's warm shallow seas and funnel bays amplify V08; Duskreach's northeastern warm current, volcanic arc, and monsoon jointly feed V10, rather than "here has a magical storm by nature."
- The twin moons only produce V08's 15–25 m extreme tidal range at resonance bays and funnel-shore straits. Open-coast normal tidal ranges are about 1–4 m; narrower bays, shallow-sea platforms, and same-phase months are what climb into the higher tier. One cannot write the whole planet as tsunami every two days.
- The Arctic is mostly seasonal sea ice, island-chain ice caps, and V14's fjord glaciers; the Antarctic has more continuous sea ice, Duskreach's southern land ice, and offshore ice shelves. V12's warm water and ice valleys come from local geothermal counter-examples and do not represent an overall warm south.

4.4.4 Tectonic parent skeleton

- Seven narrative-scale plates are used: Aethelgard Plate, Azure Ocean Plate, Vorhan-West, Vorhan-East, Keld Microplate Swarm, Duskreach Plate, FAR-5 Plate; the small plates at the poles and the Southern Girdle are not individually named.
- V05 is the active extensional boundary between Vorhan-West and Vorhan-East; the High Inland Sea is an uplifted enclosed basin on the western rift shoulder; Organ Country is the southeastern basalt province. All three are generated by the same extension–magmatic event.
- Keld is a microplate and island-arc collage zone: V04 comes from the drowned old carbonate platform in shallow seas, V08 from the funnel bays of the back-arc basin, and V09 from the fixed sea-land wind front on the northwestern side of the archipelago. The three wonders share a sea basin, but are not the same landform.
- The old collision suture between eastern Aethelgard and Keld / Blue microplates forms V02 Marneth; the flood-basalt province in the south later uplifts as a whole, and rivers cut through it, forming V01 Orun, V13 Glass Plains, and the leeward V07 Draa / Great Ochre. The Axiom Scar is only a deep-time site within that arid province.
- The great river of Duskreach's northern edge, on leaving the interior tableland, forms V03; the volcanic arc on the east coast uplifts the windward mesa, forming V11; the warm bay within the arc anchors V10; the rift geothermal and glaciation of the same plate's south jointly form V12.
- V14 belongs to the ice-carved re-cutting of Aethelgard's old northwestern orogenic coast and the Arctic island-arc junction; no separate isolated "wonder mountain range" is created. FAR-5 locks only the continental outline and the possibilities of plate boundaries, not interior mountain systems, so that SURVEY INCOMPLETE remains a real information boundary.

4.4.5 Five-century ecological succession and ecological clocks

Ecological age is listed by trigger event, and different systems have different accumulation times:

- Earliest microbes: about 604 years (since about 2490).
- Later soil systems: about 566 years.
- Pioneer vegetation: about 549–589 years.
- Post-Sky Fire natural succession: strictly 521 years.
- Since 2563 core collapse: only "531 years" is defensible.

Ten stages of succession:

1. 2472–2490: hydrology, minerals, and enclosed culture infrastructure.
2. 2490–2505: microbes, water bodies, and substrate remodeling.
3. 2505–2545: engineered pioneer-community spread.
4. 2545–2573: soil networks, pollinators, wetland invertebrates, and early forest corridors.
5. Year 0–10: acute Sky Fire losses and refuge survival.
6. Year 10–50: CI-led repair, re-seeding, and isolation.
7. Year 50–110: static-program running, embryo-bank population rollout.
8. Year 110–250: natural succession and local selection after loss of unified coordination.
9. Year 250–400: over centuries, people, ships, seeds, food, and cultivars are carried between places, causing seed exchange, escape, domestication, fires, and secondary spread.
10. Year 400–521: stable but clearly artificially originated regional biota form.

Regional vegetation (aligned with MAP-E5):

- Aethelgard: Thread forest-farmland-grassland mosaic; Orun windward forest / moss / lichen vertical belts; leeward meadow, shrubland, and desert; wild grassland and open woodland in eastern Marneth.
- Vorhan: rift savanna, dry grassland, plateau inland-sea wetlands, eastern basalt pioneer communities.
- Keld: maritime forest, karst, tidal halophyte, and brackish communities.
- Duskreach: northern warm-wet forest, cloud-mist forest, eastern volcanic belt, southern subpolar communities.
- Polar regions: boreal open woodland, tundra, and ice-margin ecology.
- FAR-5: only remote-sensing level is retained; no confirmed unexplored ecology is filled in.

Biological rule revisions (superseding all older conclusions in world `1.4 World · Ecology`):

- "3–5× growth" applies only to a few engineered pioneers and crops, and decays across generations; it is not a whole-ecology property.
- Simpler understory is jointly caused by initial seeding structure, missing dispersers, fire, grazing, nutrients, and Sky Fire filtering; the claim that "521 years is not enough to form a shrub layer" is no longer asserted.
- Do not write "all imported organisms are harmless" / "no parasites / flies"; only limited claims such as "no human-disease-vector mosquitoes" and "no large dangerous predators" are retained.
- Plant–architecture coexistence = engineered biofilm mineral passivation, root stabilization, and zero-compaction agriculture; "root-acid strengthened alloys" is not used.
- K2V vegetation locks only visual grammar: deep-green / olive leaves, part of highland species with lilac veins, deep gold / amber ripe color; unverified spectral percentages are not locked.
- Quiver-Leaf, Sentinel Pine, Sun-Berry, and a few other species are retained; only species with a narrative function are named.
- Arid-region vistas such as salt-dome / whispering crystal plains / rust-band cliffs / East Mesas belong to landform families (§4.6 keeps the archive), and the original text is temporarily kept in world, awaiting the migration of a terrain appendix.

4.5 How settlements are born

There is no single settlement production line. Only a few common modes of birth are registered here; they may mix, they may fail midway, and they do not constitute stages that must be executed in sequence.

A · route-first
A line is walked repeatedly by people → someone starts staying overnight at some point → someone regularly leaves water, food, and a place for repairs → the seasonal stop gradually becomes a place where people live.

B · place-first
A good piece of land, a local surplus, a unique ecology, or a craft first keeps people → later, ships come because "there are people there worth going to," and a line gradually forms.

C · inherited-point
Some convenience or consequence left by the past lowers the cost of staying → people do not necessarily know its origin → new life goes on growing on top of it. That past may be CI, but it may also be an early Archeon settlement or a dead Thread.

D · old-human-place
An old settlement / old Thread has already declined or moved on → some years, decades, or even a century or two later → new people move back in for wholly different reasons.

E · failed settlement
People tried → wind, route, family, resource judgment, or somewhere else changed → it did not grow → the map keeps a name, fruit trees, an old mooring, a weather saying, or one or two households. It is still history, not "the failed content is deleted."

A settlement is a life congealed out of people, place, and repeated relationships; air lanes often take part, but they do not monopolize birth rights. Not every settlement is born of CI, and not every place that stays becomes the stepping stone of the next, more distant lane.

Resource-abundance principle:

- Every settlement entering prose must answer: "what is here in more than the locals can use up?" The answer can be water, wind, heat, basalt, salt, wood, resin, fiber, fish, medicine, fertile earth, seeds, weather knowledge, a natural mooring, or some processing craft.
- At the same time it must answer: "why has this surplus not yet made it the center of the world?" The correct constraint is usually season, preservation, weight, corrosion, ceiling, refining temperature, number of skilled hands, or route reliability, not vague poverty.
- Discovering a resource and establishing a repeatable route are two halves of the same story. Resource explains why it is worth coming; the Thread explains why one can come again after having been once.
- Exploration scenes bring back samples, growing seasons, wind tables, sustainable yields, and the tools needed next time; the default reward of exploration is the knowledge of a sustainable resource; CI / Axiom specific plot lines are separate.
- The everyday base color of a place comes from local abundance, tension from the few specialty items that cannot be locally substituted. In that way a medicine ship, filter cartridge, or precision valve late will still hurt, without turning the whole planet into a chronic famine.

4.6 Wonder library · VISTAS OF ARCHEON (v0.1 expanded)

The sense of the fantastic comes from real geological / climatic processes pushed to giant scale, not from every place hiding a supernatural device.

The correct axes of magnification are horizontal scale and time scale, not just height. For an airship civilization moving at 60–90 km/h, a vista is a time structure, not an image: what truly makes the scale is "how long it occupies the horizon, and how long it takes to pass."

4.6.1 The eight questions for a vista (baseline for wonder design)

① Parent process: which real geological / climatic process? Magnified how many times?
② Geological parent structure: what tectonics does it hang from? A wonder grows out of the P0 skeleton, it is not a decal.
③ First sight: how far away is it first seen? What is it first mistaken for?
   First sight is misperception. Every wonder should at first sight be taken for weather, cloud, mist, or "something wrong with the horizon"; the instant of recognition is the instant of scale landing.
④ Time structure: distance of first sight → how long it occupies the horizon → how long to pass through / around.
⑤ Nested scale: three scale anchors readable in a single frame (a cliff > the waterfall on the cliff > the ship beside the waterfall).
⑥ Wind-and-terrain character: every landform is at the same time a wind-and-terrain character — what it does to air, which directly pushes routes, hazards, and old hands' shortcuts. A wonder directly affects flight decisions.
⑦ Concealment: which part has never been flown over, never been verified? Every wonder retains a "even it hasn't fully seen itself" area.
⑧ Comparison line: one sentence in a pilot's voice pointing to another (preferably one the reader has never seen) wonder. Chains of comparison lines are the anti-capping mechanism.

Specification tiers: the wonder library always keeps three tiers — full design (6–8 that can enter the story) / half design (a paragraph) / rumor (one line, unverified, of which at least two or three should later prove false or diverge from the rumor). All three tiers coexist, so that the map is always larger than the story.

4.6.2 Full-design candidates (15 slots · push 6–8 into story first)

V01 · Orun Great Wall
  Parent process: continental-margin basalt-plateau escarpment, horizontal ×3–5. Main-wall relative height 2.5–3.5 km; total extent 1,800–2,500 km, with bay-like notches, headlands, and outlying mesas standing alone 10–30 km from the wall. First sight: on a clear day, from 120–150 km away, "a second horizon on the horizon." Time: occupies the horizon a whole day; flying the main section along the wall takes 1.5–2 days. Nested anchors: wall > the group of aquifer-breach waterfalls (The Weeps) > the ships in the waterfalls' mist. Wind-and-terrain character: the diurnal upslope on the windward face = a free elevator for the along-wall lane; downdrafts at the cliff top = detour list; the wall-shadow sweeps 20–30 km along the foot each day, and the settlements within the shadow band tell time by "wall-shadow hour." Concealment: the weather at the top of the cliff-top plateau is another world, and few ships have ever been up. Comparison line: "You think Orun's wind is bad? Go to South Organ, then tell me."

V02 · Marneth Ice Crown
  Parent process: orogenic belt + high-plateau ice cap, magnified from a single peak to a mountain system. Extent 1,000+ km; crown ice sheet at 4–6 km elevation; outlet glaciers pour into ice falls tens of kilometers wide; cloud banners run for hundreds of kilometers. First sight: 200 km away, a white line floating above the haze layer, taken for a cloud bank — the instant of recognition is the discovery that it does not move for three days. Time: exists as background of the horizon for days; no one crosses, only detours the flanks. Nested anchors: ice crown > one outlet glacier > the smoke of a seasonal station on the glacier's lateral moraine. Wind-and-terrain character: at dusk the downslope wind pours from the ice sheet, and "land before sunset" is iron rule for the foothill lane; the cloud banner is a continent-wide weather forecast. Concealment: the interior of the crown has never been overflown; resonance ice caves and crystals are only old pilots' rumor. Comparison line: "At least Marneth lets you see it. The ice in the south grows straight out of the fog."

V03 · The Smoke Wall · the great-waterfall belt
  Parent process: a great river system dropping over a continental escarpment (×20). Parent structure: the intersection of a plateau escarpment and a continental-scale river system (locked to Duskreach's northern edge, center 14°S, 45°E).
