Waterfall belt 40–80 km wide, main drop 300–500 m, dozens of braided great waterfalls; the mist column rises 2–3 km. First sight: from 100–150 km away, "an unbroken plume of white smoke," taken for a fire; within 30 km ship hulls get wet with mist; within 20 km the roar overwhelms the engine. Nested anchors: mist column > single waterfall > observation pile at the waterfall's lip. Wind-and-terrain character: the core of the mist column is a no-go zone of sinking air; the spray-forest belt at the cliff foot (feral after CI seeding) extends over a hundred kilometers, with year-round rainbows; the lane detours through the upstream lake region. Concealment: rumor has it there are old CI hydrology tunnels running through the cliff body behind the falls' curtain; no one has verified it. Comparison line: "The Smoke Wall at least stands still. Standing Storm will chase you."

V04 · Karst Sea · the stone-forest sea
  Parent process: tower karst + shallow-water drowning (×30). 600 × 300 km; tens of thousands of 100–400 m stone pillars rising from wetlands and lake water. First sight: from altitude, it is a green-gray set of teeth filling the horizon in every direction, taken for "a scorched forest." Time: cross in 3–4 days, no continuous ground the whole way. Nested anchors: pillar sea > one pillar top wearing a village > the rope ladders and hanging platforms trailing down the pillar walls. Wind-and-terrain character: the wind through the "streets" between pillars is squeezed into a canyon flow; local pilots read the way by echo and wind whistle; foreign ships as a rule ask a local pilot to bring them in — one of the few places in the Loom where non-locals do not pilot; the only region in the whole planet where all movement is by air and water. Concealment: the "quiet-streets zone" of the deep interior is entered even by locals only in a dry year. Comparison line: "In the Stone Forest Sea, if you get lost you still have ten thousand pillars to hold on to. In the Draa, even your shadow is your own."

V05 · The Rift · the great-rift wind corridor
  Parent process: continental rift (×1.5). Parent structure: the opening plate boundary between Vorhan-West and Vorhan-East, central axis about 105°E. Twin walls with relative heights of 1–2 km, valley width 30–80 km, extent 1,500 km; a beaded string of long lakes, volcanic cones, and geothermal fields on the valley floor. First sight: on approach from the plateau side, "a straight line where the earth cracks open" disappearing into both ends of the horizon, taken for the shadow of a cloud. Time: as a lane, two days flying inside the valley. Nested anchors: rift > volcanic cone on the valley floor > the geothermal steam plume on the flank of the cone. Wind-and-terrain character: the rift compresses the regional wind into an axial one-way flow (The Draft) — riding it into the valley makes one day into two; the return leg must exit the valley by way of the plateau. The Loom's first "one-way street," a lane whose geometry is decided directly by geology. Concealment: the northern end vanishes into an unmanned plateau, and its far reach has not been surveyed. Comparison line: "The Draft only takes one toll: don't think you can go back the way you came."

V06 · High Inland Sea · the highland inland sea
  Parent process: closed high-plateau water body (×10). Elevation 2,500–3,000 m, main water body 600–800 km across; the sole rim breach falls into a dry deep valley as Rim Falls. First sight: climbing through clouds, "a sea has appeared in the sky" — first sight is always accompanied by ear pressure and thinning air. Time: circumnavigation of the lake takes days; heavy-loaded ships simply cannot get up here. Nested anchors: inland sea > island town in the lake > reed-colored sail at the island-town wharf. Wind-and-terrain character: elevation is national border — heavily loaded ships have insufficient buoyancy at this altitude, and the inland sea has its own thin-air fleet and shipbuilding tradition (larger envelopes, lighter frames, different sails), a self-contained ship-craft civilization circled off by altitude; the lake body makes its own weather, and afternoon lake-wind thunderstorms are inevitable. Concealment: the far-side plateau of the inland sea is almost never flown. Comparison line: "Ships that can get up here can't reach far, and ships that can reach far can't get up — the shipwrights on the two sides of the inland sea have laughed at each other for three hundred years."

V07 · The Draa · the sand-whale sea
  Parent process: giant compound dune field + singing sand magnified. A subtropical rain-shadow basin. Field 1,000–1,200 km; whale-back mega dunes 400–700 m high, with 3–6 km between dune crests. First sight: from altitude, a sea frozen in a long swell — mistaken for the sea often enough that "sea" is written into the name. Time: crossing takes 15–20 hours of pure flying, but with no anchor, no water, no reference — managed like a trans-oceanic leg: wait the weather window, fly in pairs, and navigate by reading the dune-crest strike (as sailors read swell). Nested anchors: dune sea > the slip face of a mega dune > the sand-smoke lifted from the slip face. Wind-and-terrain character: dawn along-field wind and afternoon turbulence; the lee vortex behind a mega dune can swallow a low-flying ship; when a slip face slides, the whole dune "sings," a low-frequency hum going through the ship's hull. Concealment: an old CI transport line is buried under the sand according to old maps, sometimes exposed for a few hundred meters between dunes and buried again the following year; no one has dug it up. Comparison line: "In the Draa you will miss the pillars of the Stone Forest Sea."

V08 · Twin-Moon Tidal Coast · twin-moon tide coast
  Parent process: funnel-bay great tidal range + tidal bore, twin moons overlaid. Parent structure: the convergent gulf group facing the open Blue Main tidal basin in Keld's northeast (center 10°N, 48°E). At twin-moon "double spring tide" the range is 15–25 m; a tidal bore of 4–6 m water wall rushes 80–100 km inland along the fjord; the intertidal band is 5–10 km wide. First sight: from the air the sea-land boundary is not a line but a ten-kilometer-wide, silver-braided band shifting with the hour — mistaken for "a badly drawn sea chart." Nested anchors: tidal band > a bore charging up a river mouth > a net-gathering skiff chasing the bore. Wind-and-terrain character: sea charts split into a high-tide and low-tide sheet; miss the schedule by an hour and freight walks three more kilometers over flat; the bore day is festival and harvest at once. Concealment: the "recoil-wave zone" where the bore strikes the cliffs in the deepest bay has never been surveyed. Comparison line: "The sea here moves house twice a day. Whether your watch is right, the shore will tell you."

V09 · The Roll · the long-cloud river (an atmospheric wonder)
  Parent process: roll cloud (equivalent to real-world Morning Glory clouds — reality is already fantastical enough). Parent structure: the fixed nighttime land-breeze front around 17°N, 38°E, between Aethelgard and Keld. A rolling cloud tube 800–1,200 km long, 1–2 km high, and a few hundred meters thick, moving at 40–60 km/h along a fixed corridor; it appears only a few dozen days per season. First sight: at dawn a white cylinder cuts across the sky and rolls toward you — the first instinct of a beginner is to turn around and flee. Time: once you catch up you can "surf" on the lift line for half a day to a day. Nested anchors: cloud tube > the lift lip at the tube's leading edge > the ships queued at the lip. Wind-and-terrain character: the Loom's most literal wonder — the sky is itself geography; a ship riding the Roll uses half the fuel to make a trans-oceanic leg; miss the seasonal window and it is two weeks of ordinary flying; riding the tube is its own craft and its own cloth in Keld. Concealment: no ship has followed the cloud tube's dissipating end to see where at sea it "falls the curtain." Comparison line: "Wonders on the ground will at least wait for you. The Roll only waits for the wind."

V10 · The Standing Storm · the resident storm (an atmospheric wonder)
  Parent process: lake-and-land breeze nighttime thunderstorm (×2) + terrain-locked standing wave. Parent structure: the confluence of a warm bay + volcanic arc + monsoon triangle at 24°S, 98°E in Duskreach's northeast. A seasonally semi-permanent thunderstorm complex, anvil top a hundred kilometers wide, discharging lightning on the same bay 200+ nights a year. First sight: 300 km away, a corner in the night sky flickering soundlessly for hours, taken for a distant city. Time: through the whole storm season it is right there; the lane sets its schedule by its diurnal cycle — around the bay at dawn, out before sunset. Nested anchors: anvil cloud > a single rain shaft below the cloud > a ship at the edge of the rain veil that finds the coast by lightning. Wind-and-terrain character: the whole region's lighthouse, calendar, and no-fly core; the surrounding settlements' roofs, sayings, and children's songs are all grown on its rhythm. Concealment: the sea bay directly under the storm's eye has no sea chart. Comparison line: "Other places look at the weather forecast. We look at its mood."

V11 · Cloud Forest Steps · Cloud Forest Steps country
  Parent process: windward mesa steps + cloud-mist forest (×5). A 600-km arcing windward cliff belt, 4–6 great steps, each with a drop of 300–800 m; below the second step is drowned in the sea of clouds all year. First sight: from the sea side it is a green wall grown out of the clouds, retreating upward level after level — you cannot count how many levels because the lowest is always in the cloud. Time: a day flying along the steps; half a day to climb level by level into port. Nested anchors: step belt > the canopy of one level > the ship in the mast berth above the canopy. Wind-and-terrain character: ships enter port in the sandwich layer above the canopy and above the cloud sea; each step has a climate, a forest, and its own products. Concealment: "below the fourth step" is the local umbrella term of the rumor. Comparison line: "In Steps country, 'down' is not a direction; it is a legend."

V12 · White Stair · white stair
  Parent process: hot-spring travertine terrace (×50). Parent structure: on the edge of the southern Duskreach geothermal province at 48°S, 70°E, in the same province as the Warm Glacier Valleys. A 20–30 km wide mineral-terrace slope, total drop 400–800 m, hundreds of warm-water pools from top to bottom. First sight: from a distance it looks like snow hung on a mountainside, but the weather is plainly not cold; on a cool-season morning, the whole slope turns into a glowing cloud-mountain. Nested anchors: white slope > the travertine lip of one pool's edge > the people in the pool. Wind-and-terrain character: Archeon's center of recuperation and bath culture; the steam column is a natural beacon of the winter lane; CI geothermal well groups make the terrace an order of magnitude larger than its natural state. Concealment: the gas concentration at the terrace's top hot-spring source is high, and no one lives there permanently. Comparison line: "Marneth's white is the white that pushes people away. White Stair's white is hot water heated and waiting for you."

V13 · Glass Plains · mirror plain
  In the dry season it is 200 km of obsidian matte ground; within 48 hours of a rain it becomes a mirror sea laying the whole sky on the ground — night flight across the mirror under twin moons is one of the Loom's three great nightscapes. In the hot season the "chimney" updraft is a free elevator to old hands, and the source of newbie grave rumors. Concealment: the central "shadowless zone" (where the ground is so flat that depth perception fails) is left as a mixture of pilots' superstition and real risk. Comparison line: "Mirror plain will give you two skies. On a day you can't tell which is real, don't fly."

V14 · Fjord Coast · deep-fjord coast
  Parent process: glacial fjords (×1.5) + twin-moon tide amplification. Parent structure: the junction of Aethelgard's northwestern old-orogenic coast at 55°N, 35°W and the Arctic island chain. Sea arms cut 150–250 km inland, walls 1,500–2,500 m; tides in the fjord funnel amplify into a mouth race. First sight: from the sea, a row of split-apart mountains; from the land side, a sea hidden in the mountains. Nested anchors: fjord wall > a kilometer-long waterfall on the wall > a lean-shelf small station halfway up the falls. Wind-and-terrain character: at dusk the ice-sheet wind pours down the wall — "the fjord exhales at sunset"; berths are all in the lee corner of the tributary-fjord confluence; the knowledge of these positions is a local pilot family's estate. Concealment: at the very end of the deepest sea arm there is said to be a door of a CI marine station, but the tide is too swift and no one has approached. Comparison line: "The fjord's wind follows the sunset — more punctual than the Guild."

V15 · Organ Country · pipe-organ country
  Parent process: columnar-basalt province + wind-eroded holes (×100, acoustically amplified). A 300 km columnar-forest plateau, canyons like the pipes of an organ. On typical wind days the whole province emits a low hum near the human hearing threshold, coming into the soles of the feet through the ship's hull before it reaches the ears; local navigation is half "listening to the mountain" — South Organ is a minor third lower than North Organ, a living sea chart to old pilots; on storm days the whole province plays in ensemble, and settlements shut their windows to celebrate the festival. Concealment: the deepest resonance canyon cannot be entered by ship — the sound pressure itself is a wall. Comparison line: (back-reference) "— so I say, Orun's wind is only big, Organ's wind is alive."

Landform families:
  Skyleaf; Warm Glacier Valleys;
  Salt-Diapir Badlands / Rust-Band Cliffs, which can appear in various arid regions;
  Falling Plateaus / Fog Bowls / Sky Mesas / Lava-Tube Roads, distributed across the whole planet. Atheria's Falling Plateaus is a livable small member of the Falling Plateaus family; see §4.7.

Whisperflat lies in an old shallow-lake basin in the arid south of Aethelgard, a regional-scale wind-imaging landform of the salt-diapir / dry-lake-crust family. A medium-speed airship takes several hours to cross; by day the surface is pale gray, milk-white, and pale teal, and at night a light breeze makes the mineral crystalline sheets light up in cold green thin lines, painting the direction and branching of the near-ground wind layer on the ground. Strong wind beats the light patterns into dust and noise; only when the ship stops can you hear the crystalline sheets' extremely faint dry rustle. It is an organ of the Axiom-line lane, not the planet's sole wonder.

4.6.3 Rumor layer (one-line spec · unverified · some should be false)

The following always stays a single line. If any later enters the story, at least two or three should be shown to be wrong, exaggerated, or something completely different from the rumor.

- The Second Wall: on a far continent there is said to be a cliff that makes Orun look small. No repeat flight.
- The River-Swallowing Cave: a great river vanishes into a cave straight in the highlands. Old maps have coordinates.
- The Absurdly Round Lake: a perfect circle, bottomless. Those who have sounded it contradict one another.
- The Never-Extinguished Beach: a stretch of coast has an eternal flame in the crevices between stones. Fishermen grill fish over it.
- The Sea That Glows Seven Nights After a Double Full Moon: south of Keld. Recorded in an old log of a CI marine station.
- The Walking Island: after a seabed eruption, a raft of pumice drifts as a fleet, appearing once every few years.
- The Compass-Crazed Basin: passing ships' compasses all spin in circles. The old lane detours 50 km around it.
- The Still-Wind River in the Sky: an old captain insists there is a stable wind river at high altitude that halves a Keld crossing. His fuel logs support him; no one else has flown out to it.
- The Green Valley Under the Ice: it is said that beneath the far-southern ice margin there is a whole warm valley. Those who have seen it are all "a friend of a friend."

Writing calibration line: "You think Orun's wind is bad? Go to South Organ, then tell me."
As long as this line exists, the world will not be capped by Orun.

4.6.6 Vista–Settlement–Resource Binding

A wonder cannot only answer "what does one see, flying over?" It must also answer "how do the people nearby live by it." The table below locks the type of relationship; unnamed settlements remain OPEN, and are named only when the corresponding continent enters the story, and must not for that reason be written as unmanned land.

| Vista | Settlement / user binding | Local surplus and everyday use | Loom function | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| V01 Orun | Riverbend sits in the water-cut of the main-wall system; Cliffwatch and Windbreak are core towns at the plateau edge and the southern end of the bypass edge; the wall-shadow band has its own ribbon of villages | Aquifer, waterfall water power, irrigation, basalt raw material; residents tell time by "wall-shadow hour" | Along-wall updrafts, around-wall edge, navigation by The Weeps | 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| V02 Marneth | Skyleaf sits in the enclosed crater on the north flank; Cinderlode on the east side's grass sea / volcanic black plain | Meltwater, geothermal flower fields and medicine, highland crops, ice-crown weather knowledge | Kestrel's Gate seasonal line; the crown cannot be crossed | 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| V03 Smoke Wall | Upstream lake-district ports, cliff-edge observation communities, and downstream spray-forest belt; the core mist column has no permanent residence | Enormous fresh water, water power, wet-forest material, and hydrologic data; resource is used at the outer ring of the core | The main lane detours through the upstream, and the mist column is at once a no-go zone and a hundred-kilometer beacon | 【REGIONAL NAME OPEN】 |
| V04 Karst Sea | Shellharbor (a multi-port city on the edge of the Karst Sea), tower-top villages, hanging-platform ports, and local pilot communities | Tower-top planting, wetland / water-way harvest, carbonate materials, echo-piloting craft | Foreign ships as a rule find a pilot who knows local water to lead them in; all movement depends on air and water | 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| V05 Rift | Lake ports, geothermal towns (OPEN), and the return-leg plateau station Driftmoor strung along The Draft | Geothermal, mineral salts, rift-lake produce, and cheap process heat | The Loom's one-way street; ride the wind in, return by way of the plateau | 【NAMED BINDING · return station; the in-valley lake ports OPEN】 |
| V06 High Inland Sea | Island towns, shore ports, thin-air shipyards (OPEN); the foothill great city Gearstead | Highland wetlands, reed and lake produce, an independent light-frame ship craft | Altitude forms an independent regional network; heavy goods switch ships at the foothill to ships fit for thin air | 【NAMED BINDING · foothill; inland-sea island towns OPEN】 |
| V07 Draa | Bound only to the ports on the two edges, weather camps, and companion zones; no permanent points set inside | Core value is crossing knowledge, dune navigation, and the occasionally exposed assay clue; no regular industry is forced in | Managed as a trans-oceanic leg: weather window, paired flight, no midway resupply | 【WILDERNESS LOCK】 |
| V08 Twin-Moon Coast | Tidemark (a tidal-bay town · river-mouth port) and tide-chase harvest ships | Salt, intertidal food, shallow-sea aquaculture / harvest, tidal power and dual-chart craft | High / low tide dual charts; bore day sets the port schedule and the festival | 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| V09 The Roll | Seasonal staging ports at the two ends in Aethelgard and Keld, Roll rider guilds | The most direct surplus is usable wind: fuel savings, express delivery, and a specialized ship craft and cloth are raised on it | Seasonal surfing line; if the season is off, take the ordinary trans-oceanic line | 【ENDPOINTS REQUIRED】 |
| V10 Standing Storm | Settlements at the periphery of the warm bay, no one within the no-fly core | Rain water, warm-bay and volcanic-soil produce, continuous weather record; roofs and calendars remade for the thunderstorm | Around the bay at dawn, out before sunset; the natural beacon of the whole region | 【ANCHOR REQUIRED】 |
| V11 Cloud Forest Steps | Verdance (a first- / second-level step port); the remaining per-level ports, forest-belt communities, and exchange between upper and lower levels | Each level has a climate, a forest, and a crop, medicine, or building material; upper and lower levels are one another's near-neighbor trade | Along-step flight, level-by-level climb into port | 【NAMED BINDING · first level; upper levels OPEN】 |
| V12 White Stair | Hearthstone (a plateau-outer-edge bath town, recuperation communities, and a winter air station); the source has no permanent residence | Geothermal, mineral salts, warm water, recuperation, and winter greenhouses | The steam column is a winter beacon; a supply / rest point on the southern line | 【NAMED BINDING】 |
| V13 Glass Plains | Edge observation stations and post-rain night-flight staging field; the central shadowless zone has no permanent residence | Obsidian assay, thermal observation, and post-rain short-window traffic; whether large-scale mining scales up depends on subsequent ecology / craft audit | Hot-season updrafts, twin-moon night flights, depth-perception risk | (MARGIN ONLY) |
| V14 Fjord Coast | Ports at the tributary-fjord confluences, lean-shelf stations, and pilot families | Natural deep-water / leeward moorings, fresh water, cold-coast produce, and ship-hull maintenance | Sunset downdrafts; the knowledge of position decides safe mooring | 【ANCHOR REQUIRED】 |
| V15 Organ Country | Valley-mouth settlements at North / South Organ, and the mountain-listening pilot community | Basalt, stable wind, acoustic maps, and the mills, sawmills, drawing sheds, and acoustic-instrument crafts that work by borrowing the wind; festival grown from the province's low hum | Listen to sound and tell the region; the deep resonance canyon is a permanently forbidden core | 【ANCHORS OPEN】 |

Five preparation baselines:

1. Write clearly at least one slice of vista that a front door / workshop / port can see for a long time, not just a panoramic view from an airship.
2. There must be at least one local surplus that ordinary people use daily, and one specialty item that must be exchanged from elsewhere.
3. Specify the nearest T2+ hydrogen / repair point; if a wonder has no port within it, specify the last departure port before entering it.
4. Specify one "too plentiful here to use up / especially well made" local surplus, and at least one thing that has, along that line, been repeatedly carried back by people from elsewhere; the exchange must not consist only of lifesaving material.
5. Retain a stretch of land that is not tamed by the settlement's presence. Life-binding adds scale; it does not cancel the unknown.


4.6.7 Mid-range Terrain Register · lane mid-scenery

Full-design vistas (§4.6.2) are planetary wonders; mid-scenery is the continuous geography along a lane within P2/P3 radius — so that Anna, at each new place, is first struck by a non-visual signature (smell, soundscape, air humidity, color of light) before she sees the terrain. Mid-scenery is not a downscaled version of a great wonder; it is what gives "moving around" its texture as a continuous world.

Format (one paragraph per entry, inheriting a simplified version of the vista eight questions):
- Edge / nearest settlement it hangs from
- Landform family (§4.6.2 archive family)
- First sight misperception + how long it occupies the horizon
- Effect on flight decisions
- Non-visual signature (at least one)
- One everyday use

---

MR-01 · Crestline Gate / gateway cliff mouth

Hangs from: Frontier–Crestline Edge, 68 km south of Atheria. Landform family: Falling Plateaus. Crestline (40k, population including the two-sided core of the cliff mouth, the northern-terrace farmsteads, and the life-belt spread along the Ishkar) is built on both sides of the notch where the Atheria terrace belt drops to the Frontier plain in its last cliff — the Ishkar converges into a swift narrow channel here to pass through the cliff mouth, with the cliff walls only 300 m apart. Flying north from Frontier, from a distance it is a break in the horizon, the cliff shoulders on both sides like split door-posts; closer up, on the cliff shoulders on both sides, rooftops in patches emerge, along with the fields of the northern terrace and the life-belt spread along the Ishkar — this is a town of forty thousand, not just a wind mouth; the moment of passing through, the south wind switches to inter-cliff turbulence, the ship jolts sideways, and then Atheria's low waterfall bass rushes toward you. Non-visual signature: before the mouth is the dry-grass hot-dust smell of the Frontier plain; after the mouth it switches immediately to the wet-stone-and-water-mist of the Ishkar — the smell arrives before the sight. Flight effect: on a strong-wind day the cliff mouth becomes a natural wind tunnel, limiting the class of ship that can pass; local skiffs run flush along the cliff wall, and larger ships detour on the western wide slope. The natural cliff height carries the vertical clearance of the T2 port; the open mooring apron and the hydrogen-fill shed sit on the wide terrace to the north, not crammed into a 300 m narrow mouth as a tower. Everyday use: the river fish sun-dried on the two sides of the cliff mouth is the cheapest protein exchange between Atheria and Frontier. Edge of the P1 day-return circle; first stop of P2.

MR-02 · Millhaven Sky Mesa / high-plateau isolated mesa

Hangs from: Frontier–Millhaven Edge, 1,139 km northwest of Frontier. Landform family: Sky Mesas. Millhaven (40k; the mesa-top old town occupies about a third, the rest distributed along the mesa-base windmill belt, mill villages, farmsteads, and a few leeward streets) grows around a flat-topped basalt mesa rising from the highland face; the mesa top is about 200 m above the surrounding plateau, sheer on all four sides, top surface about 5–6 km² (mesa-top old town built-up area about 3.5 km²; the rest is mesa-top kitchen plots, drying yards, and windmill bases). Approaching from the east, you first see a shadow on the highland's grass face that does not move — mistaken for a giant cumulus congealed in place; only closer up do you make out that it is cliff wall. Occupies the horizon for about half an hour. Non-visual signature: the mesa top has a stable updraft year-round, and a skiff can circle here without engine; the ear feels a slight drop in pressure first. Flight effect: approach to the top can only be from the leeward side — the windward-side clifftop downdraft pushes a skiff back down; Iris-line heavy ships moor at the mesa-base T2 flat wharf and go up and down by lift cage, with the hydrogen-fill shed and gas tank also at the base, so as not to make the whole tabletop into a port. Everyday use: the highland wind unceasingly drives a whole row of windmills, and Millhaven is the largest single windmill and mill belt on the Iris line, and also the most reliable milling and re-power stop; several leeward streets and mill villages at the base keep the life of forty thousand from being crammed onto the mesa top. Overnight stop in the middle of the P3 Iris long voyage.

MR-03 · Iris Hollow fog-port arrival sequence

Hangs from: Iris Hollow (60k), Aethelgard west coast, facing Azure Main. Landform family: west-coast wet forest + cold-current foggy coast. Approaching from inland, first the plateau flattens, the tree belt thickens, the air grows heavy; then the ground vanishes below a gray-white fog blanket, and only the tallest heartwood canopies emerge from the top of the fog. Iris Hollow is built in a U-shaped hollow where the coast has retreated; the fog pours down the cliff wall from the sea — the city is in the fog most of the time. Occupies the horizon: the fog blanket is visible from about 100 km out, and the last 30 km into port is flown in the fog. First sight misperception: "the sea has already begun" — but that is fog, not sea; the true sea appears only when the fog lifts. Non-visual signature: Atheria's air is dry mineral and waterfall mist; Iris's air is salt, rotted leaves, and cold — the first time Anna smells the sea is before she sees it. Soundscape: the fog swallows sound; the engine's hum becomes muffled, a human voice does not carry twenty meters, and the approach to port is by fog-horn arrangement and short-range radio. Flight effect: on heavy-fog days it is all by instruments and fog-horn to enter port; cliff-wall echoes are distorted in the fog and cannot be used for distance. Everyday use: the cold-current upwelling brings marine yields so plentiful that Iris exports pickled fish and sea salt inland. Endpoint of Anna's first true long voyage at 11–12; her first sight of the sea, her first climatic contrast.

The salt smell of Iris and the "salt on his clothes" when Edmund returns in §8 S13 echo each other, indicating that he has been on the west-coast line, but the link is not spelled out.

MR-04 · Coppergate–Windbreak line: Orun in the distance

Hangs from: Coppergate–Cliffwatch–Windbreak Edge, 700–1,270 km south of Frontier. Landform family: flood-basalt plateau (Orun parent). South of Coppergate, the ground gradually shifts from Thread mixed farmland-forest to low shrub and exposed basalt ground. One morning, on the southern horizon, a straight line appears where none should be — half a degree above the skyline, its color darker than the ground — mistaken for a strange long cloud. That line does not move for a whole day, is still there the next day, is still there the third. It is Orun's main wall in the distance (from here, still 300–500 km from the main wall). Non-visual signature: the wind grows larger, drier, with a faint bitter basalt dust in it; diurnal temperature range widens — hot by day, sharply cold by night. Flight effect: the distant updraft of the main wall can already be felt as a faint sustained tailwind by long-voyage ships. First sight is locked in the P5 fifteen-to-sixteen-year-old working period; P3 does not use Orun in the distance, and only the same working phase or slightly later brings her in for a closer approach.

MR-05 · Amber Reach / mesa-island city arrival sequence

Hangs from: Frontier–Thornfield–Ironvein–Amber Reach Edge; Amber Reach (60k), about 2,390 km southeast of Frontier, main-line actual flight about 2,500–2,700 km. Landform family: East Mesas / Sky Mesas. Beyond Ironvein, the rolling hills break into isolated basalt mesas; approaching from the northwest, the lower halves of three to five flat-topped mesa islands are first wiped away by heat-dust and low cloud, leaving straight black shadows suspended between sky and land, resembling at first glance a fleet of overly neatly stopped great ships. Another hour or so of flying, and the bridge network, the mesa-edge cantilever workshops, and the lighters lifting from the valley floor become clear. Non-visual signature: dry grass and iron dust fall away, and the air fills with the sweet-bitter smell of sun-warmed wood, resin lacquer, and seed-grain baking; wind passes through skybridges of different spans, giving off a continuous whistle of pitches. Flight effect: mesa-edge updrafts can hold a skiff aloft but form a sharp sink zone on the leeward side; Class Vs do not go up on the mesa, and enter the T3 ground-anchor field along valley-floor markers, with cargo and transiting people distributed to the mesa islands by lighter. Everyday use: mesa-top orchards, seed-grain sifting, and cantilever workshops jointly supply resin, envelope material, carbon-fiber precursor, and silicone products. CI ancestor: the valley floor and the bases of several mesa islands were originally a group of CI seed-processing secondary stations — dry wind channels, constant-temperature underground bins, moisture-vent wells, weight-sorting ramps, and continuous experimental planting zones that had undergone decades of soil improvement. The machines are long gone; the shape of the buildings still prescribes how the place is used: five hundred years later, human-made wood sifting racks, wind-sifters, and drying rooms all cram into these spaces. Amber's seed-grain sifting and resin / envelope-material processes are grown out of this shape, not out of "discovering a relic." Endpoint of Anna's second named long voyage at twelve or thirteen (P3), and the first time she sees, from a Class V, a place where "the city itself is a landform."

---


4.7 Atheria · falling-terrace module

Sense anchor:
On the north side of Frontier the plain drops in many terraces, like an enlarged farmland terrace.

4.7.1 Terrain

- Location: 27.8°N, 0.5°E; north-northeast of Frontier (26.0°N, 0.0°E), 154 km via the Crestline gate; regional ship usually 2–3 hours, headwind or heavy load 4 hours.
- A livable small member of the Falling Plateaus landform family: a north-high, south-low continental terrace belt, extending east-west over a hundred kilometers; Atheria occupies a stretch (about 15–20 km wide) where the Ishkar north branch cuts across the terrace belt.
- Profile (north→south, working values):
  Upper Shelf (windy dryland pasture, windmill line)
  → Cliff A about 150 m → First Terrace T1 (terrace surface about 2 km wide)
  → Cliff B about 150 m → Second Terrace T2 (terrace surface about 2 km wide)
  → Cliff C about 120 m → Lower Plain (agricultural plain unfurling toward Frontier)
  Total drop about 420 m, horizontal depth 6–8 km.
- Water system: the north branch of the Ishkar comes from the upper shelf and falls at each cliff as a waterfall / water ladder; CI-era water channels divide water laterally at each terrace surface (kin to The Long Cut legacy); water wheels, mills, and fermenting caves are embedded in the cliff body and at its foot.
- The cliff edge is the wharf: an airship hovers flush with the cliff face, mooring line tied to a cliff-edge post, gangway laid onto the terrace — deck and ground are almost at the same height, no need to build another terminal that separates ship from life. Walking from the port-terrace eatery, drying platform, and berth front onto the ship is just the space continuing outward: heartwood terrace planks meet the gangplank, meet the ship's deck; the rail height, mooring hand-signals, the cloth drying in the wind, and the touch of the wood underfoot all belong to the same local life. The big ships tie up at the cliff, the small ones drop into the field.
  (Rewritten by the borrowed-height axiom: Atheria borrows falling cliff-terraces.)

4.7.2 Layers and population (total 45k · working values)

**Position:** Atheria is a mature, prosperous, and quite ordinary T2 terrace city, not the northern capital of Aethelgard or the "secondary center" of Frontier. The narration describes it in such detail only because Anna grew up here.

- Harbour Shelf / Port Shelf: main skyport (leeward band at the foot of Cliff A + main berths along Cliff B, 6–10 berths), storage belt, ship racks, capstan sheds, heavier pressure / metal work, and port-side eateries. About 5k people.
  "One level up" — going home to look at the ships means going up a level.
- Home Shelf: the main town Terracehead straddles both banks of the Ishkar; residences, food shops, weaving rooms, mills, fruit-press houses, small repair shops, and courtyards are mixed in the same life-belt; the terrace-city small wharf has 2–3 berths. About 18k people.
  The Eyrie sits on the projecting basalt outcrop on the southern edge of the Home Shelf at the top of Cliff C (the outcrop details — a 30–50 m vertical short cliff face, a constant-temperature shaded workroom, a small berth, the bell — are carried over verbatim from world's current canon), overlooking the fields spread out below Cliff C.
- Cliff-foot ribbon villages of each terrace: several dozen, each 100–800 people, totaling about 13k; some grow around mills, fermenting caves, or fruit-pressing sheds, some are only channels, fields, and a row of houses, and are not all called "workshop villages" uniformly.
- Lower-plain farmstead villages: about 9k, with anchor rings in the fields (used for harvest-season mooring).

4.7.3 Farmland (working values)

- Terrace-surface fine-work belt (T1/T2 canal irrigation: vegetables, beans, orchard, wine fruit, forage): total about 60–70 km².
- Lower-plain grain belt: about 150–200 km² (including rotation fallow).
- Upper-shelf dryland farming and pasture: about 80–100 km².
- 45k people at 0.35 ha / person is roughly only about 158 km² equivalent farmland; Atheria's actual 290–370 km² is clearly above the baseline, so "grain, beans, and stepwine plentiful enough to be casually carried away" is local abundance, not that it bears the granary duty of a capital.
- Atheria is not the "northern granary node" of Frontier, and Frontier is not a demand center it must serve. Both maintain their basic daily lives on their own; stable traffic lets local surplus enter one another's lives.

4.7.4 Architecture and transportation

Atheria's architecture is first determined by the three falling cliffs and the width of the terrace surfaces, not by the "Archeon-generic terrace skin." The continuous life-belts of the Home Shelf and Harbour Shelf are mostly two to five stories, but what actually fills the field of view are low houses, fruit trees, canals, courtyards, drying platforms, mills, and patches of ground still being planted; from anywhere you look out, you can quickly see the buildings end and the fields begin again.

Common ordinary dwellings come in two forms: one is basalt / geopolymer stone base holding one or two stories of heartwood timber, with kitchen, storage, and water trough in the cooler lower story and sleep beds and windows in the upper; the other is a live-and-work long house stretched along the terrace surface, one end for living and the other end perhaps a weaving room, an oven, a fruit-press room, a herb-drying rack, or a small repair shop. It is not that every household "lives inside a workshop." The organs of public life are more varied than the workshops: a shared stone bread oven for the whole terrace, mills, water troughs, laundry lines, a long house often borrowed for storytelling suppers, a fruit-wine pressing shed, a laundry platform on the canal, a warm wall the children love to sit against, and a few broad steps that fill with people by evening.

Color comes from material and use: heartwood is red-brown when freshly cut, deepening in the sun to dark chestnut or silver gray; basalt walls and house bases are cold gray to almost black; roofs are mostly gray tile, deep wood shingle, and local copper edging, with oxidized-copper green mainly on rain gutters, pipe joints, rail caps, and the edges that often meet water. Stepwine, dyed cloth, and herbs turn the drying platforms into seasonal color blocks, so from an airship Atheria is not a "gray-brown city," but a life-belt broken by fields, fruit trees, red-earth cloth, pale bed sheets, and deep-green bean racks. On the port shelf, along ridgelines, and at a few of the most wind-catching cliff edges, narrow long strips of cloth are often hung: narrow trial-color strips left by the dye shops, wind cloth specially woven to be very light, a length of color casually left up high by a household — all of them can be raised when the wind first arrives. They do not form a unified flag code, and no one requires every house to hang one; over hundreds of years people have simply grown accustomed to letting the surface of life draw the air for them.


The regional ships tied up at the port and this life-belt belong to the same material world. Their deep heartwood decks and thin panels, matte-black basalt secondary load-bearing bones, local light-alloy / brass nodes, tight-framed small glass, and canvas cloth already come from the same material world; the components that truly carry heavy loads need not all be exposed on the outside, and a piece of hull that looks thick from outside is often only a thin face over a light-core sandwich, an inspection cover, or an aerodynamic skin. Metal concentrates most easily at mooring lugs, hinges, bearing seats, valves, rail caps, and the nodes people touch often, and does not make a whole floating volume out of thick metal. Seen from a distance, one sometimes cannot tell whether a color belongs to an eave, a drying platform, or a ship. The correct feeling is not "there are airships under the city," but **that the city has taken some of its weight off, cast off the mooring lines, and flown out.**

The Eyrie belongs to one extreme of the southern edge of the Home Shelf: its front platform hangs straight over the open side, its back bites into the basalt outcrop; it is half Edmund's workspace, and half kitchen, sleep berth, storage, Miriam's drying platform, and the home where Anna has been leaving things around since childhood.

Lift cages ride the outside of the cliff (the Old-cable main-load rope entry unchanged); narrow-gauge flat-cars run along the T2 canal banks (a documented exception to the borrowing-flat rule). People sit in baskets, heavy things go on flats; the truly close daily life is still on foot, on short bridges, on canal-side paths, and on the shortcuts the children have trodden out themselves.

Background traffic (vista working values) continues to obey the global working value of about fifty thousand available buoyancy craft in Year 521, but Atheria is still not an all-weather congested skyport. The skiffs / small work craft used, kept, or repeatedly passing through Atheria's life-circle over the long term can be understood by the author side as about 600–900, dispersed among the terraces and field belts. At any moment of the daylight looking out from the Eyrie's front platform, one commonly sees 5–12 skiffs / small floaters dispersed across several layers of air and field belts; on the port shelf and along approach lines, one can usually also see 1–3 Class I / II, and perhaps 0–1 Class III mooring, waiting on the wind, or already tied up. Harvest segments and clear-and-stable windows are clearly busier; a bad-wind day suddenly empties out much of the sky. Class Vs do not land at Atheria; when the weather is clear, in the far southern open sky one may occasionally see the distant shape of a large ship heading to or from the Frontier Heavy Berth — not a fixed daily show, and not a spectacle worth the whole town stopping to see. The numbers only serve as an upper bound for the vista; why a ship comes today and why it does not come tomorrow are still decided by wind, human promises, what is in hand, and the next leg of the journey.

4.7.5 Sensory anchors

- Acoustics: single-face echo: the life sounds of the next terrace are lifted up by the cliff face, the cliff behind reflects them once more, the open side eats the sound; the waterfall is a low-bass floor at all hours. Childhood-chapter acoustic scenes are re-derived from this.
- Morning fog: the fog river lies on the lower plain and on each terrace surface, and the Eyrie is often above the fog; the "the rooftops on the opposite slope emerge" image is replaced with "the rooftops of the next terrace emerge from the fog."
- View: from the front door one sees the distance — the roofs of the next terrace, the sea of fields on the lower plain, and all the way to the horizon; airships come out of the horizon, and one can watch them fly for ten minutes before they arrive. The "world = home" of childhood's first stage is now carried by the eaves and by things at hand, no longer carried by an enclosing terrain.
- Visible layers of wind: Atheria does not use one wind sock to answer, for the whole terrace city, "which way the wind is blowing." The air of the Upper Shelf, Harbour Shelf, Home Shelf, and Lower Plain is often not making the same motion: the narrow cloth up high has already been pulled taut while the drying cloth of the next terrace is only lightly ballooning; the cliff-edge cloth suddenly turns over, and the wind sock in the field may not catch up for a while yet. The locals do not necessarily take this as a knowledge system; they only glance at it in passing when leaving the house, taking in the laundry, letting a ship go, or waiting for one. To Anna, it makes "the same sky has many layers" an ordinary fact of life long before she can say any word of meteorology.
- The old name `canyon brew / Canyon City` is retired. Atheria's wine is generally called `Stepwine / three-fall step wine`, with local full name `Three-Fall Stepwine / Three-Fall Stepwine`; the fermentation caves migrate to the constant-temperature caves in the cliff body, and the wine's substance and its status as social currency remain the same. The main-town name Terracehead is still used.

4.7.6 Childhood-chapter re-anchor list (for migration)

- Carried over unchanged: three-part harmony, blue windbells, sun berries (in situ on the sun-facing cliff wall), platforms / baskets, plank-walks, tube bells, Launch Day, market great day, glider-rack season (terrace-surface thermals give the same result).
- Requiring re-wording: all "valley floor / slope face / opposite slope / valley wall" are mapped per 4.7.5.
- Improved: first look at an old flight chart (home is only a point), a high-flying ship's shadow passing over — now visible every day at home over the horizon; these seeds gain terrain support.


4.8 Frontier City · scale

Frontier's first impression is still a mountain range of pylons, but once one goes inside, the illusion that "the whole city is a skyport" quickly disappears. The 21 main towers are only the bones; two hundred and fifty thousand people actually live at the feet of the towers, on the Broad Terraces, on the slope streets between two towers, in the outer-ring residential belts, and along the long streets reaching out to the basin's farmland.

From 30–40 km out one first sees the forest of pylons and the ships tied to them; closer, the terrace mountain at the towers' feet grows textures: 3–8 story heartwood / basalt dwellings, small bridges spanning between two buildings, drying cloth, window-sill vegetable boxes, edible green walls on sun walls, roof-water troughs, narrow courtyards, and the white smoke of the public bake oven. At the top of the Spire Docks, one still sees extremely fine wind cloth, wind socks, and signal lamps scattered across different pylons and bridge levels; from a distance, they are only a few strips of color suddenly pulling taut and going slack again, not one enormous "airport flag." Once truly inside the city, many streets simply do not see the airships — the sight lines are blocked by buildings, trellises, undersides of bridges, and the cloth not yet taken in the previous night, and one only knows one is still in a sky city that has taken several layers of air together into its life by the shadow occasionally passing overhead, or by a strip of cloth high above suddenly turning to another side.

Housing is not "workshop periphery." An older Broad Terrace commonly has, on the ground floor, food shops, print rooms, weaving rooms, glass repair, small clinics, or public kitchens, and two or three stories of dwelling above; the newer outer-ring streets are more often two- or three-story long houses with small courtyards, and the back court directly connects to fruit trees, kitchen plots, rain barrels, or a very small side shed. Some people work all their lives outside the port district and yet belong wholly to Frontier: cooking, hand-copying and printing, looking after children, tending vertical farm walls, firing kilns, cutting cloth, seeing patients, running the bathhouse, milling, brewing, mending roofs, running a stall at the same spot every day between two terraces.


**Ground-to-sky continuous visual:** The ships moored at Spire Docks and the city share the same set of materials. The same batch of heartwood will become the handrail of a Broad Terrace, and also the deck and side of a regional ship; the same batch of hand-blown glass can be made thick and bright in a house window, and cut into smaller, lighter pieces on a ship; the same basalt fiber is a truss under a bridge, and a skeleton and load-bearing band inside a ship. What truly changes is load, wind, weight, and mode of use, not the civilization suddenly switching to a different formal language. A pier is therefore more like a stretch of street reached out into the sky from the terrace, than a boarding gate in the modern sense.
The color of the city is not "copper + steam." Basalt gray, heartwood deep brown, and sun-silvered gray are the base; oxidized-copper green appears on water troughs, old pipes, and eave edges; cloth, fruit, ceramic glaze, and handmade glass from different places make the same street change color with the Thread. After rain the wooden walls deepen; in the long twilight the copper roofs and the pale glass reflect a whole Broad Terrace to amber.

Frontier's day is not opened by a single clock. The neighborhoods next to old processing towers still hear a low exhaust; the basin edge first smells baking grain and wet earth; the weavers' district begins the morning with the clap of long cloth being pulled taut; the Heavy Berth side may never truly be quiet at any time in the night. The great sound at dusk is still preserved, but it is only one layer of background heard by many streets, not the sign that all people are stopping work at the same moment.

Scale-lock update: resident population about 250k, with short peaks around festivals / big-wind windows going to about 285–300k. Pylons: 21 visible main towers in total; 17 use the load-bearing cores of CI regional-materials / atmosphere-processing plants, and 4 are 100–160 m secondary towers built by humans with basalt-fiber trusses after Year 400. Only towers on CI skeletons can go 200–300 m. The population drop does not tear the skyline: Frontier is still the largest Great Junction, only no longer using three hundred thousand people to press the other mature towns into "small nodes." Spire Docks berths small-to-medium fast ships; Class V still goes to the outer-ring Heavy Berth. The vertical farm walls and the ridge windmill line are all retained.

Frontier is not a city that happened to find twenty derelict buildings, nor "an airport that grew into a city." It is a place formed by humans, over centuries of living between an array of industrial bones, layering houses, bridges, kitchens, flower pots, moorings, furnaces, markets, and daily life wholly unrelated to flight on top of one another.


4.9 Settlement form atlas: footprint, farmland, story height, skyport

4.9.1 Statistical skeleton table

Registration format: `settlement | population | built-up area km² | farmland km² and per capita | building form and story band | vertical skeleton origin | skyport rating · berths (by Class) · structural height | how large ships moor | energy layer | industrial role`. First fill in seven sites with narrative role; the rest as the story enters; Atheria's row is copied directly from §4.7 as a template.

- Frontier · 250k | built-up area about 16–17 km² (21-pylon array + terrace mountain + peripheral life belt; do not tear the skyline for the population drop) | basin farmland about 620–680 km² (Basin Seven good land, biome coefficient 0.7) + pylon vertical farming walls; the farmland around the city is enough to maintain basic daily life, and Atheria's grain and stepwine are only one common local good among many | terrace 3–8 story heartwood CLT, pylon 100–300 m ×21 (17 CI bones, 4 self-built 100–160 m) | CI regional-materials plant skeleton | T4 · upper Spire Docks Class I–III about 30–40 berths (berth count is decided by lane function, not driven down by population drop), outer-ring Heavy Berth 4–6 Class V positions (allow about 220–270 m turning / tow clearance for a 90–120 m ship; not required to be full on ordinary days), hundreds of skiff pits · top-of-tower berth 250–300 m | Class V moors at the outer-ring Heavy Berth ground anchor, does not go up on a tower | Tier 0 ×2 stacks | Large high-temperature processes: precision parts, fuel-cell stacks, one of the three Class V final-assembly sites
- Atheria · 45k | three-terrace ribbon city about 12 km long (Harbour Shelf / Home Shelf / cliff-foot villages / lower plain), built-up area about 9–10 km² | 290–370 km²; this is local farmland surplus, not "capital hinterland"; at the resident population it is roughly 0.65–0.8 ha / person | terrace surface 2–5 stories + cliff-body shaded workrooms + lift cages | natural cliff drop (Cliff A/B/C each 120–150 m) in place of a tower | T2 · Cliff B main-berth 6–10 berths (Class I–III), Home Shelf small wharf 2–3 berths · structural height = cliff height | Class V does not land; skiff / lighter meet in the air | Tier 2 hydro + Frontier hydrogen cache | Grain, beans, stepwine and several common local crafts; the narrative importance comes from Anna's familiarity, not from administrative tier
- Cinderlode · 60k | ore valley ribbon about 15 km, built-up area about 7 km²; dwellings run in ribbons on the drier two sides of the valley wall, with food shops, bathhouses, laundry platforms, narrow kitchen plots, and long guest houses interleaved between the furnace district and the residential district | about 100–130 km² (local farmland is limited; the surrounding mature lines have long carried in grain, fruit, and brews from different places; a broken line means fewer choices and a more expensive road, not that grain is cut off at once) | stone / geopolymer 2–4 stories, small windows, thick walls, shallow-sloped roofs; outside the furnace district a great many houses have nothing to do with smelting | self-built skyport towers ×2 (basalt truss, about 90 m) | T3 · tower top and tower body 10–12 Class I–III berths, valley-mouth 1–2 Class V positions | Class V moors at the valley-mouth ground anchor | Tier 0 ×1 + geothermal | Large high-temperature processes: metals, basalt fiber, titanium, SiC
- Iris Hollow · 60k | coastal cove, built-up area about 8 km²; houses follow the arc coast above high-tide line and several inland-rising streets, with boat sheds, fish houses, salt houses, and tide-terraces low down, dense housing, guest houses, greenhouses, and markets higher up | about 190–220 km² + fishing / coastal harvest | 3–6 stories, stone base + tide-resistant heartwood, upper windows many and narrow; steep roofs, dense gutters | self-built skyport tower about 110 m + sea-side Heavy Berth | T3/T4 · tower 12–15 berths, sea-side 2–3 Class V / ocean hauler positions | Large ships moor at the sea-side floating pier anchor field | Tier 0 ×1 | Large high-temperature processes: glass and optics, trans-oceanic shipyard, salt chemistry
- Amber Reach · 60k | 3–5 mesa island group, connected by skybridge, built-up area about 7 km²; each mesa island has dense housing, orchards, drying rooms, seed-sifting courts, public kitchens, and large planting surfaces, while the valley floor holds heavy-ship berths and cooler long houses | mesa top about 110 km² + valley floor about 110 km² | mesa top 2–4 stories, stone base / wood upper, the bridge net joining rooftops, drying decks, and different mesa surfaces into a daily walking layer | mesa island itself + mesa-edge tower 70–100 m | T3 · each mesa-island tower 8–10 berths total, valley floor 1 Class V position | Class V moors at valley-floor ground anchor, brought up onto the mesa by lighter | Tier 1 CFU + wind | Resin, envelope material, seed-grain sifting, carbon-fiber precursor, silicone
- Riverbend · 40k | canal core about 4.5–5 km², plus along-canal farmstead and housing belts; the town stretches along two main canals and Ishkar tributaries into a kilometers-long life belt, and bridgeheads, mills, public bake ovens, fruit-press houses, laundry platforms, and small markets form several small centers | about 140–170 km² canal-irrigated | 1–3 stories; near-water often uses basalt stone base + heartwood upper, and away from water, more wood houses and fruit-tree courtyards | open river bank provides turning clearance, no tower, 40 m mast + ground-anchor set | T2 · 4–5 Class I–III berths (set by canal-run and harvest-season lane function, not driven up by population), flat wharves, electrolysis sheds, and low-pressure gas tanks | Class V does not land; regional ships or skiff / lighter meet | Tier 2 hydro | Grain, river-smoked fish, milled flour, fruits; filter cartridges and gaskets are only one kind of outside-line need
- Skyleaf · 30k | crater lake-shore core about 4 km², with flower fields, warm springs, and terrace village groups; still not a port-encircled city, but several life settlements along the lakeshore and arable slopes visible to one another | about 90–115 km² crater floor / slope-step farmland + geothermal flower fields and greenhouses; grain stored before the sealed-air season | 1–3 stories; pale-gray stone base, deep-wood upper, steep roof, geothermal-district roofs often carrying thin steam | crater rim natural drop | seasonal T2 · 4–5 Class I–III berths (temporary mooring in the pre-sealed-season concentration period is not counted as formal berth), hydrogen-fill sheds and gas tanks | large ships do not land; before the sealed season the specialty items and gaps are made up, and staples and most daily needs are supported by production in the crater and nearby slopes | Tier 3 + geothermal | Seeds, flowers, herbs, honey, and one of the genetic-insurance sites

4.9.2 Prototype catalog (each type given footprint / farmland / story / skyport form)

I. Multi-port great city on a CI skeleton (Frontier type): built-up area 10–20 km², farmland 500–1,000 km², terrace 3–8 stories + hundred-meter-class pylon group, T4, small-to-medium ships at tower tops, large ships at outer-ring.
II. Terrace city (Atheria type): ribbon 8–15 km, ample farmland, 2–5 stories against the terraces, natural drop in place of a tower, T2/T3. A 45k-level place can perfectly well be a mature ordinary town, not automatically the regional capital.
III. Mesa-island + skybridge city (Amber Reach / East Mesas type): several mesa islands each with built-up area 1–3 km² (mesa surface itself 20–40 km², the rest is mesa-top field and orchard), joined by bridge net, mesa-top 2–4 stories, mesa-edge tower 70–100 m, T3, large ships at the valley floor.
IV. Canal town (Riverbend type): core about 3–6 km², along-canal farmsteads can stretch a continuous life-circle into a long belt; 30–50k resident population can still keep multi-center, low-rise, field-and-water-through-town, not compressed into a dense city; open riverbank mast berth or ground anchor, T2.
V. Crater settlement (Skyleaf type): lakeshore core about 3–5 km², population distributed across crater floor flower fields, warm springs, greenhouse, and terrace village groups; 1–3 stories, crater-rim berths, seasonal T2. The seasonal sealed-air-season constraint is on the entry / exit window, not on the resident population being only ten thousand; the premise is that local food, geothermal, and stored grain can carry through a whole season.
VI. Ore-valley great city (Cinderlode type): valley ribbon, limited local farmland, long-standing exchange of food with several nearby mature lines; stone-built 2–4 stories + furnace group, self-built tower, T3.
VII. Coastal / island-chain port (Iris, Keld type): bay area 5–12 km², fishing and farming, 3–6 stories, tower + sea-side floating pier, T3/T4.
VIII. Village and farmstead groups: 100–800 people each, ≤0.4 km², 1–2 stories, T1 posts or in-field anchor rings.
IX. Outpost / relay: unmanned or a few people, T0, weather mast + post + buffer shed; if a point grows over time to dozens or hundreds of residents, it is already a "small-station community" and should show a corresponding thickness of life.

The nested-terrace city is therefore one common kind, not a default skin; when entering a new continent, first choose from these nine or add a new type. The cliff-mouth gateway towns, mountain-pass seasonal towns, forest towns, ore-line life-circles, and salt-chemistry towns in the §4.3.6 table are variants of the nine on specific terrains (mostly variations between terrace city, canal town, ore-valley city, and village group), and are not made into a new type.

4.9.3 Derivation rules

- Per capita farmland baseline 0.35 ha / person (the equivalent basic ration for 45k people is about 158 km²); Atheria's actual registered 290–370 km² is above the baseline because it is originally a local grain / bean / stepwine surplus region, and one no longer back-figures from "175 km² / 50k." Adjusted by biome coefficient 0.7–1.6; large ore towns, high-temperature furnace cities, and salt-chemistry / arid-edge towns may have less farmland, but must write clearly which surrounding places they exchange food with over the long term, and preserve at least two viable sources; each place preserves at least two viable sources.
- Built-up area density in three tiers: junction core 15–25k / km² (three-dimensional terrace); ordinary town core 6–10k / km²; a dispersed-type life-circle can be significantly lower because settlement population includes directly attached village groups / farmsteads / along-canal and port-side life belts. When population is adjusted upward from 15k to 30–60k, prefer to let the life-circle grow more streets, villages, field-edge houses, and small mooring points laterally; growth is preferentially shown in more streets, villages, field-edge houses, and small moorings.
- Skyport tower rule: the turning-circle working value continues to use ≈ 2.2 × ship length. Class II's 25–40 m working range means the upper-bound ship requires about a 90 m clearance circle; Class V's 90–120 m working range means the full turn / tow space requires about 200–270 m. Tower-top berths must be above the roof turbulence layer and the valley inversion. Whether to build a 60–120 m skyport tower is decided jointly by lane function, ship class, local wind field, and available natural relief; it is not automatically triggered by a population above ten thousand: on a main-line T2/T3 lacking natural cliff, mesa island, crater rim, or open ground-anchor field, the default is to self-build a basalt / light-composite truss + heartwood platform low tower / tower; on plains, riverbanks, and valley floors, ground-anchor sets and 20–40 m masts are usable, and natural relief directly replaces a tower. The tower-top layer mainly moors Class I–III; Class IV and Class V by default moor at the tower-foot Heavy Berth, valley-floor / riverbank / seaside outer-ring ground-anchor field, and one does not require a hundred-meter ship to be pushed onto a narrow tower. The number of towers corresponds to port rating and actual use: T2 a mast / low-tower group, T3 one main tower or equivalent anchor field, T4 tower group; a population floor does not manufacture a unified skyline.
- Second function of a tower: cargo chute downward, pneumatic tube upward, signal lamp at the top, gas tank at the foot — the tower is at once the port and the town's facade.
- **Wind-field reading:** a skyport does not need to build another unified "flag code" system. A T0 with a single wind sock may be everything; a larger port, because the berth heights, cliff edges, roofs, and tower bodies are staggered, will naturally leave several passive wind-reading surfaces at different heights — wind socks, narrow wind cloths, old sail edges, light rope tails, or locally home-made small flags. What the pilot truly reads is whether they are consistent with one another: the low one has already turned but the high one has not, or the tower top pulls taut while the leeward platform still hangs — this is more useful than "east wind, so many grades." Materials and hanging methods can be entirely different in different regions; the material and hanging method may be entirely different from place to place.

4.9.4 Agricultural atlas

Field types and their machinery: terrace-surface fine-work belts (vegetables, beans, orchards, wine fruit, forage; floating plow / Canal Tender); lower-plain grain belts (Plain Cutter, Field Loafer; in-field anchor rings in the harvest season); dryland farming and pasture (Lift-Sled); vertical farm walls / hydroponic walls (sun faces of junction cities, half of the green wall is edible); orchards and resin forests (economic crops of the Amber Reach type); canal-bank belts (Riverbend type). Yields per unit are not written as absolute numbers, only as relative: Archeon has no digital agriculture but has fusion electric pumps, buoyancy-borne implements, and five hundred years of local breeding, and unit-area yield is roughly at the level of Earth's mid-20th-century good land; back-figured with 0.35 ha / person. Grain storage: terrace-surface stone cellars and cliff-body constant-temperature caves; grain runs regional lines, not long lines (§5.12).

4.9.5 Industrial atlas

Scaled by §5.2's four tiers: small station (1–5 people, one shed, Tier 3); local workshop (5–40 people, one to three rooms, Tier 2/3, changing skin, patching envelope, mending sail, turning powerplant); regional shipyard (100–600 people, footprint 1–3 ha, Tier 1/2 plus CFU or hydro, fitting medium-sized ships, changing envelope, changing bones); large high-temperature process zone (2,000–8,000 people in the industrial belt, footprint 2–5 km², Tier 0 / multiple CFUs, electric-arc furnace, electrolysis cell, continuous furnace, Class V final assembly). What is "large" in this tier is not the population, but the continuous high-temperature processes, the energy, and the furnaces.

4.9.6 Buoyancy construction

How Archeon builds 200–300 m class piers and cross-cliff skybridges: segmented trusses are pre-fabricated on the ground, each segment slung with a basalt-cloth float bladder tuned to slight positive buoyancy, then towed into place by skiff and lighter, cables paid out and the segment closed home; dozens of float bladders hanging like scattered stars in mid-air, with the truss slowly leveling beneath them, is the standard picture on any large construction site. People work on the ropes under the bladders — the trade word is "angling": a person hangs at the end of the bladder rope, going up and down by paying out the cable, with skiff bringing parts and people, and a bridge across two mesa islands can be closed in two cool seasons. This same method is both the process explanation of §6A.2 "the routine build of 200–300 m class structures," the way Frontier's self-built secondary towers are built, and the piece of evidence for "the world is growing" that the camera should most see while flying over mountains and seas. Rules: construction float bladders are registered as local public tools (borrowed buoyancy), and a large site must have a "bladder chief" managing buoyancy trim; skiff high-altitude work is one proper trade of skiff culture, not only racing.


======================================================================
5. Population, Industry, and Whether Airships Are Feasible
======================================================================

5.1 Population Working Value

Year 521 population: 1.8M; 1.05–1.1M is no longer used.

Year 521 population centers on about 1.8M, with 1.4–2.2M held only as a model-sensitivity range.

Demographic notes:

- Starting from about 120–130 people and reaching 1.8M by Year 521 requires a long-run average net growth of roughly 1.8%–1.85% per year.
- This is on the high side but not physically impossible; long lifespans, low infant mortality, gradually enriching ecology, strong family and community caretaking, and the early surplus embryo bank all improve feasibility.
- Five centuries of growth do not follow a single constant exponential curve. The first several decades are constrained by genetics, care capacity, and infrastructure; the middle period accelerates once the ecology stabilizes; the later period slows again as population disperses, family structures shift, and long-distance costs change.
- 1.8M is the population's central value, not a pretense of a precise census; the prose can say "around one point eight million." For the staged
  model, see §5.1a; going forward, only new evidence about fertility or the embryo banks may move this central value.

Why not simply five million or ten million:

- Archeon's aesthetic requires that "the civilization be complex enough while the planet remains far larger than the civilization."
- About 1.8M people scattered across a near-Earth-sized planet is still extreme thinness; what they occupy are points and lines, not surfaces.
- This number is nonetheless sufficient to sustain specialist shipyards, metallurgy, meteorology, medicine, printing, transoceanic shipping, and distinct regional cultures.

5.1a Staged Population Model

| Timepoint | Global population working value | Main mechanism |
|---|---:|---|
| Year 0 | 125 | Two embryo cohorts, advance Awakeners, and Axiom survivors |
| Year 50 | 500 | Recovery after the bunker era; still limited by care capacity and genetic management |
| Year 110 | 5,000 | The six genetic vaults come online in stages; First Threads begin reducing isolation mortality |
| Year 230 | 45,000 | Multi-settlement family networks, stable grain, and basic medicine |
| Year 350 | 260,000 | The Loom carries marriages, apprentices, and seeds; regional populations begin to diverge |
| Year 450 | 850,000 | Multiple industrial hubs and transoceanic lines mature; growth rate naturally slows |
| Year 521 | 1,800,000 | Civilization is complex enough, yet still occupies only points and lines |

Population-source lock: beyond the roughly 125 living people at Year 0, the six underground genetic vaults together supply about 2,400 usable embryo/zygote lineages; from Year 5–110 a cumulative ~1,800 people are activated, with the remainder held long-term as a genetic reserve. The full 1.8M derives from the "single civilizational cradle + underground embryo banks" model; no other continent gains native survivors out of thin air.

The whole span's equivalent long-run net growth is about 1.85% per year, but no century runs on a constant exponential; the high growth from Year 50–230 includes vault activation and should not be interpreted entirely as anomalously high birth rates in every family.

Year 521 distribution lock: Aethelgard about 1.23M; Vorhan about 230k; Keld about 190k; Duskreach about 110k; polar/subpolar regions about 20k; long-term in-transit, great-range and seasonal camps about 20k. FAR-5 has no permanent population; short-season survey crews count toward in-transit population. Aethelgard's 1.23M is not being revised down this round: the 17 named major settlements of §4.3.6 account for about 950k, and the remaining ~280k genuinely live across a great many smaller places. This lets the main towns look like places that have grown into themselves over five hundred years, while still preserving the network-not-territory blankness of the continent.

5.2 Industrial Structure: Local Surplus, Specialized Conversion

Not every village builds a whole airship on its own, but that does not mean villages sit and wait for everything to be sent from afar. Airship industry should look like mature shipbuilding: raw materials, ordinary processing, and maintenance are widely distributed, and what determines the quality of a large ship comes down to a small number of high-temperature processes, precision parts, assembly experience, and shared standards.

- 6–8 heavy industrial hubs: production of high-grade basalt fiber and woven layers, carbon-fiber main structural members, primary aluminum and magnesium, fuel cells, precision valves, bearings and joints, and large-scale envelope material; the small number of load-bearing long members, complex hardpoints, and large-scale light structural pieces required by Class V mostly come from here.
- Dozens of regional shipyards: they assemble Class I–III, some can build or perform heavy overhaul on Class IV; at the same time they produce secondary basalt trusses, tension members, light-core sandwich panels, local sailcloth, and the large-area hull skins required for a full refit. They are not simply gluing hub parts together — they are the second layer of manufacturing capability within the larger-ship structural ecosystem.
- Hundreds of local craft/ship-service spaces: reskinning and recoating, working on envelope cells, mending sails and rudders, overhauling drive systems, adjusting the center-of-gravity zone and how boxes and packs are secured; they can also continuously produce skiff, Class I, small Class II, farm implements, and port components using local wood, cloth, glass, ordinary metals, light-core panel, and standard fittings.
- Thousands of small stations/stopping points (globally): a significant share are unmanned, seasonally attended, or staffed by only a few people who watch weather, top up water, inspect for normal wear, and swap out common consumables; the number of stations should not be multiplied by population. Truly resident small stations usually also produce at least one local everyday good, not just temporarily fix a broken ship. The 250–450 permanent small stations and small communities in Aethelgard's population table this round are just the resident subset of this layer.

The 4–8 hubs are the sources of high-grade conversion, long structural members, and shared standards — not the sole source of raw materials, structural intelligence, or creativity. Archeon's intimacy of "hands able to care for the machine" comes from distributed capability; the lightness of a hundred-meter Class V does not come from the whole ship being built of rare materials, but from a small number of high-grade main bones working in concert with a great many thin layers, cavities, tensile members, and secondary structures that can be made locally.

5.3 Ship Count Orders of Magnitude (Year 521 author-side lock)

Year 521's author-side central lock for the total number of buoyant vehicles in service is about 50,000; depending on seasonal storage, deep refit, and different counting methods, floats of about 45,000–55,000 are allowed. This number includes skiff, agricultural/work craft, and Class I–V; it is not fifty thousand full-scale line-running ships. With a population of about 1.8M, that works out to only about one usable buoyant vehicle per 35–40 people; in a civilization that uses buoyancy for fieldwork, short hops, family life, ports, and long voyages alike, this density should read as the combined ecology of boats, bicycles, farm machines, and small craft, not as "one airliner for every forty people."

Author-side working distribution:

- skiff / agricultural and local work craft: about 28,000–32,000.
- Class I: about 8,000–10,000. Short-range, courier, and small family/local craft account for many of these.
- Class II: about 6,000–8,000. Mature regional lines and the everyday scale Anna is familiar with, which dominates.
- Class III: about 2,000–3,000. Medium-distance, multi-day regional lines and heavier work.
- Class IV: about 400–700. Heavy regional / long-range ships filling the scale gap between Class III and the hundred-meter Class V.
- Class V: about 80 (LOCK). Large long-line ships / floating small communities in the 90–120 m range.

The above ranges are not meant to be summed at their upper bounds; the central distribution should still land near fifty thousand.

`long-haul`, `great-range`, and `courier / packet` are hereafter understood strictly as **operating mode / ship configuration**, no longer treated as separate count-boxes that double up with Class I–V:
- long-haul mostly falls under Class IV / V, though a durability-optimized Class III can also serve;
- great-range is a configuration with expanded fuel / provisions / weather-hold, suited for transoceanic and long-wilderness segments, and mainly appears among Class IV / V;
- packet / courier is drawn mainly from Class I–III ships optimized for speed.

Sky density remains highly uneven:
- Frontier and the mature Thread rings: seeing several layers of small craft and multiple Class I–III ships across a single daylight span is ordinary background; a big T4 port has many arrivals and departures a day, but spread across the whole day.
- A mature T2 place of the Atheria kind (40–50k): the sky is not empty, and specific vistas obey §4.7; most of what one sees are skiff and Class I/II, with the occasional Class III, and the distant silhouette of a large ship is unremarkable but not always present. A population of this bracket first means more houses, more field-edge skiff berths, neighborhood moorings, and local living; it does not automatically imply a T3/T4 port.
- Ordinary branch lines: an hour might yield only one or two nearby ships, with the rest reduced to distant silhouettes and radio calls.
- Deep blank regions: one might still fly for a day or two hearing an unfamiliar call sign on the radio only once.

Class V's ~80 must simultaneously satisfy two feelings:
one, they are few enough that each may carry a long-standing name, familiar crew tradition, and a silhouette repeatedly recognized by people along its line;
two, they are many enough that transcontinental flight is not the stuff of legend. In a mature T4 city, it is normal for someone to know the names of several Class V ships; in a small settlement, occasionally sighting a large ship in the distance is not worth writing up as "the first sight of civilization in my life."

5.4 Distance and Time

Different ship classes should not blur into a single speed; the following are operating bands, not the sole use for each class:

- Class I / fast courier: about 90–120 km/h, light, short, small payload.
- Class II: about 60–90 km/h, the most common balance point for mature regional lines.
- Class III: about 55–80 km/h, medium-distance, multi-day regional lines.
- Class IV: about 45–65 km/h, one of the mainstays of heavy regional / long-haul.
- Class V: about 35–55 km/h, slow but able to fly continuously for days or weeks, capable of long-term habitation, and carrying a complete living/working surface and multi-person watch rotation.

Working feel:

- 1,000 km: regional ships take about 14–20 hours; it can be one night plus half a day, or one midway stop.
- 3,000 km: a fast ship about 2–4 days; a heavy freighter about 5–8 days.
- 5,000–8,000 km: a packet ship about 3–7 days; a Class V / heavy long-haul about 10–21 days.

What a transcontinental large ship should carry is not a "transport module manifest" but a compact living arrangement that can sustain a dozen days of continuous life:

- bunks / hammock berths and shallow lockers where personal things can actually be stowed, with hangers;
- galley / long table or a foldable common eating surface;
- weather desk / chart surface;
- a small working surface for fine work, writing, mending cloth, and ordinary ship's business;
- open deck and a clear leeward corner;
- boxes, packs, ballast, and positions that can be resecured but often stay put for days;
- rotating crew;
- apprentice;
- simple passenger cabin or common corridor;
- simple passenger cabin or common corridor;
- at least one space that any reader can tell at a glance "someone lives here": mugs, damp outerwear, bread cut open today, paper someone has written on halfway, a book left out before sleep, and a spot always taken by the same person.

**Onboard living texture (prose direction):**
