Anna, in the middle-to-late stretch of P2, naturally grows into a spot of her own — not assigned by anyone, just settled into by sitting there long enough: beside a coil of rope by the hatch, her back against a stack of wheat sacks; from there she can see a slanted strip of sky beyond the rail, and hear the engine humming evenly beneath her feet. Once she moves to a Class V in P3, she has to find it again. Personal traces in shared space: different things hanging from each person's peg beside a hammock — a scarf, a bag, goggles; a mug whose handle has cracked in a line only that one person still holds comfortably; something someone has carved into a crossbar. Rhythm: mealtime is not a bell but the sound of the pot lid and the footsteps of the cook stepping out of the stove niche; the night-watch changeover is a hooded lamp, a pencil, and a board wiped and rewritten passing from hand to hand; morning berth-tidying is out of sync — some are up before daylight, some need to be called twice. The ship itself changes: wood swelling in the rain so doors stick; the mixed air after several days of flying — wood, metal, sweat, food from the last stop, faint hydrogen seeping from the envelope cells; rigging tightening when wet and slackening when dry, someone walking a lap each morning to test it by hand.

**In-transit random texture (prose direction):**

Any of the following can be scattered into P2/P3/P5 transit passages, carrying no narrative burden — just making "we are flying" not abstract. **Weather —** entering cloud: from bright to grey to white to seeing nothing, water beading on the rail, light returning first as you come out; rain hitting the hull in a few isolated strikes and then all at once, someone getting up to cover something; a sudden crosswind, a bowl skidding half an inch on the table, someone reaching to steady it; the ship flying between two layers of cloud, grey above, white below, a narrow slit between them like a corridor; morning dew on deck, more on the metal than the wood, more on the cold side. **Astronomical —** both moons in the sky at once, two reflections on water or cloudsea; the nebula in different colors at different altitudes — reddened at low altitude by atmosphere, greener higher up; the last few minutes of dawn as the nebula fades, sky moving from honey to blue; a flash on the far horizon that is not a star but another ship catching the sun. **Geological (from the air) —** a river bending into an almost-closed loop, greener inside the loop than outside; the ground color abruptly shifting from green to ochre-red across a geological boundary; a small column of steam rising from the ground in the distance, not fire but a geothermal vent; a patch of ground unusually flat and grey — an old CI work surface, easy to recognize from the air because it is squarer than the country around it. **Biological (from the air) —** a flock of birds rising from a treeline and settling again after the ship passes; on a seasonal migration day, discovering there are, besides one's own ship, many birds in the sky flying in the same direction; some bird riding a thermal circles at the same altitude as a skiff for three turns. **Acoustic —** the sound reflected up from the ground varies with terrain: engine sound goes muffled over water, has an echo over valleys, and does nothing at all over plains; wind through rigging at different angles produces different whistles, and some people can tell the wind has changed by whistle alone.

This is how the sense of time in "sitting on an airship and traveling everywhere" arises: on waking the river below has already changed, at lunch the wind has shifted, on the second evening still no landing, and on the fourth day an unfamiliar coast finally appears from below the clouds.

5.5 Long-Haul Resources

- Buoyancy hydrogen and propellant fuel are kept separate. Buoyancy hydrogen is sealed in compartmentalized cells and only topped up for leakage and repair losses; the fuel cell uses an independent hydrogen storage/supply system, and lift-hydrogen cannot be casually burned off to make faster time.
- T2 and above sky-ports can perform electrolytic hydrogen top-up by default; T3/T4 are the ones that reliably handle Class V bulk gas, hoisting, and deep refit. T0 guarantees only anchoring; whether it has water, a fuel cache, or a meteorologist must be registered separately; the anchor piles themselves only guarantee tie-up, and other services must be registered separately.
- Long-haul / great-range ships do not need to land every day. They rely on sail economy, fuel-cell cruise, steam-assist / emergency thrust, and carry multi-day food, water, common spares, and a weather-hold reserve; when they stop is determined jointly by the weather window, the ship's own condition, who needs to get on or off along this segment, and what commitments must be picked up where — not by short-haul-style hopping from station to station, and not by an optimal schedule set by some distant center.
- Every G3 / transoceanic edge must be registered before entering the story: the longest continuous stretch without a T2+, ordinary and headwind flight time, hydrogen top-up points, full-repair points, sheltering anchorages, food and water reserves, seasonal backup lines, and where a search usually launches from after a lost contact. Without these fields, 5,000–8,000 km is just a number on a map — not a usable Thread.
- Working formulation: mature long lines depend on many layers of support stacked together — regional shipyards, T2/T3 ports, T0 along the way, weather cooperation, aerial handoff, the long-term commitments of shared ships, and the memory of people along the line. Genuine wilderness segments can stretch a thousand kilometers, but must be borne by designated great-range ships, buddy-flight rules, or extra reserves; this shows that distance is expensive, not that the Loom is a large freight enterprise.


5.6 Hydrogen

- The only lifting gas is hydrogen. Archeon's metal-rich, geologically active crust accumulates radiogenic helium, but helium extraction requires cryogenic distillation, which falls squarely on the "cryogenic cooling module" lost-technology line; there is no helium industry in this world and no one misses it. Hydrogen safety culture
- Lift constants: at sea level 1.10 bar, warm-season 15–20°C, air is about 1.33 kg/m³, hydrogen about 0.09 kg/m³, working net lift about 1.2 kg/m³ (accounting for impurities and superheat losses). Class V is a ship family of 90–120 m, not locked to a single length; effective main cell volume, author-side, is about 30,000–50,000 m³, gross lift about 36–60 t. To keep numbers from drifting through the file, existing fuel / top-up / range sanity math continues to use the **reference Class V: about 100 m, 40,000 m³**; it sits in the middle of the family and does not represent every Class V. Highland ports scale down by density: at 2,000 m, lift is about eighty percent — this is the economics behind Marneth's side port having few berths and Orun requiring a detour.
- Envelope material: the main gas-tight layer continues to be primarily laminated basalt / glass weave + heartwood resin or hub-grade silicone coating; "laminated basalt cloth" does not mean the entire ship, from main bone to outer shell, is carried by one and the same basaltic fabric. Two to three woven layers are hot-pressed into a gas-tight skin, and seams are sealed with the same-family coating. Total whole-ship envelope loss (permeation + valve venting + superheat handling) working value is 0.5–1.5% per day; regional-grade resin envelopes take the upper bound, hub-grade silicone envelopes the lower. On the reference Class V of 40,000 m³, a 3,000 km trip (about 8 days) loses about 5–10%, i.e. 2,000–4,000 m³, 170–330 kg of hydrogen; smaller and larger Class V scale by cell volume. A Class II (reference about 5,000 m³) loses about 50 m³ per day. Cell compartmentalization: Class V has 12–18 cells, Class II 4–6, skiff frame-wings 2–4. Envelope material life is 8–15 years; envelope replacement is a planned yard-refit item at a regional shipyard, and does not imply that a normal line ship is ordinarily in a state of "leaking everywhere."
- Ballast is a resupply item. Unloading 12 t of cargo lightens the ship by 12 t and requires taking on an equal amount of water or sand at the same port — "borrow the sand, return the sand" is both etiquette and physics. T0 "having good water" is therefore not only for drinking, it is for ballast; coastal and river ports use water for ballast, inland dry regions use sand; each port's ballast capacity is registered in the §6.8 outpost table. Fuel-cell product water is recovered as ballast water (see 5.7), and long-haul ships therefore need port less often than short-haul ones do.
- A full-ship gas-up is only feasible at Tier 0 / CFU stations: the reference Class V's 40,000 m³ is about 3.7 t of hydrogen, about 200 MWh of electrolysis; the 90–120 m family scales up and down from 30,000–50,000 m³. A Tier 0 station handles a reference ship on the order of two or three hours; a T2 electrolysis shed (tens of kW of wind or water) puts out only a dozen to twenty-odd kilograms of hydrogen a day, about 200 m³, enough to top up two or three regional ships for their daily permeation, valve losses, and superheat handling, but not enough to fully fill any mid-to-large ship. Therefore: a new Class IV/V completes its main-cell fill at a hub / large shipyard, an old ship has its envelope replaced at a hub on cycle; what is topped up along the way is mostly ordinary loss, and true "leak-patch" work counts as an anomalous event.

5.7 Propulsion and Energy

- Main drive: alkaline fuel cells (KOH electrolyte, nickel electrodes, silver/nickel catalyst, 1960s-grade chemistry — a hub can build them, a region can repair them) → copper-wound electric motor → propeller. PEM requires fluoropolymer membranes, which are on the lost-tech line; they do not exist. Air passes through a lime tower to remove CO₂ before entering the fuel cell, which is a "breathing thing" every ship carries.
- Fuel hydrogen is not mixed with buoyancy hydrogen (§5.5 already locked); it is stored in the ship's independent low-pressure fuel bag ("fuel bag / fuel cell bag" tradition), not in high-pressure tanks — basalt-wound tanks have a mass fraction of 3–4%, so a full load of fuel would need twenty tons of tanks, which is physically impossible. The fuel bag occupies about 8–12% of a Class V's cell volume and also provides lift; burning 1 kg of hydrogen loses about 14 kg of lift and returns about 9 kg of water, for a net weight loss of about 5 kg per kilogram, roughly two tons net over a 3,000 km trip — vented as recovered water by mileage; this is the steering common sense behind long-haul ships "getting lighter as they fly but venting less and less."
- Hydrogen-burn working values (cruise, no wind, systems included): reference Class V about 13–15 kg per 100 km (about 90–110 kW); Class IV about 9–12; Class III 6–8; Class II 3–4; courier / packet 4–6 (fast); skiff negligible. The reference Class V's fuel bag when full is about 400–450 kg → unassisted cruise about 3,000 km; with sails and monsoon assist, this extends to 4,000–5,000 km. A great-range configuration can enlarge fuel / provisions / weather-hold share without thereby becoming a separate "ship class." This lines up with §6.8's "beyond 3,000 km, full-repair points 400–800 km apart": wilderness segments in the thousand-kilometer range are carried by suitably configured Class IV/V, and ordinary regional ships do not indiscriminately cross them.
- Fire rule for steam-assist: the boiler room sits in an independent gas-tight compartment low in the stern; intake is from the bilge, exhaust points aft and down; between it and the envelope cells there is a basalt-cloth firescreen and a hydrogen-concentration sniff line; it is lit only in dead-calm headwinds, port approach and berthing, or on fuel-cell failure. Skiff and regional fast ships carry no boiler.
- On the roughly fifty thousand global buoyant vehicles, the author-side working value for global annual propulsion hydrogen is raised to about 7,000–10,000 t, which still comes to only about 400–600 GWh per year in electricity terms, an average of about 45–70 MW; most of the added count is skiff, Class I, and small craft that don't fly long distances every day, so total hydrogen use does not scale linearly with ship count. Against a Year 521 total installed capacity of 3.5–4.5 GW, this is still a small share. Hydrogen is not scarce; what is truly expensive is the right weather, continuous care, keeping supplies at the right ports, and having, over the long term, people willing to keep flying a given line.

5.8 Sky-Port Resupply Triad and Hydrogen Production by Tier

- The triad: hydrogen, water (ballast + drinking), food; the fourth item is weather. Any port's registration writes these four first, then the berths.
- Hydrogen-production capacity: T0 none; T1 none or a hand-cranked small electrolyser (a few kg per day); T2 electrolysis shed 20–60 kg per day, enough for a regional ship's daily group-gas loss; T3 200–800 kg per day, capable of topping up a Class V's trip losses and fully filling a regional ship; T4 / Tier 0 stations on the MW scale, capable of full-ship gassing.
- Low-pressure gasholders are the steampunk visual of the sky-port: T2 and above have one or two heartwood-framed, basalt-cloth-sheathed floating-roof gasholders; the height of the holder's roof is the port's public notice of how much hydrogen it has today — incoming ships look at the gasholder first, then the light array.
- Mooring: turning circle 2.2 × ship length; masts, piles, light signals, and berth interfaces are governed by Loom Accord (§13.2 #10). Height rules for tower-form sky-ports are in §4.9.3.

5.9 Energy Geography

- Energy does not go on the grid; energy goes on ships. Archeon has no long-distance electrical grid; energy flows across regions as hydrogen (in fuel bags, gasholders); steam only within a city; electricity only within a station. Tier 0 stations are therefore hydrogen exporters, and any long line has, at either end, a port that can gas up a whole ship.
- Tier 0: of CI's roughly 180 Primary Nodes, about 50–60 contained fusion-reactor-grade facilities; the Year 521 still-operating lock is 30–40, single-reactor electrical output 20–150 MW; distribution is about one-third in Aethelgard (of which the named hubs account for four; the rest are mostly de-rated, running only for a single mine, kiln, pumping, or hydrogen point, no longer holding up a city), several each in Vorhan and Keld, a small number in Duskreach, none in the polar regions. Dead reactors do not vanish: the towns they leave behind usually drop one tier and are kept going by CFU or geothermal, or fall back to seasonal-line anchorages — this is the most common physical origin of the old-line decay in §7.6.
- CFU definition lock: the compact catalytic-fusion module used at CI worksites, of which the Package brought several hundred to power foundries and remote nodes; the cores are non-reproducible, the periphery repairable. Year 521 survivors are 200–400 units, 0.5–5 MW each, scattered across mid-size settlements and a handful of mining districts. A CFU is one of the highest ceilings a place without a large fusion reactor can sustain for high-temperature industry, and is often the answer to "why this town can pull basalt fiber."
- Global installed capacity magnitudes: Tier 0 about 2–3 GW, CFU about 0.5 GW, water / wind / geothermal / solar about 1 GW; total 3.5–4.5 GW, per capita about 2 kW (mid-twentieth-century industrial-country level). This is the numeric shape of "high energy, low information processing."

5.10 Material Fill-ins and the Gasket Inheritance Crisis

- The four-tier materials table is in §6A.2; this section only registers the three items on which the route network directly depends. Envelope material: laminated basalt cloth (5.6). Cordage: plant fiber (everyday) → basalt cable (regional) → steel cable → Old-cable (record-grade). Lubrication and sealing: plant oil and graphite (everyday), resin varnish (regional), silicone (hub).
- **The gasket inheritance crisis (Year 521):** CI-formulation alloy gaskets were designed to last 500 years, and the oldest batches have now passed their design life — they are coming due in bulk across the steam network, fusion-reactor periphery, and Class V systems. Replacements are hub-grade titanium gaskets and copper-nickel alloy gaskets with silicone seals, adequate in performance but short-lived (30–60 years), so the norm has shifted from "install once for five hundred years" to "one generation replaces a round." Consequences: hubs now carry an additional perennial good (a gasket crate is a fixed load on every G3 line); craftspeople who know gasket batches become sought-after regional figures; some towns whose gasket ship fails to come in have a stretch of their pipe network revert to hand-valve operation — this is the most everyday sense of "civilization changing over" in Anna's era, requiring no one to name it as a crisis.
- Aluminum and magnesium are only originally produced at Tier 0 electrolysis stations, the rest is remelted; copper and iron are everywhere.

5.11 Industrial Geography

- Global heavy industrial hubs number 6–8, each of which must simultaneously have a Tier 0 or multiple CFU, a continuous 1,500°C furnace, an electrolyser bank, and at least a regional-grade shipyard. Aethelgard has four: Frontier (junction, precision parts, fuel-cell stacks, catalysts, Class V final assembly); Cinderlode (igneous province, metals, basalt fiber, titanium, SiC); Iris Hollow (coast, glass and optics, transoceanic hauler shipyard, salt chemistry); Amber Reach (mesa cluster, resins, envelope material, carbon-fiber precursors, silicone). Vorhan has one or two (Gearstead · High Inland Sea foothill junction; Organ Country side geothermal province OPEN); Keld has one (Shellharbor · Karst Sea port, sail-airship shipyard and secondary optics source); Duskreach has one (southern geothermal province, basalt and geothermal chemistry). At least two critical processes are not in Aethelgard: the secondary source of titanium is at the Vorhan hub, and the secondary source of optical glass is in Keld — otherwise the network again collapses into radial form.
- Class V final assembly takes place at only three sites: Frontier, Iris Hollow, and the Vorhan hub. The Year 521 global active Class V lock is about 80 ships, ship length about 90–120 m. Build cycle is 2–4 years, service life 60–100 years; this count and the three assembly yards constrain one another neatly: 80 ships at a median life of about 80 years need a long-run inflow of about 1 ship per year; the three yards' aggregate capability of one new Class V every 2–4 years each comes to about 0.75–1.5 ships per year, enough to sustain normal replacement without making the hundred-meter ship into an assembly-line product. Over its life a ship typically undergoes two or three scheduled envelope replacements, main-bone segment recerts, and large-scale refits. An "old ship" is more like a continuous ship lineage: the name, the use, certain habits of scale, a small number of long-lived members that can still be trusted, and the memory of the crew may persist across several yard visits, but each shipyard fully redoes whatever must be redone, so the finished product becomes once again a coordinated, reliable ship — not several old ships spliced together. Class IV may be built and heavily overhauled at additional regional heavy shipyards, and does not consume the whole capacity of the three Class V yards; regional shipyards number in the dozens and handle Class I–III, new build / refit, some Class IV, and the evolution of local ship types; local craft spaces number in the hundreds; small stations in the thousands (§5.2 unchanged).
- Each hub is registered on one line: `hub | continent | energy | key processes | shipyard grade | long-run local surplus | long-run local needs from elsewhere`; G3 lines, when registered in §6.7, must state why both ends have been willing to sustain this line for years, and what — beyond materials — the line also keeps alive in people, letters, and seasonal relations.

5.12 Continental Exchange and Long Reach

This section answers only three things: what a place has in inexhaustible surplus; which conversion processes are not worth replicating everywhere; and what things / people / knowledge are therefore worth traveling very far for. Connectivity brings surplus into someone else's life, but exchange begins from locality, not from abstract throughput.

Things worth occupying long-haul's limited space (by degree of irreplaceability, not by a uniform price): precision valves, gaskets, fuel-cell stacks, catalysts, and optics; medicine and filter elements; finished basalt cable and special woven layers; envelope material and silicone; seeds and genetic samples; paper, books, and maps; certain metal ingots; salt chemistry and special glass; dyes and fabrics; small amounts of local food and drink; letters and people. Grain generally travels on regional lines; long lines can carry grain in a bad year, but no mature settlement should ordinarily be written as staying alive on a distant grain ship.

Each continent is registered only by "surplus / specialty / a conversion not worth replicating locally": Aethelgard — grain, wood, fiber, and several precision processes are mature; Vorhan — titanium, geothermal chemistry, and inland-sea salt are in surplus; Keld — optics, sail-ship craft, and marine products are in surplus; Duskreach — geothermal, basalt, and cloud-forest medicinal herbs are in surplus; the polar regions — weather and ice-road knowledge is most valuable. Shortfalls are not written as "short of everything," and the reason for exchange is not reduced to goods: the same line will also carry, over the long term, kin, apprentices, healers, letters, marriages, festival news, and local judgment.

5.13 Feeding the World and the Craft Spectrum

The rough breakdown of 1.8M people is only a **capacity-feasibility reference**. As a rough estimate: about 38% of annual labor time goes to food, gardens, and animal care; about 22% to manufacturing, building, materials work, and ordinary maintenance crafts; about 4% to flying, moorings, weather masts, and daily route work; about 8% to caretaking, medicine, printing, public affairs, and craft transmission; about 1% is long-term on survey, seasonal camps, or far lines; the rest of the population includes many children, elders, and people who do not define themselves by a single title. The numbers only answer "can this civilization support these ships and processes?" — they do not answer "what is a person first called?" What is truly tight is a small number of high-temperature/precision judges and cross-regional long-term recognition and continuity of commitment, not ordinary raw materials.

5.13a Onboard Shared Responsibility and Working Verbs

What exists on a ship first is **a responsibility that someone must carry**, and only then a stable role name. On small ships, responsibilities flow with people, not departments; on Class V, though the crew is larger, responsibilities still rotate by watch and by hands-on know-how.

**Weight sense is social common sense, not crew black-box expertise.** In a place where tens of thousands of people see buoyant craft every day, ordinary people generally know: a very heavy crate coming aboard, ask first "where does it go?"; do not have a whole crowd press to the same railing at once to look at something; after unloading a big piece, the ship may top up water or sand before departing; do not, without being asked, move a heavy object already secured; when the person on watch says "leave that one alone for now," no aerodynamic explanation is required. Children learn first where they may sit, which lines not to step on, when to clear the walkway. Etiquette varies by place, but "weight changes how a ship rests in the air" is the whole civilization's bodily common sense.

Class II (Edmund / Anna's everyday ship, 3–6 people): there must always be someone able to steer and read the wind; at least two people can rotate on envelope temperature, power, and night watch; everyone who stays with the ship for long knows how to moor, how to secure things that roll, how to adjust ballast, how to clear the walkway before a jolt, how to clamp down the cook-pot. `pilot`, `副手`(mate), `rigger / sail hand` may be common terms, but they are not mutually exclusive posts. `cargo hand` does not exist in this world. Whoever has hands free hands things over; whoever knows how to tie ties; whoever notices the center of gravity has shifted calls it out first. Edmund on his own Class II most often carries pilot + pressure-system judgment, and will do any ordinary shipwork besides; his social value cannot be defined by "traveling repair customer."

Class V (P3 Anna's first sight of "a ship that is a settlement," about 12–20 stable crew, plus people who only ride a segment): a long line requires captain / lead pilot for final route judgment, 2–3 pilots on rotation, at least one long-term keeper of the chart and wind-field log, several people familiar with sails, rigging, envelope cells, and external work, several familiar with propulsion and the pressure system; the galley may, on account of dozens of mouths, produce a long-standing head cook. Beyond that, many responsibilities flow by watch and know-how: today, who logs pressure, who watches the center of gravity, who knows the heat-sensitive crate cannot sit by the stove niche, who first hears a leak in the envelope cells at night — none of these need generate a departmental name. Concrete forms of address like "deck watch / person on watch / rigger / envelope hand / engineer / the cook" can be used; "货头 / cargo officer / cargo hand" does not exist in this world. A Class V may also have apprentice, people riding two stops, people going home, people going to see kin, short-season companions; this too is why "a village in the sky" is a coherent thing.

Ground craft spectrum (visible in daily settlement life): pressure-system wrights (pipes, valves, seals, pressure vessels); airshipwrights / shipwrights (main bones, secondary trusses, light-core sandwich, envelope material, sailcloth, propulsion); metallurgy / forging; glass; pottery; basalt fiber weavers; woodworkers / heartwood; water-wrights; low-voltage electricians; printing; healers; brewers; farmers; herders; fishers; surveyors; meteorologists; radio operators. The same person, over a lifetime, may take on different combinations depending on family, season, age, and local need.

Local specialty crafts / things one is trusted to do: Karst pilot / lead pilot (Shellharbor — an outside ship does not enter without a local pilot); Roll rider (Keld–Aethelgard seasonal surf line); listen-mountain pilot (Organ Country acoustic navigation); tide-chasing harvest ship hands (Tidemark tidal bay); sail-airship wrights (a Keld-specific ship type). These names indicate "why others trust you to do this thing," not a corporate post.

Working verbs for "pressure-system wright" (prose replacement library): build / lay (running new pipe segments, splicing existing lines, pulling the first pipe into a new station — the Skyleaf water-wheel is built, not repaired); forge / shape (working copper, brass, and basalt fiber into valves, fittings, seals); diagnose / judge (hand on the pipe wall to feel temperature, vibration, fluid — judging gasket batch, seal life, the next inspection window); pilot / fly (for a walking-shuttle person, "to travel" means to fly); care for / recheck (confirming a segment still lies within their working range). "The old joint method" is a manner of making, not a repair identity. Any scene in which "Edmund / Anna is needed only because something is broken here" requires the incident itself to carry independent character causation.

5.13b Ground Life and Craft

An Archeon person does not first hold a title and then look for a matching building.
People first have family and local relationships, then things they can do, often do, and are trusted by others to do.
An adult's actual life is usually built up from three layers:

- **Responsibilities at home**: cooking, caring for people, tending the garden, mending the roof, keeping letters, preserving food, drawing water, tending the hive, and so on;
- **Long-term craft / recognized judgment**: water-wright, medicine, printing, weaving, glass, pottery, pressure systems,
  ship-craft, woodwork, brewing, forging, weather-reading, survey, and so on;
- **Seasonal and local responsibilities**: helping press fruit during harvest, going to catch a rigging line when the wind window opens, going down to the flats at low tide,
  leading someone through a pass once in cool season, firing an extra kiln or two before a festival, riding one segment when a familiar line is short-handed.

The three layers can overlap or be entirely separate. Character introductions need not always give a line of "what they do: X."

**Space does not automatically generate itself from titles:**
- farmers are mostly in the field, orchard, ditch, and at home;
- the miller is around the water wheel, stone plate, grain sacks, and flour dust;
- the potter is around clay, drying ground, and kiln;
- the weaver may be in the ground floor of the house, a shared weaving room, or between a row of drying frames;
- the healer may have a small consulting room, but may just as often be in other people's houses and on the road;
- the surveyor's primary working surface is terrain, paper, the ship, and temporary camps;
- the pilot's working surface is deck, weather, and mooring;
- bathhouses, guesthouses, eating houses, shared kitchens, and print rooms can all sustain very stable lifetimes,
  without being renamed as "so-and-so's workshop."

**Naming rules:**

**Craft nomenclature unified**
Chinese: for abstract capability / tradition, uniformly written as "手艺"; for the person, the umbrella noun is uniformly "手艺人," but once a specific scene begins, still prefer to write the specific craft or action.
English: when a cross-regional, cross-specific-trade umbrella noun for "someone who makes things" is needed, prefer `wright`; specific trades continue as `pressure-systems wright / shipwright / water-wright`, etc. `maker` is reserved only for the few design-layer contexts where the specific craft is truly unknown, or where the making motion needs comparison across ages; `craftsperson` is no longer used to define Anna / Edmund as characters.
`wright` is not a caste / job slot; it names "why others trust you to take a material and its consequences into your hands."

`wright / 手艺人` is only an umbrella noun; once a specific scene begins, prefer the specific action, material, or local name.
`maker` appears infrequently, only when the making motion needs comparison across ages / civilizations and the specific trade is unknown.
`wright` only appears when an umbrella noun is needed; a character remains, first, a person with family, local ties, and specific responsibilities.

**Apprenticeship:**
An apprentice follows a person and a responsibility, not an abstract institution.
One can follow a skilled hand, a family, a ship, a mill, a kiln, a print room,
a clinic, or a seasonal line, gradually learning to carry the load.
Apprenticeship is always person-to-person, and does not presuppose institutional enrollment.

**Character-completeness baseline:**
Any ordinary local person should at least be able to answer:
what is the first real thing they will do after they get up today?
Where does this thing happen?
Who would notice they did not show up today?
What is something they do especially well but would not use as self-introduction?
A character for whom the only answer is "works in the workshop" is not yet complete.


======================================================================
6. The Loom
======================================================================

6.1 New Definition

The Loom is every stretch of sky that human beings have learned to fly through, together with the relationships and knowledge that let those skies continue to be flown through.

It contains simultaneously:

- winds, monsoons, thermals, fog, icing altitudes, and safe windows;
- old CI beacons, weather masts, mountain passes, storm refuges, geothermal wells, and emergency water sources;
- ports, anchorages, repair capabilities, food, medicine, and lodging;
- what each place reliably yields, what it lacks, when its output comes in, and which goods are worth occupying long-haul payload;
- pilot oral tradition, chart revisions, local weather knowledge;
- craft credit, news, and cross-settlement mutual care.

The current Loom's "craft is the passport, credit is the currency, airships carry news, settlements are not superior or subordinate to one another" can be kept; what needs to change is scale and completeness.

6.2 The Loom Is Not a Central Institution

A Guild can register ships, maintain standards, keep a small pool of shared cross-regional equipment, and, during accidents or seasonal closures, help different places match up their news, but a Guild is not the Loom, and there is no "central dispatch desk." A Council can govern a settlement, but no one stands in Frontier and sees a complete planetary present, and much less does anyone decide, on behalf of the whole planet, which ship should go where today.

The Loom's real power comes from:

- who knows when this line can be flown;
- who can repair your ship at the next stop;
- whose name makes a stranger willing to send a child along with them on a segment;
- which hand-drawn weather chart has been verified by ten ships;
- which settlement is willing to keep an unprofitable winter beacon lit so a further stop remains reachable.

6.3 How a Line Becomes a Thread

Rumor / natural enrichment / old coordinates / a batch of local produce worth repeatedly carrying
→ a first charter
→ a second flight, in different weather
→ finding shelter and emergency landings
→ some place agreeing to maintain an anchorage
→ seasonal notations appearing on the chart
→ goods / mail / people starting to pass through repeatedly
→ the line acquires a name
→ it becomes a Thread

Conversely, a line can also die: a well runs dry, the wind field changes, a bridge settlement vanishes, a beacon goes untended, an alternative route proves safer. Old lines on a map should be left as faint traces, not cleanly deleted.

What old lines leave should not only be "faint lines" either. A line may leave behind a place-name still spoken over weather radio, though no settlement of that name exists in the actual country any longer; a row of fruit trees growing along a street that no longer exists; a farm boundary that in fact follows an old skyport of decades past; a town whose position today is completely different from what it was two hundred years ago, but which still uses the same name; an old pilot who still speaks of a Ghost Line no one has completely reflown in decades; a new bridge that carries the old Thread's name even though its route has curved off into another valley; two places that still send one another a certain thing, only because kinship and habit from the old-line era remain.

The Loom is not just "sky flown through today." It also carries the traces of what it has already lost, moved, forgotten. This lets Anna's map hold, at the same time, CI base sheets, old human maps, current routes, and future dashed lines.

6.4 Usable Route Vocabulary

- Old Thread: one of the earliest long cross-regional lines, with very old markers and stopping points.
- Winter Line: stable wind field only in winter.
- Green Thread: follows the thermal edge of the forest in spring.
- East Long: crosses about 1,800 km without a permanent settlement.
- Blue Crossing: one of the general names for island-chain and transoceanic lines.
- Ghost Line: old maps show coordinates; no one has completely reflown it in recent decades.

These names are samples; not all of them need to be registered as canon now.

6.5 Frontier City's New Identity

Frontier City is not the capital of the known world.

It is the Oldest Great Junction: several early CI corridors, great rivers, plains agriculture, minerals, high-altitude air currents, and old heavy industry happen to converge here, and the Loom has layered its way up to this spot.

What Anna sees from Frontier's heights is not the whole civilization, but many lines vanishing off in different directions:

- East Reach — 1,840 km
- Keld Transfer — 2,670 km
- Marneth North — seasonal
- South Coast — 940 km
- Long Blue — departure third day

She has been on some of these. Edmund on more. On the sleeve of a white-haired old hauler captain, where the cuff is rolled up, the inside of the lining shows a weave she does not recognize.

At the same moment, the regional junctions of Vorhan, Keld, and Duskreach each have their own departure boards, their own inner lines, and their own "far away." They do not take Frontier as the zero point, do not call going to Aethelgard "going to town," and do not need to pass through Frontier to exchange goods with each other. What is special about Frontier is that it is old, deep, and has many lines — not that it dispatches directions on behalf of the whole planet.

6.6 Route Textiles

Some people who fly long routes over the long term leave a small piece of woven cloth from the places they have been, but there is no unified rule, and it is not required that one collect a piece from every settlement one has flown through. The most common practice is not to take the cloth to "patch a garment," but to fold a narrow woven border into the inside lining of a jacket, the reverse of a cuff, the end of a scarf, the strap of a chart tube, the shoulder strap of a flight bag, or the inside of a commonly used pack. Places themselves do not necessarily specifically produce "commemorative cloth"; a small piece of a local common weave, dye, or narrow band is enough.

It is not a passport, a credential, or a tourist souvenir; it is only a very Archeon kind of bodily memory: you lived there through a few meals, let that place's wind touch your clothes, and someone there was willing to give you a small piece of local material. Even a truly old pilot's outer garment, seen from the front, may still look only like a well-made, well-broken-in coat; only when she rolls up her sleeve, takes it off and hangs it over a chair back, or opens the chart bag does the reader discover how many places are already hidden inside. The cloth's color, weave, dye, fiber thickness, and finishing edge can betray regional differences, but no one is expected to accurately "read a résumé" from them.

**Age scale:** young people may have only a few pieces; an old person may have dozens. Numbers represent the fact that time has given a person more chances to enter into relationship with more places; they do not indicate a legendary rank, nor do they encourage making the garment into an ever-more-ragged map.


6.7 Loom · Route Registry

Route registration format: `Edge ID | start | end | great-circle distance | actual flight distance | range grade | ship class | stopping points | season | hazards | verification status`.

Range grade belongs to the **edge**, not to the settlement:

- L0: ≤300 km; R1: 300–1,000 km; L2: 1,000–3,000 km; G3: >3,000 km.
- Additional tags: S seasonal, H high-hazard, B transoceanic.

Main trunks (first batch of Aethelgard story-region edges to register):

- Frontier–Crestline–Atheria.
- Frontier–Millhaven–Iris.
- Frontier–Thornfield–Stormperch–Skyleaf: main story-line working value about 1,800 km, L2-S-H; written as a two-to-three-day long-haul; the entire segment is cut off in the closed season.
- Frontier–Thornfield–Ironvein–Cinderlode.
- Frontier–Thornfield–Ironvein–Amber Reach: anchor-to-anchor polyline about 2,446 km, ordinary actual flight distance about 2,500–2,700 km, L2; people commonly stop at Thornfield / Ironvein, top up envelope gas and provisions, verify weather and ship condition, change out crew or ship, or entrust a thing to someone continuing to the next segment; Amber Reach's valley-floor T3 port receives Class V. P3 twelve-to-thirteen-year-old's second named long-haul flies this line.
- Frontier–Coppergate–Cliffwatch–Windbreak–Riverbend.
- Frontier–Canopy (northern line; midway route towns to be registered).
- Frontier–northwest G3 line (Fjord Coast direction; midway and endpoint to be registered, V14 anchor still OPEN).
- Iris coastal regional network (southbound endpoint to be registered).
- Frontier–Coppergate–Saltmarch–Whisperflat–Axiom: dry region far edge; Whisperflat's nocturnal micro-breeze ripples can assist in reading near-ground wind layers, but do not replace re-flight records and ordinary weather judgment.
- Cross-links (not passing through Frontier; working values, directions and mileages per §4.3.6 coordinate check): Coppergate–Thornfield about 1,300 km, the copperwork / eastern grain-and-fruit direct line between two southern-and-northern T3s; Cinderlode–Amber Reach about 860 km, the metal-to-resin / envelope-material direct line between two eastern T3s, with Ironvein not being their only relay; Riverbend–Saltmarch about 480 km, a short line trading canal-irrigated grain for salt, alkali, and pottery, not crossing Orun; Millhaven–Canopy about 1,450 km, the northwest line between the northern line's timber / resin and the Iris line's milling, midway route towns to be registered. These edges give the fifteen 30–60k towns lines among themselves, rather than all being hung off Frontier.

Route rules:

- Getting to Riverbend does not cross the Orun main wall; take the Coppergate–Cliffwatch–Windbreak detour edge.
- Getting to Skyleaf goes around Marneth's northern end through Kestrel's Gate; seasonal closure, borne by the S tag.
- Orun may be circumnavigated; Marneth may not be crossed (ceiling physics, see §6A.6 airship ceiling).
- Coastal settlements merge into the inland trunk at Iris Hollow.

Between remote places and mature places like Iris and Cinderlode, several well-worn lines already exist; they are not terminal branches waiting to be "wired in" by some center. Long routes do not have to pass through Frontier.

Global background lines:

- Vorhan already has a Rift / The Draft longitudinal chain, a High Inland Sea foothill ship-change line, and a southeastern line running to Organ Country; the three are linked to one another through regional junctions, not as terminal branches of Aethelgard routes.
- Keld already has an inter-island working line and pilotage relationship in Twin-Moon Coast–Karst Sea; local pilots, portside families, and the ships that regularly run these waters know one another, and an incoming ship usually finds a familiar hand to guide it in. The Roll seasonal window feeds into transoceanic fast lines; the ordinary sail-airship lines run year-round, and Roll is not the only lifeline.
- Duskreach already has a discontinuous main chain among the Smoke Wall's upstream lake region, the Cloud Forest Steps, and the outskirts of Standing Storm's warm bay; White Stair and the southern geothermal province feed in via cool-season lines.
- These are, at the world level, already active Loom. Their settlement names, author-side map indices, and actual capabilities of berthings are registered in the order stories enter them; Edge IDs, if used, belong to the author map only and do not become a social perspective. Before registration they may remain offscreen, but in the world they are already active, flown lines.
- At least one line among Vorhan–Keld, Keld–Duskreach, or Vorhan–Duskreach bypasses Aethelgard and runs independently. This lets the route geometry embody a network, not a radial structure.

6.8 Outpost / Small Settlement Registration: Why the Place Is Here

An outpost is not a homogeneous "service point" along a route waiting for the protagonist to pass. Many places first had a well, a geothermal vent, a field, weather observation, fishing, a mine road, a seasonal pasture, or a family, and only later did ships discover the place made a good stop; some places did grow up because of repeated ship stops, but even once grown they still have their own reasons for living.

The author registration format prefers to write:

`place name / author index |
coordinates |
terrain and water |
who is usually resident / in which season people are here |
why people will continue to be here |
what this place has in inexhaustible surplus |
one specific craft or caretaking action |
what a ship coming in can usually receive |
why the locals are willing to continue to keep space for those passing through |
inherited CI low-cost points (well / pad / beacon / old work-surface; write only what cost it lowers, not the machine) |
nearest familiar line / large anchorage (direction sanity check)`

Water, hydrogen top-up, cordage repair, bedspace, and weather logs may be written, but the place's definition always comes from its own reasons for living, not from a service-function checklist.

**Long-haul distance reference:**
To confirm that a long haul can physically survive, rough spacings can be kept:

- Within a mature weave: 30–180 km often reaches someone, a well, a shed, an anchorage, or some kind of usable care; no guarantee each site has fuel, bed, or repair.
- On farther lines of 1,200–3,000 km: places where one can shelter from weather, verify the forecast, or find water may be 250–450 km apart; places that can truly perform major repair may be 600–900 km apart.
- Beyond 3,000 km: seasonal gaps of 400–800 km can normally exist, sometimes longer.
- These numbers are only empirical references; no one has arranged them from a whole-network view, and distribution is not uniform.

A line lives for decades not because "service density is met," but because many different places along it are continually willing to leave a little something: an anchor pile, a jar of water, a made bed, someone who knows how to mend a certain joint, a phrase — "come find me first when you get here."

Every outpost has at least one "life-motion that would continue even if no airship came."


======================================================================
6A. Year 521's Everyday Civilization — the Texture Layer
======================================================================

These govern "what it feels like to live in this world" — the interior of settlements, crafts, food, sounds, festivals, technical detail.
They complement §2–§6's planetary / structural / Loom layers without overlapping.
The description takes the Thread (Aethelgard's mid-western mature route ring) as its default vantage, but only provides one local sample; it does not provide a global default skin. On entry into Vorhan, Keld, or Duskreach, energy mix, material surfaces, food, port actions, ship types, and daily rhythm must all be rebuilt from local surplus, from scratch.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.1 Society · Energy Ladder
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon's settlement distribution is shaped by the energy hierarchy, but the energy hierarchy is not a poverty / wealth / life / death table. Wind, water, sun, biomass, and regional geothermal are widespread; what truly opens the industrial gap is whether one can, all year, stably output high-temperature process heat, produce hydrogen in bulk, sustain large machinery, and have enough hands to care for the whole system. Four-layer system:

Tier 0: D-D fusion reactors — CI legacy, the core reaction components are not reproducible, but five hundred years of maturing maintenance, peripheral rebuilds, and load-modification tradition have grown up around them. Of CI's ~180 Primary Nodes, several dozen contained fusion-grade energy facilities; the ones still operating in Year 521 are distributed across the large junction cities and industrial nodes of Aethelgard, Keld, and Vorhan. A settlement with a running fusion reactor naturally becomes a regional pivot, but there is no globally unified rank name (§13.3 #13: Apex Hub is retired from everyday vocabulary). Fusion reactors are the deep hearts of these regions: 24/7 baseload electricity + heavy industry + hydrogen production (airship fuel) + waste-heat-driven steam-thermal chains. They amplify local industry; they do not monopolize an ordinary settlement's light, heat, water pumps, and everyday manufacture.

Tier 1: Steam / hot-water thermal chain + Compact Fusion Unit (CFU) — this is not a 19th-century replica in which "everything is driven directly by steam." A stable heat source first forms a steam and hot-water trunk; steam engines and small turbines can drive pumps, compressors, hoists, and local line-shafts, and from these machines further secondary systems can be split off, better suited to each specific motion: compressed-air lines feed pneumatic pipes, purge lines, and some tools; hydraulic accumulators handle lifts and presses that need smooth heavy force; hot-water loops enter bathhouses, kitchens, drying rooms, greenhouses, and winter warm walls; electric motors take over many rotating tasks not worth running a steam branch for. Visually there can therefore be exposed copper piping, insulated hot lines, gas tanks, pressure gauges, sight glasses, and steam vents, and each place's mechanical ecology is thereby different. A CFU is a smaller-volume catalytic fusion unit, allowing a mid-size settlement to have industrial capability beyond pure renewables; its most important aesthetic consequence is not "more steam," but rather **a single stable heat source able to grow many different, readable mechanical ecologies.**

Tier 2: Distributed intermediate technology — 25th-century knowledge + Archeon-reproducible. Perovskite solar panels (locally coated in production), wind turbines (natural wind channels through canyons), gravity-fed water storage (natural energy storage from the head of canyon drops), hydroelectric (Riverbend's stepped canal-and-ditch system is exemplary).

Tier 3: Fully self-produced renewables — small windmills, water wheels, biomass. The main energy of small outposts.

Overall trend: total energy is increasing, industrial capability is accumulating. Archeon is not consuming its inheritance — it is building anew on its foundation. Tier 0 / CFU counts, global installed capacity, and "energy does not go on the grid, energy goes on ships," see §5.9.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.2 Society · Materials and Building
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon is not living by picking things out of the ruins. They are "people who, standing on the foundation of 25th-century knowledge, are building a new civilization with their own hands." The materials table is therefore not a list of six things, nor an RPG-tier "the higher the grade the lighter it gets"; what truly determines how a bridge, a ship, or a sky pier grows is: **what carries compression, what carries tension, what only has to keep its shape, what must resist wear / heat / disassembly, and what is worth using hub craft to save a kilogram.** The freeze line freezes the information-processing layer (semiconductor and above, see the "electronic-capability freeze line" below), not "everything after 1900."

**Four-tier materials table:**

**Everyday grade — any settlement, below kiln temperatures:**
heartwood CLT / cross-laminated hardwood (Canopy improved hardwood + 500 years of woodwork, red-brown to deep chestnut, silvering with age; houses, bridge decks, decks, bulkheads, railings, furniture, organ resonance chambers, and some secondary ship-bones); stone and local geopolymer; fired pottery and tile; plant-fiber cloth, rope, and sail; plant resins and resin varnishes; wrought iron, cast copper, forged copper; blown and pressed plate glass; plant-oil lubricants and graphite. Also registered is a **light-core process**, not as a rare material: cork, low-density end-grain wood, and resin-stabilized plant-fiber honeycomb can serve as sandwich cores. They themselves do not carry primary tension, but with very little mass they separate two thin faces, giving decks, bulkheads, doors, whole fairings, and light platforms very high bending stiffness.

**Regional grade — continuous high-temperature furnace, hydro / wind electrolysis, produced by dozens of regional works:**
★ Basalt fiber (basalt melted and drawn; deep dark green / matte black; suitable for secondary trusses, tension rods / cables, bridge nets, Float Rail monorail, fire-resistant weaves, Class I–III main structure, and a great deal of Class IV/V secondary structure). Basalt is one of the most common high-strength materials on Archeon, but not the only answer.
Basalt cable and basalt / glass woven cloth; glass fiber; steel and brass; borosilicate glass; refractory brick; aluminum-magnesium alloy from remelt (roof tiles, lamps, utensils, light interior fittings, edge panels, some joints and skin frames); local iron, copper, and ordinary structural alloys continue to come from ore. Regional shipyards can hot-press heartwood / basalt / glass thin faces with light cores into large blocks of sandwich panel — this kind of panel is one of the main answers to airships "looking like they have thick hulls yet being very low mass."

**Hub grade — fusion / CFU stations, 6–8 sites globally, arc furnaces, electrolysers, catalytic chemistry:**
carbon fiber (pitch-based / lignin precursor; Class V longitudinal main beams, Class IV/V high-load ring frames, great-range key trusses, and record-grade structures); primary aluminum and magnesium (Hall–Héroult / seawater electrolysis; light-alloy joints, frames, shells, heat exchangers, and machinery housings); titanium (Kroll process; high-stress connections, valves, thermal / corrosion sites); silicon carbide and alumina ceramics; silicon and silicone; alkaline fuel-cell stacks and nickel / silver catalysts; optical-grade glass; aerogel-grade insulation. Add **aramid-family high-strength fabrics** as a hub small-batch narrow-use item: restraint straps, crew harnesses, tear-resistant layers, local envelope / skin reinforcement, and soft connections subject to sudden impact; do not let it compete with carbon / basalt for the "universal skeleton" position, and prose need not frequently name the chemistry.

**Legacy grade — non-reproducible:**
CI alloy skeleton (self-healing composite pipe + phase-change alloy joints; matte green-copper; pylon main load-bearing columns, the largest sky pier main trusses, a few historical anchor points); CI self-healing polymer concrete; Old-cable (UHMWPE fiber, extremely high strength per unit weight, non-corrosive, non-water-absorbing; still holds its Guild registration and its "theoretically revivable, industrially not reconnected" special status; UHMWPE's scarce status is fixed and does not change); radiation-hardened sensor and computing parts.

**Lightweight structure ROOT:**
Airships, cantilevered sections of skybridges, and any high-up members needing weight reduction are thought of first by load path, not by "what material the whole thing is."
- Primary tensile / bending paths: small amounts of carbon, basalt, light alloy, or historical structure;
- Secondary frames / webs: basalt, heartwood, glass, light alloy;
- Large-area shape-holding: sandwich panels / thin-layer skins;
- Human-contact surfaces: heartwood, cloth, glass, resin finish;
- Wear / heat / joint / bearing / pressure hardpoint: metals and ceramics appear concentrated here.

**Metal weight rule:**
Steel / bronze / brass / titanium are not banned on airships; they are **simply not wasted on places that only need to occupy volume.** Metal is best suited to bearing seats, propeller shafts, hinges, engine mounts, mooring eyes, valve bodies, pulleys, capstans, guide rails, pressure fittings, wear-edges, wearing feet, and deterministic joints between composite materials. Seen from afar, a ship may glint with many metal edges and fittings, but these high-density materials occupy only a small share of total volume. What looks like a "riveted metal panel" in an illustration, on the canon side, is explained first as a thin light-alloy face / edge cap, resin-composite fairing, or sandwich skin with removable fasteners; not as centimeters of steel armor.

**Use-family cross-cut (writer's lookup):**
Structural main paths — carbon / basalt / heartwood / light alloy / CI alloy;
Secondary frames and thin faces — basalt / glass / heartwood / sandwich;
Cordage — plant / basalt / steel / Old-cable;
Skins and envelope material — woven layer + resin / silicone coating;
Seals — resin / silicone / titanium and alloy gaskets;
Transparency — plate glass / borosilicate / optical;
Fire resistance — basalt cloth / refractory brick / SiC;
Electrical — copper, nickel, silver, vacuum-tube glass;
Soft restraints — plant webbing / basalt / hub-grade aramid-family fabrics.

The material gradient maps to ship class, but the relationship is a continuous spectrum, not a three-cell correspondence table. The larger a ship, the more it is worth spending hub materials on **the small number of main load paths that decide the whole ship's mass**; the great majority of the ship's visible volume is still finished with regional / local materials.

**Material-description principle:**
If a paragraph has already written heartwood, basalt, and brass, it need not go on to name "carbon composite" a fourth time. More effective is to convey the structure through weight and touch: a deck that looks thick sounds light underfoot; a large hatch that one person can push open; cold, hard metal appearing suddenly around the mooring eye; a thin face flexing minutely as wind pressure changes; a main beam showing only a stub through an inspection hatch. The reader should first believe "this thing really does fly," then slowly come to know why it is light.

Independent new-build capability: ordinary construction 3–8 storeys of heartwood CLT; with basalt-fiber cable, steel cable, and segmented trusses, 200–300 m class pier, skybridge, and horizontal platform can be built new as a matter of course. Old-cable pushes only a few record spans further. Integrated vertical cores above 100–200 m still rely on CI skeleton support — Archeon does not replicate the old-style pylon and does not need to treat "whether we can build another pylon" as a measure of civilizational progress. Pylon height is bequeathed; the port districts, terraces, bridge nets, workshop belts, and kilometers of city width that have grown up around it are five hundred years of human engineering.

Knowledge transmission has three layers: CI metal-plate etched records (the most authoritative technical reference) → hand-copied books (with the parts whose tool-chains are lost cut out, and local experiment and failure records accumulated in) → apprentice oral tradition ("when you hear this sound, the temperature is right"). The result is not that "no one understands principles" but that theoretical depth is highly uneven: structures, fluids, weather, agronomy, metallurgy, and pressure systems have living local theory; quantum computing, nanofabrication, and legacy reactor cores rely more on operating traditions with clear boundaries.

Selectivity of the knowledge fork: Year 521 has not "kept the hands" and "cut out the brains"; it lets knowledge that can be verified by local materials, instruments, and repeated practice keep growing, and lets disciplines dependent on vanished equipment chains lose their frontier. People can calculate loads, wind fields, heat exchange, alloy batches, and crop rotation, and will argue about method; they cannot from these skills reason back to a semiconductor fab, quantum communication, or reactor-core physics. The most heavily deformed are history and higher-order legacy systems that cannot be routinely rechecked: CI shifts from "artificial superintelligence" into "an old spirit that the earth once spoke through," and Sky Fire's causal chain simplifies down to "the sky once caught fire, and our ancestors lived through it." "Valve A first" on a fusion reactor may, over ten generations of copying, descend from an equation into a rule of accident practice; at the same time, a new shipyard may perfectly well use five hundred years of local data to write a design manual better suited to Archeon's winds and materials than the CI colonization handbook was. Tradition and innovation coexist, and the boundary depends on what can be verified, not on whether the knowledge looks like ancient technology.

CI industrial-capacity boundary (locked canon): CI does not carry a full Federation industrial chain. It can make building materials, standard machine parts, basic electronic parts (relays, analog circuit boards, motor controllers, basic sensors), biological cultivation facilities, and colonial infrastructure, but the following key components must rely on preinstalled spares, orbital precision fabs, or subsequent resupply: ASI cores, expert subsystems, medical gene-repair platforms, high-end robotic joints, radiation-hardened sensor arrays, cryogenic cooling modules, ultrapure computational substrates, semiconductor / photonic chip manufacturing equipment, precision optical systems, quantum communication modules. Standard colonial doctrine assumes regular resupply ships during Phase 2–3 to bring in spares and knowledge-base updates. Sky Fire cut this chain. CI was not destroyed in an instant, but degraded layer by layer without being able to replenish precision components: first sacrificing the language and education layers to preserve survival control; then sacrificing the expert models to preserve fixed procedures; finally etching what knowledge it could output into metal and stone. Its death was not a failure but a resource-allocation decision that took decades.

Electronic-capability freeze line — the hard-physical root of "steampunk": Archeon is not without electricity — the fusion reactors provide ample power, there are electric motors, fuel cells, basic circuits. But the information-processing layer is completely severed. The electronics of the 25th–26th centuries are, in essence, information-processing terminals with nanoscale semiconductor / quantum substrates at the core. Making them requires ultrapure material refining, single-crystal growth, photolithography, chemical vapor deposition, ion implantation — each step depends on a whole industrial city's worth of equipment chains. The ceiling of electronic devices CI could teach humans to make is vacuum-tube-grade analog circuits — coils, capacitors, hand-blown vacuum tubes, basic radio transmit / receive. Everything beyond semiconductor requires precision manufacturing chains CI cannot replicate on the ground. Archeon therefore has ample electricity and basic electrical capacity (motors, simple circuits) but no digital computation, no screens, no microprocessors. High energy + low information processing = the core formula for steampunk. This freeze line defines the technological ceiling of the post-521 world.

**Five families of readable machinery:**
"High energy + low information processing" corresponds in imagery to five physical families that can rotate through the foreground:

1. **pressure / heat**: steam, hot water, compressed air, hydraulic accumulator, gas holder, pressure vessel, condenser, heat exchanger.
2. **tension / gravity**: winch, capstan, block and tackle, counterweight, cable car, elevator cage, tension line, gravity chute.
3. **electromechanical**: copper-wound motor, relay, solenoid, bell circuit, knife switch, dynamo, motorized winch.
4. **analog sensing / signalling**: liquid-column / Bourdon pressure gauge, sight glass, barometer, mechanical inclinometer, engine-order bell / telegraph, speaking / voice tube, bell, light signal.
5. **wind / cloth**: sail, wind cloth, flag, louvre, weathervane, variable wing surface, the sound of rigging in different winds.

When a place or ship enters prose, pick one or two families from its real function and let them appear naturally. The steampunk feel comes from **being able to see how force is transmitted, how heat is used, how a person reads the machine** — not from a fixed set of props reappearing again and again.

★ Media of memory — light can be seen, not kept: Archeon has not formed a stable photographic or phonographic tradition. The boundary here is not "too poor to fire off a batch of chemicals" — silver halide photography is a chemistry this civilization can reach — but that the technical path and the institutional path never connected. One, the tech tree grew upside down: the imaging and recording of the CI era were all on the electronic end, and left with the digital tool chain; chemical photography and mechanical recording did not have a ready industry available for descent-inheritance. Two, chemical lines like dyes, metallurgical acids, and medicinal alcohol continued to serve daily needs and formed guilds, standards, and supply chains; photographic materials always remained in scattered experiments, and no one wove them into a stable cross-settlement craft. Three, the first few generations buried loved ones without photographs; handwriting, objects, stories, and three-part song took the place of memory first; later, "keeping a face in a box" was no longer widely understood as a missing convenience. Phonography likewise: transmission was rebuilt; storage never became a shared need; radio therefore is a medium of the present tense only. Overall principle: anything that enters memory must first pass through a hand. Handwriting through the hand, portrait through the hand, story through the mouth, object through use — this civilization's chosen medium of human storage is the human being. (Big cities have camera obscura darkrooms; cartographers project and hand-trace, so portraiture is rare but not evidence of resource scarcity.) Commemoration culture is grounded on this premise; specific content is developed under the author's oversight.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.3 Society · Communication
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon has no digital communication. No screens, no phones, no internet. See §6A.2 electronic-capability freeze line. What follows is the communication system that actually works in Anna's era.

Intra-city communication is not a single system laid over every settlement. Pressure districts with a stable heat source, sufficient density, and long-term maintenance population can grow pneumatic tube networks (compressed-air / steam-driven compressor sending capsules, 30–50 km/h), Frontier and several 30–60k towns with stable thermal trunks (Cinderlode, Iris Hollow, and Amber Reach's furnace / shipyard belts, Coppergate and Thornfield's port districts) being the most typical, and even then covering only those few districts; smaller route towns and villages depend more on foot, pulley-basket, bell, lantern, and short radio. Large shipyards, pylon interiors, and long ships like Class IV/V can also use very-short-range **speaking / voice tube, bell pull, or engine-order telegraph** to connect a few positions that always have someone at station — these span only one building / one ship, not pretending to be a city phone network. Signal-lamp code is used at skyports and heights; large Steam Whistle / bell only sound when the whole settlement genuinely needs to hear.

Inter-city communication: ★ Radio — Archeon's telegraph. Vacuum-tube-era analog radio, five centuries of native adaptation. Hardware: hand-wound copper coil antennas, hand-blown glass vacuum tubes, copper-leaf capacitors, and carbon-rod resistors. Wooden cases with brass knobs. Line-of-sight 50–150 km stable, ionospheric reflection 500–1000 km but extremely unreliable (affected by sunlight, nebula radiation, atmospheric state). Bandwidth is very narrow — most is short-sentence telegraph-style code. Relay towers — small unmanned radio relays every 100–200 km on high ground along the main routes. Optical-signal-tower relay — permanent signal towers on key heights, semaphore / heliograph by day, long-short flashes of oil lamp by night. Airship post — the physically most reliable bandwidth. The overwhelming majority of "communication" is airships carrying letters and parcels between settlements. Pilots are themselves messengers / newsreaders / spreaders of rumor.

Interstellar communication: dead. After Axiom shipmind went silent in Year 263 After Sky Fire, the last orbital communication capability was lost. The "corridor channel" is a historical concept — CI communication protocol standards are still etched on metal plates, but no equipment can transmit that far.

Narrative implication: without instant communication, every long journey is a real "leaving." Misunderstandings can grow in an information vacuum lasting days. News of a death may reach another settlement two weeks late.

The everyday life of letters (the life side of airship post): letters travel on the ships of familiar lines and on chance-passing ships — two to four weeks between writing and reading is normal, longer to far segments. A letter and things sent along with it are usually wrapped together in one cloth bag: a letter, a jar of pickles, a pair of shoes, a bag of seeds — someone happens to be going that way, and takes it along. Spoken message is more common than letter — pilots pass words for people, two or three words is enough ("all well"). Radio carries only coded short sentences; anything that needs detail, tone, and warmth goes on paper and on the mouth. The letter is therefore not an add-on service, it is the main body of cross-station personal communication.

The social meaning of letters: a letter carries the "this moment" of the moment it was written — by the time it arrives, that moment is already in the past. The reader's timeline and the writer's timeline are not synchronized, but the letter itself proves that someone on the other side of the world is thinking of you. Miriam does not fly ships, but her lists and letters maintain relationships along Edmund's routes — who needs seeds, whose child's cough has cleared. What she flies is the paper version of relationship. A reply is not always a letter — it can be a stone, a leaf, a spoken message relayed by another person. In a world of write-then-erase, a letter kept is heavier than words on a chalkboard.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.4 Society · Building and City
----------------------------------------------------------------------

6A.4.0 Why It Looks This Way (aesthetic coherence)

Archeon's visual style — vines climbing the green-copper alloy skeleton, heartwood and basalt structures interlocking, several pylons growing up out of the lower-level life, and the same materials continuing into bridges and ships — is not a poverty filter. It is the result of material localization, five hundred years of practiced craft, and readable engineering combined. A six-layer causal chain:

Layer one · After the integrated industrial chain retreated, materials relocalized. Federation aesthetics depended on a planet-wide unified industrial chain; Archeon has each region carry production with its own ore, forest, furnace, resin, glass, and fiber. Hand- and machine-forged metals, heartwood timber, basalt-fiber cloth, fired pottery, remelted alloys, and hand-blown glass are not "what was left"; they are a mature material vocabulary formed after five hundred years of continuous production.

Layer two · The axis of scale has shifted. Monolithic vertical megastructure is constrained by the CI skeleton, but horizontal platforms, bridge nets, port districts, terraces, canals, and whole settlements can, generation over generation, expand to the kilometer scale. Members must be approachable, inspectable, replaceable in segments, and understandable; "the hand can care for it" limits single-piece black-box scale, but does not limit the total scale a civilization accumulates.

Layer three · Five hundred years' accumulation is not a one-time master plan, but growth. Each generation continues to extend outward with the material it has most abundantly, works most fluently, and can reliably reproduce, and along the way sews in markets, kitchens, drying platforms, plants, festivals, and aesthetic choices. The result is organic and asymmetric, yet still highly finished: visible seams do not mean crude joinery, non-uniform color does not mean patchwork, old and new can once again become one integrated whole after a properly performed refit or addition. The shared appeal of medieval European old towns and Miyazaki cities also comes from here — complex, but not shabby.

Layer four · There is no unified fashion industry or "modernization" narrative pressure. Aesthetic evolution runs more like "what works, what is beautiful, what people still want to keep doing after long practice in the local weather." Regions therefore share an engineering-family feeling, and still keep their own glass colors, eaves, railings, gunwales, sail cuts, and spatial habits.

Layer five · Plants are not treated as the enemy. The roots of terraform plants in fact protect the CI alloy rather than corrode it (hardened layer); vines, fruit trees, shade layers, and building biofilm supply food, materials, cooling, and seasonal information at the same time. People keep them because they are useful, good to look at, and belong to the place — not only because "we can't clear them."

Layer six · There is no cultural-memory reference of "we should restore ourselves to some kind of future city." Anna's generation has not seen images of Federation cities. They do not feel they are performing steampunk pastiche; they are simply continuing to make houses, bridges, ships, furnaces, fields, and ports look the way they know them to look.

It is similar to the Miyazaki world not because damage and repair are exposed everywhere, but because it shares a scale ethics: complex machinery is still understood by particular people; cities and ships alike make room for food, wind, plants, personal objects, and the patina time leaves behind; technology does not erase manufacturing logic, human touch, and everyday-use traces. **Care is visible; breakdown does not dominate the picture.** Archeon is a hard-SF civilization of high energy, low digitalization, abundant materials, and localized production.

Future narrative use (aesthetic gap on Federation contact): when Anna first sees streamlined curves, mirror-glass, and nearly unreadable seamless architecture, the shock should come from "I can't see how it was made or how it is cared for," not "so what is truly advanced is very new." The aesthetic gap between the two civilizations is a difference in readability and where causal weight sits, not shabby vs. clean.

6A.4.1 Urban Pattern of a Large Junction City (Frontier City as Example)

Core visual principle: the skeleton of the CI era + five hundred years of human-grown skin — from a dense low mass a few peaks grow.

Frontier City is the fullest example of this pattern: from a distance it is not a few isolated towers, it is an undulating mountain range in cross-section — some twenty pylons, 100–300 m tall, rising from a thick, undulating terrace matrix. The pylons are asymmetric cones — each slope angle differs by CI original structure, wind direction, and port orientation. Every pylon itself is layered, with sky piers and large platforms cantilevered from its middle sections. Two pylons are connected by large platforms (Broad Terrace) — multi-layer public spaces 30 to over 100 m wide and 50–200 m long. From afar these large platforms make the pylon group's silhouette not "a few isolated peaks" but a continuous low mountain range. The pylon's load-bearing columns are the CI-legacy self-healing alloy skeleton (non-reproducible), the outer layers are five hundred years of human-accumulated heartwood woodwork, basalt / glass secondary trusses, light-core sandwich platforms, thin alloy tile, and vines — the skeleton borrowed, the skin self-grown; the higher and larger the span, the more visibly it uses "thin faces + cavities / light cores + a few strong joints," and does not continue to write the whole pylon skin as heavy-metal stacking.

Skybridges are the workhorse of transport. Because private ground vehicles are not default and near-neighbor living density is high, skybridges carry 90% of the city's everyday short-distance movement; float rail, cable car, and work vehicles handle longer or heavier flows. In high-density districts, buildings above three storeys are almost all connected by bridges. Skybridges are not just transit — they are clotheslines, signal-line supports, rainwater channels, and places where street vendors set up temporary stalls.

Sky pier is the basic unit of a skyport — not a tower. Every sky pier is a lily-pad-shaped platform cantilevered from a terrace edge or a pylon's middle section. Small pockets on the edges of large platforms are skiff take-off and landing points — enough for a skiff to be set down, with a mooring pile and simple hoist arm, scattered along the market and square edges; skiffs come and go on demand.

The skyport is the city's heart, not its edge. The piers in pylon middle sections, the skiff pockets on large-platform edges, and the piers on terrace edges together form a three-dimensionally distributed mooring network. Signal lamps (a color-coded oil-lamp system) are set at the tips of every pier — an incoming airship first reads the overall lamp-array pattern to judge which pier is free.

Density is a gradient, not uniform. The core is densest; further out it loosens into houses, small shops, courtyards, weaving rooms, mills, side sheds, common furnaces, and scattered heat-work spaces needing to be kept apart, and gradually fades away into orchards and farmland — no clear city wall, the city like a drop of ink slowly soaking into paper.

The city's sound is also layered: the bottom layer is water flow and the low-frequency hum of heavy machinery; the middle layer is human voice, hammer strike, and market clamor; the upper layer is wind and the whoosh of airship engines, and the "clack-clack-clack" beat of a cable-car gondola's wheels passing over station rollers. People on different levels have different "main melodies."

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.4.2 Ordinary Settlements' Building–Life Relationship
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon has no "workshop-town" as the default settlement skin.
Houses exist first because people need to sleep, eat, store, shelter from wind, draw water, care for children and elders;
work spaces are added on according to climate, noise, fire, humidity, materials, neighborly relations, and local habits.
The same craft can grow completely different architectural relationships in different places.

When writing a settlement, first decide six things:
what shape the terrain presses the house into | where the water comes from and where it passes | the cheapest local structural material |
where people eat and sleep | which things are suited to being done at the edge of the home | which things must be shared or held away from the dwelling.
Port grade, industrial grade, and route status are added afterwards; life comes before functional positioning.

**Five relationships of living and doing (mix as you like, not an architectural taxonomy):**

1. **Pure dwelling + external public organs.**
