   Home is mainly kitchen, bunk, storage, drying platform, small courtyard; grinding grain goes to the mill, big-oven baking goes to the common oven,
   bathing goes to the bathhouse, pressing fruit goes to a seasonal press shed. Atheria and many small river villages can adopt this type widely.
   A house of this kind does not automatically attach a workshop just because someone in the family has a craft.

2. **Living-doing mix.**
   One floor / one end / a side wing carries an eating house, weaving room, print room, small clinic, glass repair, cloth-cutting,
   fermentation, simple woodwork, or small repair; the other part is still clearly the home.
   Frontier Broad Terrace and the Atheria longhouse are already canon.
   Prose should let a reader tell at a glance "where life is going on, where work is going on today."

3. **Shared productive organs.**
   Mill, water wheel, stone oven, ceramics kiln, glass furnace-house, fruit-press house, brewery / fermentation cave, drying yard,
   washing platform, dye shed, sawhouse, rope shed, ship rack, and so on may be used in common by several households, one street, or a whole small town.
   What they generate is a fixed set of sounds, heat, dust, smell, waiting, and meeting patterns,
   not merely "this place has manufacturing capacity."

4. **Spaces that must be kept apart because they are hot / dirty / large-scale.**
   Smelting, continuous furnaces, heavy forging, Class V shipframes, large-area resin work, strong dust, or dangerous pressure processes
   naturally form furnace fields, shipyards, industrial belts, or genuine workshop clusters.
   These spaces may be very important, but housing, food, and public life still occupy the main body of a settlement.

5. **Work without a fixed room.**
   Orchards, fields, ditches, roofs, tidal steps, mountain passes, mooring berths, ships, survey camps, weather masts,
   drying platforms, and whole routes can all be where a person mainly does things.
   "Doing something habitually" does not mean "having a workshop of one's own."

**Public buildings are not an administrative-building checklist.**
An ordinary Archeon settlement more often grows out of life's needs:
common kitchen / big oven, mill, bathhouse, longhouse guest house, clinic, longhouse where story-nights and dinners are held,
well-shed, water trough and washing platform, press shed, greenhouse, drying yard, print room, small mooring, etc.
A place may lack most of these; a place may lack most of these, but has at least one or two life-oriented public spaces.

**Regional check for houses:**
Each new settlement answers at least three of the following, rather than putting on a uniform steampunk skin:
- What are foundation / wall / roof each resisting: wind, tide, heat, snow, damp, slope, thunderstorm, or dust;
- How does local surplus enter the house: wood, basalt, salt ash, clay tile, glass, vine, warm water, fruit tree;
- Which door of the day, which stretch of eave, which patch of yard is really most used;
- Whether history has left a size, a habit of material, or a drift of use; if not, that is also perfectly allowed;
- What one first sees from the doorway: another household, field, ditch, cliff, tide, bridge, orchard, or sky.

**Principle of local-life diversity:**
A new place has at least two scenes from life that would exist locally regardless, not only workshop and port:
mill, water, oven, bathhouse, orchard, drying platform, printing, weaving, fruit-pressing, kiln, salt pan, greenhouse,
guest house, roof, courtyard, tidal steps, canal gates, a wall the children claim, or a household's kitchen.
This decides whether a place feels as if it has been alive for many years before Anna arrived.

**Completeness baseline:**
Take the airships out of the scene, and this place still supports a whole morning.
Remove the word "workshop," and it is still clear at least three kinds of people are doing something, and where.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.4.3 Ground–Sky Continuity
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Airships are not another design language suspended above the settlement. **They are buildings the settlement grew after it learned to move.** This sentence only locks civilizational continuity; a ship is nonetheless not a flying house: the ground is organized by terrain, water, and neighborhood; a ship is organized by weight, wind, hull longitudinal axis, center of gravity, and continuous voyage; form must therefore differ. What is continuous is not only material, but also human body scale, interface common sense, weight intuition, how life occupies connective space, and how a place carries its craft into moving structure.

**Eight points of continuity:**

1. **Continuous material, discontinuous mass distribution.**
   Houses, bridges, piers, and ships share a material family — heartwood / basalt / light alloy / brass and copper / cloth / resin / hand-blown glass; on the ground, stone, thick wood, and metal can carry more mass, while the sky version automatically becomes thinner, more hollow, more sandwich / tension / hollow member, pressing metal into hardpoints. The cross-sections of house and ship naturally differentiate by load.

2. **Continuous edges.**
   Atheria's cliff edges, Frontier's skybridge edges, ordinary houses' drying platforms, and ships' gunwales all belong to the same bodily experience of "people who live for a long time on open edges with railings." A gunwale is not only for scenes of danger; in steady flight it is also a place to see weather, talk, eat, and let the wind blow through.

3. **Continuous mixed use.**
   A settlement does not lock living / making / eating into separate function-boxes; a ship is the same. Galley, long table, chart, fine work, hammock, porthole, and deck borrow space from one another. Class II space is not partitioned into cockpit / engine room / cargo hold / bunks — four modern-vehicle modules.

4. **Continuous interface literacy.**
   What a Guild is really worth standardizing across regions is first what human hands touch and could get someone killed if got wrong: main hose couplings, mooring-eye size family, basic valve open / close direction, emergency-line markings, common light signals / hand signs, certain handhold / clip logic. Local ships may look quite different, but an Atheria person setting foot for the first time on a Keld ship should not feel as if entering alien machinery. Standards handle "the hand knows where to begin"; local craft handles "why we do it this way here."

5. **Continuous weight literacy.**
   Not every Archeon person can calculate buoyancy, but they widely know weight is not moved casually. Coming aboard, one asks first where a heavy thing goes; when a crowd presses to one side, someone naturally calls them to spread out; after a large piece is unloaded, topping up ballast is not mysterious. This norm extends from harbor steps, float rail, float sled, and skiff all the way to Class V — a social coherence deeper than visual material for a "lighter-than-air civilization."

6. **Continuous connective space.**
   Skybridges, harbor steps, gangplanks, Class V common corridors, and lee decks are all first "connecting two places," but Archeon people do not thereby keep them as pure corridors. People wait, sit, eat, hang cloth, write, watch the wind, briefly set baskets on the edge. **Transit space allowed to be occupied by life** is the strongest social grammar shared by ground and ship; only when the space really needs to be cleared — for departure, for berthing, or before bad weather — do people swiftly turn it back into a working corridor.

7. **Continuous local accent.**
   Shipyards belong to local culture. Beneath a shared physical standard, ships can show origin in window proportions, gunwale shapes, drainage, sail cut, wood finish, galley venting, local wind fences, and color. A reader may first feel "this is an Archeon ship," and slowly come to see "it probably comes from that side."

8. **Continuous sedimentation of life.**
   Houses are changed by generations; ships are changed by the places they pass through. But this layer of change enters first into mugs, food, cloth, glass, knot-styles, storage habits, recipes, and local practices left after a full refit — not into a salvage layer of "different waste pieces spliced together everywhere."

**Ports are seams, not terminals.**
An effective arrival scene, occasionally, does one "space with no cut" pass: deck's thin wood / composite face → gangplank → harbor-step wood; ship railing → skybridge railing; port glass → house glass; galley heat → dockside eating house / household kitchen; sailcloth and wind cloth stirring together in the same gust. Seams are entirely different in different places; the above is only an example, not a fixed sequence to be repeated every time.

**Regional baseline for ships (answering three is enough):**
- In which wind / rain / salt / sand / cold does this ship mainly work, and what has its form thereby changed?
- Which few places are the real main load paths, and which large volumes are actually just light shells / cavities / living space for people?
- Which materials and crafts are cheapest and best around the shipyard, and where is regional origin therefore visible at a glance?
- Which stretch of deck, which door, which pane, which lee corner do people onboard most really use?
- What interfaces and actions can an outsider stepping onto this ship for the first time use without relearning?
- How is the galley linked to deck / common corridor?
- What have the routes it has run left on habits of life, rather than how many patches on the hull?

**Interior description principle:**
Ship interior description should make one feel "this is an Archeon ship," not a generic vehicle. Prioritize finish, cavity and lightness, spatial habit, light, wind, food, where people commonly touch, and a small number of truly heavy joints that let one feel the whole ship has always been calculating weight. An older ship shows lived-in-ness through sedimented life, not cracks and patches; a hundred-meter Class V's exterior heft comes from light-core structure, not thick riveted steel plate.

**Principle for using "workshop":**
"Workshop" is used only when it truly names a space whose main body is making / assembly / repair. When one can name mill, kiln, weaving room, fruit-press house, furnace-house, ship rack, print room, clinic, bathhouse, eating house, drying yard, courtyard, orchard, ditch, mooring, etc., use the specific space name. If one only wants to convey "people work here," change it into the action the person is doing. Each time "workshop" appears, there should be a specific room behind it.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.5 Society · Evolution of Buoyancy Engineering
----------------------------------------------------------------------

This is Archeon civilization's most distinctive engineering identity. It is not an aesthetic choice; it is a consequence of historical path dependence. Six-layer causal chain:

Layer one · The CI era had real industrial-grade vehicles. "Reaper #4," recorded in the Atwood ledger, was a product of Federation 25th-century industrial grade — streamlined, mirror-alloy chassis, needing no embedded buoyancy.

Layer two · After the robot recession, ground mechanization collapsed. In the regional recession period of roughly 2623–2713, the CI-legacy reapers went out of service one by one.

Layer three · But airship-making capability was preserved. Airships were among the earliest complex technologies CI taught humans — hydrogen production, compartmentalized cells, basalt / wood composite, light-core sandwich, tension structure, a small amount of high-grade main bone, and readable mechanical interfaces were passed down through apprenticeship. Later large Class IV/V would add hub carbon fiber and light-alloy hardpoints on a few main load paths, but "airship technology is preserved" has never meant "all ships rely on a single basalt skeleton."

Layer four · When they had to reinvent ground vehicles, all they had in hand was buoyancy technology. Around 2700–2900, workshops scaled the internal-cell architecture of airships down onto field equipment.

Layer five · This engineering philosophy was discovered to be agriculturally superior. Buoyancy-embedded vehicles do not compact the soil — Archeon's terraformed soil layer is only 521 years deep. By Anna's era, this had shifted from "what we could do" to "what we should do" — an agricultural ethic proven by practice.

Layer six · Cross-scale aesthetic consistency. When one engineering philosophy runs through a civilization's every vehicle — from the 90–120 m Class V airship to the 1 m-class float plow — it moves from engineering choice into cultural identity. Archeon people would not say "we use buoyancy engineering" — this is simply how they make things.

Narrative use (reveal on Federation contact): when a Federation visitor first sees a Plain Cutter drifting above an Archeon field — "why did these people build such an elaborate solution just to keep a machine from compacting the soil?" The answer is: because their soil is the entire yield of 521 years of biosphere, and they remember what losing it means. Buoyancy is not just technology — it is Archeon's ethical commitment to "lightness."

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.6 Technology · Airships
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Airships are Archeon's blood vessels, and its most complete moving architecture. Buoyancy comes from hydrogen, propulsion from fuel cells (primary), steam-assist (backup), and sails (auxiliary and energy-saving); lift constants, envelope material and loss, fuel bag, hydrogen consumption, and fire rules see §5.6–5.7. This section governs form and texture only, and must obey §6A.4.3: **a ship is not a machine outside the city, it is a compressed version of the same engineering and life carried into the sky.**

The Gust is the Class II line ship Anna has cared for and flown for a long time — jointly kept by Atheria, Frontier, and several places along the line that have used her over a long span. There can be a ledger entry naming route trust as her shell, but the way Anna knows her day to day is more concrete: deep-chestnut resin-sealed heartwood deck, matte-black basalt load-bones, brass polished to a warm sheen where hands most often rest, small window panes casting slightly different colors in different weather; at start-up, the low hum of the fuel cell spreads evenly out from underfoot. She is not a ship that needs strange noises, cracks, and improvised patches to show "personality." Kassia's Razor, meanwhile, presses the same material philosophy down to its smallest form: sharper motor whine, open, light, with strong short-range burst.

The classification of airships runs from Class I small craft to Class V large long-line ships. A ship's ownership / stewardship is a gradient (§13.2 #9): skiff mostly cared for by an individual or a family; Class I/II are often looked after long-term by a family, several households, a settlement, guild, or cooperative; Class III/IV are often carried by one regional relationship or several places together; Class V's long-term steward often crosses multiple settlements. Rescue, giving up the ship in a disaster, and not abandoning someone in distress are Loom obligations. The Guild registers ships, maintains interoperable standards, and coordinates a small number of cross-regional public tools, but does not schedule the whole Loom on anyone's behalf. Skiff does not fall within the airship classification — it is a single-person / few-person open-cockpit flyer, closer to the intersection of tool, light craft, and bicycle ecology than to a minimum Class I.

★ Aesthetic anchor — Mortal Engines continues to lock only silhouette on the airship: non-zeppelin, non-hot-air-balloon, non-Federation streamline; buoyancy cells embedded within a hard shell / skeleton, not directly read from outside as one big balloon. The reference does not authorize uniform brass, exposed piping, gears, or a "shabby steam" skin. Class V is a floating large ship / small community lived in over the long term; Class IV is a heavy long-line ship; Class II/III is the wood / composite sailship scale most possible to genuinely live in on local familiar lines; Class I and skiff are lighter, sharper, more open. Family feeling comes from shared engineering logic and material system; each class's form differentiates naturally by scale.

**Class I–V Scale Working Bands**
Length only locks silhouette, mooring, and turning, and does not by itself determine class; envelope volume is a broad working band and does not require every ship to hit precisely.

| Class | Length working band | Effective lift-gas magnitude | Primary feel |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Class I | ~12–25 m | ~0.4–1.8k m³ | Small craft / courier / short regional lines; barely forms an "indoor society" |
| Class II | ~25–40 m | ~1.5–6k m³ | The 3–6-person living/working scale Anna knows well; the main body of regional lines |
| Class III | ~40–60 m | ~5–12k m³ | Medium-distance, multi-day, more people can truly live in rotation |
| Class IV | ~60–85 m | ~12–28k m³ | Heavy regional / long-haul; space begins to clearly split into multiple common and watch zones |
| Class V | ~90–120 m | ~30–50k m³ | Hundred-meter-class long-line ship / floating small community; about 80 worldwide |

Adjacent bands are allowed to overlap slightly due to hull proportion / high-altitude optimization / sailplan / cell layout. Class ratios differentiate naturally by use. The smaller a Class I, the more open; the larger a Class V, the more it needs a long longitudinal axis, compartmentalized cells, broad and slow motion, and genuine living space.

Buoyancy-share spectrum: Class V about 95% buoyancy, wing surfaces are only stability faces and sails. Class IV about ninety percent; Class II/III about seventy to eighty percent; Class I varies more by model. Skiff has only thirty to fifty percent buoyancy, aerodynamic lift dominates — the existence of the launch ledge is proof. The cells in the frame-wing only ensure a stall becomes a "slow sinking" rather than "a drop."

Different classes also use wind differently. A skiff is light and its wings carry more lift, so it best feeds on slope updrafts, thermals, cliff-edge corners, and broken wind that vanishes in tens of seconds; it is like finding the next foothold on a continually crumpling piece of cloth. Class II / III have enough thrust yet still actively seek continuous wind layers, borrow the sail to save hydrogen, and avoid a stretch of dirty wind not worth pushing straight into. Class IV is more willing to wait for a long wind rather than chase a short one. Class V is actually the least free of all: mass great, turning slow, form long, it would rather wait half a day at the fringes than push a whole "sky village" into a gust only fit for skiff play. On the author side one can continue to note: **small ships chase the wind, large ships wait for it.**

**Local ship types are not just paint differences.** A regional shipyard lets its own climate and life enter into the ship: a wet-coast ship may have denser drainage, small dense windows, more salt-resistant outer cloth; a highland ship is lighter, with wing and sail area more mindful of thin air; on the Atheria / Frontier familiar lines, a Class II may favor open lee decks and railings scaled to terrace railing. Differences come from long practice, and belong to a continuous spectrum, not to regional marks.

**Structural reading of a Class V:**
A 90–120 m outward form does not mean 90–120 m of thick wood / steel shell. The reference ship is about 100 m, 40,000 m³; a few carbon-composite main longitudinal beams and high-load ring frames carry the hardest-to-remove main load paths, and basalt / heartwood / light-alloy secondary frames hang the cells, decks, and living spaces between them, while the large exterior is kept by thin composite / sandwich fairing. Visible heartwood may be deck, bulkhead, railing, face layer, and local secondary members; visible metal is mostly at hardpoints, machinery housings, wear-edges, and places people touch. From afar it can therefore still have that "wood-and-brass great ship" silhouette one imagines, while up close it continually exposes evidence of mass being saved: cavities, thin faces, light doors, narrow frames, tension lines, and joints that only suddenly become very heavy in critical positions.

**Class V large long-line ship:**
Effective main cell volume about 30,000–50,000 m³; all fuel / gas-fill sanity numbers continue to use the 100 m / 40,000 m³ reference ship. Total takeoff weight varies by model; reference remains about 40–50 t; useful payload is not a single "cargo-hold number" — part of it is objects, materials, and food entrusted at various places along the way, and part always belongs to people, bunks, water, kitchen, work rooms, ballast, and weather margin. The upper deck lays plank along the spine, sets railings and body-line points; the middle deck can sink half a level into a common corridor with portholes. Windows may be composed of small hand-worked panes from different firings and different places, with slight color differences, but the composition should be a consciously chosen shipyard design / a set-piece reorganized after a subsequent full refit — not "whatever we picked up over decades and jammed in."

The meaning of Class V is not "one trip saves a settlement," but that places thousands of kilometers apart may enter one another's everyday life over the long term: a precision part one place cannot make, a resin another has in inexhaustible surplus, a person going to see kin, a batch of seeds, and a few letters may all travel together. Any mature settlement can operate normally on days when no Class V arrives.

**Hundred-meter ship interior communication:**
On a 90–120 m hull, a small number of speaking tubes, bell pulls, engine-order bells / mechanical telegraphs may connect helm, forward and after deck, and propulsion / pressure spaces. They connect only a few fixed positions — a physical consequence of "this ship is too long, a shout does not always carry through." They differentiate naturally by local shipyard and crew habit; internal communication is always point-to-point physical linkage, not a departmental phone system.

**Aerial handoff:**
Formal Class V berthing exists only at T3 and above — so a long-haul Class V passing many T0/T1/T2 places without a Class V berth simply cannot land there — among them mature towns of thirty or forty thousand people like Atheria, Riverbend, Skyleaf. That missing link is supplied by local small craft: skiff / lighter / Class I/II rise to meet her. The Class V nickname "sky village" first names life density: someone lives there long-term, someone rides only two meals, someone gets off midway, someone goes to see kin, and someone always keeps a spot for someone at a distance. On deck there will be one afternoon's warmest lee corner; in the common corridor a bench someone always claims; the galley's heat runs out one particular door; children, apprentice, and segment-riders quickly learn where to sit, where not to stand in the way. It lets places thousands of kilometers apart enter one another's everyday, without turning any mature settlement into a dependency node waiting for a big ship to save its life.

Speed parameters:

| Type | Cruise km/h | Max km/h | Notes |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Class V (90–120 m; reference 100 m) | 35–50 | ~60 | 40 km/h average × 10–12 h/day = 400–480 km/day |
| Class IV | 45–65 | ~80 | Heavy regional / long-haul; often the vehicle of great-range configuration |
| Class III | 55–80 | ~100 | Medium-distance, multi-day regional lines |
| Class II (Anna's Gust) | 60–90 | ~110 | |
| Class I / courier boat | 90–120 | 130–150 | Sacrificing payload / living space for speed |
| Skiff · everyday | 20–40 | — | Bicycle-speed band |
| Skiff · racing | — | 80–120 | Thread Run canyon-wall skimming |
| Razor-class high-performance skiff | — | 120–150 | Short-range burst |

Skiff stall speed ~15 km/h — below this speed aerodynamic lift is insufficient, and cells in the frame-wing take over. This is the safety floor that allows a 10-year-old to fly a skiff.

Airship ceiling — why Marneth cannot be crossed: full-load operating ceiling ~2.5–3 km, absolute light-load ceiling ~4 km. Marneth crest > 5 km, higher than the ceiling itself — crossing Marneth is not "dangerous," it is physically impossible. Eastbound around Marneth's northern end, entering Skyleaf through the Gate, are the only two ways.

★ Transparency is by pane, not by area — Archeon has continuous glass production but has not preserved high-throughput float-plate industry; local furnaces are skilled at stable firing, blowing, and pressing small-to-medium panes. The limits come from flatness, annealing, transport breakage, and vehicle weight, not from glass feedstock running out. Vehicle side: the slow ones, those that fly in fair weather, are open; only the fast ones, those that push through headwind and rain year-round, are worth the small-pane composed window. Personal windbreak panes are called goggles. Buildings can have whole bright faces — window frames can be dense, can be heavy; a vehicle's frame must be light. This world leaves the big transparent panes to houses and the weather to those on the road — this is why goggles have become the pilot's identity object.

**A ship's lifetime:** a line-running ship has both a number and a name. The name can carry through very long spans, but the hull is not permitted to be dragged endlessly into wreckage. Regional and hub shipyards, on cycle, will replace envelope cells, redo outer layers, recheck main bones, replace fatigued sections, reseal wood and resin surfaces; a completed refit should turn the ship back into a coordinated whole. What is truly kept as "old" may be a still-strong long beam, a helm many have gripped, a hull proportion, an inner panel etched with years of routes, or a storage habit only this ship's crew still upholds. A ship's age therefore reads more like a house's history of additions, less like a wreckage stacked up.

**The inside of a Class II:** it is not written as four boxes — cockpit / engine room / cargo hold / bunk room. The upper deck has mooring posts, sail lines, and a pressure gauge, and a lee gunwale where one can actually sit in calm weather; the helm position is half-enclosed, blocking wind but not sound, and stays in direct relation with the deck; the galley is small, but its heat can travel through one door out onto the common face; a table can hold eating, chart-spreading, writing, and fine work at once, and be cleared to give the walkway back its space; hammocks and personal shallow lockers are pressed between the hull's longitudinal load lines; boxes and packs are arranged around the center of gravity rather than stacked into a warehouse look; the cell-inspection corridor is only entered into prose when the story needs it. A Class II is a moving life pressed into tens of meters of hull — home, street-side, doing-corner, and shipwork all folded in — not a scaled-down industrial facility.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.7 Technology · Buoyant Haulage and Short-Distance Movement (inside a settlement)
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Core principle: private ground vehicles are not the default; continuous rails are built only where terrain merits them; an ordinary day of life is highly near-neighbor; cross-district movement is carried by cable car, float rail, waterway, and airship. All systems that need to move weight try to let buoyancy carry the load and let a minimum of energy guide the direction.

① Skybridge walking — 90% of everyday short-distance movement. A three-dimensional network. Function overload: transit + laundry + stalling + socializing. Most people's ordinary days happen within a radius of about 500 m of home; this describes daily density, not that they have never left in a lifetime.
② Gondola cable car — medium distance + vertical difference. Open kiosk-style. Speed 15–25 km/h. The "clack-clack-clack" of the cable passing over station rollers is Frontier's signature sound.
③ ★ Float Rail — in local speech "the rail car." A basalt-fiber monorail; a hydrogen cell on the roof of the car brings the car near neutral buoyancy. The rail does not carry weight — it only constrains direction. Speed 25–40 km/h. Visual: a string of deep-green giant-silkworm-cocoon-shaped cars gliding slowly on a single overhead rail. It is a regional system worth building only where vertical difference, one class of heavy goods moving in the same direction year after year, and energy jointly justify it — it is not an irreplicable relic.
④ Pneumatic tube network — steam-pressure pneumatic mail, speed 30–50 km/h. For letters, small urgent parts, and Guild-registered duplicates / consignment slips.
⑤ Down-chute — gravity-driven, one-way down; common at mines, valley ports, and settlements with height difference. It is one branch of "letting weight walk itself."
⑥ Sky-Lighter — 2–3 m long, 1 person, foot-pedal + small steam-charge tank. Speed 8–15 km/h. A middle-class symbol. Anna does not have one.
⑦ Lift — vertical cage. Hydraulic / steam.
⑧ Pulley-basket — small items between neighbors, hung from cord and basket.
⑨ Narrow rail — a specialized niche. It mainly appears at the base of main skyports, in mines, in salt fields, and inside large workshop and port districts; it does not carry inter-city passenger traffic.

Frontier's evening sky is layered: in the near view, streetlamps and Broad Terrace crowds → between skybridges, skiff / sky-lighter skim briefly across, some only crossing one or two pylons → mid view, several Class I / II threading in and out of piers at different heights, each along its own altitude and direction → farther, a string of gondolas' warm yellow lamps moves along a diagonal, and a Float Rail slides slowly between two pylons → at the outer edge of Heavy Berth one or two Class IV/V silhouettes may occasionally be seen; the hundred-meter ship need not be there every evening, and when it is, no one stops daily life to gather and stare → at the highest pylon-tip, a signal lamp → across the sky's roof, Sky Fire's Remnant, the river of light. The overall feeling is **that both city and sky are being used by people.**

Settlement transit quick reference: Atheria uses lift + cliffside slope cable car + step-face sky-bridges + canal-bank narrow rail + stone-arch bridges. Iris Hollow has Float Rail + sky-lighter. Amber Reach is Float Rail-dominant. Riverbend is canals + walking. Terrain-limited settlements like Skyleaf run mainly on walking + pulley; larger populations do not automatically lay a flat road network.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.8 Technology · Work Vehicles
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Core principle: all Archeon ground vehicles are "composite structures with embedded buoyancy" — not zeppelins. Buoyancy cells are hidden inside wooden / alloy shells.

① Skiff-Plow — a single-person tilling unit. Buoyancy cancels 70–85% of the weight.
② Field Loafer — a mid-size platform for harvest season. Zero soil compaction.
③ Load Drift — a cargo box whose interior is 40% buoyancy cell. Thorn-goat provides direction and friction, not load-bearing. Short-distance 10–30 km, 4–6 km/h.
④ Plain Cutter — floats 30–50 cm above the field surface to harvest. A signature visual.
⑤ Canal Tender — a canal-maintenance floater. When crossing a levee it activates the cell to "hop" over.
⑥ Lift-Sled — used in mountains and terraced settlements.

Common identifying feature: they are not aircraft, not ground vehicles, but a third kind of thing — buoyancy as a "weight-management tool," not as a "means of flight."

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.9 Technology · Skyport Physical Capacity Grades
----------------------------------------------------------------------

T0–T4 indicate only what a berth can actually accommodate and how deep a maintenance it can carry out; they do not indicate "what role it plays in the network."

The physical basis of size continues: airship's mast-turn / tow turning-circle diameter, author-side ≈ 2.2 × ship length. Class V is now locked to 90–120 m, so any place truly able to "receive Class V" must, in at least one berth direction, provide about 200–270 m of safe clearance; that space can come from riverbank, valley floor, sea-edge, below a mesa, a plains anchor field, or the outer Heavy Berth of a T4 city, and does not have to come from a high tower top.

| Grade | What this place can actually do here | Common facilities |
|---|---|---|
| T0 | Let a small ship briefly stop, watch wind, tie up; no one guaranteed present | 1–2 ground anchor piles, wind sock / local equivalent, signal lamp |
| T1 | One Class I / II or smaller Class III can overnight; a person can shelter from wind, do shallow inspection and ordinary small-part work | 1 mast or 2–3 piles, shared awning, hand-cranked wind gauge, box-drop space |
| T2 | A few Class I–III can moor at once; can locally top up ordinary hydrogen loss, wait out bad weather, do regular ship maintenance and consumable replacement | 3–5+ berths, electrolysis top-up shed, low-pressure gas holder, inspection / ship-service shed, level dock |
| T3 | At least one real space can receive a 90–120 m Class V; can perform scheduled yard work, replace large components, let several groups of people stay for days | 8–15 berth network + at least 1 ~200–270 m class large-ship clearance / anchor position, large gas holder, shipshed / yard, Guild registry |
| T4 | A large city with multiple mutually distinct port districts, able to build / systematically refit Class V; large ships are usually at the city's outer edge / lower-level large-clearance berth, not required to squeeze into every pylon pier | Multiple port districts, Class V Heavy Berth, shipyards / shed halls, everyday life around them |

**Grade notes:**
- The grade definition contains only physical capacity, not network roles like "node / hub / junction."
- Space-names use concrete forms like "level dock / box-drop space / berth foreyard."
- T2/T3/T4 may become important places over time through people meeting there, but the grade itself only describes facilities.
- A small port may still be very important on one Thread; importance comes from relationships and geography, not from grade.

Tower skyport height rules, hydrogen-production capacity by grade, ballast, and gas holder see §4.9.3 / §5.8; this table concerns only grade and facilities.

Distribution rule: every named settlement is at least T1; today's Aethelgard main-story settlements are mostly in the 30–60k resident range, but port grade is still decided by real route function, not automatically upgraded by population. Towns of 30–60k people that carry two or three converging mature familiar lines are often T2/T3; a 40k place that mainly runs on its local river valley, forest land, or seasonal life may perfectly well maintain only T2. Unnamed secondary route towns are mostly in the roughly 1–8k range; off-line agricultural life circles and seasonal points may keep T1+ / seasonal T2. On main trunk routes, a T0 emergency anchor field every 50–100 km; on hard segments, a Windbreak-style relay every few hundred kilometers. The named ports locked into today's Aethelgard table are only the lowest visible layer; among about 35–55 secondary route towns, 700–1,000 villages / farm communities, and 250–450 permanent small stations, which of them hold formal berths is registered as regional tables come in. Any region with a stable 30k+ population or a continental transfer function must state where the nearest T3 / Class V position is.

Capacity phrasing: T1 lets a ship tie up, lets people disembark, tops up ordinary food and water, and hands a small amount along to the next segment; T2's electrolysis shed lets it top up airship hydrogen over the long term; T3/T4 carry Class V, deep refit, and bulk hydrogen production. A small port is not irrelevant to a long line just because it cannot receive Class V — the aerial handoff of skiff / lighter across a few minutes, and the interlocking of regional ships and long-haul, is the Loom's capillary. This layer is an organic capillary, not a transfer system.

**Port prose rule:** port grade describes physical capacity, and does not determine the space's mood. On entering a scene, ask first what one steps into off the gangplank: cliff steps, market terrace, longhouse side, tidal steps, orchard outer ground, valley-floor stone platform, wide steps beside the common kitchen, or only wind and a single awning. The port is not a settlement's "logistics organ" hung on outside; a mature port more often is the edge of local life just grown enough to let a ship come in. An arrival scene lets "shipboard life" and "ground life" join naturally.

Naming disambiguation: §4.7's Atheria terrain terms are Upper Field / Port Step / Home Step / Lower Field (Shelf series), not using T-prefix, and do not conflict with this section's sky-port grades (T0–T4). Atheria's main skyport is on Port Step; sky-port grade is T2.

Size-threshold sayings — "Anything that floats is bigger than you"; the cable-hauling chant / berth-pull chant; the launch ledge — a lip platform at the skyport's edge, where a skiff kicks off its mooring and trades a drop for airspeed;

Seven entries: borrowed buoyancy, leak whistle, warm wall, ship-words in the house, ride-along day, know-the-knot, return-the-tool.

Wind-mouth hand signs: at the berths and cliff edges, the wind is strong and shouting is worse than gesturing. The wharf has a small set of hand-sign vocabulary — slow, steady, release, enough. A child learns them before reading.

The value of lightness: a floating world anchors value in the light. "Is it worth flying?" is the touchstone for everything. A gift given to a flyer, the lighter the more decent.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.10 Society · Clothing
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Clothing is just clothing. Differences come from material, local dye, personal taste, and use environment, not from status assignment.

Everyday wear has three layers: a close-body cotton / linen shirt (light color), a functional work middle layer, and an outer wind- / waterproof coat.

Colors come from the earth: ochre red / clay red (the most common and cheap, from iron oxide pigment), indigo (more formal), yellow / gold (Quiver-Leaf bark), green (indigo + yellow blended, most laborious), black (many rounds of overdyeing, most formal).

Differences in clothing are functional choices: those who often work with oil, resin, ash, and abrasives pick heavy stain-resistant cloth; those who fly wear a windproof coat with a scarf because the wind on deck is strong; those who tend a furnace or forge add a thick leather apron because sparks really do burn. The same person wears different things on different occasions. Edmund wears that coat because he has that coat, not because of some uniform dress code.

**Correct aging of long-worn clothing:** seams get let out, waists get taken in, sleeves get pieced, linings get changed, cloth gets redyed, gets rewaterproofed, buttons get replaced; when it truly wears through, of course it can be patched, but a patch is only one maintenance act — not core aesthetic, medal, or identity language. A well-made old coat still looks first like a whole coat. Route textiles are covered separately in §6.6; they are marks of relationship, and do not carry patching function.
----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.11 Society · The Shape of a Day
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon has no single "ordinary day" that stands in for every settlement. The same sun rises, and different places wake to different things first.

Atheria's Home Step morning still begins with water and the inside of the house. The bread oven's back wall is warm all night; only after the first batch's scent has come out does someone push the window open; the lift baskets of the next step begin their clack-clack, Ishkar's bass running on beneath. Someone goes to the mill, someone to the orchard, someone pulls damp cloth up to the drying platform, and someone spends the whole morning at home doing things wholly unrelated to flying. Port Step doesn't really get busy till later; then the short motor noise of the first batch of skiff, and the lower propeller / fuel-cell hum of Class I/II, layer into Ishkar's waterfall bass. People who live here do not look up and count ships each time — precisely because what is in the sky is ordinary enough, it can become one layer of the soundscape, not the subject of every shot.

Riverbend's morning is cut open by the sluice gates. After upstream lets the water down, the water wheels do not wake simultaneously: one, far off, begins slapping water low; then a second follows. The people at the mill test flour damp; those living by the canal lift up the basket they pressed under a stone last night; by the common oven, someone is already waiting for the second batch. Whether or not the ship comes today does not change what this street does first.

Tidemark has no fixed "morning." The twin-moon tide cuts a day into stretches of when to go down to the flats, when to bring things in, and when to let the water return; some go to the tidal steps while it is still dark, some wait for their first hot food till the tide is out. At high water, last night's walkable stone path vanishes; at low water, a whole stretch of temporary ground reappears. An outsider who tries to find people only by their own clock will easily miss the busiest hours.

Hearthstone's first sound is often the stone gate opening water. Hot water refills the bathing channels, mist rises inside the windows, someone in the greenhouse wipes down glass, the kitchen of the longhouse guest house sets a pot going first. In cool season, some have just come off a ship to sleep, while others have already finished their first bath and are heading upland; the town is at its brightest, oddly, before dawn breaks — because the greenhouses and bathhouses have kept their lamps on all night.

A highland waypoint with only a dozen households is different again. When the wind is not right, the morning can be so quiet that only pots, water, and the eaves make sound; when the wind window suddenly opens, people scattered across drying yards, fields, and homes can all appear within ten minutes on the same slope. Here there is no such thing as "the morning gets busy."

A great city like Frontier equally has no single dawn call. Around the old processing tower one still hears the low exhaust; the weaving district first with the sound of the cloth frames pulled; the basin edge first with the smell of baked grain; Heavy Berth has not stopped all night; a residential terrace may only truly wake once the sunlight passes over the building in front. The dusk main-note is one layer of city background heard in common by many people, not a bell of all-city clock-off.

The morning on a ship is comparatively steady: the night-watch person writes pressure, wind direction, envelope temperature, and last night's changes on the chalkboard; early risers first drink something hot, then use ear, hand, and eye to reacquaint themselves with the ship. The deck wood is often still dewed, the gunwale metal is cold first, the galley end has already warmed; someone gets out of the hammock and hangs the coat in its fixed spot, someone claims the small table stretch closest to the window. Black Iron is common in Aethelgard; Vorhan, Keld, Duskreach can be entirely different — what people drink on the ship, what is in the pot, which curtain is in what weave, all may slowly change with the places a ship has long passed through.

Lunch need not be uniformized into "the workshop long table" either. In big settlements the long table is often where the news is densest, but the table may be in the back room of the bakery, the bathhouse kitchen, the orchard press shed, a house courtyard, a portside eating house, or a household's shady spot that happens to be free today. An edge station may only have three people around one pot; Tidemark may need to wait for a group to come back from the tide flats before eating together; when Skyleaf is harvesting flowers, lunch simply appears under a shed at the edge of the flower field. An outsider is waved to sit down because people here are eating, not because there is a unified "long-table lunch institution."

Nights branch equally. Some tell stories; some keep firing kilns; some go to bathe; some watch the tide; some just close the window and sleep. The nebula and the twin moons are a shared sky, not a shared schedule.

After Anna grows up, many kinds of "how a day begins" are lodged in her body: damp stone and a water wheel, salt wind, geothermal white steam, the sweetness of milled flour, mud and seaweed uncovered at low tide, the sound of the first pot lid touching a stone stove at some small station. When entering a new place, let the reader first meet what will really happen there first, and only then look for the name on the map.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.12 Society · Eating
----------------------------------------------------------------------

521 years — enough for a civilization to have developed regionally proud eating traditions.

Flour — modified wheat. Root vegetables — "Yellowhead."
Protein — rock-hen eggs, thorn-goat milk and cheese, River-runner fish.
Vegetables — "copper-leaf" and a variety of beans.

Regional specialties (Thread sample): Frontier City weaver's noodles.
Atheria Three-Fall Stepwine. Riverbend river-smoked fish. Skyleaf
high-protein grain fermented porridge + flower-field honey. Amber
Reach wind bread + seed-sifting system.

The above is only a Thread sample, not the global menu. Each new
region entering prose adds at least one staple preparation, one
preservation method, one festival food, and one ordinary ingredient
that only appears there in bulk; Keld's tide tables, Vorhan's altitude
and geothermal, Duskreach's heat and humidity and cloud steps must
actually change what people eat and how they preserve it, not just
change the dish name.

Food is a measure of distance. A meal on the ship is an edible map
of one route: last night's soup, home port's bread, pickles from the
previous stop, fresh fruit not yet finished from the one before that,
and occasionally a local sweet someone specially tucked in. Anna
tasting a bread she has never seen does not need to look at a
map — her mouth has already told her, "you are not in Atheria." Like
a settlement's long table, eating alone on a ship is also unusual.

**Abundance first:** when Anna arrives at a new settlement, the food
description writes abundance first, then shortfall. "What does this
place have in ridiculous surplus?" enters the frame before "what
does this place lack." Somewhere resin drips so much it sticks to
shoes; somewhere the wind is strong enough to line the whole roof
with wind-drying racks; somewhere hot springs are so many people
use them to wash tools; somewhere in a shallow bay a certain
foodstuff comes up in a single day faster than one can eat it. Trade
arises from surplus meeting, not from global poverty.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.12a World · The Biological World
----------------------------------------------------------------------

### CI's Selection Logic

CI's embryo payload was limited. Every species slot was optimized for utility / footprint. Selection criteria: multi-functionality (providing at least two of protein + fiber + traction + pollination + soil improvement at once), low feed competition (not competing with humans for grain), small genetic-bottleneck risk (a viable population buildable from a small founder population), high environmental adaptive resilience.

**Absolute exclusion:** large carnivores, parasites, mosquitoes/flies and blood-sucking insects, and highly invasive rodents (rats). CI does not gamble on ecological risk — a colony of only a hundred or so people cannot withstand a rat plague or an epidemic.

**Excluded are the harmful and the non-functional, not "the not-useful-enough."** CI's ecological model needs enough trophic diversity to sustain system stability — an ecosystem of only sheep, chicken, bee, and fish would not thermodynamically last fifty years. CI therefore carried dozens to over a hundred animal species: multiple freshwater fish (not only River-runner), multiple bird species (waterfowl, forest birds, small insectivores), multiple invertebrates (beetles, spiders, snails, soil arthropods beyond earthworms), frogs and small lizards (important links in wetland and riverine ecology).

**No dogs, no cats.** In CI's utility function, companion animals ranked below food production, pollination, and soil improvement. Archeon people have no cultural concept of "keeping pets." If Federation makes contact with Archeon in the future, this may be one of the smallest yet most confusing cultural gaps between the two.

**No horses or donkeys.** In the CI era robots did the heavy hauling, and no draft-animal transport was needed. After the robots went out of service (about Year 80+), thorn-goat took over some pack function — they were already right at home on cliffside paths — but there is no riding animal. Archeon people travel long distance by airship and skiff; they do not ride anything.

### Core Species (whole-Archeon distribution)

**Thorn-goat:** the descendant of a genetically modified goat. Its digestion adapts to local coarse vegetation, its hide is thick, and its horns grow short, hard, calcified spines. Bad-tempered. The most important draft animal and protein source. Its milk is rich and slightly saline; made into hard cheese, it keeps for over a year — airship long-haul standard ration. Almost every settlement raises them. Canyon-type settlements (Atheria, Riverbend) have the largest flocks; cliffside vegetation is natural pasture, and the goats come and go along walkways and cliff paths.

**Ridge-hen:** a modified fowl. Short and fat, thick-legged, wings so reduced they can only flutter but cannot fly. Their eggs are the most everyday source of animal protein. Almost every household keeps a few. Their call is not the crow of an Earth rooster — it is a short, throaty "cluck-cluck-cluck," one of the base layers of every settlement's morning soundscape. Atheria's ridge-hen are everywhere on cliffside houses' windowsills and walkway railings, and occasionally flutter down from a higher place onto a lower walkway, setting off a flurry of hen and person dodging.

**Bee colony (modified honeybee):** a key CI terraform species. After modification, extremely low aggression, no sting, more radiation-resistant (survival rate through Sky Fire was much higher than that of other insects). Hives are distributed across every settlement with farmland. Honey is Archeon's most precious source of sweetness — sugar is hard to grow. The flower-field honey of Skyleaf's basin floor is pale in color and rich in floral scent, and is acknowledged all across Archeon as the best.

**River-runner fish:** the fish of the Ishkar River and its tributaries. Smoked dried fish is an important protein-storage method. CI carried multiple freshwater fish; River-runner is only the one most often named in narration, because Atheria is directly linked to the Ishkar system. Other river systems have other names and other flavors.

**Soil microbiome + earthworm:** the invisible but most crucial terraform legacy. CI seeded soil-improvement microbes and earthworms before any other creature. Without them, Archeon's rocks would never have become soil that could grow grain.

### Geographically Specialized Species

**Canal-duck:** unique to Iris Hollow and plain settlements with paddy fields. A size smaller than an Earth duck, grey-brown, quiet. It eats insect eggs and water plants in irrigation ditches and paddy fields — natural biological control. Salted duck eggs are an Iris Hollow specialty. Almost never seen in Atheria — the canyon has no wide open water.

**Songbirds (2–3 species):** a few "non-directly-productive-function" species CI introduced. Reason for inclusion: CI's models had computed that long-term isolated small human communities' mental-health indicators positively correlate with soundscape richness. The grey-necked sparrow is most common — Atheria's cliffs have them everywhere, their call thin but pleasant. Cliff swallow builds nests along cliff faces, its flight-path a small-scale version of a skiff. In the river valleys a certain long-legged wader walks in the shallows. The three species cover most of what Archeon people hear in daily bird sound.

### 530-Year Divergence · Year 521's Living Ecology

Six hundred years after CI seeded (including five hundred years without management), a few founder species have already differentiated into biome-adapted variants. No one has done a global census — differences are what Anna encounters along the way.

**Thorn-goat's geographical face:** the variant on highland tabletops has longer, denser hair and is a stub shorter than the lowland ones, pushing its muzzle into tall grass rather than nibbling cliff shrubs. The variant of the dry hot hills has short flat hair, a smaller frame, and a worse temper. Coastal variants — if anyone has tried keeping them — are not on canon record but not ruled out. Different biomes' thorn-goat differences let Anna see "here is different" first from the animals in each place.

**Ridge-hen's local accent:** ridge-hen from different settlements already differ in size, plumage, and call. Atheria's cliffside ridge-hen are used to vertical life — falling from a height does not panic them, and a few flaps land them at the next rail. Frontier plains ridge-hen walk more, fly less. The kind near Iris Hollow are smaller-bodied but lay larger eggs.

**Bee-colony behavioral divergence:** stingless bee colonies have divergent behavioral subgroups along their nectar sources. Where highland flower seasons are short, bees show tight, concentrated windows; where Skyleaf's basin flower fields run through a whole season, bees are slow and diffuse. Settlements along certain routes exchange colonies to keep genetic diversity — done by beekeepers by experience, not by genetic testing.

**Small-animal invisible layer:** in the world Anna passes through in P2–P3, she should at any time encounter the presence of whitelist animals — they carry no narrative function, they are what should be in the air:

- **Birds are the living things Anna most encounters in flight.** On Class V decks a bird has landed, taken off, left a dropping and half a feather. Grey-necked sparrow are always around the market and the long table. Cliff swallow nest under the belly of skybridges. At skiff altitude, one occasionally meets a small hawk riding a thermal — it circles at the same altitude as the skiff. On seasonal-migration days, besides one's own ship there are many, many birds in the sky flying in the same direction.
- **Insects —** butterflies, dragonflies, beetles (including ladybirds), moths, crickets, fireflies, ants, water bugs. Moths butt at the hooded lamp at night; at dawn dragonflies skim the water; occasionally a beetle from some earlier stop crawls out of a crack in the deck.
- **Amphibians —** frogs (nightly calling by the ditch is the base noise; low-frequency chorus by the pond), salamanders.
- **Reptiles —** small lizards (already have grey slate lizards), small snakes (CI-introduced non-venomous species, rarely seen by people, but present in cliff cracks and grass roots).
- **Small mammals —** rabbit or rabbit-like (grass movement), small rodents (shadow at the corner — the CI-introduced kind does not eat grain, completely unlike the excluded invasive rat).
- **Fish —** river fish, lake fish, intertidal creatures. Freshwater fish are not only River-runner — different river systems have variants of different size and flavor, only that in daily life they are not particularly named.

### The Plant World · Six Hundred Years After CI Seeded

Phase B seeding (about 2478–2490) released several hundred plant species: pioneer herbs → pioneer trees → agricultural crops → climbers. Six hundred years later, CI's seeding lines have long since blurred — five hundred years of natural reproduction, wind-borne spread, water carriage, human deliberate or accidental transport have made the vegetation map far more complex than the original triage chart. People of Anna's era do not distinguish "CI planted it" from "it grew itself."

**Agricultural plants (already registered in §6A.12):** modified wheat, Yellowhead root, copper-leaf vegetables, various beans, fruit-tree series.

**Forest and belt:**

Archeon has no "primal forest" in the Earth sense — every tree is a descendant grown within six hundred years from a CI seed. But six hundred years is enough for forests to have differentiated into visually distinct biomes:

- **Heartwood belt (Thread's western edge → Iris line):** the descendant of CI's first-generation windbreak corridor. The forest belt sown five hundred years ago is long dead, but the successor forest continues to breed along the same line, and is now a mature dark hardwood belt tens of kilometers wide. In P3, the heartwood wall is the first edge Anna reads from the air. Heartwood is Archeon's most important source of structural timber.
- **Tremor-wood patches:** the descendant of accelerated-growth trees already 25–30 years old by Phase C. Leaves continually tremble in the breeze — from far away, the canopy looks like it is breathing. The most common tree species in the Thread core zone, alternating with farmland.
- **Highland low shrubs:** east of Marneth Belt and across the highland tabletops, UV exposure is stronger; drought-tolerant pioneer species CI seeded have grown into low dense shrubs. Not one species — a short felt-carpet layer formed by a dozen together. From flight altitude it is only a color difference — grassland pale copper, shrub zone deep grey-green, boundaries following rock layers and drainage lines.
- **Coastal sclerophyll forest (Iris side):** salt-mist-adapted evergreen broadleaves — thick shiny leaves the wind does not stir. Approaching the coast from the highland, vegetation goes from dry grass to low shrub to this shiny dense forest, and the transition zone is the edge of Iris's fog blanket.
- **Canyon climber belt (Atheria type):** climbing plants in cliff crevices live on seepage — root systems very deep, the vine surface goldening off in cool season and greening back in warm. From the deck it is only stripes of dark down the cliff face. Part of the vegetables, herbs, and flowers for Atheria's forty-five thousand people grow on these cliff faces.
- **Old CI windbreak / water-storage belts:** cutting the continent in unnatural straight lines — easy to recognize from the air. Locals call them by local names; no one particularly thinks of them as CI.

**Grassland and flowers:**

- **Highland meadow:** the grass on high tabletops runs from light copper to deep gold, and wind traces bands of light and dark across it. In late-middle warm season, some grass species produce tiny flowers — mostly yellow, few white, petals small and broken, not beautiful individually but lining an edge with them.
- **Canal-side / field-edge wild layer:** field-bank weeds, ditch-wall fuzzy mosses, floating water plants, purple-flowered small herbs. Some night-blooming flower gives off a sweet scent at dusk that cannot be traced to any one plant. Around the anchor ring, from the trampled hard soil a low grass still squeezes through — no one plants it and no one pulls it.
- **Blue windbell:** Atheria's unique cliff-wall flower. Already registered on E-line — P1 pollen on the nose tip, P3 absence (not there in the distance), P4 the eye passing over it. A canon species, only here, not spreading.
- **Sunberry shrub:** on Atheria's sunward high cliff, produces deep-orange fingertip-size fruit for only a few weeks each year. Registered in P1.

**Vegetation as geographical signal in flight:**

Reading vegetation change from the air is part of Anna's N-line (reading the sky / reading the ground). By P3 she should at least have accumulated the following visuals:

- Continuous farmland breaks into terraces, green turning to grey-brown and bare rock;
- A stripe of dark green too straight — the descendant of an old CI windbreak (the heartwood wall);
- Greener inside the river bend than outside — where water has passed, vegetation is thicker;
- Approaching the coast, leaves go from thin to thick, from dry color to bright green;
- Highland meadow's light-and-dark stripes moving with the wind — from flight altitude, like a hand smoothing over a piece of cloth.

### Biological Lexicon · Overall Writing Rules

**Absolutely barred from narration, dialogue, and the design layer:** cattle, horse, dog, cat, donkey, mule, mosquito, rat, cockroach, locust, lion, tiger, rhinoceros, elephant, bear, shark, whale, stinging large hornet, large predators, parasites, and the "pest" family generic references. English equivalents are equally barred.

**Vocabulary openness is not species introduction:** Earth biological terms not on the blacklist may be used as generic labels or metaphor. Naming a new species still requires passing the canon gate. Unnamed insects, birds, fish, and grasses can be zero-load background — animals with no narrative function are not named.

**Statement:** Archeon's biosphere is extremely "clean." No mosquitoes, no flies (pollination is entirely borne by bees), no rats. Camping at night needs no insect protection. This sounds like heaven to Earth ears; to Archeon ears it is only "everyday." Animal diversity is far lower than Earth's — a quiet night is quieter than Earth's, no dog barking, no large-animal night-cries beyond frog. This is texture, not loss.

**Plant lexicon:** no blacklist. Earth plant varieties CI seeded exist on Archeon as local variants of Earth generic names. Prose is free to use "tree," "grass," "shrub," "flower," "moss," "vine," "fern," and other generic names to describe the environment, without naming each individual plant. Special varieties needing names (blue windbell, sunberry, Yellowhead, copper-leaf, tremor-wood, heartwood) are already registered as canon.

**"Not carried" is not "will never exist":** CI's original seed list is not the endpoint of the Archeon biosphere. Five hundred years of natural selection, human deliberate or accidental introduction (an insect that came along on a ship, a variety a settlement specifically bred), and natural divergence of CI-seeded species, have together produced an ecology richer than the original list, yet still on the sparse side.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.13 Society · Exchange
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Exchange is not one globally unified system. It is a gradient that changes with relationship density,
distance, and local surplus. Ask first "what is the relationship between these two people, and how far does this
thing have to travel," then ask how it settles.

Aethelgard's mature Thread ring (Frontier and the 30–60k familiar-line towns ringing it — Atheria, Crestline, Coppergate, Thornfield, Millhaven, Iris Hollow — and the mature
routes among them): great amounts of exchange between familiar people are not settled at the moment the action happens.
Today someone patches a stretch of your envelope cloth; tomorrow your household's long table has two more bowls;
a workshop keeps a place open for a ship that always runs this line, and next season that ship brings back the thing it needs, in passing.
Households, workshops, and ships that come and go often keep their own ledgers, but a ledger is first a way of remembering "who still owes whom what,"
not a way of converting every act into
a unified price.

A well-cared-for set of tools, a house that can still keep the fire going, a season of dry goods that will not spoil,
a relationship in which someone is willing to keep a berth and a bed for you — these look more like "having room to spare"
than a bag of copper.
An accumulated pile of copper subdivisions can make things easier for a person at the market, but is very hard to convert automatically into command
over other people's basic living — water, heat, ordinary food, and local energy do not open to a purse.
The prose therefore does not need to establish "hoarding coin" as a social problem; what really carries weight here is
whether one can complete a promise, whether a thing arrives on time, whether craft and relationship are still there.

Larger commitments often enter ledgers or "physical-goods credit." Salt, standard metal ingots, gasket crates, envelope batches
can all become settlement objects a region is willing to recognize; they can also be recorded directly as work-to-be-done, berths-to-be-kept, or
a batch of things to be supplied. A Guild's role only reaches as far as unified format, common seals, and traceability in dispute;
it does not do planetary clearing. A ledger keeps its weight at the next stop because
the people there recognize the workshop, port, ship, or person who signed the ledger, or recognize people who know them; not because
there is a whole-network account back office settling everyone.

Middle zone: copper subdivisions circulate more thinly. An Amber Reach workshop may take copper, but may prefer
to take resin and envelope material. Here exchange again becomes concrete: you have a gasket I really need; I have resin more than I know
what to do with; you help me get the roof through this cool season, I let you stay till the next line opens. Value is not
a number that exists apart from place; it always carries "what does this place have most of, what least, and how far is this thing from source."

Edge stations and transcontinental far ends: a certain kind of gasket that cannot be locally made,
brought from Keld, arrives at a new station in Duskreach, and those few boxes are worth more than any copper subdivisions — not because
they are abstractly more valuable, but because right now there is no other way to obtain them here. Exchange returns directly to one sentence:
"You brought what we needed; what do you have here that you can use on the next stretch of your road?" This is distance pressing abstract price back into geography.

Markets: the large settlements of the mature Thread ring have permanent markets, usually on the ground layer easiest to walk to.
Stalls are semi-fixed wooden frames and canvas; some families have kept the same spot for generations.
No one plans the whole market top-down into modern commercial zoning, but fire, smoke, smell, weight, moisture aversion,
water access, and human flow naturally cluster like things together: an oven is not stuffed into the middle of a stack of envelope cloth, and heavy metal is not
carried across the whole street each morning. The market therefore looks clustered without any administrative zoning map.
The season's local food, wood, salt, fiber, minerals, and the light-and-rare medicines, valves,
cloth, seeds, and news brought by distant ships mix together among these clusters.

A new station's "market" may be only a flat piece of ground beside the skyport, three sheets of canvas, and a few boxes. Someone lays out
what they have too much of, waiting to see who happens to need it today. Fifty years later it may grow into a row
of fixed stalls; maybe not — maybe this station is just right for three sheets of canvas.

**Scale of exchange:** do not write every exchange as a barter scene. First judge the scale:
- an extra hand-me-down lunch is usually not settled at all;
- for two strangers' small everyday purchases at market, copper subdivisions are simplest;
- for continuing back-and-forth between familiar people over several seasons, human favor and handwritten ledgers get involved;
- between ships, ports, and workshops for long-term commitment, harder physical-goods credit and witnesses are needed;
- transcontinental far-end exchange most weighs local surplus and whether this line can continuously be flown just now.
"Wealth" is preferentially written as sustainable capability and repeatedly delivered relationships, not preferentially as a pile of cash.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.14 Society · The Age of a Message
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Communication has no instantaneity; radio carries only coded short sentences, and detail goes on paper and on the mouth. Communication not only shapes emotion and social resource, but also relational structure.

Without instant communication, information has age. Two to four weeks between a letter's writing and reading is
normal; longer to far segments. A transcontinental message may take months.

Letters travel on the ships of familiar lines and on chance-passing ships. A letter, together with the things carried alongside it,
is usually wrapped in one cloth bundle:
a letter, a jar of pickles, a pair of shoes, a bag of seeds — someone happens to be going that way,
and takes it all along. Spoken message is more common than letter — a pilot passes a few words for someone, two or three
is enough ("all well").

The emotional face of a letter is unchanged: it carries the "this moment" of its writing —
by the time it arrives that moment is already the past. The reader's timeline and writer's timeline are not synchronized, but the letter itself proves
someone on the other side of the world is thinking of you.

Message as social resource: a pilot returning from Duskreach carries several months
old Duskreach on her. Her words have weight at the port — not because her position is high, but because
she brings the fresh outside world unavailable here. No one can know "Duskreach now,"
only "Duskreach then." A pilot's route experience is all the "some place then" accumulated on her body — that knowledge will age, become outdated, be overlaid
by later people, but at the moment of her arrival it is the freshest at the port.

Between "I saw it" and "I heard about it" there is a social-weight gap. Someone who says "the Keld
tide is still like that" and someone who says "I hear the Keld tide has changed" — regardless of
which is right, the former is usually asked two more questions first, because she can at least answer when, where, and how long she looked. A stranger's jacket lining, bag strap, or chart tube showing a stretch of unfamiliar weave will make bystanders guess "maybe she has been over there," not treat it as proof; what really carries weight is whether she can name the time, the place, and what she saw.

But "I saw it" does not mean "I saw right." A witness may have been there only half a day, may have
seen an abnormal day, may have misread another place with the logic of her hometown. Archeon
people, in judging a message, naturally ask its age and its route at the same time: "When was it?" "You saw it
yourself?" "Where did you see it?" "Who did you hear it from?" A ninety-day-old eyewitness account may sometimes be less useful than a three-day-old
secondhand message whose source chain can be spoken clearly.

Message therefore is not a true / false pair. It carries time, source, how many mouths it has passed through, and how long the speaker
was there. A careful person will say all of these together; someone who often turns "I heard" into
"I know" will, over time, naturally have her words weighted lower by others. There is no unified
fact-checking institution here; only a relationship network that remembers over time "how this person usually speaks."

Stories deform along the way. Something starting from Duskreach, passing through three ships, two ports'
chalkboards, and one old pilot's mouth, is another story by the time it reaches Atheria. No
one is lying — distance and retelling are themselves deformation. Some stories' propagated version is more interesting than the original.
Some key information loses its most important word on the way.

Message carriers are layered by use, not lined up on a simple speed table. Metal etching is most
permanent but extremely rare, in the CI-legacy-grade technical-record category; ink letters are slow, but preserve detail,
tone, and warmth; chalkboard notes are public, brief, visible to anyone and erasable by anyone; spoken message
is most common, fastest to relay, and easiest to deform in retelling. Sailing warnings, weather codes, and
a few operational shorts may travel by radio, but subject to distance, relay, weather, and bandwidth,
they cannot replace paper letters. The everyday world runs on spoken message, chalkboard, paper letter, and short code.
In a world of write-then-erase, a letter kept is heavier than words on a chalkboard.

Narrative interface — the delayed-fragment mechanism: in a world without instant communication, "knowing" is a thing
with a speed. A person's decline can precede any direct conversation, seeping in fragmentary form
along the routes into another person's life — "haven't seen him this year" "last I saw him was —"
"your dad hasn't been out much lately." Each fragment is not an alarm. Even added together, they
do not constitute a firm judgment. But they sit in a person's body, waiting for the day
one direct look lights them all. Message's delay is not merely inconvenience — it is
the shape of relationship and loss in this world. News of a death may take two weeks to travel to another
settlement. An "all well" may arrive after the person who wrote it is already gone.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.14b Society · The Shape of Relationships Shaped by Message
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Message is a social resource; its delay also shapes how Archeon people understand relationship, identity, presence, and loss.

A person exists in the world simultaneously in many outdated versions. The Edmund Anna knows at this moment
is not the Edmund Iris Hollow knows, and not the Edmund some station three hundred kilometers away knows.
People in one place still say "Edmund? Last time he came he even tuned that pump for us" — but
that "last time" may already be two years ago. A person on the Loom
is not a synchronously updated entity, but a series of delayed echoes propagating along the route.

After Edmund dies, this becomes suddenly cruel: some places already know he is dead.
Some places still think he simply did not come this year. Places further out even still hold on to "next time
Edmund passes, let him look at this." Death is not a global-simultaneous state change.
A person dies in different parts of the world at different speeds.

Likewise, a person's reputation runs ahead of or behind the person. When young Anna
comes to an unfamiliar node, someone hears Freedman: "You're Edmund's kid?"
Who they know is the Anna others spoke of years back. Or the reverse: on her first
arrival somewhere, the person there has already heard she did something on another line. Anna: "Who told you?"
The other thinks a while: "Can't remember. Some ship from the east mentioned it last cool season."
Reputation on Archeon is not a profile, but a slow weather that diffuses along routes.
It cannot be fully controlled.

Silence has no single interpretation. Not receiving a letter may mean: he did not write. He wrote, but the ship
did not sail. The ship sailed, but re-routed. The letter is stopped at a station waiting for the next segment. The seasonal line closed.
The letter carrier forgot to hand it over. He thought someone else was already carrying it for him. Even two letters may be crossing each other
in opposite directions. So absence of message ≠ message of
absence. Archeon people may, more than instant-communication world people, refrain from taking "no reply"
directly as rejection. But it also means when something has really gone wrong, silence itself cannot
immediately raise an alarm.

That is why the disappearance of Kassia's parents is especially terrible. At around twelve to fourteen, time and route were already
very ordinary domestic vocabulary for leaving home: which day they left, how many days later they should normally be heard by which mast,
after a missed weather window roughly which way they would detour, how late is late enough to truly worry.
These are not numbers she uses to fend off the world. As a young child, Kassia counted because at some point the count would end,
and her parents would come home. Time gave the far away a shape.

So for the first days, even the first weeks after they were out of contact, everyone had reasonable explanations. What she first asked was not
"are they dead," but:
"Who last saw them with their own eyes?"
"When was that?"
"Has this stretch been searched?"

What really changed her was not one bad piece of news, but that after the expected days had passed, new reasonable explanations kept
extending beyond: they may be wind-held, may have rerouted, may be waiting for the next segment. No one was lying to her,
but the words meant to reassure kept moving with the waiting. What did not move was the time of last eyewitness, the signals actually received,
the search segments already flown, and the stretch still empty.

From then on, she less and less could tolerate people, for the sake of comforting, turning "may" into "will,"
and cared more and more whether a thing was eyewitness, retelling, or only a guess that sounded very much like an answer.

Crossing conversation. Anna writes "I'm going to head south." Edmund on the same day writes on the other side
"don't take the southern line in cool season, wind isn't right." Both letters are moving on the Loom at once. By the time Anna
receives it, she may already have gone, come back, and had something important happen. What she reads is someone from the past,
trying to change a future that has already happened. The order of letters may invert: the one that arrives first is not necessarily the one written first, the one written first is not necessarily the one that arrives first. The reader must restore the timeline herself.
Archeon's communication naturally has a kind of conversation
archaeology — not the archaeology of CI, but of each other.

Promises also have age. A letter saying "next time you come I'll keep a place for you," a promise of "let's ride together in cool season," a sentence "I still want to see you" — all set out truly from the moment they were written; but by the time they arrive,
the weather, the route, the family, even the writer herself may have changed. Receiving an old promise does not automatically
impose the past on the present. Archeon people are very used to distinguishing two sentences: "he meant it when he wrote it,"
and "does this still count today."

This makes a kind of physical latency exist within relationships. Two people may both have not lied, may not have intended to
hurt each other, only each made reasonable decisions in a different "now." Many misunderstandings are not communication
