failure but communication travel time. What a relationship requires is not to be always synchronous, but that when
two versions of each other from different eras finally meet, they are willing to explain again today's self.

The "I" in a letter is often no longer there. This does not even require death. A fourteen-year-old
kid writes "I'm sure I'll never fly long lines." Half a year later that letter reaches someone's
hand, and the writer has moved with the family onto another line. The letter preserves not only information
but a person's older state. Someone you love sends "I miss you very much" — but that
was written by a him from sixty days ago. You cannot be sure whether today's him still feels that way. But that
sixty-day-old missing was completely real.

"Who brought it" becomes part of the letter. "A pilot from the northern line brought a letter for you"
already implies: she has seen the writer, she knows the weather over there, she may have two or three sentences she
didn't write down. Real communication often becomes three-layer — the letter itself, the carrier's
spoken addition, the local person's interpretation. Miriam writes "Edmund is staying home more these days."
The carrier arrives and casually adds "he looked tired." Passing through a third person it becomes
"I hear Edmund isn't so well." To Anna it becomes "your dad hasn't been out much lately."
This is not a rumor system. This is normal human relay.

The ethics of carrying letters. If you are going along that line anyway, carrying a very light envelope for someone costs
almost nothing. So it easily becomes the most everyday civic behavior — seeing
someone in need, casually carrying the relationship one segment further. Not heroism. Just an ordinary social
convention. Some very plain habits: do not open other people's sealed letters. On arrival, hand the letters over before
handling your own transactions. If the route changes temporarily, hand the letter to a trusted person to carry on. If
the recipient is no longer there, mark down when and who told you, and do not just discard it. Some letters cannot be spoken; only their recipient can accept them.
Some ordinary spoken messages can be written in the corner of a chalkboard,
to wait for a familiar face to pass. The Loom's social trust is not an abstract institution but thousands of people every day
willingly completing tens of kilometers of relationship for those who are not present.

Junction is a place where time piles up. A far junction is not only where things pass between ships and people;
it may also hold: letters waiting for someone, letters where no one remembers where the writer is now,
spoken messages for a pilot who did not come this year, names crossed out and rewritten, a piece of paper with
three different dates on the back. An old junction's message wall is in fact the sediment of the whole place's past
few months. Anna can tell at a glance who has been running lately, who has re-routed,
who is being sought, who has not answered anyone in a while. Even "a name appearing less and less often" itself
can be a person quietly withdrawing from this net — no exposition required.

Relationship itself becomes geography. A relationship has its own route health.
If a route gradually shrinks: two people did not fight. No one decided to grow apart.
Yet meeting is harder and harder. Spoken messages slower and slower. Mutual acquaintances fewer. In the end
the relationship really does fade. Conversely, a new Thread opening may suddenly turn two
originally distant families into regulars. Connectivity does not only decide economy.
Connectivity decides who has the chance to continue being a person in your life.

The Loom certainly carries things in motion, but that is not its deepest definition. What it truly sustains is continuity: an absent person
can still continue to exist in your life; a change at a far place still has a chance to reach you
before it entirely expires.

A jar, a bag of seeds, a part, a letter, and a person riding two stops may occupy the same small piece of one ship's
space, but they do not form "logistics." They form: who still keeps a place for whom,
who still remembers there is someone waiting at the next stop, who is willing to join their segment onto someone else's next segment.

And what weather, distance, route change, and death do is continually pull at that continuity.
Edmund's ring is contracting, and he too is contracting; his body reaches those places less and less,
his messages still reach them for him. After his death, a few last shreds of Edmund
still keep moving on the Loom.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.15 Society · Strangers and Trust
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Inside Aethelgard's mature Thread ring — most people and most people
are separated by two or three layers of relationship. Someone comes off a ship; the port has someone who recognizes his face,
or recognizes a color of cloth on his coat. No one needs to introduce themselves —
a community grown over five hundred years works this way: you arrive, someone waves you to sit down,
and after eating you help with the work. A ten-year-old kid rides a familiar-line ship to somewhere she has not been,
and the locals naturally set out one more bowl.

Middle gradient — the edges of the mature Thread ring and cross-region lines. They do not know you, but know the knots
on your ship. Do not know you, but know your port. Do not know your name, but seeing a piece of cloth
on your coat, they know who has vouched for you. Do not know you, but you have carried a letter for someone, and that name
they know. The trust is not yours — it is your network's behind you. Each layer of indirect
recognition moves a stranger from "we don't know who you are" toward "you probably have some relationship
with some place."

These clues are evidence, not credential. Knots can be learned, cloth can be borrowed, a port name can be gotten wrong,
what a good person said for you five years ago may already no longer apply. They will not turn a stranger into a "certified
approved person" instantly; they only decide how much risk the locals are willing to share with you first. Recognizing your port
may be enough for one extra bowl at the table, not enough to hand you the key to a main valve.

The global route has broken this. Now there are true strangers — someone from
Duskreach knows no one in Aethelgard. The cloth on their coat is a color and weave Aethelgard
people have never seen. The accent is different. The food is different. The way they hold a tool
is different. "You build differently from us over here" is, inside Aethelgard's mature Thread ring, an
interesting regional difference; at continental scale, it becomes "I'm not sure whether you can
touch my equipment."

At the far edge, if none of the above indirect recognitions can link up, trust starts from low-risk shared life.
First one more bowl, first a place to sleep; the next day help lift a beam together, press a leaking cell,
carry a pump, or be let, in front of others, to touch a small task that will not kill anyone. What people watch is how you handle a tool,
and whether you will admit you have not seen this rig before, whether you put it back when done, whether
in error you first hide it or first speak up.

"Watching your hands" does not turn mechanical skill into a person's moral exam. Someone who cannot fix a machine can be seen just as clearly:
how you care for someone worn out, how you treat a borrowed bowl, how you relay a message you are not sure of,
whether you stop when someone says "no." "Doing a thing for someone to watch" truly measures not
skill score, but what kind of person you are inside shared consequences.

The first layer of trust is small. On a second meeting, someone may be willing to lend you a better tool; later, let you stand a night watch alone,
carry a letter that cannot be lost for him, touch an appliance whose failure would delay a whole family; later still, someone will be willing
to speak your name in another place. There is no moment called "identity authentication complete." Only after many small risks have
not been wasted do people gradually become willing to share bigger consequences with you.

Trust can fail, be misjudged, be dragged down by old information. Someone with truly no relationship can also enter the Loom from zero. Textile pieces, knots, and Guild marks are always only evidence, never a passport, ID, or reputation score.

**Information friction:**
Messages have age, and local experience has a scope of applicability, so people naturally argue over "which record is still trustworthy now."
Such disputes do not build a unified credibility ladder, nor require a whole-Archeon verification system.
The most common handling is in fact very plain: fly it again, ask another person who has been there, keep both mutually
contradictory records, wait for the next season, first do it once by the less risky method.


Regional differences are not only flavor. Five hundred years of independent development is enough for people on different continents to grow structurally
different logics on the same craft. Aethelgard's pressure-system wrights are used to listening
first, touching later; people on another continent may be used to measuring first, calculating later. Neither is right nor wrong — each has grown
its own method from its own terrain, materials, and failures. One value of a walking-shuttle person
is bringing "how it is done elsewhere" as they walk between different logics.

Language: globally shared English — a legacy left down from CI's teaching materials, metal plates, and Atwood's ledger.
But more than five hundred years is enough for accent and usage to diverge. Inside Aethelgard's
mature Thread ring the differences are small — Frontier and Atheria people hear each other naturally. Farther,
differences grow: vowels differ, some word usages drift, sailing and piping terminology may be entirely different
on different continents — the same wind is called by different names in two places. No language needs
to be invented, and this need not be systematized. In prose, let it appear naturally: someone says a word, Anna
does not understand it, the other switches to another phrasing, and then continues.

Order: everyday disputes are handled through social pressure — doing something bad becomes known to those around you very quickly; some no longer
willing to exchange with you, a skilled hand not willing to let you follow her hand, a ship not willing to hand the next segment's responsibility to you,
and some may still be willing to give you a chance to redo it right. Serious matters are handled differently by place:
some places put you to high-risk public labor to work off the debt; some pass your name along the routes —
in substance, cutting you off from this network. The latter in Year 521 records totals no more than a few dozen cases.
There is no global unified punishment name or rank — each place calls this by its own word.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.16 Society · New Places
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The Loom is still expanding.

On the maps of Anna's era there are old coordinates, dashed lines, seasonal lines, rumors, out-of-contact beacons,
and charter flights flown only once. An old pilot's map is not only older; often it is larger.
At least one continent is still survey-incomplete. Many CI points have never been rechecked.
The map changes each year. A relay that had only a number five years ago has this year a name
and children.

CI's terraform has given Archeon a world already alive for close to six hundred years. Grass that has grown
for close to six hundred years is grassland. Trees that have grown for close to six hundred years are forest. A riverbed carved for close to six hundred years is
"the river that has always been here." What humans do on top of it is not restoring a civilization — it is growing their own things
on a lush, beautiful, living world.

After nearly six hundred years, the boundary between CI "artificial" and nature is no longer worth pointing out day to day by the locals. Some places one can still see the original; more places one can only see the consequences.

A platform on a mountainside has long since lost its construction purpose, yet because it is so unusually level, for hundreds of years it has served in turn as drying yard, market, seasonal stage, and temporary mooring. Someone later found it was probably a CI level pad; the locals heard the news and the next day were still drying cloth there.

A stretch of forest has no old facility to be found, only that its tree age, species mix, and snow line are unlike the neighboring valley. It may have begun with a Phase C pioneer belt, but what really shapes today's wind, cold air, and understory crops is five hundred years of root systems, humus, local harvesting, and regeneration.

A region's houses tend to use similar spans. The earliest reason may have been an old foundation module; later, roofs, materials, uses have changed a dozen rounds, but the proportion has entered craftsmen's eyes and hands. Nobody calls it "CI standard."

Somewhere's glass has a blue-grey no other place has. The starting point may have been just a very early ore assay and a smelting judgment; five hundred years later it is already street windows, bottles, lamp shades, and local aesthetic. Technology history has become visual culture.

An ordinary orchard's trees may be distant descendants of some trial orchard. The original orchard is long gone, and the fruit has been selected over hundreds of years into another flavor. Around it have grown baking, brewing, dyeing, harvest seasons, and one Thread that passes fixedly by each year. People did not inherit a CI tree; people and tree have changed together for five hundred years.

Even weather experience can be legacy. A pilot only knows "past that patch of deep green, descend a little later, cold wind will come down." That sentence has passed through eight generations. No one needs to know whether that wind was originally related to some terraform tree belt long since swallowed by forest.

So the most important question is no longer "is this natural or artificial," nor even always "is this CI or human." A place after long enough time — its history enters sizes, flavors, wind, habits, and names; the shape of the whole place is the legacy.

The culture of a new settlement is not a copy of any single source. A place that has grown up beside an old seasonal stop
long abandoned, thirty years old, with residents from three or four different regions. Its
food is mixed — one person brought Atheria's flour method, another brought
Amber Reach's seed-sifting method, and together they collide into a new bread neither side
knows. Its festivals are invented — someone proposed a town anniversary, everyone stood there
and negotiated a bit, and that became the place's first tradition. Its customs are negotiated —
whose method is authoritative? whose tools work best? try first and see.

Such a place is not "without culture" — it is where culture is being born. Children grow up
here: their culture has no source region — this place is their source region.
The first generation "who don't know what else is like." One day a pilot takes one of them
onto a ship — when that child first sees a large settlement of the Thread,
what she sees is not "civilization" — it is another kind of unfamiliar.

When a stop becomes a place, no institution announces it. At first perhaps only three people at a wind gap
put up shelters, and passing ships came to know the spot as "that flat patch." Then someone stopped there every cool season; someone built a hearth they weren't going to tear down; someone who came back the second year found their cup still on the same shelf; someone was born there; someone said "back home" for the first time and meant this place. By that point it was already a settlement, and it did not need anyone to issue it a founding certificate. Names often grow the same way: first a direction word, a well's name, someone's nickname — and after enough people say it long enough, no one remembers which day it became official.

Settlements die too, and not always to disaster. A well's flow slowly changes; a Thread shifts its season; the young leave with their partners for somewhere else; the ship that used to stop every week comes only twice a year. In the end all that's left is a seasonal shed, the name still alive on the map though no one really lives there anymore. Decades later, that name may survive only as pilot slang for a crosswind or an old anchoring ground. The Loom's growth is not just adding points at the edges — it is constantly turning old points back into geography.

For a pilot, these new places are proof the world is still growing. You come to a place that has only coordinates on the map and find someone already there, with sheds up, pipes hooked in, the first row of vegetables planted — or you find that there is nothing there at all, only wind, nearly six hundred years of grass, and a row of fruit trees still bearing along a street that no longer exists. Both discoveries are good.

Settlements change in far more directions than "small to large."
A young place: your first visit, only two households and a patch of freshly tried terrace; a few years later someone has extended the roofline, a public hearth has appeared next to it; some years after that, Anna is already hearing people complain the docks here are too crowded.
An old place brought back to life: your first visit, only one household remains, the old fruit trees still in rows along a street that vanished long ago; a few years later a new seasonal line passes through again, and shelters go up in the empty ground; some years later, no one could tell from the present layout that this place once flourished, declined, and lived again.
Big can become small, old can become empty, empty can become new, and after a migration a place can grow again; the same settlement can move two or three times and still keep the same name. A place you return to is not necessarily the same size each time, and growth is not necessarily a young settlement's upgrade animation.

Archeon has both old places and a young map at once. This combination is very rare.
It is not the American West, "entering unpeopled land." It is not Europe either, "every inch of soil with two thousand years of history." Rather: the land is very old, and people have been here for a long time, but the connection between people and land is still growing. The forests are old forests, a village may have been lived in for a dozen generations, a Thread may be three hundred years old, a bridge may have been rebuilt twenty times. But at the same time, new settlements are still being born, new routes are still being named, old routes still die, some continents' inner networks are only just beginning to weave dense again, and globally there remains an entire region that outside charts provisionally record as FAR-5, survey-incomplete:
The Loom does not yet have a commonly recognized inner map, nor a shared cross-regional collective name for it; this does not mean nobody lives there, and it certainly does not mean the people there don't have their own place names.

"Unknown / unnamed / unmapped" is always relative to a specific someone. Aethelgard charts may have no name for it; the locals may have had a name for a long time. The Loom may not have verified it; there may well be a complete society there.

To put it another way: this is not post-apocalypse, and it is not really "civilizational rebuilding" either.
It is a planet whose gardener turned the soil ahead of time and then left five hundred years ago — and humans went wild on it themselves. CI does not maintain civilization for Anna's generation; it removes for them the layer of "starting from zero." What the old civilization did has already sunk too deep into the causal chain — you can no longer tell where it ended and where the present people begin.

Each continent is a complete world. A local who never leaves for Aethelgard in their whole life still has a complete and rich life, and does not feel they live at the edge of the world. So when Anna goes to Keld, it is someone from a complete world arriving in another complete world.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.17 Society · Music and Sound
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Strings — thorn-goat gut and Quiver-Leaf wood. Percussion — metal drums, wood drums, tubular bells. The Steam Whistle Organ grows out of branch lines of the steam pipe system; the largest is in Frontier City. Other regions have entirely different sound frameworks, to be registered when they enter prose — do not treat Frontier's sound as a planetary template.

There are work songs, sailing songs, hawker's songs, drinking songs — and songs that are completely useless: love songs, ugly heartbreak songs, nonsense children make up themselves, songs that have lived two hundred years just because one rhyme was too funny to lose, coarse songs no one will sing until they've had too much, old songs where no one remembers what the first verse was about but the chorus is easy to sing. An abundant society allows art to waste time; a song can be a song, with no hidden practical function.

Many sailing and work songs really do encode practical information — wind direction, old route markers, harbor characteristics, the sequence of a stretch of water. The same song will drift between regions, and the information inside it will drift too. So songs are not authoritative charts. Some old verses still sing about a line broken a hundred years ago; a young pilot will change the words there, or may deliberately keep the old ones, only because their grandparents always sang it that way. A song can at once be a tool, an error log, a local accent, and a historical fossil.

When entering a new region, "how the people here make unnecessary sound" is itself evidence of cultural depth. Music is far more than a navigational tool, and every region has its own sound framework, not necessarily akin to Frontier's Steam Whistle Organ aesthetic.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.17b Society · Play, Pictures, Weddings, and Things Around the House
----------------------------------------------------------------------

§6A.17 already established that "an abundant society must allow art to waste time."
This section extends the same principle to ordinary daily life: Archeon people should not always be repairing, flying, trading, rescuing, or debating routes. Much of the time, they are just whiling away an afternoon.

This section is entirely texture material, not a global folklore table.
Each region takes one or two categories from it and, from local materials, weather, and people, grows its own version;
different settlements have different games, weddings, nicknames, and jokes.

**Jokes and nicknames.**
Friends can end up with a nickname for a few years or a lifetime because someone always puts the cup back in the wrong place, because a certain ship makes a nasty sound in cold weather, because of a particularly embarrassing landing, because a certain kind of bread was baked so hard it turned to stone, because an accent mangles a place name in a peculiar way. Nicknames are not identity titles, and they don't need to hide a life-wound. Some exist only because everyone laughed once when someone was thirteen.

The same goes for local ribbing. Neighboring settlements can dislike each other's bread for being too damp, their roofs too flat, their knots ugly, their black-iron too weak, their windbags hung out like laundry; cross-regional crews will make fun of each other's arrival habits. This teasing is part of everyday familiarity, unrelated to regional hostility. Strangers, in fact, are usually more polite.

**Bet on the weather, but keep the stakes small.**
At the docks, along the long tables, on long-haul ships, people bet on whether the wind will shift tomorrow afternoon, whether this rain will pass before supper, which windbag will flip first. The loser washes the dishes, gives up the first sweet biscuit, takes a boring shift for someone else, mends a cuff, or stands a round of black-iron. It gives people something to say about the weather they are all waiting through. The stakes are always small — Archeon has no gambling houses, no debt spirals, no class that lives by betting on weather.

**Games are allowed to be lousy.**
Dice, dominoes, grids scratched into a table's edge, long-haul games whose rules are passed only by mouth, improvised games made from knots and small pieces of wood — all of them can exist. Rules drift between ships, families, and places; some house rules are obviously unfair, but no one changes them because "we've always played it this way." A game can be purely a game. Children and adults alike can spend an hour on a stupid game that does nothing at all for civilization.

**Children take the world and play with it, but not every game is education.**
They can make weird knots from bits of old rope that won't hold a ship at all, draw entirely wrong "world maps" in the dirt, race homemade glider toys of cloth and wood to see whose crashes into the wall first, invent ridiculous names for far-off airships, race leaves down the ditches after a rain. Occasionally these games teach them a real skill; more often they are just play.

**Pictures live on the surface of daily life.**
Hand-drawn maps can of course be beautiful, but they don't get elevated into "the most important visual art of the whole civilization." Small pictures on a hull, notches on a door frame, printed woodcut stories, temporary festival flags, a lopsided child's drawing of Sky Fire's Remnant, a face someone in a workshop kept sketching for ten years on the wall without wiping off, a not particularly skilled portrait hanging in someone's home — all can exist. Pictures don't need to be institutionalized into museum culture, nor do they all need to carry practical information.

**Weddings vary by place.**
Over five hundred years, of course people marry, split, marry again, live together, move to the other's place. There is no ceremony common to all of Archeon. A wedding in one specific place might simply be: the long table longer than usual, people from distant routes arriving late one by one because of the weather, someone singing an ugly song, the household bringing out something they don't normally use, a friend giving a very practical tool, another friend deliberately giving something completely useless but beautiful.
Marriage does move recipes, tools, cloth, accents, and practices between regions along with the people, but marriage is first of all going to live with another person; the things just come along.

**Tools and household objects keep on living, but do not automatically become an heirloom plot device.**
§6A.18b already established that the dead's belongings are not gathered up and moved out. To refine it further:
a specialized tool may end up in the hands of the person who actually knows how to use it, not necessarily a blood relative;
the handle of an everyday jug is polished bright by generations of palms, the glaze paler than the base;
a table's edge carries the height-notches of several generations of children;
a heavy quilt has had its lining changed and its edging re-dyed over twenty years and is still the quilt everyone recognizes;
the oldest thing in a house may be a bowl everyone thinks is ugly but no one can bring themselves to throw away.

The point of this continuity is ordinary possession:
things stay because someone keeps using them, not because they have been declared "relics."
Family history is best hidden in the objects no one bothers to introduce.

Games, weddings, and nicknames are the background hum of life, not a tourism checklist. Useless activities are allowed to be simply useless. Local ribbing is a mark of familiarity, unrelated to hostility. Family inheritance is tied together by use and care — knowing how to use it, being willing to look after it, and just happening to have been here all along can matter more than a surname.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.18 Society · Flying
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The pull of flying isn't speed. It's that everywhere you arrive is new.

An old pilot who has run a mature Thread for years and a young pilot doing survey out on the edge don't see the same sky. The old pilot finds each year's tiny changes on the line she has flown for twenty years — that tree is taller than last year, that station has two more houses, the wind through a certain gorge is off by five degrees this year. Her world is no smaller than the younger pilot's; she just measures largeness differently: she has come to see time as a kind of terrain. The young pilot is finding a first path through country she has never seen — everything she looks at is a first time, and nothing has a name yet. Her world is fearsomely big, so big that sometimes she isn't sure whether she is exploring or lost.

Two pilots at the same port drinking the same pot of black-iron. From the front, the old pilot's coat isn't remarkable — dark cloth, well-fitted, cuffs already worn soft by her hands. When she reaches for the pot, the underside of her cuff turns out, exposing a narrow ring of woven trim, color and weave changing segment to segment; the young woman's own jacket has only six segments, that other coat might already have forty. At nineteen Anna thought she had been to many places, but in that ring of trim she saw a cloth completely unfamiliar, and didn't even know which side of the world it had come from. The old pilot didn't need to tell any legend; she was just pouring tea. What's tender about this scale is not that an old person has worn her life into a ragged map, but that time has quietly gathered more world into the lining of a coat that was already well made.

Not everyone wants to fly. The world of the people who stay on the ground is no smaller than a pilot's world —
it's just another kind of largeness. A workshop-master who has lived in the same settlement all her life
understands the local pipe network more deeply than any long-haul carrier. Long-haulers bring comparative experience; locals
supply depth. The two kinds of knowledge meet across a workshop's long table.

The truly appealing thing about pilots is that they possess many kinds of everyday. The body of a person who has long lived in one place
knows one local morning best; Anna's body carries many. Atheria
is fog retreating, the sound of water, exhaust drifting up white as feathers. In Keld she may wake and first look at the tide.
In Vorhan she may first smell the geothermal steam. At some highland station, the first thing in the morning is to knock the frost
off the rigging. Her wealth is not the number of coordinates she has visited, but the many kinds of "how a day begins"
her body carries. She likes finding out how other people make a day work — watching how people
handle plumbing, how they hang things to dry, how they take the wind, how they patch roofs, how they bake bread, how they
read an afternoon, how they moor a ship. She doesn't always want to bring the practices home. Sometimes she just wants
to know. So she is in fact a very Archeon kind of explorer — not one looking for
"things she has never seen," but for "ways of living she has never seen."

The rhythm of a day in transit:

Dawn — the night watch writes barometric pressure, wind, and envelope-temperature changes on the interior chalkboard, then goes to sleep.
Morning — the flying leg. Most of the time, nothing happens. The wind comes from one direction, the ship goes
in another, the ground below changes slowly. Someone patches a length of sail, someone reads the barometer,
someone whittles a spare rudder pin. Children and new crew are given the simple tasks that can be watched from either end:
minding the envelope temperature, counting cable clips, helping in the galley, checking the boxes and parcels to be left at the next stop.
Noon — a meal. On deck or below, depending on the wind.
Afternoon — approaching the destination, or the flight goes on. Afternoons are the easiest time to get drowsy, and often the least
stable weather.
Golden hour — the long dusk. K2V warm light strikes hull and sail. Visibility is good, airflow often steadier.
If there's no rush, this is the hour to stand on deck for a while and let the whole day settle back into
the body.
Night — the sky goes fully dark; in the seasons and hours when the light-river is visible, Sky Fire's
Remnant cuts across the heavens, the two moons each in their own position, the deck wind cooler. Night ships run
only small lamps enough to make out the cables and instruments. The lamp of another ship in the distance looks like a moving star; the radio
occasionally carries a weather cypher from hundreds of kilometers away. Hearing an unfamiliar call sign doesn't mean anyone nearby can come to help,
but it proves this sky does not belong only to the current shot.

Most of life in transit, nothing is happening. Mending a sail, whittling a rudder pin, eating yesterday's soup, dozing, watching the forest below slowly change color. Then at three in the afternoon, a landform you've never seen rises from the horizon. Boredom is what gives wonder its scale. What really makes an exploration age hold together is not a wonder every fifty kilometers, but a day and a half of nothing.

Arriving at a place. Every place should have its own arrival grammar. It is not always
"airship draws near → city appears → land." Some places you smell first — dry hay,
iron rust, sulfur, or baking bread, half an hour before you see them. Some places you hear first — a distant
long whistle, or a waterfall, or the rhythmic knock of port-terrace machinery. Some places you see the smoke
first. Some places, you see a flight of birds change direction first. Some places you see nothing at all,
until a mooring tower suddenly appears under the forest. Some places you arrive at night, seeing only
three lights, and only in the morning do you find you have moored beside an enormous canyon. Some places
you can see the mountain from two hundred kilometers off, but the last twenty kilometers you can't find any people. See the
mooring tower first from far away, then the roofs, then finally the people — this is common, but not the only way.

The first time each place appears, Anna has already sensed something before she has fully seen it. On return visits, these perceptions also change: the same place smells different first thing in the cool season versus the warm season.

Wind, too, is part of the perception of arrival, but each place shows wind differently. In Atheria, you first see long strips of cloth strung between the cliffs moving in different directions at different heights; on the wet coast it might be a single heavy wet sailcloth lifting just at one corner, or salt wind bending the plants beside a house; on Amber's table-islands you can first read wind from the whistle of the bridge cables and which way the sieved seed-dust is drifting; on a plateau, no cloth is needed either — the light and shadow across grass, which side of the smoke column is being shaved, where the birds are circling, which face of the waterfall the mist is clinging to, all speak. The existing surface of local life naturally reveals the air.

Coming back has its own grammar, too. On the first arrival, Anna is reading a place; by the tenth return,
the place has begun to read her back. Someone notices she has changed her tying pattern; someone notices she has one fewer person with her this year;
the same child has grown tall enough that she doesn't need to crouch to speak to them; and she notices someone who was always on the platform
has not come out today, an extra table beside the old hearth, the sound of a familiar bit of water gone quieter.

A return is not the arrival scene played again. An effective return at least exposes some small piece of "what has changed in this place"
or "what has changed in the person coming back," and preferably explains neither. The most powerful
change is also allowed to be: the place has hardly changed, and Anna can no longer walk into it in the body she used to have.

When you return to the same settlement, familiarity makes tiny changes visible. The first time, scale is set by wonder; on return, time is set by difference.

Three kinds of stop:

- Quick stop (about two hours): top up water or fuel, deliver mail, read the chalkboard, unload a few boxes or parcels,
  buy a bag of local bread. Not everyone has to go ashore.
- Overnight stop (one or two nights): moor, disembark, find the common house or be led to
  a long table. The next day walk through the market for half an hour, come away with something you just like now
  and whose use may only show itself at the next stop.
- Trapped stop (weather): a storm pins the ship in the anchorage, sometimes a day, sometimes three days.
  A minimal anchorage has only posts, a windbag, and a flat patch of ground; a complete relay has shelter, a well,
  hydrogen top-up or repair capability; do not mix the two. Several unfamiliar ships share a fire, comparing maps,
  swapping local food, trying each other's tools, or each falling silent. When the weather is good again, everyone goes
  their own way, perhaps never to meet again, perhaps meeting again at another anchorage.

Sound as a navigation tool:

- The hum of the engine: load, propulsion setting, and bearing condition can all alter the spectrum. A given
  ship, listened to over the long term, develops its own baseline; deviation is a lead to check.
- The sound of rigging: wind through cables and sailcloth of different thicknesses makes different sounds. A humming note
  sharpening might mean a gust or a change in the loading angle.
- The sound of the envelope wall: material makes very faint creaks or scraping as it warms in sun, cools in shade, or adjusts to pressure.
  Someone who has looked after the same ship for a long time can feel from these whether it is heating up,
  cooling down, or behaving unlike itself.

Edmund's diagnostic listening and a pilot's aural navigation are two branches of the same tree —
he listens to pipes, Anna listens to the sky.

The one who comes back is changed. A pilot who has been far returns to her hometown and finds
that there is one more layer between her and this place. It is not trauma, and not awakening — it is only that once you have seen another morning,
another kind of bread, another way of building, everything at home is still there,
but there is one more angle from which you see it. Some people call that angle "the eyes from the road."
The first time Anna returns to Atheria alone in P5, every plank of the Eyrie's walkway is
just as she left it, but the feet walking on them are not the same.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.18a Society · Festivals
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Festival calendars obey the timing rules of this document. The descriptions below take Thread
as the default viewpoint. Vorhan, Keld, and Duskreach have their own festivals — register those
when they enter prose, do not prefill.

Thread Festival: once a year, in the best flying season. Delegations from
Thread settlements gather at Frontier City. Trade fair, craft competitions,
storytelling meet, marriage market. Thread Run is the most
watched contest of all Thread. Closing night: the Steam Whistle Organ plays at full power, airships fly in formation across the night sky.

Sky Fire's Eve: the anniversary of the supernova reaching Archeon.
The whole of Archeon shares a fixed date, in Winter Veil. Every settlement has a different version of the legend.
521 years on, the trauma has metabolized into part of the cultural DNA.

Launch Day: each settlement's own founding day. Atheria's
falls in Melt Season. A new settlement's Launch Day has not yet become a tradition —
it may only be "the day we lit the first fire."

Bonfire Night: unique to Riverbend.

Naming Day: about a month after birth. Parents tie to the baby's wrist a
"first thread" — a fine cord in the representative color of the local settlement.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.18b Society · Farewell
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The long table after a funeral: a person is gone, the living eat. When they've eaten, they go back to work.

Things stay where they are: Archeon doesn't gather up the dead's belongings. Tools stay on the rack,
the cup stays in the cabinet — the position itself is the memorial, slowly taken over by daily use.

Two kinds of return: Archeon has two ways of sending the body off, and this is not a religious divide —
they are the two contracts a person can have with this planet.

Return to earth and return to wind are the two most common farewells across Aethelgard and the Loom, but individual regions may have their own practices.

Return to earth — give the person back to the soil. On a planet where every inch of ground has been coaxed into growing something, return to earth is not a metaphor, it is the last contract between the person and this land. The burial spot isn't specially marked —
a few years later, that patch grows the same as the patch next to it, and only the person who planted it knows.

Return to wind — give the person back to the sky. Ashes are taken by the wind at a cliff edge, on a port-terrace, from a skiff, or along a stretch of route the family chooses. There is no fixed ceremony for scattering — some do it at the morning whistle, some wait for a wind that happens to be going the right way, some open a jar over the rail the next time they run the line he used to. After a return to wind, there is no place to go and stand and look, only a stretch of route that knows, every time it is flown, that he was scattered here.

The two choices can differ within the same family. The community makes no judgment about which is more formal.
There is no Archeon-wide standard exception for children's farewells. Some settlements strongly prefer return to earth,
some families still go by what the person left behind, or the family's choice, or local custom; register these when entering a specific region.
Children's farewells also vary by place. There is no Archeon-wide law for the death of a child.

Where the name goes: in many mature Thread settlements, what most often keeps a person here is not a standardized gravestone,
but stories, the position where the tool used to sit, what people call a stretch of road, a gesture that is still repeated
each year. Some do leave a very specific name or small marker; it varies by region, and we don't lock in one funeral landscape.
The most common form of continued existence, and the one that requires no dedicated building, is that on a story night, someone's business gets told again.

Farewell is also delayed. A person dies, and two weeks later at some station someone is still holding a letter for him.
Two months later on some line someone is still passing along his message. The pipes he built are quietly at work,
not knowing that he is no longer here. The whole route slowly comes to know about it — not at a single moment,
but as a tail note gradually fading. Network shape ends more slowly than a life.

Three experiences of death. Archeon's communication lag causes loss to take at least three different
shapes in time —

The first: no news. Wait. Hope does not go out all at once, but the reasonable explanations get used up one by one.
Search craft go out; they don't find anything; after the search ends, other people's ships keep running that line as usual.
This is Kassia's version.

For Kassia between twelve and fourteen, what stayed with her first was not the sentence "the world can break."
She learned first to break the waiting down into very specific things: when the last sighting was, which stretch had already been checked,
which stretch was delayed because of weather, who first heard a new echo. In those days she still believed that as long as these things
could be laid out cleanly, her parents were still somewhere in a findable position.

What broke her later was: these records could all be right, the people who should have gone out really did go out,
and in the end there was still no answer.

So what she held onto tighter as she grew up was not "numbers can save people" — quite the opposite: she already knew that numbers
couldn't save anyone. What she held onto was another thing: don't let a phrase of comfort turn "don't know" into "know"; don't let
what a person actually saw last vanish along with them when they leave.

Disappearance has no body to farewell; the Loom weaves it into daily life and keeps running.

The second: fragments seep in gradually. Not yet able to make sense of it. The person is dead. Past messages
keep arriving. This is Anna's version — different fragments heard at different stations, unable to piece
together the whole.

The third: know of the death first, and only afterward receive letters written while they were alive. A person has already attended
the funeral. Two weeks later someone comes down an old route: "There's also this. He asked me to bring it to you."
It opens onto only: weather's nice. Bring me a little of the tea from over there when you come back. No important
words at all. This kind is the most powerful — because the Loom allows a dead man's last time
speaking to happen after his funeral.

News reaches different places at different speeds, which also means grief gets reopened. A family may
have eaten the long table after the funeral, and already returned to normal life; two months later, someone in from a distant station has only just found out,
and stands in the doorway as if this had happened yesterday. To the visitor, it did happen yesterday. The host has to
tell it again, sit a while longer, may even find that things they thought had passed have come back. This does not become
a "second funeral" as an institution; it is just an ordinary repeated farewell in an asynchronous world.

"All well." In an instant-comms world, it is only reassurance. In the Loom's world,
it may be a fact already invalidated, still on the road. When it was written, everything really was well.
By the time it arrived, things were not. It is not error, and it is not lie. It is only expired.
The message has betrayed no one. The world simply happens faster than the message.

An "all well" verbal message may arrive at some station after the person who was carrying it is also gone,
and be written up on the chalkboard. No one comes to fetch it.

Later, when Anna is grown, she may understand this kind of thing very well. Watching a small child ask "doesn't it say here he was fine?"
— she will not say the letter was wrong. She will say: "It was fine when it was written."

A new settlement's farewell: it may be the first death since the town was founded. There is no ready-made
ceremony. Everyone stands there, someone says a line, and that becomes the first tradition
of this place.


----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.19 Society · Healers and Medicine
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Archeon's medicine is not a fragment of Federation medicine, nor is it "only herbs and setting bones left." Like
Archeon engineering, it has branched: what has stayed is the branch that can be made, cleaned, measured, verified, and passed on again and again with local
materials, local glass, local metal, local chemical craft, and human observation.
What has fallen away is the branch that requires unreproducible high-end electronics, precision imaging, complex automated diagnostics, gene-level manufacture, or a
planet-sized industrial stack to sustain.

So the healer of a mature settlement may be familiar with: anatomy and wound structure, cleaning and sterilization,
setting and fixation of bones, suturing, delivering babies, dental work, burns and frostbite, respiratory and dust injuries, dosing,
distillation and extraction, ferment-derived medicines, basic diagnostics. Places able to make optical glass may also keep simple
microscopy; places that can't do without. There is no "same medical grade everywhere here." Medicine, like
any other craft, grows different strengths along local materials and long-accumulated case knowledge.

Knowledge is passed on through household and community practice, Guild-registered apprenticeships, case books, formularies, dosing records,
and the process of working with real patients together. New apprentices first clean, boil instruments, note breathing and temperature changes,
prepare fixation materials; once they do these steadily, they are gradually allowed to touch more irreversible things. The authority of a healer comes from
long-term repeatable results and from other people being willing to give you their bodies, not from any planet-wide standardized certificate.

Ordinary medical care must be as locally self-contained as possible. Every long-term settlement should be able to handle the most common injuries and illnesses,
and should have at least a few kinds of medicines, mineral salts, ferments, fibers, or care methods it can readily get. What long-distance routes
really matter for is filling in the layer that cannot be done locally: some active plant that grows only at altitude, some tool that requires
a special forge temperature, decades of accumulated case experience with a rare condition in some region. So "waiting for the medicine ship"
can be very tense, but only when someone is specifically waiting for that non-local medicine or tool; you cannot write
Archeon so that every delay leaves it without basic care.

The geography of medicine is still the geography of the Loom. The geothermal warmth and soil at the base of Skyleaf's crater make Ember Crown
the true local surplus and specialty, but it is not a planetary miracle cure, nor is it the single lifeline
for every respiratory condition. It can genuinely act on a certain layer of Lia's gray-lung symptoms while doing little for another layer of fibrosis /
scar — this matches the treatment register of §A3-9: get an "insufficient" answer first, then break the
disease down more specifically.

Gray-lung and other chronic industrial illnesses exist in daily life; they shouldn't appear only when the plot needs them. Places with long-term exposure
to a particular dust, smoke, fiber, or workshop environment develop their own body vocabulary: which sort of cough
is just the morning cold air, which sort of breathing means today you should stop, which slope will visibly slow a particular person.
This knowledge is not mystic folklore, nor is it a degraded substitute for modern sensors — it is the local baseline that
long observation and basic medicine have grown together.

Medicines also have an age and a source. Important medicines, extracts, fermentation batches, and things that need storage conditions get labeled
with origin, batch, roughly when they were made, and who handled them; this is not to mimic modern pharmaceutical
barcode, but because a world without instant communication must know "when was this bottle still what
it was originally." Healers, like the pilots of §6A.14, ask first: from when? from where?
who made it? how has it been kept along the way?

Medicine is a branch of Archeon engineering, unrelated to the medieval healer. Basic care is locally sustainable; distant routes fill in "the layer that can't be done locally." Every long-term settlement has at least one local medical strength or one readily obtainable material. Ember Crown has real value but is not a cure-all, and Skyleaf is not a planet-wide medical single point of failure. Diagnosis relies first on observation, touch, hearing, simple instruments, and a long-accumulated body baseline — the scientific knowledge preserved across five hundred years is still working.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.20 Characters · Anna's Profile
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Anna Freedman

Full name Anna Freedman | Gender female | Age at story's opening 19 | Height / weight 168cm / 59kg | Build lean and wiry | Skin light wheat | Hair long, blonde | Eyes blue / blue-gray | Facial contours on the firm side |
Signature outfit: white shirt + deep-red short jacket + narrow red scarf + short apron (no pockets) + gray trousers + worn boots

**Clothing origin / visual:**
The red jacket was made by Edmund when Anna was about 10, after she had begun keeping short-range itineraries on her own. The reason was always ordinary: Atheria's red-earth dye is cheap, takes color well, hides dirt, and is easy to spot from across a berth. It is not a deathbed relic and it is not a route credential.
Between 10 and 19 the jacket has been let out, resleeved, relined, patched at the shoulder, had its fasteners changed, been re-dyed several times; by nineteen, only part of the structure may still be from the material of that ten-year-old's jacket, but Anna and the people along the routes still take it for the same jacket.
The short apron is separate from the jacket in origin; short, pocketless, quickly loosened, it only protects the front of the hips and the upper thighs. Who first cut it, in what year the ties were changed — none of that carries load. Tools go only to belt loops, individual leather sheaths, fixed hangers, in-hand, or on the current work surface.
The red scarf is Anna's own choice of red, from her flying years 14–19. Practicality first: windproofing, dust barrier, warmth, wiping sweat, wrapping something in a pinch, reading the wind; she doesn't need to think of Edmund when she chooses red.
The first patch of red came from home and from her father; later, Anna kept wearing this color on her own. The three garments each have their own provenance, and together they are only the life one person has worn her way through — they do not compose a puzzle awaiting a solution.
The look aims at a person who has been moving on the Loom a long time, whose clothes have been mended in different places, and each of whose layers has a reason for use; the striking silhouette comes from accumulated life, not from an equipment list.

Gear: fingerless leather gloves, a multi-loop tool belt, a chest-and-belly cross safety harness, goggles
Smell: engine oil, metal dust, the faint scent of half a bottle of mountain-flower oil in the side pocket of her flight bag
Posture: stands with weight low, habitually scans the environment; tilts her head slightly when listening; unconsciously works her fingers when thinking
Voice: middle-to-low register, quick delivery with clear articulation; when tense or focused, her voice drops rather than rises
Father: Edmund Freedman, deceased (died of illness when Anna was 13)
Mother: Miriam Freedman, currently at the Eyrie; she looks after preserves, herbs, letters, exchange ledgers, and social debts at home over the long term, not summed up by any single title
Home: the Eyrie, a basalt outcrop at the southern edge of Atheria's home terrace; the front is the workspace Edmund left behind, but inside and out onto the back drying terrace it is first of all a home
What she does often: airship flying, long-hauling, pressure systems and shipboard craft; she makes, splices, installs, and shapes things, and she also judges and looks after. Anna pilots the Class II airship "Gust" (held in route trust), but the character isn't collapsed into a single title.
Craft foundation: a mixed craft grown from Atheria, from different ships, and from experienced hands along the routes; Edmund's "old splice" is only one usable technique among many, and it doesn't define her identity. What she leaves behind as an adult should be more the components and small objects she made, joined, installed, and that continue to be used, than a string of repair records.
Core ability: quick hands / next-step integration. Anna often sees "what's about to happen next" earlier than others, and her body enters that next step sooner too; this speed is built on ordinary Archeon common sense about weight, materials, interfaces, and wind, not on some extra sensory organ. What is truly rare in her is the transferability: the same "sense of the next step" reaches from rope, ship, and material all the way to weather, people, and routes.
Core weakness: it is not easy for her to hand the next step over to someone else; she is afraid of not catching it, and she also easily makes decisions for other people once she has seen the risk.
Bodily aftermath of the accident: irregular double-checking, avoidance, over-anticipation, sudden acceleration, and a very occasional freeze; not to be named as a fixed mechanism.

**Personality — through action**

Anna is not "brave" — she is the person who can still finish the movement in her hands when the wind suddenly changes layer. Nor does Anna prove she is smart by "hearing which bearing is going bad": while others are still saying "there ought to be something here to guide the line clear," she may already have crouched down and measured the spot; and half an afternoon later, an unremarkable brass / heartwood guide piece really is installed there, and years later when she comes back, the place her hand touches most often is already polished bright.

Her craft scenes should prioritize making / fitting / extending / handling: making a small component sized to a particular ship, finishing the joint on a newly branched water / steam line, combining two existing methods into the one that will just do here, making a cup holder, a lamp-shade plate, a rack for tools, a tool handle, or a set of ties for shipboard life. They don't have to be essential every time, and they don't have to carry an invention montage. Repair / breakdown only steps into the foreground when the incident or the plot itself has its own independent causal weight.

What is special about her flying, too, is not that "she hears more than others." She may put her hand on the copper pipe, look at the cloud bases, smell the air of the port on approach, or she may just see that someone today is taking in the line a beat slow; these are all things others in this civilization also do. The difference is that she easily connects several very small changes into the same next step: when the ship, the weather, and the people have already lined up, and when they are still short of one person's presence. She would not describe herself this way. She would say she just flies ships.

"Tuner" is just shorthand; Anna's perception is not locked to a single action — hearing, touching, smelling, seeing, handing, making, waiting can all carry the same ability.

Her biggest problem is that she no longer takes for granted that "when I reach out, things will land safely." After the Lia incident at 13, what she lost was not speed but bodily trust in action → consequence. Sometimes she double-checks once more, sometimes she asks first, sometimes she just doesn't touch, and sometimes she does it faster in order to get it over with; when cold metal, an impact sound, or Lia are on the consequence side of a movement, her body occasionally locks up. Around Lia in particular, she easily anticipates for the wounded child of the past over the present Lia. These are not tics of uniform duration and not a moral creed.

body-memory only appears in specific triggers and relational actions, drawn out naturally by the situation, not clocked in scene by scene or tallied by the narrator.
Body as narrative: Anna's third-person point of view reports first on hand and body actions, on the feedback from materials, and on whether someone else has already caught the next step, without self-analysis.

Her relationship with her mother Miriam is warm but keeps a distance. When they occasionally appear in the kitchen at the same time, they eat quietly together, don't say too much, and that quiet is comfortable.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.21 Characters · Edmund's Profile
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Edmund Freedman. Anna's father. Deceased (died of illness when Anna was 13).

Craft identity: pressure-systems craftsman / `pressure-systems wright`. Signature technique: the "old splice" — a pipe-sealing method in which copper wire and basalt fiber strand are wound alternately. People at outer stations recognize Edmund's hand from the winding style.

Specialty: diagnostic listening (P-01 · reading dead things). Hand on the pipe wall, judging seal life from vibration, temperature, and fluid state — able to predict which gasket will expire first. This is Edmund's clearly specialized craft, and it does not carry over as a core ability Anna naturally has from a young age; Anna can learn the habit of "letting the thing show its state before you move," but she does not reproduce the same set of readings.

**Edmund and the people along the routes:**
Edmund naturally kept people in mind along the routes, left notes, went back to old joints — and he also told others ordinary bits of news from home. After Anna began keeping short-range itineraries on her own, he occasionally mentioned in passing "my kid in the red jacket is also starting to run this way now." This is not a bequest, not a network handoff, not a posthumous plan.
This kind of familiarity at most gives Anna, on her first arrival, an extra bowl, a place to sleep, or a line about the weather; real trust must be built by her, later, by arriving over and over, remembering what people asked her to carry, and doing her own part well.
Later on you can confirm the origin of Edmund's radiation sickness, that he concealed the truth of his body, and you can keep the possibility that he was already afraid at certain moments; but the date of the red jacket, the widening of routes, and time-point arithmetic of any kind cannot prove that he already knew he would die when he made that jacket.
The happiness was real, and the fear was real. The countdown that emerged later cannot rewrite all the ordinary love that came before into a deathbed arrangement.

Personality (through action): Edmund is not a poet — he describes by sense, without long speeches. He shares the world with Anna through objects, smells, half-sentences. When he laughs he lights the room. He has old acquaintances, people he works with regularly, aesthetics, embarrassment, and failure. Attends to the world jointly with Anna; his silence during illness caused real harm, and the love of the earlier years is still real.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.22 Characters · Lia Redwood's Profile
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Lia Redwood. Auburn hair, pale green eyes. Same age as Anna, or one year younger.
Family: Sara Redwood (mother), Kaelan Redwood (father).
Residence: Atheria (childhood through P4), possibly settling later at Skyleaf.

Body: gray-lung — a hereditary alveolar fibrosis tendency from the Sky Fire era's radiation. The damage supernova radiation left in the earliest generations' genes 521 years ago has mostly been diluted by natural selection, but certain recessive combinations still express occasionally. Lia's symptoms: cold air, dry air, altitude change, prolonged physical exertion all speed up an episode. On good days it doesn't show; on bad days her breathing is shallow, she has to sit down. Not lethal, but persistent. She is the specific face of §6A.19's "gray-lung has taught the valley people to read their own breathing." A girl injured by a star 521 years ago.

Craft: Lia is good at precise small components — pneumatic pipe capsules, prefab braided copper-wire gaskets, pressure-valve spring assemblies. What she likes is what her fingers can really feel the error of: the spring's return being off by a hair, the braid's density loose by half a turn, the valve plate seating with the wrong sound — she can catch any of it.

Things she has made are sometimes carried away by acquaintances passing through; a few months later someone may come back and tell her that a certain spring is running well on a certain ship. And plenty of times, she has no idea where the thing ended up. Her relationship to the Loom is first and foremost the repeated coming and going of people, not the tidy pairing of "body stays put, parts travel for her": people remember what she can make, come back to find her, tell her what happened later; and she herself always retains the right to decide when to go out, when to board.

Core desire: to be taken as a person who can do things, who can judge, who wants to go far — not as a body that needs someone else to arrange the risks for her.

Origin point of the relationship with Anna: the P4 port-terrace scaffold accident. In grief / rage Anna deliberately struck the loaded scaffold's diagonal brace with a load bar; the fork-lug at the lower end of the brace, long weakened by damp corrosion, sheared under the impact, the locking pin jumped free, and once the scaffold lost lateral restraint it shifted sideways, and the sinker and other loads pressed Lia against the rail post. Without that blow of Anna's, that moment would not have happened; without the structural deterioration of the fork-lug, an intact scaffold would not have produced the same result. Before the accident, Lia was not on Anna's face-map. What the accident changed was not Anna's habit of speed, but her bodily trust in action / consequence; and what Lia later saw most keenly was not a pause you could time, but that Anna was always widening safety margins around her, anticipating risks, making decisions for her ahead of time.

later-arc function: Lia is not the namer or absolver of Anna's trauma. Her core role is to reclaim "the present Lia's" right to interpret her own body: she can accept help and can refuse it; she can take risks she is willing to bear; she can demand to be involved; she can tell Anna that the child hurt that day was real, and that this present person is also real. The foundation the two rebuild rests on body agency, shared perception, chosen proximity, shared work, shared silence, and ordinary life. Future trust is shown by Lia actively handing Anna one end of a task, not achieved by an unconditional verdict on Anna's future.

The narrative function of gray-lung is not to produce pity — it is to give Anna something her hand cannot reach. Gray-lung is not a loose pipe, not an expiring gasket. It is a persistent state where Anna's whole toolkit does not take effect.

Personality (through action): Lia carries in two hands what others carry in one, and does not explain. She is clear about her own body without self-pity. She has her own friends, her own dye works, her own aesthetic, her own wish for far places. The first time in Skyleaf's flower fields, when she is surrounded by the density of a single color, the scale shock is not "how far the horizon is" but "the walls are still there, but they've stepped back a few kilometers" — for a child raised between two walls, the reversal of spatial ratio is physical.

Lia's body is a persistent fact, not a problem awaiting solution, not the object of atonement or the endpoint of a miracle cure. The dye works is her own place. Sara's complicated feelings will not be solved by a single act of kindness.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.23 Characters · Milo Windham's Profile
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Milo Windham. Short curly hair, brown. When Anna is sixteen and he boards Gust, Milo is about 12–13 (about 3–4 years younger than Anna).
From: Thornfield (50k), a large branching town on the eastern main trunk of Thread (T3), 901 km east of Frontier.

Thornfield is not a "transit stop" that exists only for changing ships. Fifty thousand people here have their own agricultural belt, houses, taverns, festivals, specific crafts, and several generations of local life; even if no outside ship stops for a day, the town holds together as a whole.
But its geographical position makes "many directions existing at once" a daily background for children: people going to Skyleaf, Cinderlode, Amber Reach, Ironvein pass through different berths, and different accents, hull shapes, sail cuts, glass colors, and weave-trims are long mixed together in the same life sphere.

What is special about Milo is not that he lives at a station no one stops at, but that he lives in a place big enough and comfortable enough that he still sees others going on outward every day. He could spend his entire life in Thornfield; instead he has always wanted to know what is beyond the ends of those arrows on the chalkboard.

Trait: curiosity about "the ends of the lines." Not a tech geek — a route geek, a geography geek. At the Thornfield skyport chalkboard he reads every route notice, remembers which side a docking ship came from, the accents of the crew, what particular color the window panes on a certain ship are tinted, and the far-away weave patterns he can't even name. He reads ledger copies, old route charts, survey records — a fifty-year-old prospector's description of the FAR-5 coast, faint handwriting, with a line reading "no one has flown here since." His question is not "how to reproduce the Federation" but "where does the map stop being drawn? what's beyond the stopping point?"

Craft (in development): not yet set. Quick with his hands but rough; on Gust he's learning cable, envelope temperature, inspection.
Core desire: to see what lies beyond where the map stops.

Relationship with Anna: P5, Anna meets him when she runs the Thornfield route at sixteen; Milo is about 12–13 at the time. He is not a fan drawn by heroic aura — he is a boy who has watched distant places come and go from Thornfield for many years, and finally waited for a crew willing to let him actually go along for a stretch. Anna takes him onto Gust because she needs a deckhand and he needs a door — this is normal Loom operation, not fate's arrangement. By the time Anna's nineteen-year-old main line begins, the two have already run lines together for about three years.

P5 emotional function: Anna carrying someone else for the first time. In P2 she was carried (the sky caught her); in P4 she refused to hand her weight to anyone; in P5 she allows it again — Milo coming aboard is her first practice of "beginning to carry others too." He makes rookie mistakes, he looks at the nebula when he should be tense, he asks Edmund's kind of sensory questions but in an entirely different language. He forces Anna to decide what kind of one who carries she wants to become.

Personality (through action): Milo's curiosity is not passive — he actively queues up his questions and won't let one go until it's answered. When he reads an old book on deck he burrows all the way in; you have to call his name twice. His fear is not height but "if I don't board, I will never know what the end of that line looks like."

Milo is not a mini-version of Anna, and not a substitute for Edmund. His curiosity about far places comes from Thornfield's geography — a child raised in a mature junction city — and has nothing to do with trauma or loss. His life was already a whole life before he boarded.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
6A.24 Characters · Kassia's Profile
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Brown hair, brown eyes, a year older than Anna. When she enters in P5, Anna is sixteen / Kassia seventeen; at the main line, Anna is nineteen / Kassia about twenty. Origin left blank, a mature junction environment, used from childhood to people leaving and people arriving late.

Precision is native personality — as a child she remembered dates and intervals the way other children remembered birthdays. Time first gave her a sense of company: someone is not in front of me, but is on a line that can be waited along. After her parents went missing, precision gained another, defensive use: numbers promise nothing, they only report what has already happened. Her core habit grew from here — when you don't know, don't write a nicer-feeling answer in place of what you don't know.

She is impatient with Anna's "listening to the wind," not because she denies Anna is right, but because she resents that those judgments can live only in one person's body. In the search for her parents, the thing that couldn't be handled was exactly that — "I remember that day the cloud wall wasn't quite right" — once the person who said it is gone, no one knows what he actually saw. So what she always presses on is: how do you leave this sentence to the next person who doesn't know you?

Flying enters her life earlier than Anna's. At seventeen it is first pleasure and aesthetics; after twenty a layer she doesn't speak aloud is added: she doesn't like handing critical judgment over to "it should come back on its own." But she still uses wind, still glides, still enjoys the weather.

Personality: has humor, appetite, aesthetics, friends. Not the person who is normally cold and gets thawed by the protagonist. What actually holds her back is waiting / promise / absence. When she watches Lia, she first watches how Lia herself decides, then whether Anna has jumped ahead to decide for Lia.

Arc endpoint: she will not start saying "for sure" again, and she will not start believing in promises again. The change is only in a very small place — she sees a promise broken down into the actions of each day, each day's action honestly done, and together they added up to it; and at the moment it almost broke, her own hand caught it. After that evening, her precision no longer serves only to confirm absence; it also begins to serve to confirm presence.


======================================================================
6B. Earth-side · lived texture before recontact
======================================================================

Earth / Federation side has its own complete daily life before recontact — technology, archives, policy, and starships are only one face of it. Archeon already has its complete daily life; the other side likewise. Recontact is therefore the first time two civilizations, each grown up for five hundred years, look seriously at each other, not "a future world looking at a rustic world."

Both sides can be happy, both sides can take their own life for granted. Earth's post-scarcity, polis, and automation have their own fullness; Archeon's handwork, repair, and low digitization have their own dignity. The two only place agency in different positions.

Archeon's handwork comes first of all from **causality still being left in human hands**. Wood swells and shrinks with humidity, cloth and rope change tension with wind and moisture, pressure systems have working ranges that must be read, weather makes today's ship not quite the same as yesterday's; add to that the localization of information processing and precision manufacturing, and many judgments have not been hidden into invisible automatic systems. So specific people must keep listening, touching, looking, comparing, adjusting, and, at the right moment, do planned replacement and refit. The ideal of mature Archeon engineering is not "fix it after it breaks" but let people see, before it breaks, which direction it is going. A machine being looked after by a particular person is not romance appended to an aesthetic choice, but the real result of where this technology places its agency. Five hundred years on, this readable, care-able engineering has grown into a part of aesthetic, courtesy, pride, and intimacy.

Earth's handwork can be precisely the opposite.
Automated systems can make, maintain, and replace, and often do so
more reliably than a person. So a person still baking a batch of bread with charred spots themselves, mending an old clock
that isn't actually needed, slowly making a chair that a machine could turn out in minutes, tending a small physical garden,
playing an instrument they'll get notes wrong on, does it for the reason of "I want to make this thing with my own hands."
This is not an anti-technology movement, and it shouldn't become the shared habit of all Earth people; only, a
civilization where material labor has retreated from necessity to choice naturally has people who make choice
itself into a life.

So when the two sides first see each other, a very fine pair of two-way misreadings is allowed:

- Archeon people may at first take Earth's handmade work as a hobby:
  since it will not break without being done, since nobody would miss it if it weren't done, why spend days on it?
  Only later do they realize that "not being forced to do it" does not make the care false.
- Earth people may at first not understand why Archeon still leaves so many tiny judgments to specific people: why the crew must with their own hands compare today's pipe temperature, wood moisture, and sail tension to yesterday's, why the shipyard doesn't replace every interface with a self-maintaining black box. Only later do they realize that these crafts come both from a branching of the tech tree and are also already the way people continue to stand in relation to places, to machines, and to each other. There is burden here, and there is skill and choice; you cannot summarize it as "they spend every day fixing broken things."

The narrator does not judge which is more "real." The best contact is when both sides suddenly recognize: the other, too, is actively choosing what is worth carrying themselves.

Earth's deep time need not be proved by rust and ruin. A public space that has been in operation for four hundred years may look almost new, but the name, the habits of use, some unexplained courtesy no one bothers to state, a stack of amended interface standards, a procedure re-run only every few decades yet still preserved with care, will make it have age. Earth's oldness can be "so well maintained that age hides in behavior"; Archeon's oldness more often leaves age in the patina of materials, in surfaces polished by hands, in re-dyed cloth, in local scales, in landform and shape. Both are true deep time.

Earth-side's specific life appears with specific characters; there is no unified "typical day" of Static / Polis Dweller / hybrid. Earth also has wear, fingerprints, food, misplaced objects, and personal preferences; it is only that breakdown no longer governs life. Some Earth people love handwork, some never touch it, and both are normal. A mature Federation colony likewise grows its own local habits; Earth-side is not a central cultural skin.

======================================================================
7. Planetary Timelapse: 521 Years from CI to Anna
======================================================================

This section is the temporal spine of the entire document. All later content — world, childhood, and later arcs — should be able to hang back here. The causal prose for before Sky Fire and Year 0–110 is in §3.1–3.4; the causal prose for Year 50–521 is folded into the "causal prose" entries in each subsection of this section, not repeated in §3.
