Canon responsibility: §7 is the narrative authority of civilizational development and uses relative dating; §3.12 is the sole absolute-year index in CE. The conversion identity is "absolute year = 2573 + Year." Hard anchors: 2472 / Year -101 (CI seed fleet arrives); 2550 / Year -23 (advance Awakeners arrive); 2556 / Year -17, 2562 / Year -11 (two batches of embryos activated); 2563 / Year -10 (Betelgeuse collapse); 2564 / Year -9 (Axiom departs); 2569 / Year -4 (Axiom falls); 2573 / Year 0 (Sky Fire); 2623 / Year 50; 2683 / Year 110; 3094 / Year 521. This locks the interface between the two dating systems and does not lay out a second absolute-time table.

7.1 Before Year 0 — The Unfinished Garden

- Roughly 2472–2573, CI treats the habitable hemisphere from orbit as an engineering project; for detailed absolute years, see 3.12.
- Aethelgard reaches human-settlement standard first, but other continents already have many uninhabited nodes, experimental zones, and future-expansion foundations.
- The first advance Awakeners come only around 2550; two batches of embryos are activated in 2556 / 2562. Humans are concentrated in a few safe zones, and the vast majority of the lattice is still maintained by robots and orbital logistics.
- Axiom falls around 2569, giving the ground about a four-year warning window, and bringing 20–30 survivors and damaged precision supplies.
- When Sky Fire arrives, planetary engineering has stopped at "the foundation has been laid out; ecology and humans have not yet filled in."

7.2 Year 0–50 — Hands Learn the Weight

- 2573 Sky Fire, CI cascade damage, and early human rebuilding; Axiom's fall itself happens around Year -4.
- The core task is still to survive, to maintain ecology, to move knowledge from the system back into people's minds and hands.
- Atwood's ledgers, hands-on teaching, error logs, and straight-line lettering gradually become the foundation of knowledge transmission.
- This period should not be written as planet-wide toxic-land survival; disaster is severe, but the air, water, and ecological baseline CI already finished still exist.
- Humans know many coordinates of distant CI sites, but do not have the population or transport capacity to use them.

7.3 Year 50–110 — The First Threads

- Population and workshops have surplus for the first time to leave the earliest safe zones.
- Failed long-distance ground transport and complex terrain push the first true long-range airships.
- First Thread is the name of the first long-range trans-regional transport ship. It doesn't go to the stars; it only goes to the next far place; "reaching the next basin is far enough for today" is the turning point at civilizational scale.
- The first routes turn isolated settlements from "aware of each other's existence" into "able to rescue each other within one season."
- Flight knowledge shifts from old teaching material to body and oral transmission: "did you hear the sound of that spar?"

Causal prose — why airships. CI's logistical geometry was orbital drop, automated heavy-lift airship, short-range ground-effect transport, and on-site manufacture — it had never built a highway for humans (ROOT 02), so after Sky Fire there was no inheritable road network on the surface at all; the halo islands start 50–150 km apart (§3.4); the ground long-haul the first generations tried failed repeatedly against complex terrain, rainy seasons, and maintenance cost. The only thing that could inherit CI's "point-to-point, skip the middle ground" logistical geometry was the sky: hydrogen from the still-running fusion plants and geothermal electrolysis, skeletons from basalt fiber, prototypes from the few automated heavy-lift airships CI left behind (§6A.2). First Thread doesn't go to the stars, only to the next far place. It turned isolated halo from "aware of each other's existence" into "capable of mutual rescue within one season."

The first new settlements were also born here, but without a common template. Some people did use CI coordinates to find water, heat, or mineral layers still worth having; some stumbled, in failed ground long-hauls, onto slopes and wind gaps better suited to long stays; some camps were meant for only one season and changed because one family didn't leave; some places had no CI coordinates at all, and later people made them into points on the map for the first time. What matters in this period is not "humans finally beginning to cash in on the map CI left" but that humans, for the first time, began to make their own map outside of that one. Flying knowledge shifted at the same time from old textbooks to bodies and oral transmission.

7.4 Year 110–230 — Knots in the Sky

- Small long-range ships, couriers, skiffs, and weather ships begin to specialize.
- Humans set up seasonal camps along old coordinates and along their own newly found water, heat, wind gaps, and good land; some camps become named settlements.
- Frontier turns from an old administrative node into a Great Junction, but is still only the center of the cradle region.
- For the first time, different places noticeably grow different "housing answers": riverside needs high stone foundations and water wheels, terraces need lifting cages and drying decks, plateau presses roofs low, wet coasts raise the dwelling above tidewater, geothermal doors sprout bathhouses and greenhouses.
- Local differences enter food, roofs, windows, bridges, and the rhythm of a day first, and only afterward are taken by outsiders as "regional culture."

Causal prose — routes differentiate first, and only then do places begin to look different from each other. The first named new settlements were not a common Frontier small-town template copied to far places: each place first solved "where do we sleep tonight, where does tomorrow's water come from, what is best made here"; over time those answers wrote themselves into the street width, roof angle, wall foundation material, courtyard size, and which buildings would be placed at town center. Old CI spaces sometimes took part in this shape, sometimes not at all. Names came before maps, maps before route registrations; "Archeon people" did not yet exist; what existed was a group of people beginning to inhabit the terrain as human places.

7.5 Year 230–350 — The Loom Becomes a Civilization

- Routes are no longer only lifelines, but also carry apprentices, seeds, music, marriages, stories, local crafts, and festivals.
- The past begins to lose its single source: CI intervention grows into daily geography, and First Threads and early settlements themselves also begin to be "older than living memory."
- Building craft spreads along Thread but is never wholesale copied: one place learns another's glass formula but keeps its own narrow windows; one place borrows a new roof-frame joint but flattens the roof because the wind is too strong; the same heartwood sunbleaches to silver-gray on the dry plateau, but on the wet coast is long coated with dark resin.
- The first trans-oceanic island-hopping routes appear.
- Maps begin to distinguish fixed main lines, seasonal lines, charter, and survey line.
- Archeon engineering has formed its independent technical grammar, no longer aimed at restoring the Federation.

Causal prose — what the routes carry has changed, and places also begin to borrow from each other without smoothing each other out. Seeds, marriages, craftspeople, recipes, and building joints travel together; a town will show things clearly from far away, but redo them by its own rain, wind, materials, and life. By this period a place's present shape may hold at once a slope reshaped by CI, a field left in place by the first generation, a street moved a hundred years ago, another face expanded because Thread later rerouted, some glass method carried from two thousand kilometers away, and eaves and berths freshly joined on by this generation. History is no longer "recognize which piece is oldest"; the whole place can no longer be reduced to a single author.

The children inherit not only a river no one knows who dug, but perhaps an old route rerouted eight times, a town name kept even after two moves, a doorway no one knows why it was made so wide, a roof frame carried over from the next continent and then reworked by the local wind. Between the last generation who read the CI metal plates as "rebuilding instructions" and the first who read them as "reference manuals" a generation gap emerges; "restoring the Federation" recedes into history, and from this layer on, Archeon engineering is a consciously independent technical grammar. Trans-oceanic island-hopping appears; CI grid is still only the first layer of the mapping lineage.

7.6 Year 350–450 — The Second Reach

- Population crosses the threshold that sustains multiple industrial hubs.
- Large long-haul ships, regional shipyard systems, and cross-oceanic weather coordination mature.
- Aethelgard–Keld island-hopping shifts from "only a few can go" to multiple, repeatedly-honored year-round slow lines plus seasonal fast windows; Vorhan / Duskreach also see their own discontinuous but real outward lines.
- Certain nodes of Vorhan / Keld / Duskreach are reconnected; new regional cultures clearly appear.
- Some old lines wither, proving the Loom is not a progress bar that only expands.
- "Archeon people" begins to have more real meaning than "Aethelgard people," but a global identity is still very loose.

Causal prose — maturity and decline must be written together. Once population crosses the threshold that sustains multiple industrial hubs, long-haul, regional shipyards, and cross-oceanic weather coordination make 5,000–8,000 km-class voyages an expensive but normal life technology, no longer equivalent to legendary exploration. People living along mature great ports will know a few Class IV/V cross-oceanic names, may have met people from different continents, and may still live an entire life without crossing over themselves; "knowing there are people living over there" and "carrying over there inside your body" are two different things.

At the same time, some old lines break because water sources shift, ore layers run out, wind season moves, or an accident happens no one could reach in time; settlements shrink back to seasonal camps or empty out — the Loom is not a progress bar that only moves forward, it breaks, it reroutes, it grows new lines (ROOT 07). An old pilot's map is bigger than a young one's, half because he has flown further, half because on it are drawn places no one goes anymore.

From this period on, humanity's own history must be capable of producing old places. Old human strata that can enter prose include dead Threads, relocated settlements, former great stations retreated to small villages, old skyports turned to farmland, fruit trees still growing along old streets, places whose names remain in weather reports though the actual location has vanished, and routes that have not been flown end to end for decades but still live in pilots' mouths. When Anna in Year 521 sees "old," it cannot automatically equal CI.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
Old human history is not visible uniformly
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Five hundred years of human history cannot merely prove "many events happened"; it must also prove:
the way the past has been left behind was itself never fair. Some people are remembered, some leave only
one line, some stories are told wrong, some places have no one still asking the question of "what happened here"
anymore.

The following states are allowed to coexist, and not required to be evenly distributed:

**Some people really did leave a name.**
For example Haldis Vann can be kept as one of the few early long-range pilots
remembered by multiple regions. She really did fly a line that was, at the time, thought difficult to reliably cross in the cold season;
but the year, the old name of the route, why she had to fly it, even whether it was really she
who did it first, all already exist in conflicting versions. Her name is true; the story does not
have to have only one correct version. She is not Archeon's founding hero — only one of the few in five hundred years lucky enough that her name did not fall away.

**Some places have no name, only a saying.**
A pilot's spoken warning, a nickname for a wind, a joke where nobody remembers why —
these may live longer than the original event. Prose can let characters say them in passing without immediate explanation.

**Some stories are just wrong.**
Locals can explain the scale of a building, a stretch of fruit trees, a place name, or an old route as
the aftereffect of some legend, while the author knows the real reason is entirely different; you can even leave the author out as judge.
False provenance is not only a rule for CI legacy; it also belongs to humanity's own history.

**Some pasts leave only shape.**
Why the door is this wide, why the street bends around something no longer there, why houses
customarily have very thick back walls, why a row of fruit trees doesn't line up with today's field boundary —
these can be the result of one relocation, an old fire, a Thread since rerouted, a generation's
makeshift solution left behind. No one needs to know the story.

**Some settlements have already moved, and today's people do not treat "having moved" as a secret.**
The town name may follow the people; the old location falls back to orchard, field, dock, or emptiness altogether;
you may also move two or three times and only the place-name continuity remains, exactly which generation moved being already blurred.
Most migrations are only life choices; they don't have to be disaster legend.

**And having no historical event at all is also allowed.**
A place's sense of history can already be established by trees, houses, three generations using the same old table, and ordinary life — no event has to be shoved in to prove deep time.

An old place at most brings one historical source to the foreground; the others sink into background. Two consecutive locations should be entered differently. If a detail is more beautiful when the source is unknown, let it stay unknown. Old human history first adds age to the place; it has nothing to do with the protagonist's journey.

7.7 Year 450–521 — Anna's Exploration Age

- Year 521 corresponds to about 3094 CE; Anna's nineteen-year-old main era is the same year Earth first sees Betelgeuse's explosion.
- The global population is locked at about 1.8M; 1.4–2.2M is kept only as a model-sensitivity range, no longer a census wobble in the main text.
- Civilization is already complex enough for cross-continental trade, stable shipbuilding, sustained specialist knowledge, and multiple kinds of city life.
- The planet is still extremely empty. Many CI sites have never been re-inspected; at least one continent is still survey incomplete.
- The map changes every year. A relay that had only a number five years ago now has a name and children.
- Anna does not live in a golden age after the world is finished; she lives at the beginning of the second stitching-up of the planet.

Causal prose — points, not a plane. The 1.8M occupy points, not a plane. From high altitude: thousands of kilometers of wilderness, a light beside a river, hundreds of kilometers on, a town grown up beside an old CI geothermal station, another day's flight and there's a mining valley, two days north with no one, at night the three lamps of a radio relay town appearing on the horizon. Things on the map are of four grades — fixed main line / seasonal line / charter and survey line / hearsay and old coordinate — of which some in the last grade are false, and should be false (§4.6.3). In such a sky, a pilot is not a truck driver in the sky, but a composite of sailor + bush pilot + explorer + wright + trader + pathfinder; and the ship itself is not a transport box, it is the smallest mobile version of a settlement in the sky. When an old pilot rolls up her sleeve and the dozens of segments of trim from distant places show, they can hint at the Archeon she has personally traveled; and the old ship beneath her feet, refitted whole by a shipyard again and again and still beautiful and reliable, proves that this network is not held together by patched-up wrecks but by people who keep making life whole.

Two scales. The cosmic-scale tragedy belongs to the Atwood generation — only the few from Earth know what the supernova means, know the Federation, know what was lost. The planetary-scale curiosity belongs to Milo's generation: he was born here and does not, as the reader does, feel "how tragic that this civilization has lost interstellar tech"; his reaction is "you're saying people used to fly ships between stars? Really? Anyway, they say there's still a layer of doors under that dead tower in the north that hasn't been opened." Sky Fire's causal chain, in their world, is simplified into "the sky once caught fire and our ancestors survived" (§6A.2), and the emotional polarity has flipped: that light-river is the most beautiful thing in the night sky, is the childhood image of the two moons and the light-river hanging together — not trauma (1.1 §2.10). They are not a trauma civilization. They just live in a particularly strange, particularly beautiful, particularly large world.


======================================================================
8. Anna Childhood Timelapse (0–19)
======================================================================

8.0 P1–P5 usage notes

# Layer A · P1–P5 One-Page Causal Index

This index only summarizes P1–P5 below; it does not add new scenes, nor lock in extra payoff for the main text. Where it conflicts with P1–P5, the corresponding Part's main text governs; later arcs do not retroactively rewrite childhood causality.

Load-bearing tier:

A · Perception splits from the family and merges again — In P1, Edmund reads dead things, Miriam reads living people, and Anna first reads a place from her body; the three voices together compose her sense of safety. In P2, three entry points stand side by side on the same ship for the first time, but the family is not an ability squad and does not guarantee an answer at the same time. In P3 Edmund's route and Anna's separate; Miriam stays with the lists, verbal messages, and the changes in people; Anna begins to ask at the same time "what is making sound" and "who is different today." In P4 Edmund's perception withdraws from sound, temperature, and relations; Miriam is still handling the days of living people. It is only in P5 that Anna puts ship, weather, people, and the next segment of the route into a single judgment.

B · Hand — In P1–P3 quick hands are rewarded again and again in daily life: catching, steadying, stepping aside in time, handing the next step across; by P3, for the first time material passes through her hand and becomes something that had not existed before, and continues to be used after she leaves. So Anna forms early on an unspoken bodily trust — that when her hand reaches into the world, things usually gain one more continuable next step. P4 interrupts this experience in a row: Edmund's death gives her, for the first time, a consequence of "the hand has no next step"; six months later, in grief / rage she deliberately strikes the diagonal brace, and the structural deterioration and that blow together deliver an irreversible consequence to Lia. What is left after the accident is not "hands too fast" or "must be slower from now on," but a broken trust in action / consequence: irregular double-checks, avoidance, sudden acceleration, over-anticipation, and occasional freeze. In P5 the Sovana segment starts her incorporating another's present and their right of decision into "the next step" — not healing, and not remolding speed into a cautious personality.

C · Route widens / body shrinks / world collapses — In P2 distance becomes ordinary life for the first time; in P3 Anna's itinerary moves into her own head, routes keep expanding outward, but Edmund's reachable radius first shrinks on the map. In P4 she comes home at normal speed and runs into versions on the road that are all already expired; what death pulls out is a whole relational structure that connected her to the world. The six months of spinning is "the movement continues, the echo is gone"; the Lia accident falls at this lowest point. In P5, it is not that the old world is restored but that a small line she was only running to the port-terrace, now, once again crosses the horizon.

D · Red jacket — In P2 red belongs first to Atheria's dye works, drying cloth, work clothes, and daily life, and does not belong to Anna. Early P3, once Anna can keep a small short-range itinerary of her own, Edmund makes her the first red jacket; the reason is ordinary: red-earth dye is cheap, takes color well, hides dirt, and can be spotted from across a berth. Edmund will naturally mention to acquaintances "Anna in the red jacket is starting to run on her own too," but this is only a father's caretaking habit while he is alive, not a credential, not a route bequest, not a posthumous plan. In P3 red goes outward with Anna, and the places Edmund can reach grow fewer. In P4, after Edmund dies, the jacket keeps being worn; what really produces the time-lag is not that the jacket "brings her into some pre-set route" but that news and human relationships propagate more slowly than death. In different places, some recognize the surname, some the face, some the jacket, some only remember Edmund, some don't know her at all. Anna will meet, again and again, people speaking of her father in the present tense, and now and then a thought flashes — "did he already think I would come here" — but thirteen-year-old her will not organize these reactions into a stable secret theory. In P5 the jacket keeps being let out, resleeved, relined, patched at the shoulder, re-dyed. People also gradually shift from "Edmund's kid" to calling her Anna directly. Its importance comes from being worn through these years — not from having brought its own answer with it when she got it.

Threads allowed to appear repeatedly or be absent:

E · Blue windflower — The current P1 locks it only as a plant of daily life on the Eyrie's rock walls: Chip, pollen, coarse roots in rock cracks. P2–P5 have no mandatory recall shots; the absence of this flower in far places may form absence, but do not force in a shot merely to keep a hidden line alive. If a later arc uses it again, let it carry only the poetic echo of "some things stay in place," and do not let it combine with the red jacket into an answer formula.

F · Edmund's relationships scattered along the route — In P1 the jar at the door, materials from far places, and old nicknames arrive at home first; in P2, Anna enters Edmund / Miriam's already-existing life relationships along the seasonal circuit; in P3 some recognize the surname, some the technique, some the red jacket, and some don't know her or misrecognize; the "you've come to the right person" of S13 is only retold once. In P4 the funeral compresses these scattered relationships into a single day, and the questions arriving late after the death let Edmund keep "living" for a while in different places. In P5 these relationships only open the initial space; that others come to expect Anna later is because she herself comes back, remembers, and picks up the next step.

G · Four beats of P1 departure preparation — after Launch Day, in the current order: the glider frame lets her body feel air's carrying → the moored ship lets her know a ship can breathe even without flying → the chart brings the objects and people already in her life back to specific places → the change of clothes appears next to Edmund's luggage. No "I'm going to fly" declaration; P1 ends and the next day P2 begins straight from the ship.

H · From load-bearing to living aboard a ship — In P1 the kite line is tension in a single line, Launch Day is the whole terrace's shared bearing of load, and only the glider frame drops the carrying force onto Anna's own chest harness. In P2 she first learns to spend nights inside envelope-wall breathing, cable sound, meal smells and hammock; her hand shifts from cold plank to elastic rope; flying first becomes habitation. In P3 she starts distinguishing different ships' accents, and knows that a new ship means learning again. Only in P5 does she take on an actual judgment at the helm. Retire the old "five contacts" count; do not add a skiff control-stick milestone that isn't in the main text.

I · CI grows from the ground into geography — P1 gives only the old iron plaque, the excessively straight waterlines, and the stone foundation that is always a bit warmer than its surroundings; Anna treats them as ground. In P2 CI still doesn't append "ancient-object enlightenment," but Earth thread I runs separately (see the P2 Earth thread section): old objects, oral story variants, and a small piece of textual bone are scattered in through four returns to Atheria, and Anna never actively pursues them. It is P3 that concretizes CI into old stones, load-bearing foundations, old waterways, warm pools, forest belts, and the geographic aftereffects still influencing cloud and settlement shape. In P5 new nodes still grow into places from water, hot food, repeated stops, and people who decide to stay; CI only raises the zero baseline, and does not provide a reward.

N · Reading the sky — Beginning in P1 with hair knowing wind direction before the copper leaf does, wind-gap hand-signs, and ground sounds; in P2 she first hears that a ship's sound is often held in a person's hand; in P3 she begins to read the relationship among ship, cloud, cable, ground, and old CI boundary, and also to see the distance between "noticing" and "having the right to act"; in P5 in the Hagen / Sovana segment she puts the windbag, the person taking in the line, the berth, and the ship into one action. Her difference is proved only by these repeated actions, not by role-naming.

Characters and continuity of daily life:

J · Miriam's independent life axis — In P1 she reads people, food, weather, and community rhythm, and has her own drying terrace, jokes, and bad moods. In P2 her lists keep letters, jars, names, and possibly-expired words continuous; the family's shared route cannot drop her into an auxiliary position for Edmund / Anna. In P3 her half-sentence lets Edmund's route contraction surface at the edge of daily life. In P4 she lends out old molds, handles her own loss, and catches Anna when she breaks down. In P5 Anna comes home and listens to Miriam tell her about a life she did not take part in; the pickling jars and herbs cover the old workshop position, proving the home has not been frozen.

K · Edmund is first of all a person — In P1 the burned baking, the badly-told fairy tale, overdoing things, and family laughter come before the authority of the craft; in P2 he eats, sleeps, forgets tools, and looks at old scenery on the shared route, not the mentor bringing Anna on a tour of a legacy. In P3 he appears mainly through mistimed letters, different versions in other people's mouths, and increasingly short routes. In P4 the silence during illness causes real harm, and the happiness of the early years is still real. In P5 what is inherited is only a usable technique and a memory, not a post waiting for Anna to take over.

L · The world does not wait on the main line — P1 keeps at least enough ordinary afternoons with no relation to the main line; in P2 the first night has no crisis, and the day of waiting on the wind is not responsible for a growth declaration; in P3 wonder at the long-stay site must coexist with breakfast, borrowing a bed, and a stranger's life; in P4 the child with wind in a bucket is unrelated to the accident; in P5, settlement, blanket, berth, friend, and Miriam all keep changing while Anna is not there. Follow each Part's own ordinary-day pacing.

M · Anna becomes a person who arrives — P4's spinning period has no "her network being born"; she is only unable to stay long at the workshop, and so passes repeatedly by ramps, terraces, ports, and other people's small next steps of the day. In P5 an ordinary connection carries her out of Atheria, and she accumulates relationships that call her by name directly through a second, fifth, twelfth arrival. It echoes Edmund's living habit but is not inheritance, not handoff, not fated replay.

O · Gold goggles — In P2 it is only something the family has always had, now Anna's turn to use it; the first true wearing does not constitute an award. In P3/P4 it can retreat into daily background. In P5 the Sovana segment brings it back into the foreground: the same pair of goggles is still there, and the person wearing it can now maintain an itinerary, and has also begun to know she must not preempt someone else's present judgment.

P · Hagen (P4 → P5 pivot) — At the funeral in P4, the scarred stranger nods to Anna; she does not respond. In P5 no one dispatches Anna: she was only bringing a batch of ordinary goods to the port-terrace, and the next leg is displaced by a wind-window change; Hagen's ship happens to go on to the next junction, and Anna herself says "I'll go with you to the next stop." One stop, through weather and destination, grows naturally into the second, then into three coastal days and later regular runs (the three coastal days are the registered exception of following one line to a far end of about 2,400 km in one go; see the P5D voyage lock; her subsequent norm remains repeat runs within a few hundred kilometers). Hagen's recognizing the Freedman surname is responsible only for initial identification; routes always allow hitching, carrying goods, and swapping people; he does not grant permission, and he does not bring her back to the sky in Edmund's place.


## Overall travel rule · Movement is not a cut between scenes

The airship is not the fast-forward button between scenes.

When a place appears, the reader needs to know two things at the same time:

1. **How Anna got here.**
2. **After Anna is here, why people live here.**

The first is carried by transit: wind, distance, ship sound, taking turns on watch, meals, sleep, waiting, route selection, message delay.

The second is carried by the ground: local surplus, food, craft, water, market, family, work in progress, survey, CI substrate, and the local use of this land.

The narrative breathing of an ordinary voyage is not a fixed percentage, but:

**A stretch of movement → one or two ground-life paragraphs → move again.**

Three consecutive narrative stretches all on deck lose the weight of the ground; the reverse — an ordinary stop occupying four or five straight narrative stretches — makes the Loom lose its sense of motion.

Only a **long-stay** site earns the right to let the story really stop for several days.

A long-stay is not a "famous sight." It must meet at least two of the following:

- Anna has slept in the place more than one night;
- the itinerary is changed by local conditions;
- she actually takes part in a piece of work in progress;
- she comes to know one or two people who will not travel on with her;
- when she leaves, the place's state has changed slightly from when she arrived;
- it makes the map outside look larger, rather than being ticked off.

### Sky / ground narrative breathing reference

These ratios only guide the reader's felt sense; they do not stand for calendar time:

- **P1: ground about 95%.** Sky exists as sound, shadow, chart, arriving visitors, and desire.
- **P2: in transit about 35–40%, ground about 60–65%.** The ship joins several ordinary lives together.
- **P3: overall about 45% transit / 55% ground.** A Class V long-stay can temporarily flip to about 65% aboard; a survey/frontier long-stay can drop to about 20% aboard.
- **P4: the opening still has far travel; once into Atheria, ground quickly compresses to 85–90% and above.** The world hasn't disappeared; Anna's activity radius has contracted.
- **P5: recovers to about 40% transit / 60% ground.** During Gust's repair period, ground dominates absolutely; once she truly becomes a long-hauler, movement and arrival regain balance.

When the nineteen-year-old main line begins, Anna is already used to waking from an ordinary long voyage. "Waking up on an airship" is no longer an event.

---

# P1 · The ground plants the sky into her body (birth 2, 3 — about age seven / eight)
## Home before Route

P1's core is not "Anna didn't go out for seven years."

It is:

**She hasn't really left Atheria for seven years, but the world has been entering Atheria for seven years.**

Atheria must first be established as a complete life you can fall in love with. The steampunk texture here does not rely on decorative cogs, but on the interlocking of life systems: waterfalls and irrigation ditches feed fields, elevation differences drive mills, cliff-hugging passenger gondolas and heavy-cargo lifting platforms, the port-terrace changes when the market is busiest, cargo and news brought by the airships change what appears on the supper table. Heartwood, copper, cloth, glass, stone, water, flour, fruit, steam, and human hands all belong to the same layer of life.

The default vertical transport in P1 uses two body-grammars: **people ride the gondola / basket, cargo goes on platform / lifting deck.** The basket will wait for people, will open the door at a station, will make everyone shift half a step automatically because there are already three people inside, two baskets of vegetables and a roll of wet cloth; the platform waits for no one, and can take down at one time a stack of flour, half a loom, a bed, or an entire row of full baskets. Anna very early learns to tell them apart in a child's way: the basket is a small movable room, the platform is a hand that hands a weight from one level to the next.

From the Eyrie down to the Lowerlands is no longer default a long zigzag path. Anna will run the short cuts across the terraces, duck through cliff ramps, but the daily action that really changes altitude is often to step into the basket: door closes, the floor gives a light sinking, rollers start ta—ta—ta; the cliff face moves upward past the window. With each level down, the air changes flavor first — the home terrace is cold stone, herbs, and wind; the cliff-root band brings more wood shavings, hot metal, and exhaust; the Lowerlands is wet earth, ditch water, wheat dust, and sunned fruit peel. She has been used since childhood to "the room will move," so when in P2 she first sleeps in an airship, what is strange is not motion itself but that when she wakes the next day, the motion still isn't over.


### P1 · Home and all of Atheria (3–8)

P1 covers 3–8 years old; let Anna's body come to know home first, then the ground of Atheria, and only at the end touch the sky with her body. Before eight she has not been on a ship. The sky passes overhead every day — skiffs cutting through cliff currents, cargo ships taking ten minutes of flying to arrive from the horizon — but ships are someone else's business. Her relationship to wind is entirely direct: hair, string, rigging, glider frame. Character names, old side-character mini-arcs, and most existing dialogue are not load-bearing items; the scenes' actions, senses, social mechanisms, and family warmth can be kept and rewritten.

First segment · Body of the home (about 3–6): three-voice safety signal, the Eyrie, sunberries, Chip, blue windflowers, quick moon and slow moon, low-key birthday, old leather jacket in a storm, badly-told fairy tale, The Sail, a jar without a note at the door, an ordinary day in close-third. Edmund loves dead things, can burn what he bakes, and lifts Anna up and spins her around; Miriam reads living people, arranges community, and also plays, errs, has her own drying terrace and her own bad moods. Home is stable, intimate, without preburied tragic shadow. The reader sees only a home here; sky is the view outside the window.

---

First segment · Body of the home (about 3–6)

The earliest things Anna remembers are sun-heated rock, the unexpected sweetness of a rare berry, and the reasonless safety of her father's arms. The workshop's hum comes later, mixing in with the wind and the sound of bowls in the kitchen, becoming part of home.

Atheria's forty-five thousand people are scattered across three terraces cut by falling cliffs — the cable smell of the port-terrace, the smoke and herbs of the home terrace, the hot wood shavings and exhaust of the cliff-root band; step on a different terrace and your nose knows first. This population is nothing special for mature Aethelgard in Year 521; if Atheria seems fine-grained in the narrative, it is because Anna knows it well, not because it is a capital or a secondary center. Further down, the Lowerlands' sea of fields runs out to the horizon: grain, bean racks, fruit trees, and the reflection of Ishkar's water-terraces stretching all the way out of sight. Anna Freedman's childhood sits atop a jutting basalt outcrop at the southern edge of the home terrace.

The Eyrie is not a workshop with a few beds tacked on. The front half is the loudest: Edmund's long bench, hanging racks, clamps, and always one item not put away take up the outer platform; two steps further in, the room suddenly becomes home — a low hearth, a long table, a horizontal bar hung with cups, Miriam's narrow cabinet for herbs and paper, a grain box against the wall. The sleeping berths are further in and on the half-loft; the window is small, but in the afternoon it can carry the smell of herbs and cloth from the drying terrace in. Out the back door is Miriam's drying terrace: preserving jars, herbs, wet cloth, a basket forgotten one time, and a low stool that belonged to whoever sat on it first. Little Anna doesn't yet distinguish "work zone" from "living zone"; she just knows a certain part of the floor always has metal shavings, a certain corner is always warm, under which window it is good to hide in the rain, on which step you can most easily be seen by someone in the kitchen.

Looking out from the front platform, the cliff-root band's roofs come out of the thin mist, and further off is the Lowerlands: Ishkar's north branch drops step by step into waterfalls and water-ladders, the ditch network glitters, dark-green windmill blades turn slowly among the fields, cargo ships take ten minutes of flying to arrive from the horizon. The sky is full from the first page — skiffs like falling leaves through the currents between terraces, occasionally one comes around one side of the cliff and vanishes around the other. On top is Archeon's warm blue sky, with a thin honey layer beneath it. In the evening, Sky Fire's Remnant first surfaces as a faint green arc, then slowly opens into deep red silk.

**Anna's morning**

The light wakes her. In the early morning the low-angle light slants in through the cliff-face opening, first striking the quilt corner, then slowly climbing onto her face. Anna, eyes closed, sticks out one hand from under the covers; when her palm grows warm, she knows today's weather is good. The gold layer that will fade by noon still stays behind her eyelids.

Then the sounds come. Not one — several at once. The waterfall's low bass has been laid down at the bottom, like a blanket that will never be picked up. The rock hen's short clucking is returned once by the cliff face behind, while the open side eats up the reverberation; the sound goes only forward. The basket's windwheel gives its first creak; from the direction of the port-terrace the earliest airships wake, their low hum falling smoothly, another after a while — Frontier's earliest ships are already moving; at any moment three or five skiffs are passing between terraces, the whistling of wind through their metal frames flashing across and gone. On the front platform, the slate-gray lizards have crawled out to sunbathe; they dart like darts, then suddenly freeze. On the rock wall the blue windflowers nod in the morning breeze.

The dew leaves the stone, and a clean mineral smell rises. Miriam sets a bowl down on the kitchen counter, then water goes into the pot. In the half-loft workshop, Edmund turns over a drawing, the paper's corner brushing the wooden bench; a faint smell has already climbed the stone steps, mixing with the wet-stone smell of morning mist.

She walks out to the front platform. The railing is ice — basalt lost the day's heat overnight.

Fog spreads over the cliff-root band and the Lowerlands, roofs appearing in rows out of the mist, and beyond the fog line the Lowerlands' sea of fields runs to the horizon. Anna walks to the rail: from beneath the white come the low turning of the windmill, water entering ditches, and the sudden explosive wingbeat of somebody's rock hen.

The sun climbs, and the fog retreats south from the cliff base. First the silver-blue curve of the Ishkar shows, then dark green vegetable rows, bean racks, and rows of fruit trees, then the terraces' roofs with smoke pulled by the morning breeze into thin gauze. Last the water-ladders light up, and small flatbed carts trundle out of the fog. Anna judges the hour by how far the fog has retreated: rock hens push people to get up, gray sparrows brushing along the cliff wall means it's near noon, bees returning to the hive in a straight line along the terrace means evening.

By five years old Anna already reads the hours by color: sky turned honey-colored, she goes looking for afternoon tea; a copper tone spreading across the platform, Edmund is almost done for the day; the sky sunk to deep blue, still a bit of green at the edges, Miriam's voice reaches out from the house to bring her back in ahead of the night wind.

A little older, she reads the hour by wind. The wind at the cliff face turns three times a day: mornings it climbs from the Lowerlands upward, pushing the mist layer by layer away; afternoons the cliff stone has been heated, and the wind turns down from the cliff face behind, carrying the smell of hot stone; evenings the Lowerlands cool fast, and the wind reverses to run down, pulling the cooking smoke from the terraces into rows leaning south. Windbags hang at the port-terrace, and by the skyport door there is an old copper leaf that turns; on the higher roof ridges and the cliff edge, a few narrow strips of wind-cloth are also set. Anna does not think of them as three instruments; she just knows they rarely all start moving together. Her hair first sticks to her face, then a nearby cloth lifts, then the high strip is sometimes already pulled straight before the copper leaf slowly catches up. Once, in the afternoon, the wind came early, and she had already taken The Sail in before Edmund looked up at the copper leaf and it had only just begun to move. He said nothing, only put his hand on her head and pressed once, as if to confirm that what was inside was correct.

**Cool-season white feathers:** on cool-season mornings the terraces call out on their own: whichever workshop is starting up runs up a white feather at the exhaust port; she counts from the platform, three, four; Edmund's own suddenly darts out from behind the outcrop below her feet and skewingly knocks apart the fourth one's tail.

**Twilight platform:** when the sky turns copper, Edmund finishes work and sits down on the front platform, back against the sun-warmed and now cooling outer wall. Anna charges into his open arms and is swung around twice; the lights of the Lowerlands stretch into bright lines, and the bees' hum too seems to bend a little. When she lands she holds onto his trouser leg and waits for the world to steady, and immediately says: "Again."

Later she climbs onto his lap. The Lowerlands' ditches darken segment by segment, the mooring lights move slowly, the nebula first surfaces as a faint green arc, then opens into red silk. Edmund's body heat comes through the work clothes, his breathing gradually slows; Anna wants to wait until the nebula unfurls completely, but her eyelids close first. The next frame is early morning, she is lying in her own bed in the loft, quilt corner tucked to her chin.

**Quick moon and slow moon**

There are two moons in Archeon's night sky, but Anna never thinks of them as a pair.

The slow moon hangs there — big, ochre red, its surface with a layer of dim light like dirty ice; all night it hardly moves; only after many days does it change position. It is more like a piece of furniture in the sky, like the cliff wall behind, like the nebula: you don't look at it, and it is always there.

The quick moon is another thing. It is small, silver-gray, brightly clean, and it **runs**. Tonight it is above the cliff face; tomorrow night it is over by the windmills; the next night it isn't visible at all, and the night after that pops out from the other end of the sky. At five, Anna discovered this, ran to ask Miriam: are there two moons, or many? Miriam thought about it. "Two. One has patience. The other doesn't."

Sometimes you can see the quick moon in the daytime too — a faint silver-white printed on the honey-colored sky, like a plate someone forgot to bring in.

There is also a third that moves. Not a moon, but a very slow star; after a few days look again, and it has shifted. Anna watched all through one cool season and finally decided it was a ship that never came home.

On the night the quick moon catches up to the slow moon, the two moons sit close together and the nebula happens to spread out from behind them. Adults push the hour when they call the children back inside a little later. Anna, looking at the quick moon hanging low over the Lowerlands' windmill, says: "The moon has come down; I'll go down and reach it."

**The taste of sunlight and mother's hand**

The Eyrie's long table usually has coarse bread, cheese, dried meat, and the vegetables sent up from the terraces. The kind of sweetness that would make Anna lick her fingers the whole way home only appears for a few short weeks a year on the rock wall.

High on the sunny cliff wall near the Eyrie, several low shrubs press into rock cracks; only for a few short weeks each year do they bear a small fruit called "sunberries" — fingertip-sized, deep orange. Once the color deepens, both the rock hens and the children come very early.

In the first stretch of high summer, when the sunberries deepen, Miriam tucks an old handkerchief into Anna's pocket after breakfast.

Setting out at dawn — that is a secret only between the two of them. Miriam tucks a clean handkerchief into Anna's small pocket of her work clothes ("for wiping your hands — not your mouth — although I know you'll use it on your mouth"), and then the two climb up a narrow path only they know, scaling the rock wall. The way is steep; in some places you use hands and feet together; Miriam goes up first at the hard spots and then reaches down to pull Anna up.

Those shrubs press into a sunny rock cleft, their fine roots following a bit of moisture into places you cannot see. Miriam teaches Anna to pick only the ripest, the darkest: "the pale ones are still growing, leave them for next week. From each shrub, take only a third; leave the rest for the birds and the seed."

And then it is eating. Sitting on the sun-warmed rock, feet dangling over the ledge — the Lowerlands' sea of fields opening below, the Ishkar glinting between the terraces in the distance — Anna puts the berries one by one into her mouth. When the skin breaks the sour bites her tongue first, then everything is sweet. She eats too fast; the thick orange juice runs down her chin; Miriam wipes with her thumb, leaving a clean arc, and laughs first.

"Eat slower. They aren't going to fly away."

"But they're too good—"

"I can see. Your chin has had a portion, too."

The fifth-year berry-picking day, Anna clutches her small share, refusing to finish — she wants to save it for her father. All the way down she keeps the handkerchief tightly wrapped, guarded to her chest. By the time she runs into the workshop and proudly opens the handkerchief, the berries have turned in body heat and jostling into a puddle of orange mush.

She lifts the handkerchief, and lowers it. The corners of her mouth pull down; her nose reddens first.

Edmund sets down his work and walks over. He looks at her face first, then down at the mush in the handkerchief. Both his hands are covered in engine oil and cannot take it, so he bends over, brings his face close to her hand, sniffs, straightens, and announces with utter gravity: "Do you know what you have made?"

Anna shakes her head, her chin trembling.

"Jam," he says. "You have made jam. Jam is supposed to be made this way."

He opens his mouth. Anna hesitates a moment, then digs a big scoop of the mush with her fingers and presses it entirely into his mouth — presses too hard; half his beard is smeared orange. Edmund closes his eyes and chews slowly, an expression of exaggerated, nearly devout ecstasy floating on his face.

Anna wipes her eye with the back of her hand, smearing orange onto that side of her face too; she is already laughing.

Miriam walks in, sees father and daughter each with a hand of orange mush, staring at each other and grinning like fools, and sighs: "I shouldn't have let the two of you stay in the same room."

**Feeding the machine:** grown-ups say the machine needs to be fed oil, coaxed, woken up; she puts half a biscuit on the copper cover of the pneumatic pipe relay box by the walkway. The next day the biscuit is gone (a rock hen ate it), and she is satisfied: the machine ate.

**Chip and the blue windflowers**

The slate-gray lizards sunbathe on the warm steel plate of the platform.

The biggest one has a corner missing from its back; Anna calls it Chip, and leaves water for it in a bottle cap.

She can crouch for a long time, watching the fine scales under its throat rise and fall with breath.

Once Chip steps onto the back of her hand: first one cold small foot lands, pauses, then the other three follow.

She holds her breath until it jumps back into the crack, and only then does she rush into the workshop holding up that hand.

Edmund sets down the file and seriously inspects the piece of skin where no claw-print can be seen.

"This landing pad can take things now."

That afternoon she tries not to use that hand.

In the hair-fine cracks of the same rock face, the blue windflowers have very little soil, but roots much thicker than their stems.

The petals tremble in the wind, the blue thin as dyed morning mist.

Anna can bring her face very close, letting the tip of her nose pick up a bit of blue pollen.

Miriam wipes it once and doesn't get it all off, letting that bit of blue stay through supper.

**A lap of the outcrop and Miriam's day**

Anna sets a rule for herself: circle the Eyrie once without her feet touching soil.

She steps on the drying rack's crossbars, hops over the third walkway plank that likes to lift, and dives between two wet cloths hugging the drying pole.

The wind plasters a whole cloth to her face; cool water and bitter green-leaf smell wrap her from head to waist.

When Miriam lifts the cloth she laughs so hard she can barely hold the pins; the pins scatter, and Anna's hair won't lie flat again.

The shady shale is like it was just pulled from water.

Anna chases Chip up to a height from which she can't come down, sits there, refuses to call.

The terrace goes on as usual: turning grass, twisting jar lids, a distant argument half-finished over a borrowed hammer. At lamp-lighting time the hammer is found on his own roof — he set it down himself when he was fixing a tile the day before. The next day neither party mentions it, only the wife of the household that had lent the hammer brings over a bowl of soup, and the household with the hammer finishes it.

The sun warms the stone she sits on into heat; she changes footing three times, then finally slides down, scraping her knee.

Miriam walks over; when she crouches her hand already cups the back of the knee, fingertips avoiding the tear.

"Can you bend it? Bend it for me."

Anna bends once; her knee immediately retracts. She bends a second time, and this time can stand.

At the supper table she insists she did not touch the ground; the ground touched her.

Edmund earnestly supports "the ground bears active responsibility"; Miriam counter-arguing halfway, laughs until she can't finish.
# 8.0.1 P1–P5 Core structure · Travel / Ground / Time-Lag


### P1 Second layer: Atheria is a living machine, and pastoral too

As Anna can herself go down the terraces, run the market, carry things with someone, Atheria's scale opens. Not opened all at once — every time she takes a new road, drops one more terrace, the world grows another ring.

Atheria is not a dense town crammed into a cliff wall. Between the three fallen cliffs, each terrace surface has a width of over two kilometers; looking south from anywhere on the home terrace, sight lines can run all the way to the cliff edge before falling off. Scattered across the terraces are ordinary farmsteads, mills, workshops, and low houses beside the narrow-gauge tracks — heartwood brown walls, basalt gray roofs, oxidized-copper green pipe joints; fruit trees and bean racks reach to the windows, and now and then a rock hen slips out of a stone wall crack and back in again. Windmills turn on the flat parts of the terraces, not just one — from the front platform of the Eyrie there are always seven or eight blades turning slowly at various distances, like a group of clocks, each keeping its own time. Narrow-gauge flatbeds trundle along the ditch banks, sometimes carrying people, more often carrying baskets, sacks, and a length of wood just sawn. Between villages is true field; it takes some walking, or you wait for the cliff-hugging gondola to drop to the next level and then walk another stretch. The rhythm of life across the whole terrace is slower, more dispersed, and quieter than any single word can hold.

Children walking through this quiet don't find it far — they take short cuts, climb stone walls, cross through the tunnels under the bean racks, jump from one side of a ditch to the other. Adults' roads and children's roads are almost two different maps.

The fields are not background. There is really water in the ditches to be distributed today. Grain lays deep gold and amber bands across the Lowerlands; when the wind passes, the whole field's surface is like a bolt of cloth someone is slowly pulling open from one end. During harvest season it is more animated: a wooden-shelled Plain Cutter floats on top of the wheat, hissing steam, clacking underneath as it goes; the sound of the stalks falling is like someone tearing cloth inside. The cut wheat is dropped in a line behind the ship. Children stay in the cut band picking up ears, passing rope; whoever crosses the safety flag is shouted back by the nearest adult. Anna is too small; she can't go inside the cut width; she squats at the edge of the threshing floor and watches the beat — bend, gather, wind, tighten, release — the air full of the hot sweetness of wheat dust and freshly baked bread mixed together. The harvest is not an event, it is background: it comes around once a year, each year she is a little older, and the range she is allowed to approach expands another ring.

The bodily participation of the harvest (the mark of the straw rope, the hot bowl passed hand to hand) is left for the harvest-season leg's scene in P2. P1 gives only "seeing" and "being at the edge."

**Mill.** The first time Anna goes in, it is not "hearing" but feet-first: the water wheel sends a very low vibration into the floor, and the wooden gears bite one into another in the dark; the sound is so thick people unconsciously raise their voices when they speak. A slanting shaft of light comes down through a high window; only then does the flour dust in it suddenly take shape — Anna reaches in, and the light breaks apart on the back of her hand and closes again between her fingers. The miller's child steps out of the shadow with hair almost fully white; Anna takes two beats to realize that is only powder.

She leans against a wooden post. Her shoulder blades, through her clothes, can read every meshing of the gears: dong, dong, dong — with a slightly heavier one every dozen or so. She starts waiting for that one; the person next to her isn't waiting; that is the sound they have listened to their whole life. Out the door, the waterfall and wind rush back in at once, and when she speaks she still uses the volume from inside the mill. Miriam, standing a step away, says: "I'm right here." Anna immediately drops her next sentence quieter.

The mill plants "things have their own sound" in her body, but does not teach her the answer. When in P2 the airship first has an unfamiliar undertone, the reason she goes to find the source is not that she can already fix a ship, but that she is used from childhood to every continuous sound belonging to something in the middle of doing work.

**Bread oven.** The stone oven of the bread house does not go out all night. In the cool season, the warmest wall on the whole terrace is its back; someone is always sitting on the bench against that wall, even strangers. The hour the first batch is baked is Terracehead's earliest clock: some people know from the bread smell alone that it is time to get up. When Anna passes, sometimes half an end piece is handed to her; sometimes there is nothing, and she just leans a while against that warm wall, her back growing hot first.

**Smithy.** The blacksmith beats iron at the door and quenches inside. When they quench, the room takes in a breath — the heat first fills, and then is suddenly all pulled away by the water. Anna does not go in; the sound outside is loud enough. Broken things are piled in the corner, waiting to become material again.

**Colored ropes between the cliffs.** The dye works are at the lower, downwind edge of the cliff-root band. Approaching, hold a breath. But what Anna sees from a distance is not the dye works itself — it is color: dozens of drying lines strung between the cliffs, freshly dyed cloth hung in rows in the wind. Red-earth, indigo, copper-leaf yellow, some so dark you can't tell the original color. On the outermost lines, some rope ends still bear narrow strips like torn-off strips of cloth; when the clothing has been taken in, those still remain, and when the wind turns, they lift first. The full rows of wet cloth, dry cloth, and those narrow strips are never fully synchronized — the upper layer tightens first, the lower layer still slowly billowing. From the cliff-hugging gondola windows, looking down, those colors are like rows of wall-less colored curtains hanging in the air between terraces. The color is Atheria's; the cloth comes from elsewhere, or the reverse. Anna at that time doesn't distinguish these — she only knows that before it rains someone shouts "take the cloth in," and several households at once reach out their windows.

Atheria's material colors are here only the colors of daily life. Red-earth cloth and indigo and copper-leaf yellow belong together with the dye works, children, working people, and household cloth pieces; here there is no "future Anna's color." Jacket and short apron do not share cloth, do not share batch, do not share source.

**Big ship enters port · child's view.** A big ship comes into Atheria — the largest one Anna can remember. The shadow arrives first — a big patch slides over the Lowerlands' fields, climbs onto the cliff-root band's roofs, and the children's heads turn with it. Then the engine: the low hum that doesn't shake your hand drops half a note, like a very big thing slowing its breath.

The children are already running. Anna runs across the terrace lawn with them and lines up along the cliff-edge railing. The port-terrace is a hundred and fifty meters below her feet; from this angle you can see the mooring bollards and the ground crew. When the ship drifts against the berth, there is almost no sound — quieter than something that big should be. The mooring hook clangs off the cliff face; all the children leaning on the railing pull their necks in at the same time, then laugh.

A boy says he counted, from shadow to mooring you have to count to two hundred. Anna tries once, gets to a hundred and sixty and is nudged crooked by someone next to her, and starts over.

**Big ship enters port · market view.** A ship arriving in port doesn't only add a beautiful outline to the sky — the market will close later, several new jars will appear at doorways, someone brings dried fruit, cloth, glass, salt, and news from another place.

Half an hour before the ship arrives, the market starts to move. Flour sacks are stacked flat, the dried-fish seller shifts one slot toward the port-terrace end, two children are lifted by adults from the main lane to the side. Anna stands in the market; the flow of people has suddenly gained a direction — a moment ago it was cloth; now the whole thing is tilting toward the port-terrace. The ship stops; the cloth flattens back. Sometimes from the cliff-hugging gondola window she looks down at the same thing: the whole market tips slowly to one side and back. After the ship is gone, the stalls are always one slot off from where they were before the ship arrived.

The two segments can be different days: one for wonder (a big thing has come); one for the ordinary (a big thing has come and changed the ground rhythm of life).

**Waiting shed.** The shed on the port-terrace against the cliff face is the earliest place Anna learns to wait. The shed isn't big: a long bench, a board with the days ships come and chalked note of which berth is free, and an old man who is always there. On the shed post is nailed a hand-sign chart — the four most-used wind-gap gestures drawn on a wooden board, the lines blurred by fingers. The children learn these four before they can read. Anna signs "arrived" at least two years before she can write her own name.

When the ships aren't coming, this is the most idle place on the terrace; when a ship arrives, everyone stands up at the same moment. Even the old man who is only sitting in the sun stands too, though what he is waiting for is not this shipment.

The four gestures on the hand-sign board are a product of wind-gap life — when the wind is big and you can't be heard, you can only signal. Anna gets used to "the body precedes the voice in conveying information" here; this is the ground origin of her later sky-reading.

**A windbag and a lamp.** A pole in the middle of a field, one windbag, one lamp. It doesn't stand for people on the ground; it stands for people in the sky — a person on the ground may go a lifetime without needing it, but they see it every day. Anna sees it every day too. Whichever side the windbag tilts, she knows where today's wind comes from, though her hair has already told her. At night the lamp is on; you can see it from the loft window; it is a letter in another grammar, but Anna does not yet know that she is reading it.

The windbag and lamp are the smallest units of sky infrastructure already on Atheria's ground, and they lead naturally into the next layer: the outside world entering in the form of seeds.

Anna forms her earliest spatial judgments here: not east, south, west, north, but "follow the wet-stone smell down," "turn once you hear the second sound of water," "behind that row of copper roofs." The path along the ditch is the flattest on the terrace — water does not climb slopes, so people who follow the water don't climb slopes either. The old and those pushing carts take this way; children don't, they take the short.


### P1 Life window · Moving day: a house passing through the sky

P1 should keep at least one full moving day. This is not a flying-house spectacle; it is an ordinary bit of trouble that happens in Atheria.

A family's heavy things go first by platform: cabinets, pots, bed frames, preserving jars, boxes that are half full and refuse to be emptied. The truly light seasonal house is lifted whole at the end by a short-range lift ship. Before hoisting, the two upstairs households each drop their sail-tops a little, and the dye works take in their highest row of wet cloth, giving the house a flatter layer of air; no one owns that wind — a couple of shouts across the slope, everyone finds the balance again.

Once the house lifts off, what shows up as unfamiliar first is not the roof but the **underside**. Anna sees from below the old patch boards, new wood, drainage grooves, and reinforcement plates in various colors that are only shown during repair. The house passes slowly over people's heads, and inside it is still home: an unclosed drawer slides open; a table leg goes "thunk" on the floorboards every few seconds; a pot rolls to the other end of a room no one can see, and everyone below looks up at the same time. The moving householder swears on the ground: "I said tie that cabinet down."

The children don't chase the house itself; they chase its shadow. The shadow crosses bean racks, ditches, grass slopes, and people's feet; someone tries to stay in the shadow the whole time and is forced to detour by a ditch, and in the end loses to the house. Anna runs herself out of breath; when she looks up again half a curtain has been sucked out the window by the wind, as if the whole house were sticking its tongue out.

On the way, passing another household's window, someone briefly holds up a small thing borrowed yesterday, trying to return it directly since "we're passing anyway." They reach at each other for a while and can't quite make contact, and someone below shouts "now?!" In the end, the thing lands with the original owner.

The landing point is off by half a hand's width. No one gives an engineering speech. A circle of people pushes the foundation back straight, and then argues over whether the bed goes in first or the cabinet; the pot lid can't be found; only then does someone notice that a window in the new orientation puts the afternoon light straight against a wall. The moving day ends here.

This and the glider frame belong to the same visual experience: Anna's childhood keeps seeing the undersides that ground life usually doesn't let you see — ship undersides, house undersides, bridge underbellies, basket bottoms. Vertical life is her childhood visual common sense.


### P1 Third layer: the outside world enters in the form of seeds

This layer is not an independent stage that begins only after the first two. Objects, people, and pictures have been in daily life since Anna can remember — an unfamiliar dried fruit was already on the supper table, an unknown way of sealing was already on a jar, a place name has been said and the person is already gone. Anna comes to know the results first, and the sources later. The one true event of this layer is only: one day she brings these scattered things together for the first time in front of a chart. Objects / people / pictures should be scattered as fragments between the earlier life scenes.

Far places seep in through three kinds of evidence, but the way they seep is being constantly mixed into daily life, not queued for entrances:

**Object.**
Something Atheria does not grow appears on the table; Edmund's toolkit has wood shavings from elsewhere; a certain jar has a seal different from the local kind.

**Person.**
An outsider stands at the door and calls Edmund by an old nickname from long ago; a pilot says a place name Anna doesn't understand, and the next day the person is gone.

**Picture.**
Chalkboards, sketches, and charts gradually appear at the common house, the skyport, and the workshop wall.

The first time the chart truly carries weight, Anna's finger walks outward from Atheria's very small point. Crestline, Frontier, Riverbend appear on different lines; some lines stop at the edge of the paper — not because the way stopped, but because the paper is too small.

Before the chart officially bears weight, at least one of the names on it has already brushed against Anna's life two or three times — for example Riverbend: a jar's sealing method came from there; at dinner someone said "the water came early this year over at Riverbend"; someone rode a ship there the next day. Anna paid none of it any mind. Then, one day, her finger touches the same word on the chart: Riverbend. The psychological effect of the chart is therefore not "the world is huge," but "so all these scattered things that entered my life really do have places." The P2 reversal is: in P1, Riverbend comes to Atheria; in P2 / P3 one day Anna comes to Riverbend.

Names on the chalkboard, distant-place sketches on the wall, and the chart are in the same space for the first time.

Anna does not connect them.

The reader does.

CI is only the outermost layer of seed: a small mark in a corner of the chart she has also seen on an old iron plaque at the cliff root; an over-straight waterline down in the Lowerlands, earlier than any human memory; some stone foundation always warmer than its surroundings. She does not think of this as "ruin." This is ground.

### P1 Fourth layer: the body meets the sky before thought does

The strictly ordinary afternoons of P1 — an iron bucket rolling into the vegetable patch, waiting for a person half a day and they don't come, sitting eating a cold pancake, arguing over the window seat, a rain washing out the plan — must have enough page space. Only when the reader believes there are many days here that were not chosen by the story will the few later memories feel like memories rather than arranged devices.

Before the four segments of departure preparation begin, Anna's body has already taken two steps: the kite line (tension) and the Launch Day cables (shared load).

**Launch Day · A ship gets a sky (H2)**

Atheria's Launch Day is Melt Season. This year the workshop finished a medium ship, and today they are launching it.

The ship rises crooked at first. A wet cable is stuck in the groove of a new wheel — the rope's skin swelled with water and won't seat. People on the ground swap hands, ease the line, and shout across half a terrace. There is no orderly countdown; at some moment the pull leaves hundreds of pairs of hands at the same time, and the ship's shadow crosses the field ditches, windmills, and roofs.

Anna is still on the ground. The section of rope she grabbed has left a mark on her palm. What she sees first is not the pilot house, but the whole terrace giving a thing larger than a house a sky — cables, people, elevation, wind, dozens of hands pulling and releasing at once.

Launch Day expands the kite-line "one line holding tension" personal experience into a shared action. Anna takes part only in the "pulling" part, not the "flying" part. People and land first hold the thing up; then the air holds Anna — the glider frame later drops the same force onto her own chest harness.

The rest of the festival is messier: someone with their eyes covered, hand in a basket of iron, pulls out three items and holds them up; the crowd shouts wrong on two, and on the third he holds it in his hand and won't let go, saying "this one I know by feel." Someone insists on tightening an iron piece to the end with the left hand, and on the last turn slips, curses, switches to the right, switches back to the left, and finishes. A Council member speaks too long and the crowd bangs empty cups on the table. The smithy is nominally closed, but small metal toys for the children keep coming out from behind the furnace.

The four departure-preparation segments follow after Launch Day:

**Glider frame (whole-body sense) → moored ship → chart / chalkboard convergence → change of clothes.**

The glider frame is still a short glide low, hugging the Lowerlands' field belt. It doesn't throw Anna into the sky with one smooth lift; below, several pneumatic diverters along the field belt work in stages, gust by gust lifting the light frame back up.

The frame leaves the ledge and drops half a person's height. The chest harness pulls tight; the base of her tongue tastes iron. On the next beat the crossbar stops falling; her trouser cuffs are slapped up by warm air rising from below, and the whole frame lifts back a little.

At four or five meters there is no "panorama." Only **the far as ground-hugging**: stubble races backward beneath her, dirt ridges of the cut band flashing past one by one; she can't even see one ridge clearly before the next has reached her heels. In the distance a ship is tied to a field ring, and from this angle she sees its bottom for the first time — old patches and new boards jointed together, a face that always faces the ground. Farther on, a Plain Cutter is at work along the field, buoyancy taking most of the weight, the truly ground-touching work parts still moving in the soil.

The first gust of lift, Anna's whole body tenses. By the second field-band she is already waiting for it. A slightly darker dirt ridge comes underfoot — puff — the ground breathes out very muffled, and her hair, cuffs, and the glider surface's edge are all lifted at once. So the first impression she forms of flight is not "high" but a rhythm: **drop a little; the ground reaches up; go on.**

Its meaning is not that Anna "can fly" for the first time. It is that she discovers for the first time that air can really carry weight, and that the ground does not disappear because she has left it. Wind touches hair before flag; her body knows direction a bit before instruments do.

Afterwards she climbs into an unfamiliar ship overnighting at the port-terrace. It is not the work ship Edmund will later have, nor is it Gust. The ship isn't flying, but it still has temperature, small sounds, and the very low breathing inside the copper pipes.

Someone tells her:

"It's waiting for wind."

The end of P1 has no "I want to go see the world" declaration.

Ordinary days keep happening.

In the morning she squats and watches Chip.
In the afternoon she takes the cliff-hugging gondola down one level; she miscounts the station rollers' "ta—ta—ta" twice, and leans on the window to watch an empty freight platform rise slowly past on another cable.
In the golden hour, the quick moon hangs low near the Lowerlands' windmills.
The last scheduled line doesn't appear first. First the entire cliff C catches a low hum and returns it, and the wood of the loft eases with it; the ship itself comes over the cliff edge a beat later, lamps before hull. Anna counts lamps at the loft window, miscounts one, counts again. Above the Lowerlands, the rose-colored light-river presses over the fields, the windmills only silhouettes underneath it, turning slower than in daytime — not really slower, only at night she can count them clearly.

At night she lies in the loft, the waterfall's bass still there, the wood letting go now and then, the sound of the distant ship one heartbeat quieter than usual. That bass entered her body too early, at the same frequency as her breath, so she has never treated it as a sound — it is just Atheria's ground itself.

Edmund is again arranging his toolkit.

Anna's change of clothes appears next to his luggage.

Who put them there is not stated.

Edmund sees.

He does not take them out.

P1 ends.

The next day Anna is on the ship.

---

# P2 · The route becomes life for the first time (about eight — ten)
## Route before World

P2 is not "the great adventure chapter."

Its core discovery is:

**After leaving home, you can also have ordinary days.**

P1 is Atheria's ordinary life.

P2 is Anna's first discovery that the other points on the map also have people who get up, eat, wait on the weather, argue that a pot of something is too salty, take in the wash, comb a child's hair, sit silent at the doorway, sleep. Occasionally people also make things and look after pipes, but a place's existence cannot be proved by "there's work waiting for Edmund."

This is more important than seeing any first wonder.

P2 is the period in the entire saga where Anna, Edmund, and Miriam most completely share the same ship and the same route. Later memories of "Edmund on the ship" mainly come from stock built here, so P2 must not be compressed into a training montage.

The ship Anna boards is the Class II line ship Edmund looks after. It has a number, no name.

She already knows roughly what a ship looks like inside. The real new fact is not the ship, but:

**This time, after crossing the day-return circle, the ship does not send her home.**

Crossing Crestline she still leans on the gunwale: the roofs on both sides of the pass, the field belt of the north terrace, and the riverbank street stretched along the Ishkar pass beneath the ship in segments, and the smell of drying fish drifts into the turbulence between cliffs — forty thousand people in the town on either side of the pass are living, not just serving as a gate. Out of Crestline she looks back once. The cliff face changes from filling her view to a single line; the terraces of Atheria's lights are no longer to be found. At some moment she turns her head, and finds the low tone that had always been there is no longer there — the waterfall. She never treated it as a sound; only now, when it is gone, does she know it was always there. The ship engine's undertone comes up from her feet, similar to that of the mill, and also unlike it; this new bottom layer is too thin, too even, and hasn't yet been accepted by her body as "ground."

The first night she falls asleep in the vibration.

The next morning, the ground is no longer yesterday's ground.

This is P2's real departure.

## P2 itinerary ownership

During P2, the itinerary still lives in Edmund's head.

Anna knows "we're going next," but not necessarily:

why we stop here today;
which run tomorrow can connect;
where to change when the wind shifts;
which platform you can borrow a bed on;
if you miss which line you'll wait how many days.

She does not yet need to know how to get back, so she can go forward with peace of mind.

Edmund is not "bringing her to know her own social network."

He is just walking his usual annual route.

Anna is passing through it with him.

## P2 · Red belongs first to a place, asynchrony belongs first to the Loom

P2 does not put Anna in the red jacket. What she wears is an already-used, patched, life-stained old coat, the color not red. Red-earth appears with indigo and copper-leaf yellow only in Atheria's drying lines, work clothes, children's clothes, and household cloth pieces;

What really lays the groundwork for the later red jacket is the asynchronous life of things and words: Miriam's list remembers which package is for whom, which line came from which station, when a jar may already have expired; after a thing leaves a person, it still exists on the road for many days. All the reader of P2 needs to know is: Atheria has always worn this red on its body; and the people and messages on the Loom have never shared a unified present.

The red of P2 is only Atheria's color, does not hint that "later people will recognize Anna by red," and does not point to adventure, heroine, or pilot symbol. Edmund does not prepare a full set of red gear in P2, and the short apron is not locked into the same batch of cloth as the future jacket.

### P2 · skiff is a background ability of childhood life

The social feel of everyday skiff is close to that of a bicycle, a small boat, or a household tool in familiar terrain.
