Anna doesn't need a "first permitted to fly" ceremony, a "today you have grown up" conversation, a formal qualification or examination, or Edmund handing her the control stick and declaring some kind of inheritance.

In the early P2 she can approach the skiff through life first: watching others fly along the slope; passing seed sacks; hauling empty barrels; helping to move a craft; standing on the ground watching the wind flag; sitting at the rear or beside; being called to steady a side; watching an adult correct an approach angle once.

Late in P2, close to ten, on an ordinary day back in Atheria, let "the time is right" happen naturally.

Suggested scene: an everyday skiff needs to be moved from a lower flat dock to an upper berth, or fly a short loop along a familiar slope and back. Ordinary weather. No audience. No prior preparation. No "today we're specially teaching Anna to fly." Someone just says something like: "You take it up." Or: "Come back around that side, don't hug the water too close."

Anna already knows most of the movements, because she has seen them for years. Her first actually piloting it can still: come out slightly high or low; turn too early; need someone to grab the wing frame on landing; forget a very ordinary small movement; and the first thing after she's down is not celebration but someone continuing to tell her to move stuff.

This segment is at most 1–3 paragraphs and does not develop into a training sequence.

**Key feeling:** Anna later may not even be able to say exactly "the day I learned to fly a skiff." She will only remember: at some point in those childhood years, she went from always sitting to the side, to sometimes being told to shift one; and then, being told to shift one was no longer worth anyone's raised head.

**Permission layers:** being able to pilot an everyday skiff ≠ being able to run the Loom. Being able to make short flights on Atheria's familiar slopes ≠ itinerary ownership. Being able to move a skiff from point A to point B ≠ being responsible for a passenger airship, a weather window, and a route.

The real dividing line between P2 and P3 is not whether Anna can take the helm. It is that the itinerary first moves into Anna's own head.

The first solo flight is not the turning point; no one formally teaches her to fly, and no audience watches her complete the flight. From that day she does not "become a pilot" either — Archeon only has social habit, not a modern license system.

### P2 · Earth thread I

I · Earth thread — Anna's cognition of where she comes from goes from myth to fragment to reality, but the childhood stage does not form active historical investigation.

**P1:** Eira's Winter Veil story plants three load-bearing images: an old spirit turning out the lights; a woman who fell from the sky; a long scar / long line left in a desert. Anna keeps only the images, with zero historical structure. If the old childhood-arc "the cradle scoring the desert" folk image is kept, take it only as an oral variant of the third image; do not add a fourth hard canon. In different tellings, "ship," "cradle," and "the thing that fell" can be mixed together.

**P2:** Earth does not appear only once through "an old metal plate." Making use of Anna's several returns to Atheria, break the same past into four ways of existing:

Return I: object. Anna in the common house's word-desk, at home, or in an often-used space, for the first time seriously notices a metal plate with old symbols / an old handwritten page. Edmund or someone else gives only a nearly useless line: "the old ones left it." No explanation of Earth, Federation, Atwood.

Return II: oral story appears again. Winter Veil or an ordinary cold night, Eira again tells a part of the old story Anna has already heard. The point is not to "supplement lore," but to let the reader see that stories change.

Return III: a bone of writing shows beneath the story. Anna sees a repeated name / word form in old pages, old inscriptions, or copies, and a very faint correspondence appears with a sound in Eira's story. An adult can only correct a line. Anna gets a factual fragment but does not thereby turn toward investigating Earth.

Return IV: the story has already left Eira alive on its own. Several children tell, continue, argue, and get Eira's version wrong themselves. This time no new Earth information is added.

**Earth I's childhood endpoint:** the reader should get: "there really is history under these stories." Anna should only get: "long ago something did happen." Later Milo is the one who actually stops attention on the old Earth image, writing, and source.


## P2 recommended structure: two to three seasonal circuits, not one giant tour

### First round · 4–6 days

Atheria → Crestline / Lowerlands node → one field or river station → home.

Load-bearing points:

- First night sleeping on the ship;
- First time waking up in motion;
- First time eating a completely ordinary breakfast in a different place;
- First time discovering that Edmund at other places is known by others in completely different ways: someone has kept dinner for him; someone argues with him about whether some technique should be oiled first; someone returns a small tool borrowed two years ago and Edmund says "I forgot this one"; someone asks whether the message Miriam asked him to pass was received; someone recognizes Miriam only, not Edmund; a child doesn't know them at all. Of course someone might also poke a head out and say "that pipe is thumping again" — but this is only one of ten possibilities. Edmund has a life network, not a client list. The Loom scale of the P4 funeral thus reads as one person's life scattered across many places, not "the clients all came."
- First time knowing that things on the ship have to be moved before the jolt.

The gold goggles are truly worn for the first time.

Not a symbol.

Only something the family has always had, and now it is her turn to use it.


#### P2 old scarf origin · "It's cold up there"

The day she really crosses the day-return circle for the first time, before boarding, Edmund pulls an old scarf from the outside of his toolkit and hands it to Anna.

Gray-brown. Not fancy stuff, work-cloth washed many times, edges with two patch stitches in different color. A very faint smell in the cloth, and a bit of the body heat still left from just being taken off him.

He says only:

"It's cold up there."

Anna wraps it twice, all the way up to her chin. No ceremony, no "this is for you." Later, when the deck wind grows big, she instinctively pulls the scarf up to the same position. In P2 it is gray-brown work-cloth; in P5 / main line, when she has changed to a red scarf of her own choosing, the movement doesn't change.

The gray-brown scarf and the main-line red scarf are not the same object. The red scarf comes from her flying years 14–19; what is established here is the continuity of the action, not the continuity of the object. The narrator does not say "this movement stayed with her for a lifetime," only in later text repeats the movement.


#### The real freshness of P2 Round One · There is no "passenger seat" on a ship

The first thing Anna finds after boarding is not the pilot house but that every person has some very ordinary task.

Someone is putting away the freshly washed bowls; someone is bent over the cargo hatch counting sacks again; someone shifts a wet cloth away from an inspection port; someone stuffs a rolling jar into a wooden wedge; someone crouches to sort a coil of cable; the person at the stove argues about whether to add another pinch of dried leaf spice to supper. No one stops what they are doing because "a child is on a long line for the first time."

Anna's earliest participation is almost boring: steadying a stack of sliding bowls, taking two pairs of wet socks away from an inspection port, holding a pot lid for Sera, when someone shouts "foot" pulling her foot out of a cable coil about to be tightened.

**The first night has no crisis.** The only real event is: supper ends; a few lamps go out; the night-watch changes over; Anna prepares to sleep for the first time inside something still moving.

This layer pulls "flying" from spectacle back to habitation: she first finds that a ship can live a life; only afterward does she find that she wants to live her life on it.

#### The four "same event, three entry points" life segments of P2 Round One

**The ship is half a day late.** Edmund says the No. 2 valve to starboard is working hard today; Miriam says the person taking in cable at the last station wasn't right — "he was slower than last time"; Anna only knows that the smell approaching port is different from last time, the dry hay less, and a note of something burned added. All three explanations can hold at once. Edmund goes first to look at the valve. They still act on their own accustomed entry point first.

**Which side the bowls go.** The wind is from starboard; the bowls are put on the port side. The first time someone shifts them for Anna. The second time on a similar line, she moves them over before the wind picks up. Edmund sees; says nothing; Miriam sees Edmund seeing, and says nothing either. Growth shows through change of permission and default, without lines of praise.

**A cliff-face waterfall.** Edmund passes it every year and only lifts his chin: "seen it." Anna is about to ask; Miriam says: "your dad stood five minutes the first time he saw it." Edmund doesn't deny. The waterfall isn't a knowledge point; it only makes Anna realize for the first time that this "familiar-route adult" once had a first time too.

**Who at the next station needs what.** Miriam's list in hand: who asked to be told a line, which jar is to be returned, which household has one more mouth this time, which letter may already have expired, who always came to the platform in past years but didn't come out today. She doesn't drive this ship; she keeps the relationships along the route continuous — the list and the people she actually asks are the proof.


#### P2 three axes on one ship · The same world read from three entry points

P2 does not write Edmund / Miriam / Anna as three abilities taking turns on display, and does not equally distribute screen time. The three can be in one ordinary scene at once, each doing only what they naturally do:

- **Edmund reads ship and pipe.** Hand on copper pipe, valve body, cable post, or bulkhead for a beat; what he cares about is "what is different today."
- **Miriam reads people.** She doesn't fix her eye on machinery; she notices who has said less today, who has switched hands on a bowl they normally hold with one, who should have appeared but hasn't, who took a letter and slipped it straight into a pocket without opening it on the spot.
- **Anna reads a place from her body first.** She first remembers the air, the speed the ground moves, new noises in the hull, the smell on someone's clothes, whether the fog is cold or wet, from which side the wind blows hair into her mouth.

In one scene, only one axis is in the foreground; the other two pass by the edge of the action. The family is not an ability squad.

These three axes go on separating in P3: Edmund's main route gradually stops coinciding with Anna's; Miriam's relational reading stays in the list, verbal messages, letters, and judgment of life across places; Anna extends "reading the ship" to "reading the place / reading the route."

#### P2 named faces · only two, names may change before falling into prose

- **Sera (working name) · the ship's cook.** Round one asks Anna if she wants another ladle; round two doesn't ask, ladles more directly. Ladling soup she first lowers the bowl a bit so the ship's roll doesn't spill it. In the P4 funeral, if a familiar face is needed that Anna really recognizes, let her recognize only the movement of these hands, no explanation of relation.
- **Davin (working name) · deckhand.** First time using his hand to push Anna's bowl from the windward to the leeward side; second time doesn't teach. Can also carry the action of "in the morning, retightening a cable." When appearing at the P4 funeral, no identity explanation is required.

These two are always only people who have been alive on the same route for several consecutive years; they will not grow into "important mentors."

#### Return to Atheria I · Home did not wait for her

Position: end of Round One, 4–6 days.

The end of the first round must really write a "coming back" once, recognized first by the body — not a "Anna happily returned to her familiar home" line:

first hearing the waterfall's bass again; or once inside the familiar current, the wet-stone / hearth smoke / plant smells stack up again; only then does she realize she has been missing a sound for several days outside.

The Round One departure has already used "the waterfall bass disappearing" to prove her leaving. The first return must let this sound come back.

**Miriam.** She may be in the common house, on another terrace, handing a jar to someone, changing the list, or come home later than Anna. Her first response is still to ask whether Anna has eaten. Anna knows for the first time that she has been through a very big thing, but home has not stopped for that thing.

**Peers.** Friends at most briefly gather around. They are more likely to ask a very specific question: "what did they have to eat there?" "did you sleep on the ship?" "did you see any of that thing?" Then keep to their own games, arguments, help, and small business.

**Earth I · object.** This return is the most suitable time to place the first old object. Suggestion: someone is smoothing an old handwritten page at the common house's word-desk; or an old metal plate is being compared with a new copy; it is not an exhibit, and not a relic display. Anna notices symbols she cannot read. She asks a line. The answer only goes to: "the old ones left it." End.

Anna will not thereby want to know Earth. She only discovers: there have always been things at home older than anyone, only she has never looked at them carefully before.


### P2 shipboard texture main scene · Sound in a person's hand

The first night Anna meets is a soundscape without a dictionary, not only "falling asleep in vibration."

Atheria's waterfall bass is no longer behind her, and the cliff face no longer sends echoes back; without walls, it is not quiet. The engine lays down a bottom noise underneath she doesn't recognize, the envelope wall makes very fine creaking with pressure rise and fall, the wind through the metal frame pulls out a long, endlessly modulating cry; a certain cable occasionally takes force, and the whole ship seems to answer.

Anna lies in the hammock, her ears still looking up these sounds in Atheria's dictionary. Some match — wood under load, metal thermal expansion, rope friction; some don't match at all. She falls asleep with that bit of uncategorizable unease.


At night a colder gust of wind slips through the gunwale slit. Anna doesn't sit up, only pulls the gray-brown scarf up in the hammock to her chin. The smell on the cloth has been diluted a little by the ship's meal smells, wood, and cold air.

Below, "what wakes her the next day is a new sound → crew retightening a cable → sound in a person's hand" already fully carries this discovery.

What wakes her the next day is a new sound. She lies and listens for a while, can't tell which part of the hull it belongs to, and finally sticks her head out. Davin or another crew member is crouched at the rail, retightening a cable; the sound of the rope in his hand is exactly the note she just heard.

**Sound in a person's hand.**

Anna needs only to stand and watch for two beats. Later variations: once she thought a certain sound had a person behind it, walked out and found only wind; another time she thought it was wind, and at the corner saw a pair of hands. What she learns is not "sound = fault code," but that the same sound in different weather, different hands, different ships can have different meanings.

### P2 shipboard participation progression · measured by "fewer and fewer people tell her the next step"

**Early: actions handed to her.** "Grab this." "Stand on the left." "Pass this bowl." "Don't go through here yet." She only knows the current action, not why the route runs this way.

**Middle: she begins to pre-do the next step.** Wind picks up from starboard, she moves the bowls herself; before mooring she sees others taking in the hammocks, she takes hers too; when a crew member goes to grab the mooring line, she moves the barrel out of the way first. No one announces what she has learned.

**Late: she can take care of a small piece of ordinary shipboard life.** Setting bowls before mealtime; cooking a simple pot at the stove position; cutting root vegetables in wildly uneven sizes; when the night-watch comes back, a layer of the pot is already scorched; knowing wet socks can't block an inspection port; before a jolt, she flips cups over first. She first learns to live on a ship, and only later learns to let a ship go somewhere. Anna should have burned a pot at least once in P2, and Edmund still is willing to eat it, and Miriam looks at the two of them without summing up the scene.

This progression is not a syllabus. The only real change is: fewer and fewer of the next commands are given to her.


#### A settlement not carrying any discovery · arrived at, then gone

Between Round One or between Rounds One and Two there must be a T1 / small T2 stop that can stand without being named.

The ship arrives in the afternoon. Anna does only three things:

1. Follow adults putting two boxes and a jar to be returned into the sheltered doorway; one of the boxes is opened right away by someone in the house — inside is only salt, cloth, and a short note pinned to a corner;
2. Eat a hot meal at the long table; the people beside her talk about whether the wind will come early tomorrow, whose roof isn't in yet, a child who has finally started eating these past few days, that a fruit tree has borne too much this year;
3. Borrow a bed to sleep, and re-board at first light.

The ship lands on a hard patch outside town, the actual dwellings scattered along a shallow ditch into fifty or sixty households, one or two hundred people: low stone foundations, above them wood walls silvered by the sun; behind the houses are not workshops but small orchards, bean racks, and mills driven by shared water wheels. The household Anna sleeps in has its first floor as kitchen and grain storage, sleeping berths crammed under the upstairs slope roof; at night she hears the household next door sealing an oven, the sound of water through the ditch gate is closer than the ship's sound. In the morning what she first smells opening the door is baked grain and wet wood, not engine.

She may not later remember what this place is called. She only remembers the chipped bowl rim, the very low upstairs beam, that inside was drier than Atheria, that people didn't drop the door bolt until late at night, and that early the next morning outside the mill was full of the sweet smell of baked grain. Most places on the Loom are not narrative destinations; the reason the world is big is that at every point there are people who have carried the kitchen, bed, field, hearth, and the next day on to that place.

### Round Two · 7–10 days

The route can go to another branch.

Anna already knows how to take in the hammock, which side the bowls go, when not to stand on a certain section of the passage, when a crew "get up" means mooring.

Here should appear a set of dissimilar outpost lives. They are not a "task list," but four ways within P2's radius Anna is casually dropped into other people's day.

#### Sky sowing · skiff reseeding / boundary flag

At one outpost the topsoil of a long slope has stripped; the locals reseed low with skiff. In the seed hopper are not grains to be planted one by one, but grass, beans, and mixed seeds you can't tell apart. The wind is from the southeast; five or six skiffs are staggered along the wind direction, hugging the slope's ups and downs: one drops heavy, the next fills in gaps; people on the ground use long flags to mark the covered bands; wind pushes the flag over first before it becomes the seed's turn.

Anna at first only passes seed sacks on the ground, hauls empty barrels, and chases a container rolling into a shallow ditch. She looks up and sees the crossing tracks like shuttles moving over a cloth not yet woven; she looks down and it is all: blocked hoppers, seed inside pilots' collars, arguments over ratios, and already cold lunch.

Keep one messy wind-sense reaction: while Anna is minding the boundary flag, her body feels the side force change; she pulls the flag and re-plants it; at the same time the person with the official signal flag is also correcting. Two corrections stack, and the trailing dispersal ends up crooked in one segment. No one announces she did right or wrong, only the next craft fills in over the top. P2's perception at most reaches "hear / feel"; it is still far from the right to judge.

At end of work, first clean the seed hopper, roll up the flag. At supper, Anna keeps shaking seeds out of her sleeve; that sleeve's stock is bigger than anyone else's.


Here Anna only enters the skiff work rhythm on the ground; her actually piloting an everyday skiff herself later happens in Atheria's ordinary growing up.

#### A harvest-season run · wheatfield / straw rope / hot bread

The harvest-season run is the fullest of the year: wheat sacks, spare parts, a basket of things. Anna's attention is on how the crew kicks a stack of empty wheat sacks into the hold in one motion.

Before descent, the wheatfield is only a deep gold–amber band shifting with wind. The wheels touch ground, and the color instantly scatters into wheat stalks, awn spikes, hard mud, and fine dust stuck to necks. A local child half a head shorter than her slaps a straw rope into her hand: "Can you tie a knot?" No. He does it once. Second time she does it herself. From the third bundle on, her hands stop needing to fully think.

The reap-width race is not about who is a genius, but which line reaches the end first. The chant's beat comes from the waist: bend, gather, wind, tighten, release. Anna's line wins once, loses twice. Someone shouts at her "Atheria's line, you're slow!" At the row's end break, a piece of freshly baked bread is broken into her hand, still hot, the flavor different from the one at home.

The body core keeps only two straw rope marks, red. Not sightseeing, but a pair of hands going somewhere and, for a while, becoming the shape that place needs.

#### Changing windmill blades · cable / cover cloth / dust / new blade biting wind

From a low house someone pokes his head out and calls Edmund's name, then ducks back in. The old blade of the mill (square) is on the ground; the cover cloth taken off has been snatched by the wind, children and adults chase together. Anna steps into a tightening rope loop, watching only the sky. The local cable puller grabs her by the strap and yanks her back; her shoes pull out of the soft ground: "What you see is up there." Then he goes back to pulling cable.

In the afternoon the cover cloth suddenly catches wind and blankets several people holding the edge down. Under the cloth is all old dust and grass; someone coughs first, then laughter makes the people outside unable to hold the rope. When Anna crawls out, half her hair is white, and dust is still falling from her at the communal meal.

The new blade truly bites the wind — whoosh — the hub wakes from stillness, and the low vibration comes back through the soil into her feet.

#### Grass slope watching birds · waiting for the return ship / Edmund in frame

It takes an hour to gather what needs to go back. Nothing else on the grass slope. Edmund and Anna lie side by side, watching a few birds circle without flapping: one loop, another loop, each higher.

Edmund watches for a while: "Chasing insects."

Anna counts to the seventh loop: "They're playing."

"Playing at what?"

"That thing—" she points, cannot name it, "the thing in the wind."

The two lie there until the birds scatter. The grass is elbow-high, and an unfamiliar crumbly yellow flower brushes half her sleeve. On the return ship they're still arguing, he says bug, she says play, and neither wins by the time they land.

The value of this scene is in its uselessness. Not every station in P2 has to give Anna a new ability.

These four windows together define P2's world tone: **wonder and work always coexist, and work also allows a whole hour where nothing is learned.**

#### Deck night · some night of Round Two

Woken by cold, or hadn't slept at all. Scarf pulled to the chin, groped up to the deck. The night-watch is in the half-enclosure of the helm; looks at her once, doesn't chase her.

The wind at the rail is bigger than imagined. The nebula at this altitude is much brighter than in Atheria — no cliff to block; the light-river spreads right to her feet. Two moons, one high one low. A cloth on the cable post is knocked once, twice, by the wind. She puts her hand on the plank — the metal is much colder than the air; the heat of her fingertip is drawn away. She pulls back, and switches to the rope of a cable coil: the rope is alive, has a little give, and isn't as icy. The engine's undertone comes up through her feet; the thing that made her uneasy on the first night is now just part of the floor.

She sits down in a lee-side cable coil. When she fell asleep is not written. The next frame is waking in the hammock; who put her back is not stated.

#### A whole day of waiting on wind

The wind isn't right. The whole ship's people are received by a household with a long house by a ditch and an orchard next door for a day. The awning fits only some to sit; the rest naturally scatter into the kitchen doorway, under the trees, by the mill's warm wall, and along the stone steps of the ditch. Edmund naps under a fruit tree; Miriam pads her booklet on her knees and writes, a name, a line "ask about the jar," another name marked with an "x." Anna doesn't ask.

The people in the house go on with their day as usual. Someone spreads a basket of just-washed root vegetables under the window; someone flips the wet cloth upstairs; two children argue for a long time over who gets to sit on the warm stone behind the mill first. In the afternoon Anna runs along the ditch with them, through a lane between two houses only wide enough to squeeze sideways, and back under a bean rack; the ditch wall's moss is slippery, she falls once, scraping her knee green. What she finally remembers is not what this station "provided" but that the houses here are all only a few steps from water, and if you push open a second-floor window you can talk to whoever's on the opposite roof.

At supper someone brings the pot out under the tree. Very late, someone says: "we go tomorrow." No one explains why the wind hasn't come, and no one thinks anything of sitting idle a day.

In the evening a wind passes over the ditch; the drying cloth in the low places puffs to one side first, but the narrow strip on the roof ridge still hangs. On the ship, someone glances up, no one starts putting things away. Early the next morning both cloths pull flat the same way, and only then does someone loosen the first night cable. Anna doesn't ask why; that day has already taught her that some ships don't leave the moment wind comes; what they wait for is the same body of air finally saying about the same thing at several altitudes.

#### Between wheat sacks

Return trip in harvest season, hold packed with wheat sacks. The hammock has been given up to an old man on for a leg — Miriam has given hers up too, and the three squeeze into a gap between the sacks. Wheat dust in the nose. The sacks gently press her along with the ship's motion, as if breathing. Edmund's arm braces her back through a layer of wheat sack; she can feel the bone of his elbow. When she wakes, what she sees first is the coarse thread of a sack opening and a small patch of sky light leaking through a not-quite-closed hatch gap — not sky.

#### Return to Atheria II · Winter Veil / Eira's same story changes a bit again

Position: after Round Two's 7–10 days, or in the Winter Veil return segment between Round Two and the next departure.

**Eira story night.** This is not the first time Eira tells Anna the three images. P1 has already planted them. What is written here is: the same child, after a while, hearing the same story again.

Anna will start to notice: last time's version and this time's don't match. For example: "the fallen woman" this time first woke in the dark; last time someone said she was in a cradle, this time Eira says it was a "big ship"; a child insists the desert line was drawn by the woman herself; the adult in the back corrects "the ship scored it," then keeps on with their work.
**The feel of an Eira story evening.** Gathered at night; children's bodies pressed against warm spots; outside the sky enormous; a very old story growing on its own out of the room; certain images stay in children's heads more easily than facts.

The fairy-tale sensation comes from: five hundred years of oral transmission + a six-to-ten-year-old's mode of reception.

Eira is not a lore keeper. Not a history mechanism. Not someone who tells the same version every time. She is first of all an old woman in Atheria who tells lots of stories. When stories happen, adults can at the same time: mend something; shell beans; sew an edge; look for a bowl; doze; interject from the back over a certain detail; not be listening at all. Children can: rush ahead to say the ending; insist "it wasn't like this last time"; splice two stories together; refuse a correction because they like a certain version. Eira can: forget a name; tell two parts out of order; say "you tell it then"; take no responsibility for her own previous version.

Not every mistelling must have literary symbolism. Meaningless mistakes are allowed to exist: east told as west; a ship a little longer each year; a certain child insisting the old spirit last turned off the kitchen light; someone turning "fell down" into "came from behind the moon." Only this meaningless noise makes the three truly durable images plausible.

**Functions of the three images:** they live on because they are easy to remember, not because Archeon people preserved history accurately. Lights out: preserves the shape of CI going silent room by room. The woman falling: preserves the blurred human form of Atwood / Axiom. The desert scar: preserves the geographic mark left by the fall. P2 does not explain these three-layer correspondences. The reader can guess slowly. Anna does not need to know.

**Old object faint echo.** The metal plate / old paper seen in the first return may appear in the same room again. A more natural way: Eira forgets a name; someone in the back turns over an old page; reads out half a line; Eira, resenting that reading disrupts the story's rhythm, tells them not to interfere. Anna only develops a very faint feeling: this story and those strange old letters aren't entirely two separate things.

**Miriam.** In the story evening she still has to have her own actions. She can listen to only half a segment; go out midway; leave things for someone; cross an expired favor off a list. Earth thread and Loom asynchronous life are in the same room.

**Home senses.** The second time she returns, Anna no longer needs to wait until the berth to recognize home. She can recognize it from humidity, wind-gap smells, and the reverberation of the waterfall before she even enters the valley.

Anna still does not get a historical answer. She only gets a feeling: stories change, but some images never do.


### Round Three · seasonal repetition / between two returns home, the route starts to become habit

No more complete route write-out. Only keep the changes within repetition, letting the reader see that the route is turning Anna from "a child being cared for" into "a person who will naturally take a place on the ship."

At the same table the next year someone has sat in a different position; Sera no longer asks her whether to add another ladle, just lowers the bowl and fills it. Davin used to pull her from the cable coil; this time only glances at her feet — Anna has already seen herself.

The slope that was reseeded last year is greening this year, but not evenly: one band came in thin, and the other two ate it. No one brings up last year's crooked sowing.

Anna, when disembarking, begins to pick out the next thing to be moved on her own; takes in the hammock faster than before; when someone shouts "up," she no longer asks why; already knows it means mooring. At the stove she can still burn a pot. A person can be at the same time getting better and better at life on a ship, and still keep ruining supper.

Edmund is still knocking that same pipe in the same place, but the answer is different each year; Miriam's list too — one name crossed out, another with "don't forget to ask" added beside it.

#### Return to Atheria III · the time is right, but no one calls it a milestone

Position: after Round Two, in the first half of the seasonal repetition. Anna has already seen skiff reseeding at the outpost.

Keep the outpost skiff reseeding function first: Anna passing seed sacks on the ground, hauling empty barrels, watching crossing tracks, body feeling side-force change.
Then, after returning to Atheria, it happens. Some time later. An ordinary morning or afternoon.

An everyday skiff needs to move position. Anna has helped many times. This time the person beside her naturally lets her move it herself.

Optional actions: from the lower flat dock to the upper flat dock; move it away from the drying rack; loop around a familiar slope; go nearby to pass a light thing and come back.

The first segment of self-piloting doesn't need to be perfect.

No sudden storm, out-of-control crash, Edmund's touching hands-off moment, community gathering, or "her flying talent astonished everyone."

The ending is best very ordinary. After she gets down: someone is already talking about the next thing; Miriam tells her to bring something along; a friend complains she's blocking the position; or someone only checks the skiff, then says "fine."

This segment is not "Anna's first time as a pilot." It is only the reader's first clear sight: she has entered the age and life stage where an everyday skiff begins to be, like climbing steps, going down a cliff, tying rope, something she can do.

**Earth I · a bit of historical bone.** In the same return, add Earth I's third step, ideally half a day apart or on different days.

Anna sees the old page again in the common house or at home. This time a certain letterform / name brushes against a sound in Eira's story.

She asks: "Is this the one from the story?"

The answer at most advances one fact: "It wasn't the woman who fell from the sky. The ship fell." Or: "The line in the desert is real." Then the adult goes back to work. Anna: "Oh."

**Miriam / friends.** This return especially needs to show: Anna's beginning to fly a skiff a little hasn't changed how others see her. Friends can still be more skilled than her at other things. Miriam still can call her to do the most ordinary small errands. She is not "young pilot Anna." She is only Anna.

**Home senses.** The third time she returns, Anna can, before the ship has even rounded the last cliff pass, know: which side of the valley is wetter today; from which wall the waterfall's sound will echo back; which terrace is probably drying things. This is home knowledge, not pilot genius.

Home is for the first time not only "a place to come back and sleep." Anna begins to have a feeling: I have left, so I know better what this place is. The narrator does not need to sum up this line.

#### Return to Atheria IV · the story lives on its own

Position: near P2's end. Folded into seasonal repetition; do not add a fourth full circuit.

**Earth I = ZERO new information.**

Eira may not be present. Several children tell her story themselves. They: cut in on words; tell wrong; keep writing on; retell "ship" as "cradle"; put the desert line in a different direction; insist on a totally nonexistent detail. Anna can correct one, or not. She herself may even remember wrong. The function is to prove: Earth memory has already become living culture, not a channel through which the author drops lore on schedule via Eira.

**Miriam.** The list keeps changing: one name crossed out; another name with a line added beside; something Anna took out last time has come in first via another line; a certain sentence finally comes back, and the original question is already past. Continues to carry the asynchronous Loom.

**Friends.** At least once: on returning, Anna finds she has missed a small change in her friends' lives. It can be only: they've got a new name for something she doesn't know; someone has been going to another terrace lately; a game rule has changed; a certain person isn't around today; everyone is laughing about something Anna didn't experience. She asks two lines and catches up.

**skiff subsequently backgrounded.** RETURN III has already let the reader clearly see Anna herself flying an everyday skiff. RETURN IV no longer repeats "she can fly." Only use a background action to prove it has become normal: "someone tosses her the key handle / rope loop and tells her to move the skiff." Anna goes; the narration does not follow. This is more important than a second complete piloting segment.

**Home senses.** By the fourth time, Anna's recognition of Atheria no longer needs a close-up from the narrator. When she comes back she directly knows: which side of the wind; which layer of sound; which slope is wet today. Recognition of home has downgraded from "discovery" to bodily background.

Home is not a fixed box preserving Anna's childhood. When she leaves: home keeps living, stories keep changing, friends keep growing, Miriam's list keeps being crossed out and added to. Anna returns, and only re-occupies her own place.


Anna in P2 is still far from a craftswoman / wright acknowledged by others. She is now mainly: touching; hearing; following along; finding the next beat quickly after the movement is handed to her. Archeon children generally grow up with common sense about weight, tension, interfaces, and "when to move the body out of the way"; Anna's difference is only that she integrates the next step particularly quickly. **Quick hands are not a craft, but they are the raw material from which she later grows into a craftswoman.**

The one invisible measure at P2's tail end is still the same: fewer and fewer people need to tell her where to put her body next;

## P2's shipboard / ground breathing

An ordinary stop should try to use:

**Arrival fragment → ground work → a meal / a stretch of ordinary life → departure fragment.**

The ship is responsible for continuity.

The ground is responsible for variety.

The feeling the reader should finally get is not "Anna took the airship to many places," but:

**The Loom holds many kinds of life on the same timeline.**

On an ordinary morning in childhood, she moves the family's small skiff from one side of the slope to the other; Miriam tells her not to scrape the drying rack; she answers, got it. No applause, no "today you have grown up." Reading on, the reader will naturally infer: oh, this is just how children here grow up.

## P2 four returns to Atheria general rules

Every return to Atheria must advance four things at once:
A. Anna's bodily recognition of "home";
B. Miriam's offscreen life;
C. Peers and community not stopping to wait for Anna;
D. Change in Earth I's carrier.

But only in the first three of the four returns is Earth information allowed to advance a little. The fourth: Earth information amount = 0.

### Miriam four-return rules

Miriam is not an arrival NPC. In the four returns let her be in at least four different states:

I: When Anna returns Miriam is doing something else. Proves home has not been waiting for the protagonist.
II: In the Eira story evening Miriam only listens to part, while keeping up her list / object / person real actions.
III: The first time Anna is naturally allowed to move the skiff herself, Miriam does not take responsibility for certifying the growth.
IV: Miriam's list shows the asynchronous world: something has come back before Anna; a certain sentence arrives late; a name is crossed off.

Four times home, Miriam isn't necessarily at the door each time. P2's family warmth comes from: **people can come back because someone has been living here all along.**

### Friends / Community four-return rules

Each return to Atheria, the community must prove: Anna's route is not the world's clock.

RETURN I: others only briefly interested in her first trip.
RETURN II: at the Eira story evening, each remembers a different version.
RETURN III: Anna knowing a bit of skiff does not put her suddenly above her peers.
RETURN IV: Anna misses a very small friend change and reconnects naturally.

For later arcs establish that each person has an offscreen life. At the same time this makes structural precedent for Edmund's death: Anna is used from childhood to "I leave a place, and by the time I come back it has already continued a bit."

### Atheria senses return ladder

First return: she realizes she is home only when "the waterfall bass is heard again."
Second return: before entering the valley she starts to recognize it from humidity / wind / smoke / stone smell.
Third return: before the last segment of the route is even finished, she can already anticipate from which cliff wall the sound will return.
Fourth return: no more close-up on "she recognizes home." She directly acts inside familiar senses.

P2's home arc: didn't realize home had a sound → lost it after leaving → first return, hears it again → later, recognizes it from far away → finally, no longer needs to think about it.

# P3 · The Whole Sky (roughly ten to twelve years old)
## World / Itinerary becomes hers

The real dividing line between P2 and P3 isn't whether Anna can take the helm.

It is:

**the itinerary moves, for the first time, into Anna's own head.**

She knows which station she gets off at;
she knows when the next run comes through;
she knows which platform has a bed to spare;
she knows where to change lines if a gust shuts down the old one;
she knows which berth the boat home to Atheria usually leaves from.

She is still, often enough, only a passenger, a deckhand, a helper, a temporary hand on a line.

But she has begun to maintain her own continuity inside the Loom.

That matters more than any single technical growth spurt.

## P3 early · The red jacket appears after the itinerary

The red jacket is not placed at the automatic wardrobe-change seam between P2 and P3. It has to happen only after Anna has already completed one or two short-haul routes where "Edmund isn't at her elbow to hold her continuity for her": she is not yet an independent pilot, nor some heroic solo traveler, but she can already remember on her own where to get off, who's waiting for the next leg, which line runs steadier when the wind shifts, where to eat or bed down, and how to hand herself from one familiar face to the next.

There's no gift ritual. Edmund has her try on a newly made short jacket for the shoulders and sleeves; Miriam can grumble something domestic from the side. Anna asks why it's red. Edmund gives an ordinary answer:

"Red-earth dye is the cheapest; the color takes, and dirt doesn't show."

If needed, one more line:

"You can be seen two berths over."

Nothing along the lines of "after I'm gone," "everyone will know you," or any foreshadowing. Edmund already knows a lot of people along the line; once Anna begins to travel on her own, he mentions it in passing now and then: "My kid, the one in the red jacket, has started running this stretch too." It isn't a network handoff, not a passing of the torch, not a credential. At most it turns a first meeting from "no idea who she is" into "oh, the Atheria / Freedman kid in the red jacket" — enough to set out one more bowl, point to a room where she can sleep, offer a line on the wind ahead. Anna's reliability has to be built by her, later.

The red fragments in P3 are organized around a two-way motion:

- Anna's world widening: at one station someone asks "you're the one in the red jacket?"; at another they only recognize the Freedman name; farther out they've only heard about "the fair-haired Atheria kid dressed in a lot of red"; allow at least one mistaken identification, to prove it isn't an identity system.
- Edmund's world shrinking: a trip he was supposed to take is quietly turned into "you go with someone else this time"; something from a certain settlement is brought back by someone else; Miriam half-mentions that he's been running fewer of them lately; Anna does not yet call any of this illness.

Visually, let Anna go, in the red jacket, into places Edmund will no longer reach, but the narrator does not underline the sentence. In the moment of P3, Edmund's happiness is real, and so is Anna's opening world; the red jacket is first of all an everyday garment, and the reader at most feels the two trajectories drifting slightly out of alignment.

### P3 aboard-ship progression · From "doing the next step" to "knowing why the next step is coming"

In P3 early, the chart is still first of all a household object, not cockpit UI. Anna will hold the paper's corner down for someone, a cup still in her other hand; someone along the line says "not going that way today," she asks why, and instead of a lecture the person just points outside: "Look." She looks for a long time and doesn't see it. Hours later the wind actually shifts, and only then does she stitch the curve on the paper to the change in the sky.

In P3 middle, she starts looking at paper and outside at once, and starts hearing, in route arguments, the reasons that aren't strictly "flying": the clouds are turning, the wind window came late, someone at the next station is waiting on a basket of fungal starter, a certain platform has no bunk free tonight. She slowly learns that the shortest line on the map is usually useless. The line you can actually take depends on the wind, on the ship, and on whether the next dinner table will still keep a place for you.

In P3 late, she doesn't need to stand next to whoever's reading the chart to know what the ship is doing. She can be at the stove cooking something, hear the cable-note change, look up, and a few seconds later the ship starts to turn; or she can be half-asleep in a hammock, hear a segment of the envelope wall shift its rhythm, open her eyes, see nobody panicking, and close them again. She can already **sleep inside a living ship**.

But change ships, and she has to learn a little again. Every ship has its own accent: old frame, patched planking, cable tension, the position of the stove, the hands of its crew — none of them are the same. In P3, "learning airships" is not a universal language — with every new ship, a little more has to be learned again.

### The aural undercurrent is shown only through action

- **P1: things have voices.** Home, the mill, the terrace, the windwheels, the cables.
- **P2: behind the voices there is often a person.** She sees, for the first time, "that sound in a pair of hands."
- **P3: the voices have relationships to each other.** Ships, winds, baskets, crates, people, routes are all changing one another; a single source is no longer enough to explain anything.

Edmund tends to ask first, "what's making that noise"; Miriam tends to look first at "who's different today." Anna's later way of reading the world will allow both questions to coexist, but the text only proves it through her action.

Edmund in P3 no longer shares his main routes with her.

Anna rides other people's ships on cross-segments, long-haul runs, Class V, even along headings that don't overlap with Edmund's at all.

Her world widens.

His route, somewhere else, begins to narrow.

Both things happen at the same time.

---

## P3's three long anchor sites


P3 is not an alternating chain of settlement and spectacle. Anna has only two long voyages that truly reset her sense of scale, plus one small side-branch survey. Each of the three trips answers a question:

- **The Iris western line: how far the world actually goes.**
- **The Glass Plains margin branch: why the map isn't finished yet.**
- **The Amber southeast line: why somewhere this far away still belongs to the same civilization.**

Frontier City doesn't count as a fourth trip; it's the density shock she runs into when transferring between two lines. Marneth isn't a destination; it only bears the far-horizon sense of a limit on the Amber line. The spatial progression of P3 is:

**density → distance → edge → network → limit.**

Then P4 the sky suddenly narrows.

Orun is strictly reserved for P5, the fifteen-to-sixteen-year-old working period; it does not steal from P3's first-wonder density.

### Full Window A · Iris western line / Distance

The hard route does not change: **Atheria → Frontier transfer → Millhaven → Iris Hollow, roughly 2,600 km, two to three nights.**

She discovers, for the first time, that some places don't count as arrived at just because you've seen them. They first make you fly for two days, sleep for two nights, change the smell on your clothes, and only then appear slowly.

**Time structure, not imagery:**

- Once out of Atheria, the familiar acoustic of the valley falls away first; instead of stepping straight into new scenery, she enters, for several hours, a sky with no home-country acoustic reference.
- Millhaven's Sky Mesa has to "take time" in the middle ground: at first it's like a motionless cloud or a shadow, still there long after, and only in the end does it slowly slip past behind her.
- The first time she flies above the sea of cloud, the ear pressure changes first, and only after she swallows does the world get its sound back; looking down, there is no ground, no grand panorama offered, only her reflexively hunting for a river, a road, a roof — and finding nothing.
- Iris's fog blanket is already a layer along the horizon from about 100 km out; the last thirty or so kilometers are flown inside the fog. **Arrival isn't "the city appears," it's salt, cold, rotting leaf and wet wood entering the nose first — the trees have changed: the leaves are thick, glossy, unmoved by wind, half as tall as the trembling-leaved trees of the interior and many times denser. Actually seeing the sea comes later.**

**The feel of a place by the sea.** The sea itself isn't the sole destination. What Anna feels first is that a whole way of life has changed: the way the air clings to the skin; how ropes and timber are looked after; salt in the food; the way people at the pier watch the tide instead of the slope when they talk; the ship's own sound softer and wetter than inland; a two-moon tide calendar governing how a day is arranged. What the sea gives her isn't "I finally saw the sea," it's scale shock — **for the first time the horizon has no other side.**

**How Iris ends:** don't write the sea as the end of the world to the west. Once in port, let Anna see, in chalkboards, tidemarks, unfamiliar place-names, and people continuing to sail out, that the coast still runs on elsewhere: a name has "next" written beside it; another line is wiped out, leaving only half an arrow of direction; someone entrusts a message for a place she's never heard of; the people on the ship next over treat Iris as no more than tonight's dinner-and-sleep waypoint. Anna has just finished the farthest trip of her life, and yet the world, past "arrival," keeps getting bigger. This sea, too, is only one corner of an Archeon chart.

**Living weight:** at least one night not slept on the ship, and Millhaven and Iris cannot share the same "borrowed bed" image.

Millhaven's bed goes in a two-storey longhouse in the old town on top of the mesa. The mesa is windy; the house crouches lower than in Atheria, its outer walls silver-grey heartwood weathered by sun, with almost no large windows on the windward side; behind the house isn't a workshop but a small three-family drying yard for grain, always with a fine dust on the ground. Around midnight the wind shifts, and the row of windwheels along the base of the mesa switches direction almost in unison — a low hum sliding from one note to another. Anna wakes for a moment, not knowing what it was, and sleeps again. The next morning she carries a bowl outside and only then notices that here people treat the leeward door as "today's front door" — the table, benches, and drying baskets have all been shifted to the other side.

If Iris takes the other night, the lodging should be its exact opposite: three or four storeys of damp-resistant timber house stacked up from a narrow street above the tide line, wet nets and rain-cloaks hanging by the ground-floor door, salt in every window-seam of the upper storeys. Anna wakes first to eaves-water dripping into a channel, then to the smell of fish soup, damp leaves, and soap; the first ordinary thing on going downstairs might be helping two people move a water-fearing crate up higher, or just righting the drying rack the wind knocked over outside last night. The scale shock of the sea coexists with all of this daily life.

Spectacle and breakfast have to share the same location, but the space of breakfast / borrowed bed / ordinary work itself has to prove that the two towns are not the same Archeon settlement with a change of backdrop.

### Full Window B · Glass Plains margin / Edge

V13 still holds: (MARGIN ONLY). Anna does not enter the shadowless center; Glass Plains is not upgraded into P3's full global vista.

The correct version is a short charter, about 400 km south of Coppergate: a small ship, three or four people, going to an old CI secondary station coordinate to do seasonal weather / assay / windsock maintenance. The chalkboard reads only "south-line station check, need a hand," and Anna took it.

The destination is anticlimactically small:

- an old stone work-surface, a little flatter and a little warmer-grey than the surrounding ground;
- two anchor posts still usable;
- a windsock whose cloth has been replaced many times;
- a stone shed with walls patched over and over by later hands;
- a signal pole whose color has already dulled;
- a few numbers in old records to be re-copied.

Once there, the crew get on with what needs doing: measuring, recording, replacing, cleaning, eating. Anna hands things over, holds paper corners down, helps pull the new windsock up. Nobody is waiting for a "discovery."

If rain comes in the evening, Glass Plains offers one brief scene: the black matte ground sheeted with a thin layer of water; at night the two moons and the nebula fall to her feet, and depth perception vanishes for a moment — she doesn't know how "thick" the ground beneath her actually is, because underfoot looks as deep as overhead. Anna stands at the edge and doesn't step toward the center. The next day the sun comes out and the mirror shatters into wind-patterns; forty-eight hours later it is only black ground again.

**What actually matters is not the mirror.** Ask around and she learns that in the last cool season a survey crew stayed here for twelve days.

She's just sleeping in a small shed that has one more wall than it did when the last group came. Beside the old anchor posts there's a new water trough this year, and one corner of the shed has half a wall clearly newer than the rest — the color is different — and on the chalkboard there's one more line: "sweet water twenty km north / steadier wind." Nobody is talking about building anything here. It's just that every time someone comes, they leave a little of something that the next visitors will be able to keep using.

The definition of discovery here is: **connections beginning to appear, not treasure being found.**

### Full Window C · Amber southeast line / Network

The hard route does not change: **Frontier → Thornfield → Ironvein → Amber Reach, in ordinary flight about 2,500–2,700 km. At twelve or thirteen, Anna lives continuously aboard a Class V for the first time.**

The wonder of this window isn't how big the ship is, but: **a line this long is kept alive every day by a great many unheroic small actions.**

The moving society of about fifty people aboard the Class V has to be shown as life, not as a number:

- Anna may not have seen the captain all day, yet she already knows where the person in the kitchen keeps the knives;
- some crew run the whole line, some only run Frontier–Thornfield;
- a child younger than Anna or her own age, who has lived aboard for a long time, is the one telling her where the cups are and which side-hatch gets locked in the evening;
- the chalkboard has more than destinations on it: who's covering whose next leg, wind, who owes whom a cup, what parcel someone at a given station has asked to be carried;
- by the third night the dinner table is still full, but a few of the faces there are no longer the ones from the first evening.

**Every time the ship stops, a little of the life aboard changes.** No need for a manifest-montage to prove the network. Leaving Frontier, below decks there are rolls of cloth, a few finished valve parts, a person going to Thornfield for half a season, a letter that has to be handed to its recipient in person, and a jar nobody wants to make room for but someone agreed to carry to the next stop. At Thornfield some things are taken by the people waiting for them; someone puts a basket of root vegetables into the space left behind, adding a message for Ironvein in passing; someone who only rode one leg gets off, and by evening a hammock has a new owner. At Ironvein the same happens again, but with different contents, faces, and reasons. When Amber is reached, Anna could hardly say whether this is still "the same group of things aboard the same ship" that left Frontier.

People go this way too. Some run the whole line, some only stay for two meals; someone doesn't learn Anna's name until the third day, someone else on the first evening moved her cup to a place where it won't slide. That long table in the kitchen is there every night, but who sits at it changes slowly.

Anna's first understanding of a Class V isn't "what a big ship" but the sudden realization, at dinner one day, that the table, the pot, the cleats, and the side-hatch that always catches are all unchanged, yet three of the faces sitting opposite her on the first night are no longer aboard.

#### The public gallery

That timber room sunk half a level below the middle deck. The porthole is made up of decades' worth of small handmade panes, no two of them the same color; through it the sea of cloud becomes a slowly moving field of color-blocks, and half the face of the person sewing beside it goes green. On the bulkhead hang a few wet clothes, and beneath a cup, a slip of paper — Anna knows the writing, doesn't know the name. Someone is asleep against the bulkhead. The door is half open, and a gust pushes the damp of the fog inside, carrying the cold peculiar to high altitude and a cleanness that resembles nothing. After dark those panes turn into black mirrors, and there's only a shaded lamp in the passage and the envelope's breathing. For the first time she sees herself, in a window, aboard a ship.

#### The village at night

The breathing of a dozen people is not on the same rhythm. Someone climbs out of a hammock in the night to check the ballast, bare feet pass over the sunken plank, and the sound travels from one end to the other and back. At the weather desk a shaded lamp; the night watch is writing. The air isn't the same — wood, old cloth, sweat, something someone drank before sleeping, something seeping over from the envelope cells that has thinned the air a little. This is Anna's first night sleeping in the breath of complete strangers, not Edmund and Miriam's smell, not the night air of home. A rhythm along a section of envelope-wall changes. Anna opens her eyes, sees nobody worried, closes them again.

#### The box

At the long table one person eats with a box on his lap and refuses to put it on the rack no matter who suggests it. Anna doesn't know what's in it and doesn't ask. Three days later the person gets off at some station, the box still on his lap. Someone says afterward that it was fungal starter — that when it reaches where it needs to go, that person will walk into a bakery and hand the box to a pair of hands he's never met. Anna doesn't know this. All she remembers is someone who wouldn't let go for three days.

**Meeting in the air:** T0/T1 stations cannot receive a Class V. At least once, let a skiff / lighter rise up from the fog or from the heat-dust: a line hooks on, a person steps across, a bag is passed one way and a jar is passed the other, a few sentences are finished off in the wind, and the small boat drops back to the ground. Anna on the upper deck only clears the way, lends a hand for a moment, or steadies a line. A long-haul line is not "skipping over" small stations; it stays part of them through these short couple of minutes. This also carries the D line: the red jacket can be spotted from below as she stands on the deck, but "spotting" is not knowing, and knowing is not trusting.

**Amber's arrival is locked as in MR-05:** the black mesa-islands hang for over an hour in the heat-dust, at first like a great ship stopped in place; the bridge-net, the shelf-edge structures, and the valley-floor lighters grow out of it layer by layer; the smell of dry grass and iron-dust falls back and sun-heated timber, resin-lacquer, and the sweet-bitter smell of grain-baking come first. The Class V doesn't dock at the mesa; it settles on the valley floor at T3, and people and things then go their separate ways along lighters, cage-lifts, and walkways.

But once Anna gets up to the first mesa-island, the camera leaves the "cantilevered workshop." Here, first of all, is a whole platform lived onto full: near the middle, the old houses are narrow and deep, two or three stacked storeys of timber on stone footings, no back street behind but orchards and drying-yards for seed running straight up against them; the newer houses stretch out toward the bridge-mouths, ground floors of ovens, small eateries, or shops, with living quarters above; along the platform edge are public troughs, low walls that children chase each other around, and, on the windward side, always trays of seed drying. The second mesa-island can be visibly greener, its houses broken up by orchards; the third is more of a production surface, with fewer people and more drying-houses. The valley floor is another kind of life: shaded, longhouses, heavy ships, storage, and shadows falling down from above.

The CI seed-processing ancestry is still preserved in the shape of the drying vents, the temperature-stable underground silos, the moisture-vent shafts and the ramps, but readers should not just see "five hundred years later people jammed timber sieve-racks into an old space." What matters more is: the same set of inherited dimensions has been separately occupied by households, orchards, kitchens, bridge-mouths, ovens, and drying-terraces — nobody could cleanly separate the "old use" from "life today" anymore.

This line has to answer P3's biggest question: The Loom isn't a line on a map, isn't a system that optimizes freight from A to B; it's the social shape the sky takes on after many places have been willing, again and again, to keep a place for each other. Anna standing in the valley floor of Amber or on a mesa-island, watching a lighter carry two people away, someone watering a fruit tree, someone at the long table baking bread, someone under a bridge collecting a piece of freshly washed cloth, an old water-race running under the trees — nothing on its own is worth being declared the center. She stands there a while, and honestly cannot find what "the center of this place" is.

## P3 texture references · A body that has ridden lines across a continent

The following are dispersed into the three anchor sites — Iris, Glass Plains, Amber — and the transfer segments between them. Each piece is used once, or in a few small variations, not all stuffed into the same flight.

### 1. Non-visual signatures accumulate · a place comes first through the body

P3 should have Anna accumulate at least six mutually distinct "smell-first / hear-first / press-on-the-ears-before-you-see" signatures for places:

- Atheria: wet stone, water-mist, sound thrown back by the slope;
- Crestline: smells swap out abruptly on either side of the pass, drying fish and river-smoke arriving on both banks of the pass mouth along with the turbulence;
- Millhaven: a slight pressure drop, steady lift, milling and drying-grain smells;
- Iris: salt, cold, rotting leaves, fog swallowing sound;
- Ironvein: dry grass, hot mineral dust, the low frequency of the smelters at distance;
- Amber: sun-heated wood, sweet-bitter resin, the bridge-net's continuous whistle.

These memories are the antecedent to the P4 fallow period's "damp planks, smells, market sounds arranging themselves into routes on their own" — navigation-grade sensory memory has to accumulate in P3 for P4 to call on it.

### 2. Other people in the sky · her family's ship is not the default of the sky

In P3, within a Thread / junction's field of view, at least three kinds of ship "not from this line" should be seen:

- Frontier's line-route large ships: big, slow, low-pitched;
- Iris / distant-line couriers: fast, small, unfamiliar in color and accent, some of which don't stop at Atheria at all;
- Skiffs / sailing airships out of another region's tradition: sail material, sail-furling motion, and handoff shorthand all different.

The point isn't an encyclopedic ship-class survey; it's Anna slowly realizing that Edmund's methods are only one place's methods, one generation's.

### 3. Ordinary small partings · the person is gone, the line is still there

The Loom in P3 should be full of small, unsentimental partings:

A crewmember gets off at Millhaven; another switches to a different line at Thornfield; Anna spends an evening counting cable-clips with a child, and wakes up the next morning to find they've already left with a ship that weighed anchor before daylight; someone says only "probably running this way again next season," no promise.

This layer later makes a structural difference with Edmund's death: what Anna is familiar with from childhood is that "a person leaves the current station, but may still turn up on the next line." Death, for the first time, gives her a leaving that will not turn up at any next station.

### 4. Different ships have different accents

The first time she moves to a ship with a completely different crew, three or four small differences are enough:

On the Atheria-line ship the bowls are on the left, on this ship on the right; before takeoff Edmund used to tap the brass tube once, this pilot never does; someone lashes his boots to the leg of his hammock while he sleeps; this ship has to wait until the last person still watching the ship has come down to the stove before dinner starts, on another ship whoever is free eats first.

Anna isn't collecting quaint customs. Through comparison, she's finding out for the first time that "familiar" is not the same as "correct." Her own P5 flying vocabulary will grow up out of a mix as a result, not as a copy of Edmund's technique.

The first time she spends the night on a ship that isn't Edmund's, she cannot sleep — not because the soundscape is new; she has long been used to a ship. She turns over, and beside her isn't Edmund's hammock; it's a snoring stranger. The air isn't the same: not the smell of brass filings in Edmund's tool-bag, not the sweetness of Miriam's herb-cloth. She'll sleep on many ships later, and never again miss this particular quiet. But the first time still stretches the night out long.

### 5. Everyday version of small danger · difficult work done quietly

The danger in P3 does not escalate into plot crisis. At least twice, let Anna watch skilled people handle a small risk:

- Docking in a crosswind, an old cable-hand rests his hand on the cleat and waits until the last beat before he ties on. Anna asks why not sooner, and he only says: "Too early, and you're fighting the wind."
- One envelope-cell slowly leaks — no rupture, no fall. The crew changes a liner in mid-air, Anna passes tools; through the opened maintenance hatch she sees, for the first time, that the inside of an envelope is bigger than she'd imagined.
- In the fog, no "heroic intuition" is used, but fog-horns, radio, instruments, old landmarks, and cross-confirmation among several people take the ship in.

The mood is not thrill, it is **competence making the world credible.** Skilled people are quiet when they do hard things, and the beginner watches from the side.

### 6. Reading the sky / reading the ground · the stages of noticing

The first time Anna is allowed to stand near the navigation position on a Class V, she gets only one sentence and a gesture, not a full lesson.

Someone doesn't look at the map first, but at the clouds:

"There's a river under that."

Or:

"The forest canopy is a bit shorter there, the wind will turn."

From then on Anna starts reading the ground from up high: a deep-green band means water has passed through; too-straight dark lines could be old channels or old pipe; the sudden white on a river isn't stone, it's water beginning to spread out somewhere much farther on.

At another port, Anna sees for the first time someone who doesn't bother asking "which way's the windsock." The windsock on the pier is already blowing east, but a short piece of cloth on a mast partway up the slope is still pulling south, and behind a house the stove-smoke is running along the ground at a lower level. The pilot only says "a bit longer" and keeps the ship out for another circuit. The next time back, Anna goes first to the several places whose winds aren't at the same height. She still can't make the call to bring a ship in for anyone; only, from here on, "wind direction" is no longer, in her eyes, always a single arrow.

Another time, let a pilot lay a hand on the brass tube along the rail and close his eyes for ten seconds, then say the wind has turned — the tube has cooled a shade in the last five minutes, a southerly bringing the damp cold of the Azure Main up. Anna reaches for it too, and feels nothing. This gesture is the same shape as Edmund's waiting on a pipe wall, only what he's waiting for isn't a gasket but the sky. Do not let any character point out the connection. The captain, the helm, the cable-hand, the envelope-watcher each have their own gestures and shorthand; she doesn't yet have a place among them, but she stands among readers.

### 7. One concrete Class V meal

At least once, press the "moving society of about fifty" into a single meal:

At the stove a single pot serves three rounds in a row; those watching the ship eat in shifts; someone has just come off a skiff and the earth on the soles of his boots is a different color; someone gets called away halfway through eating; an empty seat doesn't mean somebody's missing, that person is on another level of the ship. Anna remembers five faces on the first evening; on the second she finds two of them are gone; only on the third does she realize that a certain person always paring things in the kitchen doesn't ever "come down off the ship" — he lives on this line.

Let readers get the village feeling on their own; if "a village in the sky" is ever spoken, let the crew let it slip in a half sentence.

### 8. A very small place, but one where people actually live

At least once, the Class V doesn't land. The ground is only a field, twenty or thirty roofs, one short street, one T1 windsock. A small skiff comes up with two boxes to trade, takes back a person and three sacks of goods. Anna may not even have caught the name of this settlement.

Function: to make the reader understand that a small dot on the map isn't a failed version of a big city.

The people here don't exist for the sake of a shipping line. They have fields, meals, roofs to patch, a wind they know, days that don't need any outsider's participation. Ships are willing to stop because there's always someone who'll take a line, offer a mouthful of water, or because there's a certain thing here that comes up as a surplus every year; and a ship, going elsewhere anyway, will pick it up in passing.

The connection is what grows out of these lives repeating themselves, not a "network role" this settlement was ever required to fulfill.

### 9. Frontier City · Density, does not take a fourth anchor site

Anna's family-accompanied close-range first visit to Frontier at eleven still holds as locked. The first-wonder here doesn't rely on "more advanced," it relies on **too many things happening at once**:

Twenty-one pylons / terrace ranges / skybridges / the gondolas' "clack-clack-clack" / Spire Docks / Float Rail / market awnings / vertical farm walls all entering the field of view and the ear together. Atheria's difference in height is natural slope and cliff; Frontier's difference in height is what people have stacked out of foundations, bridges, terraces, and towers. The first time she stands on Broad Terrace looking down, underfoot isn't a cliff-face but more terraces and skybridges, and overhead isn't the sky but another pylon's cantilever.

Give Anna one very small human anchor: she stands still on Broad Terrace only because she doesn't know which level she's supposed to cross the crowd on; or she looks up at a skybridge, and the next second below her someone tells her to move — they're carrying several baskets across the platform's edge. The city-wonder has to be bumped into by everyday life right away.

### 10. Marneth · Limit, no expedition scheduled

On the Amber line's Thornfield → Ironvein leg, the V02 distant view stays: a white line on the northeastern horizon that doesn't move all day, higher than any ship can fly. Anna looks at it a whole day. Nobody suggests going.

Its function is only one sentence: **there are things in the world that don't turn into a route just because you want to see them.**

### 11. Cinderlode restraint rule

P3 may keep the canon of Anna having "come there with Edmund as a child," but no full third tour. At most 1–2 bodily fragments:

- The floor there is warm underfoot at night;
- Some of the sound comes back from beneath the ground;
- There is a steam column at the far end of the sea of grass;
- Some cargo out of Cinderlode is opened at another station.

The full volcano-mine-valley palette is reserved for later (Cinderlode has sixty thousand people, it isn't a single-pole iron kingdom; Ironvein has its own smelting too) — Cinderlode is best used later to give Anna the feeling of "I thought I'd seen a large world as a child, and it turns out there's another whole world further east." P3 has to make sure the world, at the end of childhood, is still not used up.

## P3 · The CI substrate has to become concrete

CI in P3 isn't an "ancient thing cameo," it's what explains why three ordinary people can start living relatively quickly in the neighborhood of an old coordinate, why a continent-scale route line can get so much low-cost support.

The core sentence:

**CI is not maintaining civilization for the people of Anna's era; it is doing away, for them, with the "from-scratch" layer.**

A totally blank site has to solve from zero: stable water, wells, water quality, flood drainage, geology, load-bearing foundations, wind, arable soil, mineral information, energy access. Decades may not be enough.

A site landing near an old CI intervention point may, five hundred years later, already naturally possess four or five of these:

- The well a hundred-plus meters down there is still tapping a stable aquifer;
- The thing beside it that looks like a shallow ditch is the old flood drain, a storm won't wash the houses away;
- Three hundred meters away a work-surface has long been compacted and bites into bedrock;
- The unnaturally straight belt of trees up the slope is an old windbreak, propagated on its own into a whole mature forest strip;
- Old assays record copper two kilometers to the east;
- The old thermal well can no longer regulate itself, but the bore hasn't collapsed, and a mechanical pump slotted in still brings hot water up;
- A gravity-fed water channel has not been "operated" in five hundred years, it just keeps running downhill.

So the everyday tone of "reconnecting to a coordinate" should be:

The water's there. The ground is flat enough. The slope won't slide off in the first rain. There's wood to cut. The wind knows where to come from. Someone has surveyed what's underground. So set up a shed first.

Not "we've discovered ruins." Three people flying in aren't facing wilderness survival; they're facing abundance.

### The three ages of CI have to coexist

Don't have it all be the same kind of "old installation" the whole way through. Let Anna see, along the way, three completely different ages of civilization — the Amber line can carry all three layers at once.

**First kind: has already become nature entirely.**

From the air, an unnaturally straight deep-green band. On landing, it's only trees, damp earth, and vegetation somewhat thicker than what surrounds it. Underneath could be an old water channel, an old pipe, or a rerouted river segment; after five hundred years of seepage and root spread, the earth on either side of the line is simply thicker. Locals only call it by a place-name; children pick things in it, and no one thinks of it as CI. The tens-of-kilometers deep-green band outside Thornfield can carry this layer.

**Second kind: still an extraordinarily useful foundation.**

Ironvein's T2 flat pier: several huge, faintly warm-grey self-healing geopolymer slabs, larger by scale than the timber shed later built on top of them. It's been five hundred years; grass has grown along the edges, one corner has had a drainage groove chiseled into it by locals, another side has heartwood laid on it, and new basalt-fiber masts are bolted directly into what were originally its load points. What CI put down at the time may not have been designed for airships at all — it was only the loading pad of a regional material station — but today's heavy freight airships love it here. Characters saying "park it over on the old stone" in daily speech is enough. No explanation.

**Third kind: the shape of the function is still there, the human content has been completely swapped out.**

Amber Reach's seed-processing ancestry (already logged as hard canon in MR-05): drying vents, huge temperature-stable underground silos, moisture-vent shafts, ramps that sort by weight, excellent drainage, a whole test-planting area improved over decades of soil work. The machinery is long gone. But the shape of the buildings determines how people use the place — five hundred years later, the timber sieve-racks, winnowers, and drying-houses that the humans themselves made are all wedged into these spaces. Readers will suddenly understand: **CI isn't here, but the reason the city grew into this shape is that CI decided it.** That is much stronger than "there's still a magical machine inside a ruin."
