**Temperature of a CI scene:** Ask of every CI scene, does this thing feel like touring the past or like using today? Prefer the latter. A well drilled five hundred years ago, somebody washing pots beside it today; an old concrete foundation, cloth spread on it to dry today; a CI water-race, a child crossing it barefoot; an old wind-measuring tower now only a stump, and locals tying an airship to it; an old temperature-stable underground silo, with this year's Amber seed piled up in it. Not "the old civilization is still alive," but "what the old civilization did has entered the chain of cause and effect so deeply that you can no longer tell where it ends and where today's people begin."

### CI points have to be lopsided, and it's the lopsidedness that produces the Loom

A Secondary Station has never been a universal starter pack.

One point has fantastic water but no shipbuilding timber; one point has geothermal and glass sand but so-so grain; one place has so much resin it sticks to your shoes, but no continuous high-temperature furnace; a mine point has shallow resources but such rotten wind fields that a large ship can barely get in; another place has ordinary resources but sits on a big CI-era load-bearing foundation, and just happens to be at the junction of two seasonal wind corridors — so five hundred years later, inexplicably, it has grown into a ship-transfer town.

This bites directly into ROOT 09:

**It isn't that every place lacks everything and therefore must trade; it's that every place has something in nagging excess, and no single place has all the conversion steps.**

So Amber's resin has to move, Ironvein's iron and copper have to move, Millhaven's milling capacity has to move, Canopy's heartwood has to move; basalt itself is not scarce at all, what's actually expensive is the handful of 1,500 °C places that can turn the stone into serviceable fiber. This is not a scarcity economy, it is a **conversion + connectivity economy.**

Every time P3 enters a distant settlement, before writing prose, at least answer three questions:

1. What is here in unusable excess?
2. Which step of conversion can this place not do / not worth doing itself?
3. Which Thread most recently carried this surplus out and brought the shortfalls back?

### The sense of support for continent-scale routes

What a Class V / long-haul route actually needs is not modern airports the whole way, but a string of reliable opportunities:

Something to tie on to; water; being able to top up hydrogen or connect to the next supply point; being able to avoid weather; a hot meal; being able to swap rope, patch an envelope, forge a fitting; and if things go really wrong, being within a few days of somewhere with deep repair capability at T3/T4.

The existing T0–T4 system already lays down this backbone. So a continent-scale route line isn't "city — city," it's:

**T4 — T1 — T0 — T2 — a stretch of empty sky — T1 — T3**

Where some of those T0s are, in fact, CI-era foundations.

So in prose, civilization should fade out gradually, not switch binarily:

Big city → farm belt → manned T1 → seasonal cargo shed → unmanned T0 → a well labeled "good water" on a map → a question mark unverified for three years → and only then, sky with no line on it.

That's the spatial texture of an exploration age.

"Someone" sometimes even precedes a roof. After long-abandoned ground, a small piece of cloth is suddenly moving on a thin pole in the distance; only closer in does one see two houses behind the slope, one stove-smoke plume, and an anchor-ring half-buried in grass. That cloth doesn't necessarily have a name, doesn't necessarily belong to a Guild, isn't necessarily a territorial flag. It's more like a living waymark on an old road: it means that at least someone has watched this wind here many times and knows at what height a moving thing is most useful. Elsewhere there's no cloth at all — a line of smoke, a pruned canopy, a stone cairn, or a light is enough. "The first piece of cloth" is not a fixed shot for discovering a settlement.

## How P3 uses fantastical landscape

Wonder is not a synonym for a quest destination. Anna's adventurer temperament is defined by response — she gradually begins to ask "who's come through here," "is there water below," "when does this wind reverse," "if this line could be run, where would it connect to." The terrain in P3 doesn't hand her answers; it hands her questions.

CI's terraform gave Archeon a world that has already been alive for six hundred years. Grass that has grown for six hundred years is grassland; trees that have grown for six hundred years is forest; a channel maintained for six hundred years is "a river that's always been here." Seen through Anna's eyes, this is not reconstruction on top of ruins — this is an abundant, beautiful, living world whose bones happen to be metal and concrete, in the same way that Earth's bones happen to be stone. The writing temperature shifts from "recording a civilization" to "you want to go there." A pilot's attraction is not only in the flying itself, but in every place having its own smell; when you leave, there are still more unseen places ahead.

The following five terrain sections are dispersed among P3's three route trips (Iris line, Glass Plains charter, Amber line). They aren't five anchor sites; not every one needs an overnight stop. Some are only flown over, some borrow a gust of wind, some are one-and-a-half days that Anna forks off from the main line. Taken together, the five give P3 a spatial progression running from "deep time → physical constraint → open-hearted exhilaration → continuous surface → civilizational variety."

### 1. The heartwood forest wall · midway on the Iris line (about 23°N, 20°W · between Millhaven and Iris)

**Flown over. Doesn't set down.**

West of Millhaven, high-plateau grasslands hold for a whole day. Then the color of the ground begins to change — first, strips of deep green, thicker than the grass. Fly on a bit and the strips turn into a continuous surface, and then there is no ground below: it's all canopy.

This is the boundary where the western edge of the Thread enters the CI first-generation windbreak corridor. The strips planted five hundred years ago died out long ago on their own, but subsequent forests kept propagating along the same line, and now it's a mature heartwood belt tens of kilometers wide. From flight altitude it isn't that "the trees are tall"; it's that the behavior of the clouds is suddenly different: over the grass the cumulus bottoms are smooth, over the belt the cloud bottoms are lower and torn into wisps. The air is changing, too — first humidity, Anna's lips no longer cracking; then dew begins showing up on the gunwale each morning from here on.

Nobody gets off here. Anna asks the person next to her, "why is it suddenly all trees here?" and the reply is "old line," or "always has been." No full explanation.

Anna for the first time reads a CI boundary from the air — not a ruin, not archaeology, but a line drawn five hundred years ago that still today decides where the clouds go. The consequences of the old engineering have grown into nature itself; it is also, once again, in the middle of P3, her ground-reading-from-above being practiced.

### 2. The mesa fault zone · midway on the Amber line (about 17°N, 22°E · between Ironvein and Amber)

**Class V pushes through by the valley corridor. Doesn't stop.**

South of Ironvein, the Thread's continuous farmland starts to break up. Bare rock outweighs field; the ground drops one section, then another, and finally becomes scattered flat-topped mesas and heat-dust — the outpost of the mesa-island group.

For the first time, Anna sees from the deck a fissure that cannot be walked across. A vertical cliff several hundred meters wide, two walls staring at each other, nothing to see at the bottom, and the heat pushing the mooring lines outward. An old CI ground infrastructure — a transport ramp or pipe channel — runs from the northern mesa edge right up to the break and is cleanly severed. Five hundred years of rain along the break face have washed out white salt-stains. On the mesa opposite, the same line continues at a slightly different height — the two used to be joined.

Somebody on that opposite mesa is hanging out laundry. Two hundred meters away. Without flying, there is no way to reach them.

The Class V pushes on slowly along the wider valley corridors between mesas. Sometimes both cliff walls are simultaneously on either side, and the ship's shadow on the rock is narrower than the ship.

Anna sees for the first time that flying is not a convenience but a necessity. She also sees the ground itself in motion — that old line has been displaced three meters by geology. CI's bones and the planet's bones in one frame: one metal and concrete, one basalt, both working in time; the former has been cut by the latter.

### 3. Rimstone Tarn · a high-plateau side-branch off the Amber line (about 18°N, 24°E · roughly 60–80 km north of the main line)

**Anna splits off from the Class V, takes a skiff up for a day and a half. The high point of P3.**

The Class V ties up at a T1 station below. On the chalkboard is a coordinate and "need a hand" — a skiff has to bring sealing pieces and a few letters up to the mesa top. Anna takes it. This is her first time, without Edmund and without the Class V, going, on her own judgment, to somewhere off the main line.

An old volcanic mesa in the transition zone from Ironvein to Amber. The lava cap has protected the sedimentary rock underneath. The mesa top sits at about 1,200–1,400 m elevation, 15–25 km², cliffs of 200–300 m along its edges. At the center of the mesa top, the lava cap has collapsed into a shallow depression; rainwater and groundwater seepage pool into a shallow tarn — two or three km across in the warm season, no more than four meters at its deepest. The water is on the warm side: below it is a CI-drilled geothermal test bore, five hundred years on and still bringing warm water up.

**The skiff climbs.** Threading between heat-dust and broken mesas, she smells grass first — not the mixed farm-forest of the Thread, purer, only wind and grass. Cool but not cold. The air thins a little; the ears know.

**She crests the cliff edge.** An unbroken sweep of high pasture runs out from underfoot to the tarn's shore, the color of the grass going from pale copper to deep gold, wind walking bright-and-dark bands across the grass's surface — like a hand slowly wiping down a bolt of cloth. The tarn reflects. Far off on the pasture there are several long-haired somethings moving, half a head shorter than a thorn-goat, mouths burying themselves in the grass as they walk. CI foundation blocks and the bases of wind-measuring poles come up out of the grass, warm-grey, edges already melded with the ground. Far off, one thread of smoke — a bread oven.

Anna steps off the skiff, and the wind blows all her hair to one side. Her foot goes into the high grass, knee-deep.

**On the ground.** Three to five people, resident. A pair from Ironvein originally there for assay and weather logging, who found the warm-water well and the wind good enough to keep a small herd of highland animals and a patch of root vegetables. The stone-walled house is half built, the other half canvas. An old man from the Amber direction runs the wind-electrolysis shed at the mesa edge — tops up a little hydrogen for passing skiffs; there's a machine that won't start, and every time he kicks it twice.

She helps carry things under the shelter. The heat of the bread oven comes up through the mesa's basalt, and the smell of bread mixes with the smell of the plateau grass.

**The warm-water well.** Water spills out over the top of a mossed metal column, running along a track that has already worn a channel of its own toward the tarn. She crouches and puts her hand in — warm, not hot. A layer of orange mineral sediment on the bottom. Looking up, from well to tarn, a band of deeper green — where the warm water has passed, the grass grows taller and denser. For six hundred years this well has been irrigating this line.

The old man kicks the generator twice. On the third kick it starts.

**Afternoon at the edge of the mesa.** Below is thin fog and broken mesas, further out the Thread's farmland, further out still the horizon. To the southeast, the Amber mesa-island group like dark ships stopped in mid-air. To the north, Marneth's white line. For the first time she can see, simultaneously, two directions of "far" — the one already been to, and the one not yet — coexisting at the edge of this plateau.

**Evening.** As K2V is about to set, the whole pasture turns red-gold. The tarn's surface turns a copy of the sky upside down on the ground. Frogs at the tarn's edge — that stuttering call from the irrigation channels here becomes a continuous low chorus, because there's more water. A dragonfly skims the tarn's surface, wings trailing a wet mark. She sits on a CI foundation eating her bread. Something comes up out of the grass to sniff her hand — shaggy, unafraid of people; she has nothing for it, it stands two beats, and goes off.

**Night.** The nebula runs from one side of the horizon to the other. The tarn reflects one over the other — one river of light overhead, one river of light below. No brass-chain sound; there are no big ships here. Only wind, grass, the generator's occasional hum, the warm-water well overflowing silently. She falls asleep in the grass. Her clothes are half-damp the next morning.

The skiff goes down. She looks back once — the mesa top is already a deep-gold line above the cliff, and is quickly covered by cloud. The Class V is still waiting below. Someone at the long table asks her how it was up there. She says: "There was bread."

The temperature-core of P3 is not discovery, quest, or vista. A place that doesn't yet have a name; CI's bones have become terrain; a few people have grown their own lives on top of it. At the same time, the itinerary-ownership is completed: Anna, for the first time, on her own judgment, went to a place that isn't on anyone's line. It forms a long-distance rhyme with the P5 settlement birth but doesn't repeat it: P3 comes here only once, and the world owes her no return. Its geological family (a closed high-plateau body of water plus geothermal) exists at planetary scale in Vorhan V06's High Inland Sea; if Anna ever reaches V06 later, the P3 memory will come back and then be crushed by scale.

### 4. Salt dome · in the middle of a Glass Plains charter (about 16°N, 1°W · about 200 km south of Coppergate)

**The charter's skiff borrows a lift and flies on.**

Midway, a round anomaly on the ground: two or three kilometers across, a pale swelling, and around its edge a ring of low shrubs half a head taller than what surrounds them. Above it there's an isolated updraft — the pilot doesn't detour it, borrows the lift, saves a stretch of fuel.

Anna smells mineral salt inside the updraft, dry, with a trace of sulfur. Below, a few people are doing seasonal salt-crust collection; the tarps are the color Coppergate uses. Beside them a skiff is flying, dropping not seed but fresh water — watering the shrub ring; the shrub roots keep the salt from spreading outward.

The charter borrows the lift and flies on. Anna looks back; the salt dome has become a white circle in the heat-dust.

There are things in the world that aren't settlements, aren't vistas, aren't route nodes; they're only a state of the ground, and people around them unfold the minimum of a life. P3's Archeon isn't "city — route line — city"; it's a continuous surface where something is happening everywhere.

### 5. A Keld sail-ship · Amber Reach valley-floor T3 harbor

**Doesn't leave the harbor. Only sees a ship she doesn't know.**

Once Amber is reached, at the valley-floor T3 harbor there's a ship Anna hasn't seen before. The sail is larger than any Aethelgard ship's, the frame lighter; there are lengths of cloth of different colors wound around the lines. Somebody says it's a Keld sail-airship — designed for riding the Roll, its big sail feeds on surf-line lift, sacrificing carrying capacity for speed. The seasonal window is short — miss it, and you fly straight and honest for two weeks.

Anna will not, in P3, ride a Roll, and will not even know what the Roll is. All she hears is that the sound of that ship's sail in the wind is different from any ship she knows. Not a hum, not a whoosh; more like a very big drum being lightly, continuously tapped. On deck someone is furling a kind of knot she doesn't recognize.

P3 spatial ceiling: Iris gave "there is more beyond the sea," Glass Plains gave "the map isn't finished," Amber gave "somewhere this far still belongs to the same civilization," and the Keld sail-ship gives "in the same sky, there are entirely different ways of flying." V09 Roll enters Anna's inventory as a sound not yet explained.

### The spatial progression of P3's five landscape sections

The heartwood forest wall = CI's geographical inheritance become today's climate boundary. (**Deep time** — passing through.)
The mesa fault zone = why flying is a necessity. (**Physical constraint** — push through.)
Rimstone Tarn = the abundant, living world. (**Open-hearted exhilaration** — a day and a half branched off.)
Salt dome = the ground is not backdrop; everywhere someone is living alongside its state. (**Continuous surface** — borrow a gust.)
The Keld sail-ship = the same sky, different ways of flying. (**Civilizational variety** — only seen in port.)

None of the five is a quest destination. None of them is Anna "going there on purpose." **Wonder is not the destination she reaches; it's what the world does to her body as she passes through.**

---

## P3

Before twelve, Anna's growth is still mostly perception, reflex, route knowledge, and an ever greater number of ordinary actions handed to her by other people.

Twelve is when **craft** truly crosses a threshold. The threshold is not "the first time she successfully repairs something," it is the first time she has material pass through her hands to become something that didn't exist before and will keep on being used by other people afterward.

Two passages usable; not required for both to be developed full scenes:

- On a ship, someone needs a line guide / stopper-frame fitted to the dimensions of an existing gunwale-frame. Anna measures the position, chooses a small piece of heartwood or brass, shapes it, tests the fit; a skilled hand looks it over at the end, and the piece stays. The next time she boards this ship, that unremarkable fitting already has use-marks on it.
- Somewhere in the Amber direction, a new branch of water / steam line is being connected. Anna doesn't "save the system"; she independently finishes one of the fittings / a small valve group / a shaped seal beside a local craftsman, and the old hand, once he's checked her work, installs it into the new line for real. The point is that this line didn't exist before, and now it does.

No genius invention, no "the child saved everybody," and both passages don't have to land on pressure systems. Anna can make small things for shipboard life, connection pieces, wood- or brass-work, or a length of line-continuation; her craft should be broader than repair from the start.

The real change is still just one sentence:

**For the first time, there is a place, after she leaves, that continues to use something made by her own hands.**

This forms a distant structural echo with the future "arrive somewhere, and let that place feel it was the right person who came"; at this moment Anna does not understand it, and does not need the narrator to sum it up.

### P3 · The short apron and the red jacket, sources separated

The short apron can appear naturally in the middle of P3, around age twelve: the old apron got short, the range of work changed, Anna cut a piece herself, Miriam helped with the hemming, or a tie was replaced later. Its specific origin is not load-bearing, and does not lock in Edmund.

Visual and functional canon: the apron is short, only covering the front of the waist and the upper thigh; no pockets; the ties are simple and quickly undone; hardwearing, washable, patched at the edges over and over; doesn't foul the legs when climbing ladders, stepping across gunwales, or crouching. It only fends off oil, resin, ash, grit, and wet dirt; it doesn't hold tools, and it doesn't identify her. Tools go on a belt loop, in a separate leather sheath, on fixed hooks in the ship or workshop, in her hand, or on the current work surface. If the cloth color happens to be near red or reddish brown, it's only common dye or long-term staining, and does not build a second inherited-red emotional line.

---

# P3 later half · The world widens, Edmund's version begins to fracture

Edmund's decline cannot rely mainly on being seen all at once when Anna comes home.

ROOT 11 has to move into the character layer.

Starting in the second half of P3, what Anna receives out on the far lines is not a continuous Edmund.

It is many Edmunds of different dates.

### Fragment A · Not there

Anna arrives at a station on Edmund's circuit she's been to with him before.

Someone very ordinarily asks:

"Edmund not coming through this season?"

Anna says he's due to come on the next round.

Nobody thinks this is bad news.

### Fragment B · An old letter, so ordinary it is almost boring

A dozen days later, another line brings a short note from Edmund.

Written twenty-something days earlier.

Contents ordinary.

Might only be about a tool somewhere, food at home, or a single line:

"Wind's early on the western line this season, don't take the old pass."

Anna reads it, folds it away.

The reader also relaxes for a moment.

The key thing is:

**The Edmund on the paper is no longer the Edmund in reality.**

But Anna doesn't know it yet.

### Fragment C · A blurred sighting

At the next station, someone says:

"Last time I saw him, he sat around at Crestline for an extra half afternoon."

Anna's first reaction isn't "what's wrong."

It is:

"When?"

The other person thinks for a while.

The date given is either earlier than the ordinary short note in Anna's hand, or later.

The information begins to slip out of registration.

### Fragment D · Anna sends word home

Anna sends a message of her own back from a forward long-stay.

She writes about her now:

The plateau's well;
the machine finally running;
the wind here that calls up from under the stone;
a fitting Edmund will like.

She does not know what state Edmund will be in when this paper reaches Atheria.

The reader doesn't either.

### Fragment E · A healthy version arrives late

After one or two disquieting messages have already come, Anna receives another Edmund, mailed earlier.

The tone is still whole.

This makes "news" for the first time crueler than not knowing:

**Anna doesn't only fail to know the present; she is constantly being handed presents that have already expired.**

---

## S13 · The one line, only once

Anna returns to Atheria from one of her own far trips.

Edmund also came back not long ago from a line that has already been shortened; he carries the smell of wood-smoke or resin.

There's talk at home.

Someone repeats something Edmund had said in passing outside:

"He said, arrive at a place, and let that place feel it was the right person who came — that's enough."

Anna is peeling fruit.

She does not look up.

The line isn't spoken to her.

It isn't a deathbed word.

It isn't a teaching.

It isn't the narrator summing up Edmund's life for him.

In one lifetime it gets relayed only this once.

Then Anna leaves again.

This should be one of her last fragments of contact with "the whole Edmund," yet not an important scene at the time.

---

## P3 → P4 · The routes narrow before the body does

Edmund's decline first shows on the map.

The old circuit is shorter by one segment.
Shorter by another next season.
Someone else covers a certain section for him.
A given station this year received only his records, not him in person.

Anna does not get a clear chart labeled "father's illness progression."

She only gradually finds out:

The lines she herself runs are more and more.

The lines Edmund can run are fewer and fewer.

The three "ordinary afternoons" still hold.

The countdown does not contaminate the happiness of P2/P3.

---

# P4 · The wrong present / the sky narrows (roughly twelve to fourteen years old)
## The Wrong Present

P4 does not begin with Anna already keeping vigil at Edmund's side.

The opening still belongs to distance.

Because this Part is the first time this Part truly turns the vastness of the Loom over to its other face:

**"I want to know how he is right now" is not a question that necessarily has an answer.**

P3 made distance a freedom.
P4 makes the same distance, for the first time, a cost.

---

## P4 first segment · Far away, a message with weight is received

Anna is on a line not close to home.

Someone brings a short note from Miriam or a reliable spoken message.


For example:

"Your dad isn't running the eastern leg now. Come back along the lines you've got. Don't switch around just to catch the fastest ship."

The first thing Anna does is look at the date.

Twenty days ago.

So "isn't running the eastern leg now" has no stable meaning.

And twenty days on?

Nobody knows.

A few days later, an update:

"He's at home."

Nine days ago.

Anna asks:

"How is he now?"

Nobody can answer "now."

It is not any person's failing.

It is distance.

---

## P4 going home · The Loom will not shorten because she is in a hurry


Anna still has to wait as usual for the next line, change ships, miss a wind-window once, sit beside somebody else's dinner, sleep at night, and go on the next day.

The world does not enter disaster mode.

One settlement is bringing in cloth that's been drying all day; at another berth people are arguing over whether a certain jar belongs on this run; a route runs half a day late for crosswinds.

One night is spent at a place with only anchor posts, a windsock, and a signal light. Very few lights. The crew talk little, not because of her. She lies there listening to this ship, and cannot find a single note that matches — it isn't Edmund's ship, and none of the words in her sound-dictionary are familiar here. She gets up and goes to the deck. The nebula is still there. The brass chain is still sounding. Her hand rests on the gunwale, the metal colder than any cliff-face in Atheria. She sits a while, and goes back in.

Anna can say less than usual.

But she cannot cut the space out of the story by being "faster."

The more ordinary this section is, the more the way home hurts.

---

## P4 home · She finally runs into the true present

Edmund is still alive.

This matters more than "she got back too late."

Only after she pushes the door open does she see, for the first time:

**All those Edmunds on the road were out of date.**

The man in the old paper reminding her of the wind at the pass;
the man who, in someone's mouth, sat around at Crestline for an extra half afternoon;
the man of whom "the right person came" was repeated;

and this Edmund now, between them, is a stretch of time Anna has never seen with her own eyes.

She hasn't missed a single event.

What she has missed is change itself.

---

## P4 middle · Voice becomes a second layer of the illness

Once home, the original spine is entered:

**Ordinary afternoon → the second tap → voice-only → nothing next in her hands → death.**

The first phase still has to have good days.

Market still opens.
Hot meals still eaten.
People still come around.
The three of them can still laugh together.
Edmund still rests his hand on a gunwale or pipe wall and waits a beat.
The clumsiness in his life is also still there.

Then the small hammer goes from one tap to two.

The delicate work he used to finish himself is quietly taken over by someone else.


Sometimes it's Miriam.
Sometimes it's the person who did that work anyway.
Sometimes Edmund himself calls out another name.

This step matters: what Anna loses is not "a position waiting for her to inherit," but a specific person gradually withdrawing from many specific relationships.

There is no "last flight" ceremony.

After a certain day, he simply doesn't board that line-ship again.

The ship goes on.
Who sleeps in which bunk on the next round, who covers an extra shift on the wind, which station holds gas for it, who takes up the little bit of work Edmund used to do — this all gets picked up again by the people along the line, inside the old relationships. No "Edmund's position" is handed over; a specific person has simply subtracted himself from many specific relationships.

### voice-only

When Edmund's body can no longer enter the vocabulary of his old work, his voice remains.

The old letters from P3 therefore turn around and cut:

For months Anna had been maintaining a whole person off those delayed voices.

Now the whole person is in the next room.

And she can't say as much.

### Nothing next in her hands

The final bodily confirmation before death still falls to the hands, but the hands are not diagnostic equipment.

Anna has been used, since childhood, to her hands finding the next step whenever something changed: hold, hand over, connect, move aside, make the missing thing. Even not knowing the full principle, after the action there was usually another action.

The last time, what she touches is Edmund.

It can just be holding his hand, steadying his shoulder, pulling up the corner of a blanket — the specific prose isn't locked.

This time, no action can connect the thing back.

**Her hand is still on Edmund, but there's no next step in her hand.**

Let the hand stay there.

---

# P4 death aftermath · The Loom lets the dead keep arriving a little longer

After Edmund dies, the information cannot synchronously stop at once.

This is the second time ROOT 11 truly enters the character layer.

This layer disperses 2–4 fragments across weeks / months, each independent:

- A slow-line comes into port, and someone still asks: "Edmund not running this round?" Anna answers: "No more."
- Something ordinary Edmund entrusted while alive, a very small record, or a reply, comes back after his death. The less it resembles a last word, the better — for example, "wind's early on the western side, don't take the old pass," or "that scarf of yours, did you leave it on the ship again."
- Anna wears the red jacket to a nearby settlement, and the first line she gets still places her in "Edmund still alive" relations: "Isn't your dad coming in a few days too?" She has to say, again: "No more."
- Someone might only know "he was ill," or have the date of death wrong, or be holding an expired version; Archeon does not have a unified present.

By the time the funeral is over, there are still, on the Loom, **local pockets of time where Edmund is alive.** Because the red jacket keeps moving, it is the very thing that keeps carrying Anna, again and again, into these lagging time-layers. Her father's death is not a single day's event; it goes on arriving along the routes.

These ordinary questions get stitched, by thirteen-year-old Anna, into an over-complete explanation: after the jacket appeared Edmund began running fewer routes; many places already knew her; people came to the funeral she didn't know; after his death people still asked after him by old relations. She gradually comes to believe: "he knew all along, and set this up in advance." The narrator does not correct. Miriam is not the arbiter of the truth either; she may know only part of it, and may never have known how much Edmund understood at each stage.

The dead man is not saying goodbye. It is only a person who no longer exists, and many not-yet-updated relations, arriving again a few more times at their own speeds along the Loom.

---

## Funeral · Edmund's relations are scattered across many places

The people who come carry the scale of the Loom.

There are ones Anna knows.
There are ones seen a time or two on some P2 seasonal circuit.
There are ones who know only the red jacket and the Freedman name.
There are ones completely strange.

She doesn't look at faces, she looks at the cloth. On the jackets there is cloth she doesn't know — salt-stained, woven differently, colored with earth from somewhere else. On one person's shoulder is a shade of dark blue she hasn't seen even in Iris. The one pair of hands she recognizes, she recognizes not by face but by the gesture of holding a bowl down — Sera presses the bowl a bit lower so it won't slide when the ship rolls. It's the same gesture as in P2. Sera says nothing.

Hagen appears here.

There is a scar on his face.

He nods, once, in Anna's direction.

Anna does not respond.

Anna had until now been reading distant people's familiarity with her as:

she rides along a lot;
Atherians are easily recognized;
Edmund just happened to have passed through.

The funeral presses many relations that were originally scattered across different times into one day. It does not prove to Anna that Edmund had a complete plan; it only gives her more material that will later be pieced into a misunderstanding. The reader may see that her father's world was bigger than she knew, but the author must not lead them into unpacking a mystery-box.

---

# P4 later half · Space contracts

After the death, Anna's physical radius shrinks in order:

The thousands-of-kilometers Loom
→ the return route
→ Atheria
→ the eyrie
→ the ramp between cliffs
→ one room
→ the several small things that still pass through her hands every day.

The six-month fallow period is kept.

Part of the fallow lands in the long night. The ramp between the cliffs frosts over; there are fewer ships at the port steps, the market no longer pulled off-center by ships, all the stalls shrunk to the leeward side. The pipe-bell sounds once before she reaches the workshop — when Edmund was there, that corner had a bottom-note of hot metal, pine resin, and brass filings mixed together; without him, only stone and cold. The nose is still waiting for a temperature that will not come again. The few flights of stairs she takes less often make the gondolas' clack-clack-clack sound clearer than it did in her childhood.

She often wears the red jacket outdoors; the pocketless short apron only gets tied on for jobs that fend off oil, ash, or wet dirt. The two items don't form a set.

There aren't necessarily big events.

But she rarely comes back empty-handed.

Not because she goes looking for things to do.

It's because Atheria simply has too many small things that fall onto anyone who's always moving.

---

## P4 · Atheria local continuity · Passing through repeatedly, not building a network

What Anna does in Atheria is the shrunk-down version of P2/P3 continuity — things done in passing, naturally, not a task-list waiting for her:

Someone at the port steps hands her a short note: "You headed for the household steps? Take this to Nera in passing."

On her way down to the household steps she gets stopped again: "That empty jar has to go back to the lower plain tomorrow, don't take it home."

A batch of fruit at the market was to be taken by a ship that was expected to come; the chalkboard has changed the berth. Anna glances at it and moves two of the baskets into the shady shed on the other side, so the people waiting don't hunt up and down the street when they get there.

An outsider is standing at the wrong lift-basket mouth; she just says, "the one you want is one level down," and takes them, in passing, to the branch.

Someone asks if the southern line is still coming today. Anna doesn't know. She goes to the port steps to check the date and the windsock, and by the time she's back she's told the same answer to three households in passing.

A message has been written too early, and its recipient has already gone elsewhere. Anna does not decide, on anyone else's behalf, whether the contents are still worth it; she asks first "when was this written," and hands the paper to someone who knows the person's new whereabouts.

A jar that was supposed to be back before dinner isn't back. The next day it comes in on another line, with half a bundle of dried leaves stuffed into it as well. Nobody explains.

A child suddenly has to go with a relative down to the lower plain. Anna knows which basket runs a little later and which platform has less wind, so she gets the child and the bag to the right spot at the station, and then goes on her way.

By walking the same set of slopes, steps, ports and markets over and over, she starts to become one of the reliable "passings-through" — no formal title required.

People gradually come to know:

**Anna might come by today.**

So a paper, a package, a name, an empty jar, a place at the dinner table, a temporarily reassigned berth, will change hands briefly through her.

She has not inherited Edmund's network, nor is she running her own identity.

It's just that she can't stay long in the workshop, so her body, moving repeatedly along close-in routes, holds up for a while some small next-steps that already exist. Only after P5 will the repeated reliability slowly let farther-off places begin to expect Anna herself, again, to arrive.

---

## P4 fallow period: ordinary days still happen

The workshop's smell has already changed. The corner where someone used to be is now colder, quieter. Anna, passing through, still very lightly smells the air; the nose is only still waiting for a temperature that won't come.

But the Atheria outside the eyrie doesn't cooperate with her standing still. In these six months, small changes that carry no symbolic meaning really do happen on the household steps: two short paths over, a household extends its second-floor drying-terrace by one more room, because someone new is now living there long-term; the old bench beside the bread oven has had one plank swapped, the color glaringly pale; a low stone wall Anna used to climb as a child has been taken lower, to let carts through; the bean-trellis under one household's window died out a season and has been replanted with something else; at the port steps an eatery has changed owners, the broth is different, the bowls are the same.

Small friction between mother and daughter, kept: Miriam lends out to Joren a set of old moulds Edmund hadn't used in a long time. Anna comes back and sees the empty patch on the counter, stands a moment, doesn't ask; Miriam doesn't explain. The next day, Miriam only says: "Joren will bring them back." Anna says "mm," and goes out.

Three days later they come back. Anna puts them back in their old place, and finds that the dust on the counter has already drawn a new outline — the old edges no longer match the bottom cleanly. Meanwhile, outside the window, someone is brushing resin on newly extended eaves, and the smell drifts all afternoon.

**In six months, dust has redrawn the shape of a counter; people have redrawn a few very small lines on Atheria as well.**

These changes are not in her service, and are not responsible for consoling her.

The air of the same phase still contains absurdity and warmth having nothing to do with grief.

Late in the long summer, a child stops Anna beside an irrigation channel and very solemnly informs her: the wind is loaded upstream, one bucket at a time, and poured back out downstream.

"What if the bucket leaks?" Anna asks.

"Then it rains."

The child answers and runs off.

Anna switches her bag to the other hand and keeps walking.

The world of P4 goes on running for everyone. Precisely because absurd small things still happen, the Lia accident has room to land in Anna's life as an independent, concrete event.

---

# The Lia incident · Positioning bar, brace, drop-crate, two halves of responsibility

This is the first time Anna sees Lia as a specific person.

Before the accident, Lia already had her own family, friends and life, but none of it was in the map of names, voices, or faces Anna knew; they have no shared prior in P1–P3.

The location is an ordinary drop-off point at the port steps or household steps, not the workshop.

That afternoon a batch of ordinary things from the line came into Atheria: a few sacks of grain seed, two boxes of glass jars, some household goods. Anna has been helping in this period to sort what's arrived and get it to different homes, so she is there too.

She is not doing anything special.

She's just with others, taking things off the platform and sending them in different directions.

One of the crates settled onto the surface a little off position. Anna has the positioning bar in her hand; all she needs to do is wait for the person beside her to re-take the line, and then nudge it straight.

The loaded rack sways lightly in the wind. There's a small hollow-sounding rap where the brace connects.

Tap.

A while later, another one.

The sound is just the hollow rap at the brace's join, unconnected to Edmund — no old writing, no old marks, no old tools, and nobody just happens to bring him up. Six months are already inside Anna's body, and grief doesn't need an external switch.

She listens to it a few times.

For six months, every day she's been doing actions: getting up, eating, walking, handing things across, setting them down, going home. In the past a lot of those actions used to leave a very small "afterwards" in the world — something got caught, a fitting kept being used, the next station really did receive it. After Edmund's death, she has kept doing them, and yet nothing she does touches that already-happened result. The actions are all completed, but they don't seem to land anywhere; the world outside keeps running as usual.

This time she suddenly, very clearly, wants a result to happen.

Not to fix.
Not to help.
Not to prove that she can.

She turns, and swings the positioning bar at that brace.

**It isn't a slip. She swings on purpose, and she connects on purpose.**

She doesn't know Lia, and she doesn't want to hurt anyone.

**She only wants, with her own hands, to make something change.**

The impact runs back up the positioning bar into her wrist. But the result she gets is far bigger than the small result she wanted: a fork-ear, long corroded by damp, is sheared off by the impact, a locking pin jumps out, the rack loses its lateral restraint, the load shifts sideways, and the consequence lands on the specific person standing there.

Lia was beside it, waiting with her family for something belonging to their household. She's just standing there — neither in a danger zone, nor taking anything on for Anna.

The load hits her against a rail post; her left side, chest and abdomen, is compressed; someone else takes scrapes, several glass jars break, a sack of seed grain splits.

Anyone standing in that position would have been injured.

Lia's underlying lung condition makes her recovery slower and the long-term consequences more complex, but **it is not the reason the accident selected her.**

The other half of the causation pieced together afterward:

That fork-ear has been taking in water for a long time; the previous inspections were divided among different people, each of whom looked at their own segment, but the brace connection was not reconfirmed in the fully loaded, hanging state.

Joren keeps the mixed accountability:

"The fork-ear underneath, that's on us."

A beat.

"Swinging the positioning bar over there — that was you."

Anna:

"I know."

The two things stand at the same time: there really is a structural hazard, and she really did swing the bar.

The most frightening thing about this accident is not a competence failure:

**Anna at that moment was not solving any problem. She was just angry, she wanted her own voice to be louder than the world's. The structure amplified that one blow, and the person who actually gets hurt is another human being.**

---

## Miriam's dam-break line · Load-bearing exception preserved

Miriam presses down again on the already stacked sheets of paper:

"You've been pretending you're fine for six months."

Anna cries in Miriam's arms for the first time.

The hand on her back is the same hand that wiped fruit juice off her as a child, held under her knee, patted her, and pushed her away.

This "six months" continues to obey the existing calendrical load-bearing exception.

---

## The hand pulls back · Kept unnamed


The backshock enters the palm first. The fork-ear breaks. The positioning bar drops straight down out of her hand to her feet — not thrown. The surface is still shifting sideways, things are still sliding, people have already rushed over. Anna's hand — the hand that was just holding the bar — makes, for the first time, a motion it hasn't made before:

**pulls back.**

Letting go of the positioning bar is only a post-impact reaction. The truly new motion happens after the hand is already empty: instead of reaching, as before, toward the thing that is about to fall, it draws back as a whole, the fingers curling in. Anna will still throw herself in to help; that instant has already entered the body.

Her hand used to be half a beat ahead. Often it saw the next step before others did: the cup about to spill, and the hand had already gone; the ship about to dock, and the passageway was already cleared; a place missing a small fitting, and she'd already begun measuring. The world had always rewarded her for compressing judgment into action.

What the Lia event truly tore open is not "speed" itself.

**This time the hand also executed her intention accurately — she wanted the brace to take that hit. Where she was wrong was in mistaking the small result she wanted for the totality of the consequences she could control.**

So what she fears after the accident is not "will the body still automatically go out"; it is: seeing the next step doesn't mean I own everything that happens after that step; being able to act doesn't mean I have the right to bear the consequences on someone else's behalf.

The bodily aftereffects of the following months still have to be uneven: sometimes she confirms once more before handing something over; some jobs get done faster instead, as if to be finished; she goes to Lia's door empty-handed and walks away again; sometimes she asks "do you want my help" first and waits for an answer before moving; a great deal of the time nothing about her hand is off at all; only with certain cold metal, certain impact sounds, or when Lia is on the receiving end of an action, might her body suddenly freeze a moment.

What the later arc really has to work on is action, consequence, and consent: Anna has to stop overwriting the present Lia with the Lia of the day of the accident.

At the end of P4, Anna is not healed. She has only learned, for the first time:

The hand can make things, and it can also make a consequence that cannot be recalled;
and "stopping" itself still does not automatically give the right answer.

Confirming, avoiding, asking beforehand, sudden speeding up, over-prediction or occasional freezes — these behaviors stay scattered and uneven, always just the reaction of the moment, not a nameable fixed move. The starting point of subsequent scenes is "what consequence is Anna afraid of right now," not "did the three-second thing show up today."

---

# P4 coda · Lia as a specific person

## First time: got to the door, didn't go in

Early after the accident, Anna at least once actually walks up to near Lia's house.

Her hands are empty.

This itself is not natural, because in that period she usually has on her a piece of paper, a jar to be taken somewhere, a small bag, or something someone has just handed her.

The door is not tightly closed.

From outside she can see a line of light inside the room, and can hear someone talking.

She stands a while, hands still in her pockets.

Nobody comes out to finish this step for her.

Anna in the end turns and walks away.


It only proves that she has begun wanting to go, and her body still cannot let her get close to that room.

---

## Second time: Lia at the window

The day she really sits down, the window **is already open.**

Damp cold air from the north side comes down the terrace and lifts a small piece of cloth by the window over and over.

Lia's recovery is slow, and there is still a ventilation device in the room. The stool Anna sits on is too short; her knees are almost higher than the seat.

Lia looks at her a while, and says only:

"Your jacket is very red."

Keep this line.


On the edge of the bed there's an unfinished game of button-chess; a card drawn crookedly by a friend is under a weight on the windowsill; someone outside the door, at some point, calls Lia's name directly. The room is not "the injured person's room"; it's only one day in which Lia is, for now, living in it.

Wind comes in again in a gust.

Anna sees Lia pull the blanket up a little.

She stands up on reflex, and her hand has already touched the window-frame, about to close the window halfway.

Lia:

"Don't."

Anna stops.

Lia is looking outside:

"I want to hear it."

Anna takes her hand back, and sits down.

Outside, someone is arguing about a pot and a mismatched lid, the sound coming and going; a bit further, the lift-basket reaches a stop, the rollers clack-clack-clack a few times, someone calls another person's name.

Lia laughs a little, then draws it into her ribs.

Anna instinctively moves forward.

Lia raises her own hand and stops her.

Lia's body is her own to command, and Anna's restraint is only one of the factors on the scene.

They only sit there a while, exchanging the occasional sentence not about the accident — no long apology, no formal forgiveness.

Anna is only one of the people who passed through Lia's day; the friend at the door, the unfinished game, the argument outside, the next meal all go on existing.

What this leaves is another thing:

**Anna saw a discomfort, and for the first time didn't decide, on the other person's behalf, that it had to go away right now.**

The B-line here shows a new "craft" for the first time: not more accurate handling of the problem, but knowing when her hand is only one of the options; the other person still has the right to decide whether the next step needs it.

What Lia wants is the wind and the sounds outside.

What Anna can do is stay there.


# P5 · The Sky Opens Again (about age 14–19)
## World Again

Anna has never forgotten what the sky is. P3 already carried her, across other people's long lines and her own itinerary, over some 2,600 km, to see the coast side, Class V, and places that only came several nights later. What lies at the heart of P5 is something else:
**The small thread she had been holding all along, for the first time, grew a little longer than Atheria; and then, through coming home again and again, it slowly grew to a continental scale she could carry herself.**

P3 was Anna held up by the Loom, also beginning to know where her own next stop was.
P4 was her holding up someone else's small next step inside a settlement, and the first time she knew her own hand could also become harm.
P5 is her permitting these next steps to cross the horizon again, and gradually shouldering "whether the person at the other end can actually receive this stretch."

P5 is not inheriting Edmund's network. Hagen may recognize the Freedman surname; others may also give her a little room at first because of the red jacket — but this inherited recognition is only responsible for cracking the door open at the very beginning. What P5 truly wants to happen: someone who at first asked about Edmund, after Anna has arrived reliably enough times, no longer mentions her father, and asks directly instead: "Anna, which way this time?" or "Same line next run?" That the address itself changes naturally is already enough.

## P5 · The Same Jacket, Always Changing

The red jacket is not a relic being preserved. After age ten, its side seams have been let out, its sleeves extended, its cuffs replaced; the lining, shoulder patches, fasteners, and re-dyeings with red earth can all happen scattered across different years, each surfacing as needed. By nineteen, a good portion of the material may no longer be from the batch Edmund once touched, but Anna and the people along the line still call it "that red jacket": continuity comes from a history of use, not the purity of the original material.

At fourteen, the cuff wore through for the first time. She could have taken it to Miriam. She could have taken it to someone else. She didn't. She dug out from home a length of red thread close in color to the jacket, sat on the platform in front of the aerie, and sewed it herself. No one had taught her. The stitches ran crooked, the thread ends weren't hidden, and where she closed the seam a small lump rose. She looked at it for a while. She didn't undo it.

From fifteen on, she was on the road more than at home. The jacket lived the days of a walking-shuttle person with her: cables rubbing at her shoulder, gunwales wearing the sleeves, oil staining the cloth, rain soaking it and the sun drying it and rain soaking it again, fasteners loosened aboard by something catching on them. Torn, it was mended; same red, no second color. Crooked stitches weren't undone. The mend marks stayed. By sixteen or seventeen she could patch a cuff mid-conversation without thinking about it at all.

The red scarf appears in the flying years of ages 14–19, and Anna chooses red herself; the practical layer is always in front — windbreak, dust guard, cold air, wiping sweat, wrapping something in a hurry, reading the wind. When she chose, she wasn't necessarily thinking of Edmund, and the narration doesn't announce "inheritance becoming choice." The reader will see it on their own: the first red was put on her by someone else; later, she kept choosing red.

---

# P5A · Hagen / A Line That Was Only Meant to Reach the Pier (about age 14)

A batch of ordinary things bound from Atheria toward the coast has arrived in Anna's hands: a few boxes of fermentation cultures, a few small packets of seedlings, two letters, and several empty jars headed back to households along the line. They aren't expensive, and none of them carries a life-or-death crisis. Missing this run only means the next round of bread, planting, or trading is a little later; the world will not fall apart because of it.

Anna's only task was to bring them to the pier and hand them off to the scheduled continuation.

But that day the wind window changed. The wind sock beneath the pier still ran along the cliff, but the narrow wind cloth up on the higher tier had already been pulled straight by a crosswind; the scheduled small boat waited two loops out there, then finally moved to another berth. The continuation on the chalkboard was wiped and rewritten. Anna doesn't need anyone to explain "the wind window changed" to her — she saw the air saying different things in two places first, and only afterward saw that line of writing being wiped away.

The goods weren't lost.

Only that the "next step" was suddenly no longer where it had been.

Anna stood in front of the chalkboard and looked at it for a while.

Hagen's boat was just then heading toward the next junction.

Someone said:

"You can leave them here, they'll go on the next run."

Anna looked at the date, then glanced at Hagen's line.

She said first:

**"I'll ride to the next stop."**

No declaration.

No one congratulates her.

She really had only been planning to go one stop further.

By the next stop, the continuation had shifted by half a day because of the weather; a portion of the goods could be exchanged here, another portion still lay in Hagen's direction.

Anna looked at the chalkboard again.

And so "one stop" became a second stop.

Then, in the end, it stretched naturally into three days on the coast.

**Voyage exception:** This once, following Hagen out to the Azure Main coast, about 2,400 km — the only far-end single arrival at age fourteen in all of P5; it was the line that carried her over there, not her choosing distance. After she came back, her ordinary state remained repeated continuations within a few hundred kilometers (see the voyage-scale lock in P5D).

What truly carried her out of Atheria was not a grand decision.

It was that she didn't let go of the thread in her own hand when it crossed the boundary of her own front door.

---

## Hagen's position

Hagen recognizes the Freedman surname.

He saw Anna at the funeral.

He is neither a stand-in for Edmund nor tasked with "bringing her back to the sky."

He only confirms two things:

"How far are you riding?"

Anna gives a stop name.

"Got a berth?"

Anna nods, or goes to see for herself.

Hagen lets her aboard because this line has always allowed hitching, taking things, changing hands; Anna is already familiar enough with itinerary that she doesn't need someone else to open the way for her.

---

## First Night En Route · Giving a Night Back to Motion

What mattered most that first night wasn't the risk.

Anna was, once again, lying inside a moving ship.

Some of the sounds belonged to a grammar she knew: structure shifting under load, ropes going taut, the residual warmth of the stove position, someone walking through the rear cabin.

Most of the sounds were nothing at all like the sounds of Edmund's line ship.

She didn't fall apart.

She woke once in the middle of the night.

She listened for ten or fifteen seconds.

The ship was still moving.

No one came to tell her "it's all right."

She knew for herself that this was a strange ship doing its normal work.

She turned over.

She fell asleep a second time.

The next morning she woke, and the ship was still flying.

The P2 echo lives here: at eight or nine, "waking to still be on the road" proved the world would hold her; at fourteen, the same thing proved she could still hand her body over to a line that wasn't in Edmund's keeping.

---

## Ashore · Spatial Grammar Turns Horizontal

The lower reaches of Atheria already ran to the horizon, and by P3 Anna had already ridden long lines, seen the coast side and Class V — the strangeness here is not "first sight of distance."

The strangeness comes from **spatial grammar turning horizontal**.

Life in Atheria always has height: cliffs, terraces, waterfalls, gondolas, differing soundscapes above and below.

But the wheatfields and gentle slopes of a coastal settlement scatter tier by tier in the horizontal direction.

Anna stepped off the ship and offhandedly said "we're here," her body still waiting for some cliff face to send the sound back.

None came.

Those two words were carried away by the wind directly.

It wasn't silence.

The wheat waves rubbed at different speeds; the sea pressed a low frequency down beneath everything; the mast lines were drawn into thin sounds by the salt wind.

There simply was no familiar wall to send anything back for her.

Her first step onto the grass sank half a finger.

By afternoon, when she heard someone call her name she still occasionally looked toward the "back" of the sound first.

There was only the wheatfield, still lying flat and rising.

---

## Three Days on the Coast · Really Letting the Reader Live Through Three Days

### Day One: Things Delivered

Anna went with the locals to deliver the cultures, seedlings, letters, and empty jars from Atheria to each household.

First to the cool storeroom, then to a bakery, then to the doorstep of one house.

One jar was addressed to an old residence; the person accepting it said that family had moved to the row nearer the sea; Anna went a little further with the new direction.

There was no technical problem she needed to solve to prove herself.

What she really did was only keep clear "whose this is, when it came, where it goes next."

When the things were all delivered, her hands were for the first time empty.

### Day Two: Nothing That Has to Be Done

Anna walks along the field paths.

The direction of the wheat waves is the wind's direction, their speed the wind's speed; occasionally a still band is only a stretch of wheat shorter than what stands beside it.

She walked to the market and found the stalls set further apart than in Atheria; she smelled flour, drying grass, sun-warmed seaweed and salt wind.

She just walked.

Lunch was a very soft local bread, dipped in a root broth.

The person cooking put the bowl in front of her.

Anna reflexively asked how the meal should be settled.

The other person said the cultures from Atheria last year had already turned into a whole season's bread here.

The piece in Anna's hand was part of that batch.

This beat brings the Loom back from a map into food:

A thing that looked ordinary in Atheria, after leaving, entered someone else's daily life, and then returned to her hand in the form of warm bread.

### Day Three: Still Only Living

She helped sort out where a few light items that came in that day were bound — one lot inland, one lot staying on the coast; and glanced at the chalkboard for someone about to change lines, telling them that run wasn't "tonight," it was old writing from the day before that hadn't been fully wiped off.

In the evening she sat on the empty grass slope by the port, or on the edge of the wheatfield.

The Azure Main caught the orange gold of the setting sun.

Sky Fire's Remnant rose slowly from another edge of the sky.

The coast didn't give her an answer.

It only, for three days running, didn't ask her to explain herself.

---

## The Real Anchor of P5's Reopening · "And Then?"

Before the return trip, she already knew that everything she'd brought was delivered, and she knew which line to catch back to Atheria.

Things could have ended right there.

But as she stood watching Hagen wipe down the chalkboard, she looked a little longer.

On it was a batch of goods just in from the coast side, which at the next stop would split at some junction into two lines.

Anna pointed:

"This one — after it gets there, which line does it go?"

Hagen answers with a stop name, or says:

"Depends on tomorrow morning's wind."

Anna nods.

She doesn't say anything more.

That single question is the anchor of P5 — her itinerary brain, after being still for a long time, has begun once more to care:

**And then?**

The reader should know, half a step earlier than Anna's own self-narration, that her life has already begun moving in the direction of skyport again.

## Hagen's Line · Long Night Season

Some night after she's begun running the line with Hagen. Hooded lamps, brass chains, cold. The nebula is brighter than any other time — long night season lets you see the thin red filaments usually washed out by dusk. Hagen doesn't speak. Someone has gone to sleep, and the helm is empty. She walked over and took a stretch — not because she was taught, not because she was allowed, only that when her hand went down onto it, no one lifted her hand away. In half an hour the wind shifted fifteen degrees. She saw the near wind sock turn first, then the narrow cloth up on the higher mast finally catch up; the sound of the sail line on the other side also shifted key. She heard that someone had already brought a line in, confirmed the berth direction still had margin, and only then changed course. No one noticed. This is the last rung of the N line: "doing" no longer needs to be seen.

---

# P5B · Sovana

What Sovana tests is whether Anna can hold ship, wind, people, berth, and the next stretch of route inside her judgment at the same time — not whether her hand is steady.

Failure is allowed; one judgment made too quickly is allowed; a read that is correct and an action that is correct that still didn't give the person next to her enough time to catch it is allowed.

The Sovana passage can land like this: Anna sees the crosswind changing, her hand already on the tiller. In the past it was easy for her to compress "I've seen the next step" straight into an action; this time she keeps the wind sock, the person on the other side of the boat who is still hauling in line, the body at the berth's edge that has not yet stepped back — all held inside the same next step, waiting for the confirmation she actually needs before moving. Sometimes it is still as quick as instinct; sometimes she has to wait an extra beat; time itself is not the answer.

**The upgrade on line B is not slowing down; it is that the next step, at last, no longer contains only Anna herself.**

She begins to distinguish: what I have seen; what I am able to do; who has the right to decide when their own part of it happens.

The gold goggles come back into the foreground here.

In P2 the person who put them on for the first time still needed someone else to tell her where the next stop was.

Now the same object sits on the face of someone who can already sustain her own itinerary, and who has also begun to know when not to step ahead.

The object hasn't changed.

The relationship has.

---

# P5C · Gust (about age 15–16)
## An Unfamiliar Class II Already at Work

When Anna first truly begins a relationship with Gust, it is already running a line on the Loom — no long rebuilding period on the ground.

It may have just changed crew composition, just picked up a new regional line, or Anna may simply be entering its existing rotation.

The point is:

**It does not need Anna to live. Anna needs time to be worthy of understanding it.**

On the first run, she doesn't know which side the wash basin is stored on.

On the second, she still estimates its turn response too quickly — the great envelope of Gust always answers half a beat later at certain angles than the ships she is used to.

On the third, she is woken at night by a low sound, sits up to listen; the person beside her doesn't even open their eyes: that's just the sound Gust makes when the temperature difference is large.

On the fourth, she finally knows that a certain outer cable tightens first in wet wind, knows which hatch, when opened, will carry the kitchen's heat onto the deck, knows that at a certain kind of T1 station the crew will let people off first before taking things aboard.

On the sixth, she no longer needs to ask where the hanging bunk is.

On the tenth, when a new hand sets a cup down on the wrong side, Anna moves it over without thinking, the motion the same as one an old P2 crew member once made to her.

One day she is crouched in the lower hold checking a piece of framing, her palm pressed against it — the wood is a different material from elsewhere, the grain denser, the nail pattern different too. Joren passes and glances over, says "that section's older than the ship," and walks on. Anna's hand doesn't move. She waits a beat. The wood is half a degree cooler than what surrounds it; she doesn't know whether that is the material or the location. In P2 she watched Edmund do the same thing countless times — hand down, wait, not rushing to give an answer. She didn't understand then what he was waiting for. Now she is doing it herself, and she still doesn't fully understand. But her hand doesn't want to leave.

These repetitions are her relationship with Gust.

**Having loved one ship doesn't make you automatically understand another.**

But "understanding" refers to these:

how it answers in which wind;
when a line will go tight first;
which kind of turn will make the people in the aft cabin lose their footing first;
which of the crew's shorthand phrases means someone is already ready;
when a sound is worth looking at, and when it's just Gust's own accent.

The intimacy between Anna and Gust comes from daily use, not from technique steadily taking over, and not from ownership or a declaration of inheritance. The real judgment is:

On some entry into port, someone asks:

"Which line's Gust going today?"

The one answering doesn't look at anyone else first.

He looks at Anna.

She looks up at the wind, then at the chalkboard.

Then she names the next stop.

The relationship is completed right there.

---

# P5D · The Young Walking-Shuttle Person (about age 16–19)

After Gust returns to the Loom, the childhood arc has finally, fully cashed in on exploration age. When Anna enters an unfamiliar place now, what she receives is no longer only wonder — she has begun to carry real weight for whether a line can go on holding together.

## P5 Distance Reference

P3 already had the long haul Atheria → Frontier → Millhaven → Iris Hollow, about 2,600 km, two or three nights. What P5 upgrades is her grip on a stretch of line, not the distance itself.

At fourteen or fifteen, repeated continuations of one or two hundred to eight hundred kilometers are her ordinary state; she may take an extra stop or two on the spot, but more important is the second, the fifth time back to the same place. P5A's single run out with Hagen to the Azure Main coast (about 2,400 km) is the sole exception: that time the line carried her over and carried her back, and for more than half a year after she didn't go that far again.

At fifteen or sixteen, working lines of about 700–1,800 km become her ordinary working scale. Coppergate–Cliffwatch–Windbreak, Orun's outskirts, Sovana — all belong to this stage. Orun's first sighting is still here.

At sixteen or seventeen, the continental trunks and branches of 1,800–3,500 km enter her ordinary life; she overlaps with the long-line lives of Class III/IV, and occasionally Class V, comes to know people who run farther still, and starts being called by name directly from farther-off places.

At seventeen through nineteen, Gust's itinerary can naturally stretch to 3,000–5,000 km, allowing a seasonal through-run once or twice that approaches spanning a large portion of Aethelgard. It is not a "continental express" private to Anna — she changes segments, picks up a leg someone else left behind, gets off at one junction and boards another familiar line, and pulls back to shorter routes when the wind season demands it.

**Trans-oceanic boundary**
The mature trans-oceanic lines of Year 521 already exist. In late P5 Anna has seen Class IV/V vessels in from Keld / Vorhan, can pick out a few foreign accents, knows some of the ship names or seasonal windows of the Blue Crossing; she has even helped once on a coastal leg before an ocean crossing, or ridden one very short stretch. But in the childhood arc Keld / Vorhan / Duskreach are still only distant names and accents, not yet her familiar personal geography. Real trans-continental voyaging she "lives inside" is saved for Act 2.

Anna's sense of adventure comes from her willingness to enter the "incomplete."
She doesn't go to discover a spectacle the author has readied for her; often, by the time she arrives, things are only just beginning.
The world keeps growing after she leaves.

## P5 · A Stripe of Gold on Each Shoulder

Seventeen or eighteen. She has just completed a solo long haul — an ordinary line Edmund had thought about running in his lifetime but never got to. Not a hero event. She comes back to the aerie, drapes the jacket over the back of a chair, and sleeps for a full day.

The next day she sits down and finds, at the very bottom of an old chest at home, a short length of gold thread — Miriam has never once mentioned it was there. It could also be a small coil she traded back at some previous stop. The narration leaves it open.

She sews one stripe of gold on each shoulder. Left one, right one.

Miriam saw and said nothing.

Everywhere else on the jacket remains uniformly the same red. The gold is the only thing on the entire jacket that isn't red.




## Late P5 · Fragments of the Old System

Late P5 permits two or three very light fragments of old-system talk mixed into ordinary days: some old pilot, maker, or resident along the line who casually mentions Scar, old field housing, whether such-and-such "still recognizes rhythm," or someone who has gone in a stretch and heard a very faint pattern-sound that returns only after long intervals. No portable old objects are given; these are only sayings, and it is these sayings and where the records came from that Ep51–55 will follow.

The formal name "Axiom Core" hasn't yet appeared at this time. After these conversations, everyone naturally returns to meals, weather, the next run, or someone else's life; no one gives coordinates, and no one presses further. What Anna remembers later is "who said that sentence," not a task on her list. If Milo is present, he'll remember one more name or old Thread designation than she does, but even the two of them together can't piece together an answer.

---

## Going Far by Arriving Again

Preserve at least two kinds of repeated arrival: one big junction, one lived-in settlement (Riverbend-type refers to the texture of life, not scale — it is a multi-centered canal town of forty thousand people). Going far is proven by the second, the fifth, the twelfth time.

### Frontier-type Repetition

The first time she spends a night, the layered human voices and mechanical low frequencies keep Anna from sleeping; her ears keep taking them apart.

Some years later, in one year she pulls in many times; she walks into the market without slowing at the first two stalls' rising steam, and only turns aside when she reaches the familiar broth.

At dusk the big chime sounds and the stall-keepers' speech automatically drops half a beat; for the first time Anna doesn't look up, she only nudges her bowl forward.

The place has not turned into background.

It is her body that has taken it into ordinary life.

### Riverbend-type Repetition

A wait for a continuation makes her stay three extra days. What she really comes to know is only the upper stretch of canal — Riverbend runs along two main canals for a few kilometers, and other canal sections she has only passed through these years.

The first night she borrowed a bed in a narrow long house by the canal: the stone foundation is very cool; the wooden floor on the second story lets voices from downstairs float up. Two steps outside the window is water; the borrowed blanket smells on both sides of river-smoked fish, flour, and wood smoke. Early the next morning the upper canal releases water, and the water wheels wake one by one from far to near; only then does Anna know that the quiet "street" of the night before is actually broken by the sound of water into many segments in the day.

The next day she went along with a household moving fruit baskets from the riverside up to a slightly higher pressing house; in the afternoon there was nothing that had to be done, so she sat on the warm wall behind the communal oven and watched two children trick each other with a piece of dough. On the third day, the continuation still hadn't come, and she already knew which small bridge on this stretch of canal gets hot in the afternoon, which canalside path pushes a cart most level, which water bucket at whose front door anyone is welcome to use.

The next time she comes, the second story of the house where she stayed has added a small side room; the open ground where the nets used to dry has been built up into a grinding shed, so the sound of the water wheel has thickened. No one mentions last time. Much later she comes again, and the tree beside the communal oven is now enough to cast a shadow over the whole long bench; entering this stretch of canal she doesn't look at the berth first, she looks at the water level and knows which bridge to take; the canal segments downstream are still, to her, someone else's town.

These passages prove that Anna's life really does cross distance: she has slept in many places, eaten in them, gotten her sleeves dirty in them, and comes back again and again to find people have changed the roof, the room, the tree, the road, the meal, the question. The place has not been frozen waiting for her.

### New-Point Repetition · The Map Keeps Growing While Anna Is Alive

The first time: there is only a well older than anyone's memory, a windbreak shed, three or four people, and a temporary bedding. One person has planted half a ring of root vegetables behind the shed; another has stacked two crates as a table. There is no fixed name. Anna only remembers the well water is on the warmish side, the wind knocks all night at a corner of the shed, and there are frogs somewhere far away.

The next time: beside the shed there is now a bread oven — not a "service facility"; it is that the two people who slept on the boat last time have moved their crates into two actual small houses; one house on a stone foundation, one all wood, clearly not built by the same batch of hands. A newly planted tree by the well casts shadow enough only for half a table. Someone has started calling the place by a nickname; another still says "that well." The wind knocking at the corner of the shed is half-blocked by the tree now.

The time after that: between the two small houses there is now a drying rack and a small vegetable plot; behind the bread oven a rain-shelter roof has been added; for the first time the chalkboard writes that name beside the direction of its destination, and the spelling isn't even standardized yet. Anna doesn't realize she is witnessing a settlement birth; she only knows that this time she doesn't have to ask "which side is the well on," and that at night she no longer hears that old sound at the corner of the shed — the tree has grown taller, and the houses too are blocking the wind.

The CI legacy is still only responsible for lifting the zero-point higher; what really makes this place become a place, out of "by the well," is people who leave behind beds, stoves, vegetables, eaves, children, and the next season.

---

# P5D.1 · Three Returns Home: Home Was Not Waiting for Her

The Anna of 14–19 should return to Atheria several times, and the main axis of returning home is Miriam, the aerie, and a settlement that keeps changing without Anna in it; Lia only occasionally crosses her route among these — going home has never been "to see Lia."

## Sixteen · The News Gets Home Before She Does

Anna had meant to tell Miriam in person that she was preparing to run longer lines, but a note carried by a fast line got there first, and brought only half a sentence:

"Anna wants to run long lines."

No with whom, no where she'd be staying, no when she'd be back.

When Anna actually got back to Atheria, Miriam was working with others to bring in a piece of awning the wind had pulled loose.

She thought she was the last to know; Anna thought she hadn't yet had the chance to say anything.

The two of them, holding the same piece of cloth, talked their way through two incomplete versions until the work was done.

When the wind came, the cloth muffled the words first; someone shouted for them to switch hands.

The rest of the questions could wait until tomorrow.

Asynchrony can also do no more than make a mother and daughter misunderstand each other for half a day.

## Seventeen · Lia Is a Chance Meeting

Anna brings home a spool of thin blue-green thread, meaning to patch the winter curtain of the shared room; when she gets home she learns the curtain has already been mended, by an earlier batch of coarser thread.

Miriam sews the thin thread into a completely unnecessary sleeve pocket.

Anna says the position is too low.

Miriam deliberately sews the other side just as low.

The next day Anna turns into the dye workshop for something — nothing to do with Lia.

Lia is in the middle of her own work.

A wet piece of cloth slips off the rack, and the two of them grab it at the same time.
