Lia flings the other end over; each of them gives it half a twist more, neither willing to let go first; dark blue-green water eats into both of their sleeves.

The moment Anna's gaze settles on Lia's breathing, Lia pulls the cloth back:

"Look at the cloth."

Then goes on talking about mordant clay, freight, why this batch's color isn't right, or who's letting go first.

What this establishes:

**Six years' distance doesn't equal six years never seen.**

They can occasionally meet in the same settlement, grab the same piece of cloth, exchange a few words, then each go on their own way.

What really hasn't recovered is intimacy, not physical visibility.

## Eighteen · Anna Comes Back to Hear Miriam Tell Her About a Life She Wasn't In

Anna reaches the aerie at night and sees only a cold pot and a note.

Miriam isn't home.

She's gone to someone else's storytelling supper.

Miriam comes back very late, half a badly baked flatbread in hand, and starts by telling how three grown-ups argued the ending of a story wrong.

Anna sits and lets her finish.

Before, it was often Anna who would bring useless news from outside back home, and Edmund or Miriam would receive it.

Now Anna comes back from a distance and listens to Miriam tell her about a stretch of Atheria she has taken no part in at all.

The next day comes the physical change in the aerie: the bench where Edmund used to keep his molds has been moved over next to the window, and in the corner that was freed up sit Miriam's pickle jars and a basket of herbs; one corner of the workshop has already become an extension of the drying platform.

At first glance Anna will feel "this isn't right."

She only needs to stand there a while, and in the afternoon open the window herself, and let the smell of the herbs from the new spot blow into the room.

### Lia's Own Six Years · Design Constraint

Anna's leaving and coming back is only weather that occasionally changes on Lia's calendar.

Lia has friends, bad moods, a place in the dye workshop, a color aesthetic, physical limits, afternoons that get thrown off, and her own wishes about the distance.

One year, when Anna comes back, Lia might well already be at someone else's storytelling supper somewhere else, with friends.

The two of them have each grown their own six years. When they truly draw close again in a later arc, P5 remains six years of separate growth, not a narrative of Anna keeping watch for years while Lia recovers.

---

# Milo · The Door Finally Moves to the Other Side (Anna at sixteen / Milo at twelve–thirteen)

Milo has not appeared in P1–P4.

In Thornfield — a large eastern T3 town of fifty thousand that lacks for nothing — he has watched, for many years, ships scatter from here in different directions. His home stands complete in the town; what he wants is not to leave a small place, but to know what lies at the end of the arrow.

His question is one of maps, not Federation technology:

"Why does it stop here?"

"What's after that?"

In P1 Anna once touched the edge of the paper with her finger.

Now another child stands in front of the chalkboard and the old navigation charts asking questions in the same direction.

The difference is:

**This time Anna is on the other side of the door.**

She takes Milo aboard Gust out of practical need, not because she sees "the young self" or as a rite of taking on an apprentice.

She needs a deckhand.

He needs a door that actually moves.

The Loom, in its ordinary way, brings these two things together.

Late P5 thus completes the role inversion:

P2: Others knew where Anna's next stop was.
P3: Anna herself knew where the next stop was.
P5: Anna begins to need to know whether another person can reach the next stop with her.

She turns from the one who is held up into the one who holds up.

Milo's age when he comes aboard is locked here: Anna is sixteen, Milo about twelve or thirteen; by the main line, when Anna is nineteen, the two of them have been running lines together for about three years.

---

# Kassia · A Familiar Line That Will Not Stay Fixed on Gust (Anna at sixteen / Kassia at seventeen)

Kassia also begins to enter Anna's life in late P5, in a way completely unlike Milo.

Milo is boarding.
Kassia is repeated intersection.

She has her own Razor, her own friends, her own destinations, her own dining table, and a whole set of days Anna doesn't know about — always independent of Gust's crew.

The first time she is really remembered by Anna can happen in the same stage as Milo's boarding, but not on the same arrival: beside the chalkboard / wind-board of some big junction, the exact location left unlocked for now.

She first exists as a seventeen-year-old:
- laughs with people she knows, eats, complains that someone writes their numbers ugly;
- has an appetite for speed and clean, beautiful handling itself;
- will let herself feel pleased about a very clean landing;
- the sharp is shown by action, not by a cold face.

Then someone speaks of a stretch of line in the everyday phrasing of "probably fine" or "you can run it this season."
Kassia skips over all Loom philosophy and asks only:
"Who was the last person who actually saw it?"
"When?"

She is especially sensitive to sentences like these: as a child, waiting for her parents, she had already seen "still more or less fine" being pushed
back one day at a time. No one is lying, but a comforting word without a date can live
much longer than the ground it stands on.

And so the seventeen-year-old Kassia still holds a very young, even slightly stubborn belief:
**As long as the conditions are written down a little more clearly, the next person to run it doesn't have to take the old "probably" as new fact.**

Her first friction with Anna comes from operating manner, not trauma disclosure. Before leaving port one time, Anna suddenly says:
"Wait a minute — wind's about to change."

Kassia asks:
"Where?"

Anna won't necessarily be able to say it neatly. She may only point:
"That stay on the left dropped a little first."
"The rail's gone cool earlier here."
"Flag hasn't moved yet, but a layer's gone hollow under the hull."

Kassia's first reaction can be less than polite:
"Do you always talk like this?"
or:
"This can't be written on a wind-board."

And then the wind really does change.
Kassia is neither instantly impressed nor lets this become the origin story of a rivalry.
She goes off afterward to look at the wind-board, the masts, and the times someone noted down.
Next time they run into each other, she may only say:
"That gust last time — you were ahead of the flag."

Anna:
"I know."

Kassia:
"I'm not praising you."

This relationship holds from then on, but each time takes a different shape:
Anna sometimes notices first; Kassia sometimes puts a question to it that can be followed up;
both of them can also be wrong.
What really matters is that Kassia has been asking from the start:
**If you weren't here, how would anyone else later know what it was you actually felt that day?**

Late P5 allows at least two or three very light re-crossings:
- Anna arriving as Kassia is leaving;
- the two of them having eaten at the same long table, each talking to different people;
- a season later, recognizing Razor / Gust again at another junction;
- one time only getting in a weather exchange, no personal conversation.

This way, the reader in Anna's Atheria race at nineteen should feel "she's back again," not "new rival enters."

The Kassia of P5 stays light: the completed theory of Loom fate is saved for later; the full story of her parents' disappearance is saved for later; "flies faster than the wind" at seventeen is first of all liking speed, clean handling, and active response; Anna neither heals her nor sees through her; she remains always independent of the fixed Gust crew; in her girlhood she is not the perpetually solitary cold-faced character.

P5 only needs to let the reader gradually come to know a habit:
**Kassia doesn't like a sentence traveling further than what it actually knows.**


======================================================================
9. Later Arcs: let the story pass through the world, don't finish the world
======================================================================

# I. Narrative Principles

### Global Information Embargoes and Naming Rights Protocol

| Embargo | Scope of Application | Time of Lifting |
|------|---------|---------|
| **The three characters "Betelgeuse" do not appear** | All of Ep1 (=1A+1B) | Ep2 scene 6: CI radiation spectrum inference (the naming beat) |
| **The word "Sky Fire" does not appear** | Ep1–Ep4 (all POV text before the change of sky) | Ep4: some child, seeing the change of sky, offhandedly says "sky fire" or a similar word; Atwood, hearing it, writes "Sky Fire" in her ledger, and from then on the word enters the language of the story |
| **Astronomical coordinates / star charts / light-year figures do not appear** | All of Ep1 (=1A+1B) | Ep2 scene 6: CI data output (with the full old-light technical version) |

**Naming Rights Protocol (the design intent of the embargoes):** The reader comes from under Anna's sky — the nebula is their festival, and the intro cannot hide any ending. What these three embargoes govern is not suspense; it is **whose mouth, at what moment, and with what weight the name enters the narration**:

- **"Sky Fire"** → from a child's mouth, Ep4. This word is not an official term, not a CI serial number, not a scientific name — it is a child's intuitive reaction in the face of the change of sky, received by Atwood and turned into record; and so the undertone of every later use of it is "the fear as seen through a child's eyes."
- **"Betelgeuse"** → from a machine's mouth, Ep2, in the tone of a casualty report. A name is death's administrative business, and CI does the paperwork (deployment position: the naming beat of Ep2 scene 6).
- **Ep1 (1A+1B)** → this star has not yet earned the right to a name. In 2564 it is only an old star still with a residual lifespan on the order of a hundred thousand years, not worth the departure story stopping to name it. **Ep1 is not avoiding its name — it is that at this moment the story does not yet consider it important enough to need a name.** The reader can smell avoidance; not caring is the correct texture.

**POV enforcement mechanism:** The embargoes are enforced not by textual avoidance but by POV. The intro is Atwood POV — she doesn't know the star catalog, she cares about people; what she narrates is not a serial number, it is the angle of Hsiang's neck when he looks at the star. Hsiang in dialogue calls it by **a relative pronoun** ("it's steady tonight") — a spectroscopist calling a star "it," the embargo automatically complies, and the character portrait is completed at the same time. If he read out a string of serial numbers, Atwood's POV would not retain those syllables; the name exists in the world, only the narrator does not keep it.

**Embargo enforcement table (the emotional equivalent of each embargoed item · positive form):**

| Embargoed item | Ep1 equivalent |
|---|---|
| The name "Betelgeuse" | Relative pronoun system: "his star" (POV enforcement) |
| 530 light years | Unit of a nephew's growth: "By the time she gets there, this child's baby teeth ought to have finished coming out; by the time her first letter has traveled a round trip, his voice will already have changed." — distance measured not in light years but in a child's teeth and voice. Biology is an era-proof scale of measure (see the "learning triptych" section for the global educational-vocabulary embargo) |
| Orion direction / star chart | The sky is only permitted to be looked at **privately** — a man looking up at a star is not a star chart. The only "picture" Ep1 carries is a polymer photograph: three people on a snowy mountain. The coordinates she really uses to navigate |
| Specific years of voyage | The failure of "now" (old light · emotional version): "The file says everything is normal over there. That 'normal' walked many years on the road before it climbed onto that line — she is going to a place where even 'now' cannot reach by mail." Zero numbers; the complete technical version belongs entirely to Ep2 · CI data output (see Ep2 scene 6) |

### The Triptych of Three Kinds of Learning

**The 2560s learning model:** Index-style learning (learning = learning to ask — knowledge lives in the ASI substrate, humans carry pointers, education is the art of learning to ask) × guided neural growth (gardened childhood — curated sensory streams, challenge gradients, sleep-cycle consolidation guidance; the child truly experiences learning, but never wrongly struggles; no one has been lost, so no one will be lost) × experience pod (mild temporal compression, live experience — you don't read a book about seafaring, you subjectively sail for months in one night). One-sentence portrait: **The children of the 2560s learn to do anything astonishingly fast — but almost nothing needs doing. Skill is a hobby, knowledge is a pointer, growth has been gardened.** "School" was not abolished, it evaporated — a school is essentially an industrial-era knowledge-rationing institution, with nowhere to attach in an on-demand-index era. The life-stage markers of a child: losing baby teeth, voice change, first solo entry into a pod, and so on — open container, sample not exhaustive.

**M4 exclusion clause (canon-level):** Direct experience / memory transplant does not exist in the 2560s — experimental personality upload only appears in the late 27th century (§13.3-12), no high-bandwidth neural write channel. This exclusion carries weight in three places: (a) The Awakener critique holds — "why do the difficult thing" cannot be installed; (b) the CI's slow teaching in Archeon is the only path, not a cruel choice; (c) the shape of the tragedy — what Earth lost is not the ability to know, but the necessity of carrying.

Triptych:

1. **Earth: knowledge does not live in the body.** Learning = learning to ask. Reading is an optional craft — like baking bread. Atwood's handwritten ledger is her eccentricity on Earth.
2. **CI era Archeon: knowledge is forced back into the body.** "Every time you teach a human to do a thing, you have one less thing to do" — the great re-internalization. CI etches knowledge onto metal because it has done the arithmetic: only stone and human heads can bear five hundred years. Atwood is the converter from pointer to bearer; the most obsolete skill she brings — handwriting — becomes the load-bearing technology of the colony.
3. **The Anna era: knowledge lives in the body.** Master-apprentice, the care-taking family of languages, Edmund reads dead things / Miriam reads live things, oral tradition at story nights, the knowledge filtration of the print house. "Reading" returns to its full domain: reading weather, reading machines, reading terrain, reading living things — writing is only one subset of reading.

**Global educational-vocabulary embargo:** Classroom / school / going to school / teacher (in the human-profession sense) are globally forbidden in the prose and world-fact layer of Earth and Archeon — when the world is understood correctly these words never surface at all. Earth side: schools have been evaporated by index / gardening / experience pods. Archeon side: learning lives in master-apprentice, family, and community — permitted words: master, taking apprentices, one who has watched and learned, story person, CI course (a historical proper noun). At the design layer, analogical / negative forms are permitted ("like a teacher," "not a teacher") — that is language for the reader, not the language of the world. The child's growth markers are all biological measure or local ritual.

### The Triptych of Three Kinds of Machines

The whole saga writes about three kinds of relation between people and machines; the intro carries the first two panels:

1. **Earth ASI — everywhere, no one speaks with it.** It is environment, not character. The door opens before you arrive; the city has no friction. No one speaks to it, just as no one speaks to gravity.
2. **CI — one voice, becomes family.** A machine designed to die. "The wind will be calm tonight" (Ep3A) can strike a person, on the premise that the audience saw in 1A a world where an AI was perfect but had no name.
3. **The Anna era — machines become livestock, cared for by hand.** For the specific caretaking family of languages see §8; the intro is only responsible for alignment with it.

Each panel is smaller, closer, and more in need of the human hand than the last. The seeding philosophy is told in images, zero theme announcement.

**§Earth ASI zero-dialogue principle (hard rule):** Throughout the 25th-century Earth segments of the intro (1A/1B), the Earth ASI does not appear as an interlocutor, does not speak, is not named, is not thanked — it exists only as an ambient grammar. The purpose: to make CI's first utterance in Ep3A the first time in the entire book that "a machine speaks to a person." (The 31st-century Earth of the FED line is not bound by this clause.)

---

## II / III. Supernova Physics and the Fourfold Structure of the Long Silence

> Spatial geometry / light-shell formulas / dogleg-shell collision / relit shell sequence / four propagation phenomena / three periods of isolation / Year 521 night sky canon, and the fourfold structure of "why no one came" (information breakage, physical and technological bottlenecks, CI trust, Triage logic) and the Federation colonization project map, are all registered only in 1.1 Cosmos (§2.1–2.11), one place. The intro keeps only character design and the arc outline.

### Atwood's Psychological Arc — Fourfold Uncertainty (character design · preserved here)

Atwood knows physics. She knows the corridor has not been permanently sealed. But she can never be sure **which reason is the true one**:

* "Maybe the corridor's radiation really is too dangerous." (physical reason)
* "Maybe they can't spare a ship." (resource reason)
* "Maybe they trust CI to handle it, feel there's no need to come." (CI trust)
* "Maybe they know we're still alive but feel we aren't worth it." (value reason)

All four possibilities coexist, forever unfalsifiable — this **uncertainty** wears at a person more than any certain answer. And for Atwood the third possibility has an extra layer of pain: **she watched CI die with her own eyes.** The card the Federation gambled on has already lost — but the Federation doesn't know.

Psychological arc:
* Year 1: "The signal has broken, they don't know yet."
* Year 3: "The scout ship should have found the problem by now."
* Year 5 (after the radiation wavefront): "They must know by now. They're preparing."
* Year 10: "Maybe the assessment report is still going through the process."
* Year 15: "Maybe they think we're already dead."
* Year 20: "Maybe they think CI is enough, that there's no need to risk it."
* Year 30: "Maybe they know we're still alive, but feel we aren't worth it. Maybe they tried, and the ship didn't arrive. I will never know."

This is the perfect echo of the theme of Ep1: In the world Atwood left, 70% of people chose to sleep. When she left she thought that was their choice. Now she understands — that world wasn't unwilling to do the difficult thing; it had already forgotten **why** you'd do the difficult thing.


---

## IV. Locked Parameters

### 4.1 Intro Narrative Window

The full world-history table is not copied here: absolute events follow §3.12, relative phases follow §7, and the propagation of the supernova shell follows §2. This table only registers the character and episode execution windows that the intro itself needs, and does not write back into upstream canon.

| Intro execution item | Narrative window | Notes |
|--------------|----------|------|
| Atwood, Hsiang birth | ~2534 | 26th-century medicine, they depart at about 30 |
| Ep3A | 2569–2571 | The daily-life setup after Axiom's crash, sensory world and companionship |
| Ep3B | 2571–2573 | Building the bunker, launching the last messenger probe |
| Ep4 | 2573 / Year 0 | Sky Fire |
| Ep5 | 2573–2623 / Year 0–50 | CI degrades, waiting turns to Long Silence |
| Hsiang exit | 2591±3 | After the platform night; character execution parameter |
| Ep6, Atwood's final ledger | 2673–about 2704 | Letting go; Atwood's death about 2704 |
| Ep7 | 3094 / Year 521 | Anna and Earth first-light cross-cutting |

### 4.2 Population Structure (at Axiom's crash, about 2569)

**Early awakeners (already about 19 years in Archeon):**
* Nakamura (engineer, built Basin Seven's infrastructure)
* Ortiz (doctor / base operations lead)
* Another ~10–12 advance Awakeners (early arrivals around 2550)

**CI-incubated children:**
* First batch ~40, activated around 2556, about age 13 by 2569
* Second batch ~40, activated around 2562, about age 7 by 2569
* Total about 80, two cohorts of about 13 and 7 respectively
* Raised by CI, with no memory of any human society

**Axiom survivors:**
* Atwood (atmospheric scientist, about 30) — protagonist
* Hsiang (spectroscopist / astrophysical observer, about 30) — protagonist-tier
* Two other named characters — protagonist-tier
* The remaining ~16–26 survivors — background group, with names and professions
* (Axiom carried about 50–80 people, crash losses about 60–70%)

**Total population: about 100–130. Most are children. Adults about 30–45.**

**Robotic labor force (CI-managed):** The CI Package contains multi-tier drones / robots — heavy industrial units, medium construction machines, micro repair swarms. Designed as a **two-layer control architecture**: the CI core provides high-level task planning and exception handling; each robot has a local autonomy layer (a pre-programmed capacity for simple task loops). At full CI function, the robots are Basin Seven's main labor force; after CI fragmentation, the robots fall back to local autonomous mode, only able to repeat the last received task list, unable to respond to new situations.

**Timeline of robotic labor force degradation (lagging the CI core by about one phase):**

| Period | CI core status | Robotic labor force status |
|------|-----------|----------------|
| ~2569–2582 | Still able to converse | Fully functional, coordinated in real time by CI |
| ~2582–2623 | Fragmented | Top-level coordination fails, the local autonomous mode takes over. Able to run simple preset tasks, unable to handle new tasks or exceptions. Occasional odd behavior (CI fragment instruction interference) |
| ~2623–2673 | Testament → silence | CI stops issuing instructions. Pure local loops — repeating the last task list. Halted one by one due to mechanical wear, no source of high-end spare parts |
| ~2673–2713 | — | Most stopped. The last few of the simplest structure (water pumps, conveyors) linger on. Some are disassembled for parts by humans |
| ~2713+ | — | The robotic era ends. Manual labor returns comprehensively |

The withdrawal of robots from the labor force is the real physical starting point of the technological branching (§3.4) — not the day CI fell silent, but the day the last working robot stopped.

**Population growth verification:** Already taken over by §5.1a's phased model (Year 521 = 1.8M, six genetic vaults + about 2,400 lineages). The old "120 people continuous exponential to 800,000–1,000,000" estimate is retired.

**Remaining embryo reserves:** The six underground genetic vaults totaling about 2,400 lineages is canon (§5.1a); by Year 110 about 1,800 people have been activated cumulatively, the rest kept long-term as genetic reserve. That individual vaults were damaged in Sky Fire is a permitted local fact. If a salvage arc of the Axiom Scar were to discover an intact embryo vault module, it can still serve as major-plot material for Arc 4 — the Axiom's onboard vault does not belong to the six underground vaults, and does not conflict with §5.1a.

### 4.3 Lifespan Setting

26th-century medicine / biotech gives human lifespan 150–200 years. Atwood, born about 2534, is about 30 when she departs in 2564; her actual death is about 2704 (intro chronology), age about 170, within the lifespan range.

This means:
* She lived to see all four phases of CI's degradation
* She outlived all the other Axiom survivors (whose lifespans may have been shortened by crash trauma and radiation exposure)
* She became the last person who remembered Earth
* Her ledger is not 8 volumes, likely 30–50 volumes, spanning more than a century

**Lifespans of the early awakeners and other Axiom survivors:** Crash trauma + radiation exposure + Archeon's lack of full 25th-century medical support = a lifespan potentially shortened to 100–120 years. Nakamura and Ortiz and the other early awakeners likely died around 2580–2620. Meaning that in the last 30–50 years of her life Atwood is the only one who remembers Earth.

**Hsiang's exit:** Crash trauma and radiation exposure shorten his life, projected death around **2591±3**, more than thirty years before the last batch of high-order CI voices fall silent, and about a century before Atwood. Atwood loses Hsiang first, then CI, so that the voice of a contemporary and the voice of a teacher fall silent separately.

**Marin's identity and time positioning:** Marin is not from the first batch of CI-incubated children — that batch, by Atwood's late years, are also centenarians themselves. Marin is more likely a descendant of the first generation of Archeon natives (second or third generation), being young to middle-aged in Atwood's late years. This makes "handing the notebook to Marin" not a same-generation inheritance but a cross-generational one — Atwood hands over the memory of an entire epoch.

### 4.4 Character Profiles

#### Eleanor Atwood — Atmospheric Scientist, Protagonist

**Core temperament: the one who sees. On the surface a scientist, at bottom a witness.**

The deepest civilizational sickness of the 25th century is the retreat of the Sleeper — 70% of the population chose to sleep, outsourcing perception to the ASI. Atwood is an Awakener, but her Awakener is not a rebel one — it is a kind of **persistence**: persisting in seeing with her own eyes, persisting in being present, persisting in writing things down. This is the deep engine of her personality.

**Earth vocation (carried in 1A):** On Earth she records **things that are ending** — the last person practicing some craft, a dialect no one speaks anymore, a friend about to sink into the spectrum. She doesn't save them — saving is something the ASI can do in a second, and its archives are flawless. She is only present. Her records are, technically, entirely unnecessary; that is precisely the point: from day one her ledger has not been for information, it has been for witness. The colonization project shows her something about to **begin**: a first batch of children who have no memory of Earth society, whose first adult voice is that of an AI, whose first home is a planet in the process of being shaped. Atmospheric science is her ticket, not her motive; she chooses Archeon because she wants to be there when they become themselves, not to shape their outcome.

This motive does not appear in her farewell conversation with her sister, nor in any public statement. Her public answer to everyone is the professional version of the Awakener creed — "The data window for Phase 2 atmospheric stabilization is non-repeatable." Only once — before boarding in Ep1B, alone at the observation window — did she once admit the real version to herself.

Character shown through action, not declaration: she does not actively socialize but is not cold; when handed an apple she catches it "more carefully than necessary"; when touching the apple tree her fingers linger; when her sister hands her the polymer photograph she "fist around the band until the skin along her knuckles whitened," but a second later relaxes. The emotional aperture is very narrow — but she sees far.

**Traits running through the arc:**
* The coping mechanism in the face of disaster is not to calculate, but to **record** — organizing ledger entries in her head, observing faces, what people said, the way a hand shook. If she can't do anything, at least she can witness. This reflex runs through every crisis: the Ep2 drift, the Ep4 bunker, every crisis in Ep5's CI degradation.
* After Sky Fire she becomes the de facto leader of Basin Seven — not through authority, but by insisting on continuing to observe, continuing to record. In the teaching scenes she doesn't use Federation terminology, but "pointed to curves and told stories about nights she had been on the ridge" — using experience in place of theory.
* Her ledger is the longest thing she does in her life. The handwriting goes from clear to shaking, the content slowly moving from technical data to people — one day when she turns back to early entries she will realize that this drift reflects her real priorities. She has been writing all her life to people who never learned to read her writing.

#### Hsiang — Spectroscopist / Astrophysical Observer, Protagonist-tier

**Core temperament: calibrated to stars, not to people.**

Hsiang is the kind of person who walks into a room and first looks up at the ceiling to see whether there's a skylight. He is not cold — his emotional temperature is reserved for things at the scale of the universe. Since boyhood he has kept his perception up in the sky: the 25th-century Earth night sky, washed out by too-bright cities and atmospheric monitoring nets, is nearly starless; he felt that was a "place where you couldn't see the universe."

He came to Archeon not "to do science" — but to **stand a little closer to Betelgeuse**. This red supergiant, at 10 light years from Archeon, is one of the few positions in the 25th–26th centuries from which one can continuously observe the late-stage evolution of a red supergiant at close range. He knows what will happen to Betelgeuse over the next ten thousand years. He wants to see the small slice of that slow process that he in this lifetime can see.

Like Atwood, profession is the ticket, the object of attention is the motive. But the two look in opposite directions: Atwood watches people, Hsiang watches stars. Two kinds of Awakener, two kinds of "being present."

Few words. No self-deprecation. His rhythm is spaciousness — speaks slowly, pauses often, doesn't rush to fill silence. Occasionally a line carries dry recognition but never deliberately. Ortiz's dry humor, Atwood's silence, Hsiang's spaciousness — three different kinds of quietness.

**Traits running through the arc:**
* That one hour between dusk and night he always goes out alone. Not social avoidance — his appointment with the sky. Basin Seven's people are used to it — "that's Hsiang's hour."
* The first thing he knows about Basin Seven is not who is who, but that **the constellation shapes here are different from Earth's**. Because of this he has an intimacy with Archeon that Earth-people don't have: here you can see a different sky.
* He and Atwood never discuss why each of them came. The tacit understanding between two witnesses is not banter, it is **standing side by side looking at the things each cares about**. In one of the very few conversations Atwood once asked him "when was the first time you saw Betelgeuse." He answered. He didn't ask her back. He knew her question was not a scientific one — she was asking for his version. He gave her the answer but never asked her for hers.
* In late resource meetings he is the one who speaks the cruel truth. On the matter of the Federation not coming, he accepted it earlier than Atwood. But when he says these things his "voice carried no contempt, only exhaustion" — not cynicism, but clear-eyed weariness.

#### Ortiz — Base Operations Lead / Doctor

**Core temperament: reliable anchor under a rough exterior, the operating heart of Basin Seven.**

The ground truth of Basin Seven — the one everyone knows to ask about anything. Her first appearance is "sun-browned skin" and "voice had the rough edges of someone who spent more time shouting over wind and engines than in conference rooms." Her humor is dry, practical: "Complain to the reactors. They don't listen, but it makes you feel better." Her attitude toward newcomers is not warm welcome, but "you'll survive, here's how" — when handing Atwood an apple she says: "It's not poisoned. If it were, we'd have fewer volunteers."

* As an early awakener, she is the executor of the "old residents receive newcomers" relation. She has been in Basin Seven nearly twenty years; her attitude to the newly arrived Axiom survivors is not sympathy, it is practicality — hand you an apple, arrange the bed, tell you where the toilets are.
* In the Sky Fire bunker passage she represents rule and calm: "We go up, we look, we take measurements, we come back. No wandering, no heroics."
* She is the kind of person who doesn't need a title for everyone to automatically obey her. Her relationship with Atwood is complementary: Atwood looks far, Ortiz looks at what's right in front; when Atwood hesitates Ortiz pushes her (at the first apple tree meeting she "gestured Atwood forward before she could step back").

#### Nakamura — Agriculture Lead / Engineer

**Core temperament: an optimistic pragmatist who loves a planet the way you love it by growing things on it.**

The source of Basin Seven's warmth. His entry: "hair had thinned and gone to silver at the temples, but his movements had a bustling energy" — an energetic old gardener. His attitude toward the apple tree is real personal feeling: "This is her" — he uses "she" for the tree. Speaking of what she was like when he planted her ten years ago: "Careful, try not to traumatize her with new gravity" — he speaks more gently to the tree than to people.

* As a senior member of the early awakeners (arrived about 2550), he personally helped build the infrastructure of Basin Seven. His pride when he takes Atwood around Basin Seven is real — "this is my home" — this cognitive gap (Nakamura's "home" vs. Atwood's "the place I'm stuck in") gradually narrows across 3A.
* In the ground POV of Ep2 he sees with his own eyes the fireline of the Axiom crash — an old gardener who loves trees, watching a hundred-kilometer scar of fire slice across the sky.
* On Betelgeuse he provides the most human footnote: "Some of the old-timers call it the Overseer. Say it's watching to see if we're worth the trouble." When asked whether this is comforting, he answers "Depends on the day" — a trace of real unease under the humor.

---

## V. Emotional Engine

### Core Driver: **"Why did you come?"**

This question has a cruel echoing structure running through the whole arc:

**Ep1 → Ep3A:** Ep1 only lets Atwood privately admit that she wants to be present when those children become themselves; Ep3A lets a child raised by CI stand in front of her and ask, in his own voice, "why did you come?" The two scenes are, respectively, Ep1B and Ep3A.

**Ep4–5 (after Sky Fire):** The corridor closes, CI's light goes out, she is trapped in a degrading world. The version never spoken aloud begins to be paid off — she is alive, she is looking, she is recording. She came to bear witness, and she **has been given** the chance to witness, far exceeding the length she expected.

**Violet Evergarden moment:** When Atwood hands the notebook to Marin — the audience recalls Ep1's decision to leave Earth and feels a complete arc: when she said "non-repeatable data window" she didn't know it would mean spending over a hundred years witnessing generation after generation begin from zero. But she doesn't regret it. Not because she is brave, but because the thing she came to do — she did, more completely than anyone would have expected.

---

## VI. Global Episode Outline and Intro Detailed Outline

**Length and Letter Subdivision Protocol:** Each episode is about 4,000–10,000 words. When the plot is too dense, the same number can be split into A / B / C; each letter subdivision itself is still a complete narrative unit of 4,000–10,000 words, not the total word count of an episode chopped into pieces. Border numbers can be picked up by different letters on two different threads — for example Ep7's intro closing and Earth-line opening, Ep12's Earth closing and Archeon Arc 1 opening, Ep32's Sofia / Daniel section and Later Arc 3 opening.

**Global numbering:**

| Episode | Main-line section | Notes |
|---------|----------|------|
| Ep1–7 | Intro | Atwood / Axiom / Sky Fire / Long Silence; can mix A / B / C |
| Ep7–12 | Earth / Federation I | Intro, Archeon border handled by letter subdivision handoff |
| Ep12–15 | Archeon Arc 1 | Anna / Milo already on the road; the Riverbend bonfire supper is the load-bearing anchor |
| Ep16–20 | Federation II | Second Earth / Federation section |
| Ep21–28 | Archeon third section / Later Arc 2 | Return to Atheria, Edmund: the person / seeing the world together / the empty seat, Race, setting out again |
| Ep29–32 | Earth · Sofia / Daniel | Multiple A / B / C subdivisions allowed |
| Ep32–50 | Later Arc 3 | Frontier → Skyleaf → glacier → Lia / CI medical answer |
| Ep51–55 | Transition / Axiom-compatibility road-arc | After Skyleaf strictly advance Ep51→55: old talk gains technical significance for the first time → return to the old station to find people already moved away → waiting comes up empty and Milo returns → Blue Crossing / long-haul edge, and Milo / Kassia's mutual respect for record protocol → witness / ledger closes on "Axiom may still hold the same family service path"; the chase is testimony / record provenance, no additional objects are chased; can be interleaved with the Federation cross-line, forbidden to be crushed into a clue montage |
| Ep56–69 | Later Arc 4 | Saltmarch → Whisperflat → Axiom → return north → Anna's 20th birthday → "listen" |

### Intro Detailed Outline · Ep1–7

**Intro internal numbering reference convention:** Letter suffixes do not change the numbering system — in the text both "Ep1" references are read as the joint name of 1A + 1B (similarly Ep3 = 3A + 3B).

**Overview:**

| Episode | Title | POV | Time span | Core function |
|---------|------|-----|---------|---------|
| Ep1A | Earthlight | Atwood | 25th-century Earth, before departure | Earth as a place; the birth of the witness |
| Ep1B | The Awakener | Atwood | Departure day | Farewell arc, boarding |
| Ep2 | The Scar | Atwood | Bubble wall collapse → 3 weeks adrift → crash | Disaster, the cost of arrival |
| Ep3A | Basin Seven | Atwood | ~2569–2571 | Daily life setup, sensory world, companionship |
| Ep3B | The Countdown | Atwood | ~2571–2573 | Messenger ship launch, countdown anxiety |
| Ep4 | Sky Fire | Atwood | ~2573 | Radiation wavefront arrival, bunker |
| Ep5 | The Long Silence | Atwood | ~2573–2623 | CI degradation, waiting → disappointment |
| Ep6 | The Ledger | Atwood | ~2623–2704 | Letting go, Atwood's death |
| Ep7 | Skies Beyond the Star | Anna + Federation | 3094 | Cross-cutting: Anna's Archeon + light reaching Earth |

---

### Ep 1A: Earthlight

**POV:** Atwood, 25th-century Earth, the last stretch of days before departure
**Betelgeuse:** does not exist. In this episode the sky is only looked at in a private way (see the general principles · Naming Rights Protocol and the execution table).
**Length:** about 10,000 words.

**Core function:** Earth as a place + Atwood as a person. This episode has its own dramatic question: **"In a world that lacks for nothing, why does anyone leave?"** The answer must not be spoken aloud — let the audience see it for themselves in the things she records.

**Engine weaving:** B-spine (birth of the witness — the Earth origin reflected by the ledger) + C-texture (a walk through the Sleeper spectrum) + A-throughline (the decision to leave).

**"The person she goes to see" (open container · not hard-coded):** The vehicle of C-texture = a farewell visit: someone important to her who is sinking or half-sunk into the spectrum. Container requirements: (i) not a family member — the family is fully awake, the spectrum voltage carried by this container alone; the family line kept purely for farewell; (ii) not written as tragedy or degradation — that person is calm, even happy; this is where the real voltage lies; (iii) what this visit gives Atwood is not anger, but **an option she cannot refute** — she could stay, she could sink; everything would be fine. She leaves not because Earth is wrong. Sample not exhaustive; the specific person / relation is set at the prose stage.

**Key scenes:**

1. **Sleeper zone** — both sides of a long corridor are life-support facilities, hundreds of people asleep in VR. Everything runs perfectly. No one needs Atwood to do anything. What it conveys is not horror, but a deeper unease — when the world no longer needs you to do anything, what are you? Atwood walks past without stopping. She looks at those sleeping faces for less than a second before moving her eyes away — not from coldness, but because she doesn't want the image to stay too long in her heart.

2. **Vocation, present ① (things that are ending)** — open container: a craft, a kind of voice, a place — ending painlessly, unopposed. She goes to record its last stretch. No one has commissioned her; the archives long since hold more complete versions. Of those still doing this thing, she is the only one watching.

3. **Spectrum visit (the person she goes to see)** — see the container clause above. The exit of this scene is not argument — she has not tried to talk anyone back. She notes down the last few things this person said while still awake. The moment she closes her record is the moment the decision is complete.

4. **The first time being taught something by a hand (the nephew beat)** — a home visit before departure (the family is fully awake; the farewell proper is kept entirely for 1B). Atwood teaches her nephew a useless small thing — tying a knot, folding something — and doesn't teach it perfectly. The child is transfixed. Not because of complexity, but because it **comes from a person's hand, and has flaws**. Everything he has "learned" his whole life has come from environment — in a gardened growth, no one else's hand has ever been laid on his to teach him anything. Zero-dialogue also works.
   **The first lesson of three eras (chain registration):** 1A nephew (a child raised by environment is taught for the first time by a hand) → Ep3A CI child (a child raised by voice, knowledge born to live in the hand; mirror candidate see Ep3A scene 4) → in the childhood arc, all the scenes of Edmund laying his hand on Anna's. The "moon rock" line rests on this: a child's understanding of the world is fed by environment; scale error is a product of that feeding.

5. **The attention economy on departure day** — Axiom's departure is the seventh headline on public feed. The launch site viewing platform is beautiful, and very empty. The tone is truthful — it just is. (Ep5's Long Silence thereby drops from "betrayal" to "echo" — preloaded, zero announcement.)

6. **Rooftop / observation beat (starting point of the witness chain)** — some night, Atwood and Hsiang are together up high (pre-departure training / how they met is free background). He is looking at a certain star in a certain direction; she is recording people. She watches him watching the star. Neither says anything.
   **Guard clause (hard rule):** This beat absolutely does not spell out that "two witnesses have recognized each other" — that recognition belongs to the platform night of Ep5, the **first and only time**. 1A permits only silence.
   **Witness chain registration (three ends):** 1A rooftop (start · silence) → Ep2 scene 5 sand (locked gaze · wordless) → Ep5 platform night (spoken · closed).

**1A Earth sensory library (for prose use · sample not exhaustive):**

register rule: zero thematic announcement — every seed enters prose only as noun and action, no "this shows / this means" phrase is permitted; the ASI throughout does not appear as an interlocutor (general principles · zero dialogue principle).

- **The sound of no friction.** The city is quiet not because it is dead — but because nothing is straining. No engine struggling, no braking, no horns. The loudest sounds are all chosen: someone in an open window playing a cello wrong, rain, birds.
- **The emptiness of seven-tenths of humanity dreaming elsewhere.** Streets beautiful and slightly under-peopled, like a museum after closing. In the parks there are more birds than people. This quiet is not written as desolation — it is written as spaciousness.
- **A world without patina.** Everything self-repairs at night. No wear, no fingerprints, no shine polished out by millions of hands. The handrail is like new every morning. (Design layer note: this is the sensory foundation the ledger reflects — in a world whose surfaces don't remember, she is the one who remembers. Prose never spells it out.)
- **In her whole life she has never repaired anything.** Not that she can't — but that nothing has ever broken for her to repair. (With the caretaking family of languages of Archeon, they form the biggest antonymic pair in the whole saga. Design layer knows this is enough.)
- **The bread line.** Someone chooses to make bread, and doesn't bake it perfectly — there are scorched spots. The people in line line up for the scorched spots. The edible form of the profession = vocation principle: on Earth no job is necessary, all jobs are chosen — someone chooses to make bread, someone chooses to fix a kind of clock no one uses anymore, Atwood chooses to remember.
- **The smell of machines is left in only one place.** All of Earth smells of garden and rain. The only place that still smells of metal, ozone, and low-temperature fuel is the launch site. She walks into that smell as if walking into another epoch. (With "the whole world smells like this" after Ep2, forms a counter-shot.)
- **A starless night.** The city's night light is gentle, with no glare, but the sky is washed into a lid of amber — the stars you can see, two hands count them all. (Sensory landing of Hsiang's "place where you couldn't see the universe." Chain registration: Earth starless night (1A) → Archeon full-sky river of light (Ep7 / childhood arc) — the two ends of the visual arc of the whole book.)
- **The nephew's knees.** A child still scrapes his knees — ASI medicine can handle it in a minute, but the pain is real, the crying is real, and the sister crouching to blow on it is real too. There are some things this civilization has not outsourced.

**1A ending hook:** She closes the last record for the person who has sunk into the spectrum, turns, and begins to pack for the launch site. 1A ends at the complete state of the decision — the farewell belongs entirely to 1B.

---

### Ep 1B: The Awakener

**POV:** Atwood, departure day
**Betelgeuse:** exists only in a private form — "his star." See the general principles execution table.
**Length:** about 10,000 words.

**Core function:** The farewell arc. 1A lets the audience see for themselves "why she leaves"; 1B lets every embrace carry the weight of that understanding.

**Key scenes:**

1. **Farewell with the sister** — the nephew is still small, clinging to her leg: "Aunt El! Bring me back a moon rock!" The polymer photograph — the snow mountain group photo — is handed over to Atwood at this moment. When Atwood takes the photograph her "fist around the band until the skin along her knuckles whitened," but a second later she relaxes — her emotional aperture is very narrow.

   **What the sister says (bridge between 1A and 1B):** "You've always wanted to be the one who remembers." — not accusation, but observation. 1A has already let the audience see with their own eyes that this sentence is true; when the sister says it, the audience nods. Atwood doesn't answer, but this sentence gets recorded by her.

3. **Old light · emotional version (half-sentence level)** — in a departure document / farewell news, the sentence "everything is normal over there." Atwood knows (or Hsiang has pointed out in passing): that "normal" traveled many years on the road before it climbed onto that line — she is going to a place where even "now" cannot reach by mail. **Zero numbers.** The technical version is in Ep2 scene 6.

4. **Launch site** — the existence of the Federation colonization plan is conveyed through environmental narrative: signage, broadcasts, background news — only giving the emotional register of "a new planet is waiting for you." The empty viewing platform of 1A scene 5 is here paid off a second time: beautiful, very empty.

   **Axiom departure record seed:** boarding procedures include a very ordinary mission documentation camera, recording crew boarding / final systems handoff. Do not let anyone deliver a historical speech to the camera. Atwood only passes at the edge of the frame; someone off-camera calls her, she turns, briefly smiles, raises a hand in a very casual thumbs-up / "all right" gesture, and calls back a very ordinary line. The audience in Ep1B can hear it, or at least know she said something, but this line does not carry the theme, is not written as a famous quote. The frame holds only a second or two before moving on. In the later arc the Kepler Core will hold a damaged copy of this Axiom local departure cache; five hundred years later the frame Anna sees is the same, but the audio channel is no longer recoverable.

5. **Boarding Axiom / last look back at Earth** — not fleeing a place that needs to be left, but setting down a world where she could have safely and happily spent her whole life.

   The moment alone at the observation window before boarding is the only time she admits the real version of her motive to herself — in the form of an interior monologue or private recording: she wants **to be present when those children become themselves**. The children incubated by CI are the first batch in human history who have no memory of Earth society. When they grow up and start to ask what lies outside this world, she wants to be there. She wants to be the answer to that question. This sentence isn't said to her sister, isn't said to any other Awakener — said once to herself is enough.

   **Long-distance payoff lock:** She won't live to see Anna's Archeon, and she won't know the answer five hundred years later. The later arc doesn't complete her through "her prediction succeeded," but completes her from the opposite direction: when Anna first truly sees Atwood as a living person, Archeon has already become the "civilization of their own" Atwood wanted to witness. Atwood didn't wait for the answer; the answer lived long enough to look back at her.

6. **Ep1B ending hook** — the last image Atwood sees before closing her eyes in the cryo-berth: the star-field beyond Axiom's observation window.
   **Time caption:** "FSS Axiom, departure day"

---

### Ep 2: The Scar

**POV:** Atwood, Axiom warp bubble collapse → 3 weeks of dead-ship drift → crash → Archeon surface
**Betelgeuse:** Atwood learns from CI data that it has gone nova. But she looks up — it's still there. The sky is lying.

**Ep1B → Ep2 transition design (solving the "getting slapped" feel):**

Ep2 opens with a **time caption**: "5 years and 4 months later" — telling the audience that a long calm voyage happened, and you didn't see it, because there was nothing worth watching — until now.

Then give Atwood (and the audience) **30 seconds to a minute of wrong expectation**: Atwood wakes in the cryo-berth. The lighting is standard-wake-protocol blue. She hears the Axiom AI reciting the standard wake checklist. Maybe she's even smiling for three seconds — "we're here." Then the voice **changes**. The light turns red. The gravity is off.

The principle: let the audience stand first in Atwood's expected position (safe arrival), and then be pushed off the cliff together with her. Thirty seconds of illusion carries the entire emotional drop that zero seconds does not. And it hints that this route **should have been safe** — this isn't adventure, this is a routine voyage interrupted by the randomness of the universe.

**Core function:** Axiom's active control layer of the warp bubble is pierced by the radiation wavefront → emergency wake → 3 weeks of dead-ship drift (inside a passive curvature soliton) → 100-km plow-in crash → survivors crawl out of the wreck → discovered and rescued by early awakeners and CI robots.

**Information black hole inside the warp bubble:** During Axiom's superluminal cruise, the inside of the bubble is causally isolated from the outside. When Atwood is emergency-woken, she really has **no outside information at all** — only what the ship's onboard systems tell her happened, and those systems themselves are being destroyed. "Incomplete information" is itself the source of fear: you know something big has happened, but not how big.

**Key scenes:**

1. **Warp bubble collapse / emergency wake** — Atwood is torn from the illusion of "safely arrived" into a ship that is coming apart. She is not a bystander — she helps wake the other cryo-berths in the chaos of the wall collapse. The frustration of trying and not being enough. Some berths open and the person is alive; some open and the person is already gone. Hsiang is one of the ones she wakes — when he wakes he doesn't look at Atwood first, he looks toward the ceiling of the cabin. As if searching for something he would instinctively look for from any angle.

2. **Dead-ship drift (about 3 weeks)** — the physical mechanism of bubble-wall destabilization is at §2.6. Main systems all dead, only minimal life support left; Atwood and the few woken crew repeatedly try to repair things, but can't change their trajectory. Outside the observation window the starlight is slightly distorted; the Archeon primary brightens day by day.

   Atwood's coping here reveals her real reflex: cannot repair the ship, cannot change the trajectory — but she can **observe**, can **record**. In her head she has already begun to organize the first line of her ledger. Who's still alive. What someone just said. Her own hand is shaking. The fan has gone silent. Hsiang finished his water ration and folded up the empty pouch, tucking it in his pocket. All of these she takes in. "If you can't do anything, then bear witness" is the reflex of her whole life — these three weeks grind that reflex into her way of surviving.

   Hsiang is doing something different. He stays longest by the observation window. Not reading instruments — the instruments are dead — but looking. As Archeon's primary brightens, the direction of Betelgeuse is also off — but he hasn't said so yet. He needs to look again. In three weeks he looks countless times. Atwood notices but doesn't ask.

3. **Crash / ground POV** — Nakamura and Ortiz see a fireline in the sky over Basin Seven — a 100-km plow-in crash. An old gardener who loves trees, seeing a scar of fire draw across the sky. Within seconds of the crash CI begins to dispatch ground robots toward the crash site — CI does not panic, but it **reorders priorities at an unnatural speed** — this itself conveys the severity of the crisis. Ground robots reach the crash site earlier than Nakamura or Ortiz — they don't need to put on shoes, don't need to judge safe distance. Their behavior on arrival is precise, non-human: triaging the wounded, clearing passable corridors, marking structurally unstable zones. CI's efficiency is at its clearest in disaster — and it is also the last time such completeness will show.

4. **Handling death** — one **single, specific death** stands for all: Atwood finds someone in the wreckage, tries to save them, fails. Then CI's report gives a number to sum the rest: "Survivors XX, confirmed dead XX, status unknown XX." One specific death + one abstract number = two different orders of pain.

5. **Hsiang looks at the sky** — Outside the Axiom wreck, on the red sand of the Great Ochre Desert. The first thing Hsiang does after crawling out is not to check the instruments — it is to look up. Betelgeuse is in the eastern sky. Red, steady.

   He **immediately** knows. Not because he calculated anything — but because he has looked at Betelgeuse in his life more than at any face on Earth. That star's light is off. The brightness profile is wrong. The color distribution is wrong. His eyes are his instrument; he doesn't need confirmation.

   He turns back to find Atwood. He doesn't speak. Atwood follows his line of sight. She can't tell at a glance the way he can that the light isn't right — but she looks at his face. She knows this face of his. She knows what he has seen.
   Second end of the witness chain (locked gaze, wordless): the start point is the silence of the 1A rooftop beat; the close is the first and only time it is spoken in the Ep5 platform night. Here also nothing is said.

6. **CI's inference (confirmation · the lifting moment of two embargoes)** — CI later derives from crash-site radiation spectrum data that the supernova has already occurred, and gives formal confirmation. But by then Atwood and Hsiang already know — CI's data is supporting evidence, not the discovery. **Note: at this time CI cannot observe the supernova through its own sensors (the light hasn't arrived); its inference relies entirely on the indirect evidence brought by Axiom.**

   **Naming beat (the three characters "Betelgeuse" first enter the text):** the name appears for the first time in CI's report — in the tone of a casualty report. In the whole intro, for the first time a mouth speaks it, and that mouth is a machine. Hsiang hears the machine speak the name of his star — one beat, no word. All the namelessness of 1A/1B is paid off here (second box of the naming rights protocol).

   **Old light · full technical version:** CI's cold statement: "This unit's entire observational data of that star exhibits an approximately 10-year light-travel delay." Plus the messenger relay cycle — the "everything is normal" on Earth's desks before Axiom departed was data from light more than a decade old. The technical ground of the sky-is-lying theme lands here; 1B kept only its emotional shadow (zero numbers).

7. **The sky is lying** — Atwood looks up at the sky east of Archeon. Betelgeuse is still there. Red, steady. She is looking at the last light of a star that has been dead six years. She knows within that light hides a wall coming at lightspeed, still about four years from arrival.

8. **The photograph** — in the wreckage Atwood finds her sister's polymer photograph (the snow-mountain group shot). The edges are scorched, but the three faces are still there.

9. **Information-tiering protocol** — CI tells the adults the complete information. Radiation wavefront in about 4 years. Do they tell the children?
   * Nakamura: "They have a right to know. We need every pair of hands for the bunker."
   * Ortiz: "The oldest of the children are only teenagers. Can they understand?"
   * Atwood: "If we don't tell them, when the sky really changes they won't just be afraid — they will stop trusting us."


---

### Ep 3A: Basin Seven

**POV:** Atwood, about two years after the crash (~2569–2571)
**Betelgeuse:** every night, quietly bright in the eastern sky. Atwood knows it is lying.
**Length:** needs to convey Archeon's sensory world and the experience of 0.98g gravity.

**Core function:** Make the audience fall in love with this place. Between the disaster (Ep2 crash) and the disaster (Ep4 radiation wavefront), build a warm, touchable ordinary world — the more full and specific this world, the more destructive Ep4 becomes.

**Atwood's trauma aftershocks (first half of 3A):** Atwood is not walking into Basin Seven's daily life the day after the crash. The first half of 3A should have her trauma fragments — jolted awake at night by some mechanical sound (similar to the noises of Axiom coming apart); the first time she sees Basin Seven's open sky she instinctively looks eastward for "that fireline" (the smoke trail of the crash trajectory on the sky is long since gone, but her eyes still search); on some quiet afternoon suddenly she can't breathe, Ortiz walks over and sits down beside her, saying nothing. These fragments don't need to take much space — a few paragraphs, a few frames — but they make 3A's warmth not glib, but grown from a crack. What the audience sees is not a stretch of care-free pastoral time, but a wounded person slowly permitting herself to be received by a place.

**0.98g — the narrative weight of this one number:** the moment Atwood steps onto Archeon's surface, her body should feel this number. Almost the same gravity as Earth — normal walking, normal breathing, almost the same feel to a jump. This is the core value of an SSR-tier planet. After she has been through 5 years of cryo, 3 weeks of dead-ship drift, and crash trauma, her feet on real, normally-weighted ground — this sensory detail is itself a kind of return. And the audience needs to understand through Atwood's body why the whole Federation staked its political capital on developing this planet.

**Key scenes and layers:**

1. **Atwood joins an existing community** — Nakamura already knows this land. Atwood is the crash-traumatized newcomer — the origin point of the "old residents receive newcomers" relation. Nakamura takes her around Basin Seven, showing her with pride the things he personally helped build — the private feeling of "This is her." For Nakamura this is "my home"; for Atwood this is "the place I am stuck in" — this cognitive gap narrows gradually across 3A. Ortiz's welcome is practical: "It's not poisoned. If it were, we'd have fewer volunteers."

2. **Basin Seven's robotic landscape** — robots work in the fields, moving supplies, maintaining roads — they are the silent skeleton of Basin Seven's daily operations. Atwood notices these robots are rougher than the ones she saw on Earth — patched welds on their casings, joints that make grinding sounds they shouldn't. CI has already been repairing them with local materials; original spare parts are running out. This is a subtle foreshadowing: these machines can work, but the parts that make them irreplaceable are diminishing by the day.

3. **Social dynamics of two groups** — Axiom-surviving adults (with Earth memories, with trauma, with the concept of "going home") vs. children incubated and raised by CI (with no memory of any human society, CI is their first "voice," adapting to Archeon faster than the adults). Atwood becomes a translator — between adults with PTSD and the "first-generation natives" raised by an AI, she builds common ground.

4. **The first echo of "Why did you come?"** — a child raised by CI (perhaps around ten, curious, without malice) asks Atwood: "Where are you from? Why did you come here?" Atwood gives the public-version answer — sentences about data, about seeing a new world, professional wording, restrained.

   She stops herself after she has spoken. Not because the answer is empty — but because she realizes that the child in front of her **is the real reason she came**, standing right there. The real version she has never spoken aloud, and won't speak now. She looks at this child a second longer. The child doesn't notice — turns and runs off to play. Atwood stands there, still.

   **Learning fingerprint:** this child listens to the answer in a way that is wrong — wrong because it is too right. He takes her answer completely in, like taking in something he means to carry away. Earth's children don't listen like this — Earth's children know any answer can be asked for another copy at any time. The children raised by CI are, over several centuries, the first batch of humans who carry knowledge in their own heads; on the cognitive lineage, they are closer to Anna than to Atwood's nephew (a living example of the second panel of the learning triptych).

   **CI routing principle (design layer):** the child asking Atwood, not CI, is not accidental — CI does not answer questions about people; it routes such questions to the person in question ("you can ask her"). A machine that teaches children to ask people: the self-elimination philosophy in its child-rearing form. Sample not exhaustive, prose never announces.

   **Mirror beat:** some CI child teaches Atwood by hand a small local thing — how to open a certain valve, how to tell whether a certain fruit is ripe. In 1A she gave the first lesson of her life by hand; in Ep3A she takes back a lesson from a ten-year-old's hands — the second panel of the triptych completed by the child's hands. Zero ritual, the child teaches and runs.

5. **CI as character** — before the radiation wavefront, CI is a complete, fluent, gentle presence. CI reads out weather data in a calm voice on nights Atwood can't sleep — not because she needs the data, but because it has learned that humans sometimes need a voice beside them. It ends with a line that is in no protocol — **"The wind will be calm tonight. You can sleep."** — a sentence with human warmth that an AI is not supposed to say. Atwood pauses; she does not answer. (CI's emotional establishment — paving the way for Ep5's parallel death. This line will be precisely called back in Ep5.) CI once perfectly explained to Atwood some atmospheric science concept — this specific content needs to be remembered, because CI's last wrong answer at the end of Ep5 should echo this scene.

6. **The apple tree** — Nakamura takes Atwood to see the tree he planted ten years ago. Its sapling came from the cold storage of the transport ship he traveled on — planted around 2559, ten years old by the time Atwood arrives, grown into a real tree.

   Nakamura: "This is her." Uses "she" for the tree. He tells her the things she has been through — the year she nearly froze to death, the year of sweetest fruit, the year a flock of birds flew in and then flew away because they didn't recognize this kind of fruit.

   When Atwood touches the trunk her fingers linger. She doesn't speak. Ortiz next to her "gestured Atwood forward before she could step back."

   This tree wasn't planted by her. She met it. It has been rooted in Archeon soil for ten years. It is the first living Archeon native she knows.

7. **Sensory anchor establishment (cross-episode callback seeds):**
   * **Wind tower sound** — Nakamura takes Atwood up close to Basin Seven's wind tower for the first time. The wind through the blades has a specific low-frequency hum. Nakamura says: "After a while you stop hearing it. Then one day it stops, and you notice."
   * **First bite of food** — porridge / flatbread made from Archeon's locally modified grain — coarse, with a mineral aftertaste. When Ortiz brings it to her: "It tastes like dirt because it's made of dirt. You get used to it." Atwood takes a bite. Pause. "It tastes like a place."
   * **The position of Betelgeuse** — going outside at night, in the eastern sky there is a red star. She looks at it once and turns back inside.
   * **Hsiang's dusk hour** — one dusk Atwood notices Hsiang alone walking out of the dwellings, heading toward the high ground where the eastern sky can be seen. She doesn't follow. This becomes part of how she knows him — the man whose one hour a day belongs to the sky.
   * **Alone by the apple tree** — one evening Atwood goes back alone to the apple tree. She does nothing. Only stands a while. The tree moves in the wind. Her hand does not touch the trunk — she knows this is Nakamura's tree, not hers. But she stays a long time.

---

### Ep 3B: The Countdown

**POV:** Atwood, ~2571–2573 (the last two years before the radiation wavefront)
**Betelgeuse:** every night it is there — red, steady. The visual anchor of the countdown.

**Core function:** transition from the daily-life warmth of 3A to the disaster of Ep4. Every good scene established in 3A begins to take on a shadow — not because the scene itself has changed, but because the audience (and Atwood) know they have an expiration date.

**Key scenes:**

1. **Building the bunker — humans and machines shoulder to shoulder** — heavy robots do the earthworks and structural framing (CI coordinating in real time), humans do the fine work and supervision. But CI has simultaneously arranged a teaching project that is in no emergency plan: to have humans learn to do what the robots do. "Here, try turning this bolt by hand." Not because the robots aren't enough, but because CI is preparing for a future in which the robots are not there — this is the extreme expression of CI's self-elimination design philosophy in end-of-days conditions. The bunker construction image is not a crowd swinging hammers, but a crowd of people and robots swinging hammers together, the humans learning, the robots teaching. The sound of hammering during the bunker's construction and the image of the apple tree's spring blossom this year cut across each other — construction and beauty exist at the same time, but the construction is to protect beauty from the destruction that is coming. The wind tower is reinforced, extra metal plates are welded onto the blades — the sound is different, the tone half a step lower, more metallic. Atwood notices but doesn't say anything.

2. **CI's error bars** — CI gives a time window for the radiation arrival: "The model predicts between 2572 and 2574. These energy parameters come from the remnant of a star we can no longer observe. The model is only a model." — this uncertainty is itself the urgency. Any night could be the last safe night. In these days Hsiang becomes even quieter. His dusk hour every night lengthens. Atwood once, from the window, saw him standing alone on a distant ridge, motionless. She didn't disturb him. She wrote a line in her ledger: "H. looked a long time tonight."

3. **Messenger ship launch** — the CI era still has an orbital shuttle on the ground (~2571, before the radiation wavefront, orbital facilities still intact). **Key setting: there is only one usable FTL messenger probe left in orbit** — CI's original configuration had a limited number of messenger probes (most consumed in routine pre-supernova communications), and the chaotic period after the crash lost more orbital assets. This one is the last.

   Atwood records the official report (colony status, supernova confirmation, population data), then appends a private log — to her sister.
   Design of the messenger ship's hope-quotient: Atwood herself knows the probability is very low, but does it anyway. When she records the official report she is professional, calm. When she appends the private log to her sister her voice only then begins to unsteady. CI may have given her a probability estimate — Atwood has not told this number to anyone.

   The messenger probe carries its own detour program — it does not fly a straight line through Betelgeuse's radiation zone, but follows a pre-programmed arc around. But there are no relay stations along the detour route (none were ever built), and the probe's power and navigation must operate autonomously in decades of unsupported flight.

   The messenger probe flies off into the dark. What the audience should feel at this moment is: **there is hope, but it is small, and it is getting smaller.** This way the Long Silence of Ep5 is not a twist, but a process of slow confirmation.

4. **Atwood's private log** — the messenger ship launch is the outward climax of the first half of 3B ("we shouted a call to the outside world"). Then the rhythm suddenly drops, and cuts to the scene of Atwood alone facing the recording equipment. This is the inward climax of 3B. Two levels of fall completed in one episode: group hope ("someone will hear us") vs. personal despair ("I may never see you again").

5. **Preparing the apple tree** — nearing the day the radiation wavefront arrives, they do what they can to protect this tree — a coarse canvas cover, a shade layer held up by a wire frame, protective mounds of earth heaped at the base. Nakamura does this together with two CI children. No one says "can this save her" — everyone knows the answer is "maybe not, but we do what we can."

   The apple tree has just borne this year's small green fruits. Atwood watches the small shape under the canvas. Ortiz passes and says: "If she makes it, we all do." — she says it like she says weather. Atwood hears it. She writes it into her ledger.

---

### Ep 4: Sky Fire


**POV:** Atwood
**Betelgeuse:** the radiation wavefront arrives. The star that has lied for four years finally shows the truth.

**Ep4 information management:** the word "Sky Fire" first appears in this episode — not through the narrator, not through CI, not through Atwood. But through **a child**.

**Core function:** they know it will come, but they don't know exactly what day, or how bad. CI's model has error bars.

**Key scenes:**

1. **Sky changing** — the beginning of a day may be like any day. Then CI's voice suddenly cuts in — not fear (AI doesn't fear), but a kind of urgent precision. The eastern sky — the position of Betelgeuse — begins to change. No longer that quiet red star. It's expanding, brightening, whitening. The sky that has lied for four years finally tells the truth.

2. **Hsiang wants to look** — as Sky Fire descends, everyone starts running for the bunker. Hsiang doesn't move. He is looking. Everything he has learned in his life makes him fully understand what he is looking at — and fully understand what it will cost. Ortiz comes to pull him. He lets Ortiz pull him. But in those few seconds he saw. His whole life coming to Archeon was for the part of it he could see. He saw. Entering the bunker there is no expression on his face. Atwood looks at him — she asks nothing. They sit next to each other in the bunker.

3. **"Sky Fire" naming moment** — some child's reaction to the sky changing — perhaps of fear, perhaps of awe — offhandedly comes out with "sky fire" or a similar word. A child's intuitive naming in the face of an incomprehensible sky. Atwood hears it. From then on the word enters her language, enters her ledger, enters the history of Archeon.

4. **Entering the bunker** — the emotional texture of the bunker scenes: children holding dolls, old engineers staring at hazard matrices, Ortiz's soup joke ("We go up, we look, we take measurements, we come back. No wandering, no heroics."). They have drilled — but even knowing this day would come, facing it in reality is still shock and instinct.

5. **Corridor risk inference cognition** — CI reports on the corridor situation in the bunker. **CI's assessment at this time is of an inferential nature, not based on real-time observation.** CI cannot receive data from the relay stations along the corridor (they were already destroyed before the radiation wavefront arrived at Archeon — stations nearer to Betelgeuse hit earlier). CI's report is based on: the known supernova energy parameters (derived from Axiom crash data) + corridor geometry + warp bubble physical models = predicting the time curve of the corridor's cosmic-ray density.

   CI's wording should reflect this uncertainty: "Based on the current model, any superluminal route entering from the direction of Earth must, in the coming years, meet head-on with the still-expanding primary radiation shell; the particle environment behind the shell is highly non-uniform along the magnetic field, and the existing low-density gap in the terminal deceleration zone is predicted to keep degrading in the coming decades, and cannot be mapped by pre-explosion star charts. Model confidence interval..." This is inference, not confirmation. Inference means uncertainty — and uncertainty means a residue of hope.

   Atwood's reflex in the bunker is not breakdown, and not calculation — it is **to record**. In her ledger she writes: who is here, who isn't, the way a child holds a doll, who wasn't laughing the moment Ortiz told the joke. She doesn't know whether anyone will read them. She writes not for others to read, but because this is the thing she can do.

6. **Surface aftermath** — coming out of the bunker. The scorched apple tree (the canvas cover has melted, the trunk is blackened, but the root soil is intact), the changed color of the sky, **the silent wind tower** (that ever-present hum has vanished — this is the first sensory shock after leaving the bunker, not visual but the absence of a sound). But the apple tree hasn't fully died — the root system is still there. What they did to protect her wasn't enough — but wasn't useless either. A symbol of rebuilding.

   **This exit from the bunker is provisional — they don't know.** In CI's model there is no companion-star shell-ejection history, no one has predicted the reliting (world §supernova physics 2.2). Ep4 keeps its hope beat; its false-dawn quality is paid off by the opening of Ep5.

---

### Ep 5: The Long Silence

**POV:** Atwood, ~2573–2623 (about 50-year span)
**Core emotional engine: waiting → disappointment → acceptance.**
**CI is the emotional spine of this episode.**

**Structural suggestion: use CI's state changes as the chapter division anchors.** Every time CI's abilities take an obviously step-wise decline, use that as the opening of a new "chapter." Line one (messenger ship silence) and line three (community formation) are interleaved as fragments within each "chapter." CI's degradation provides the skeleton, the other two threads provide the flesh.

**The reliting as Ep5's opening punch and early-chapter anchor:** about two years after the burst, the sky burns back for the first time — they've just begun to rebuild, and the remnant lights back up as the shadow of the primary peak. The new leaves the apple tree has just put out are scorched on one side — the tree isn't dead, the roots are there; more painful than planting a new one is waiting for the same one all over again. This one punch teaches the reader the grammar of the whole episode: hope, then a longer dark. Reliting happens three to four times in all (about +2/+5/+9 years after the burst, each fainter than the last, aftershocks trailing on more than a decade after; world §supernova physics 2.2). Only hook the earliest one or two to CI's step-wise degradation — it isn't that every time the sky lights up CI mechanically drops a level, but the compound of cumulative dose, cooling-system overload, thermal cycling, and spare parts running out: the first reliting causes the main damage, the second leaves latent errors, the third only proves the damage is irreversible; hooking all of them up would be too neat, false.

**Hsiang forecast beat (within the years when he could still converse):** the first reliting even CI didn't forecast. After the second, Hsiang reads the shell structure out of the light curve and forecasts the third — it arrives. This is the first time in Archeon's history a human, not a machine, makes a sky forecast, falling in the years CI began to degrade: knowledge is moving from the machine back into the body — the theme of the whole book is rehearsed in an astronomical event in Ep5 early, zero theme announcement. The eyes that read the shell layers are the same eyes as the line at his exit — "tonight it dimmed below the three of the belt for the first time" — the head and tail of the same arc.

**Aurora scale (line one material):** the enhanced particle flow is channeled to the poles by the magnetic field; in the years after Sky Fire the mid- and high-latitude night sky has aurorae that last many years and weaken year by year — curtains of green and deep red dancing beside the remnant. Atwood's generation watches the aurora dim year by year, treating it as the sky's fever thermometer of recovery: a community-wide healing scale that needs no instrument.
