Line one's "the flowing of time itself" can be hung on this — of the same family as seasons and the apple tree.


Three threads interweave:

**Thread one: the silence of the messenger ship — the psychological arc of waiting**

From "they don't know yet" to "maybe they won't come." No external event needed, only **the flowing of time itself**. Conveyed through seasonal change, the yearly round of the apple tree's blossoming and fruiting, children growing from small to grown. Not a dramatic declaration, but a quiet, some morning's cognition on waking.

**Thread two: CI's degradation — parallel death**

**~2573–2582 (the years when it could still converse):** CI's core cognition is still close to ASI level. Between it and Atwood there is a real intellectual intimacy — not friendship (CI is not a person), not tool (Atwood has feelings for CI), not pet (CI is ten thousand times smarter than Atwood). The most accurate analogy: **a teacher you deeply respect, who you know is getting Alzheimer's**. He taught you the way to see the world; his mode of thinking shaped yours. When he begins to forget things — your heart breaks not from losing a friend, but from witnessing a **death of wisdom**, like watching the Library of Alexandria burn. The wind tower is repaired. The sound comes back, but not quite the same — some parts are newly forged. Atwood stood under the wind tower with her eyes closed and listened for a long time.

**~2582–2623 (the fragmentation years):** CI's language goes from fluent to short sentences, keywords, pure data output. Atwood becomes translator: only she can understand what CI's "stuttering" outputs mean. **The robots begin to behave oddly.** One day an agricultural robot walks into an already-harvested field and continues to "harvest" the air — CI's task scheduling has erred, and the robot's local layer doesn't cover this case. Atwood and Hsiang look at each other — no need to look at any data panel — a robot that can't tell whether there is wheat in the field says more clearly than any diagnostic report that CI is getting dumber. Humans begin to take on more and more physical labor — not a sudden transition, but a little more each week, a machine more each month.

**Hsiang's exit (2591±3):** radiation exposure + crash trauma make his body break down earlier than Atwood's. The last time he is with Atwood is not by the bed reading data — he asks to go out. Not far, just to the platform outside their dwelling where you can see the eastern sky.

Betelgeuse is at this time already the Betelgeuse Remnant — 15 to 20 years since the radiation wavefront passed, the star is no longer red, but a complex light diffusing and dimming. Hsiang has lived to see the complete slice of what he came to Archeon to see: from red supergiant, to supernova, to remnant. His project is complete.

Atwood helps him sit. She sits next to him. For a long time neither of them speaks.

He says something about an observation of some place in the sky. Not an explanation, not science — an observation. One sentence. The parts said to himself and the parts said to Atwood, half and half. Atwood doesn't respond — she hasn't studied those things he watches. She only listens.

**Line: "Tonight it dimmed below the three of the belt for the first time."** — a completion-of-work observation only eyes that have been looking for twenty years can make; his project closes in this sentence.
**Sky reference:** from Archeon's angle, the straight line of the belt from Earth's viewpoint is a projection coincidence; the three stars are scattered, no longer in a band; but three supergiants are still among the brightest in the night sky, **and still stand as a brightness reference**. Hsiang is a first-generation Earthman, a spectroscopist; he calls these scattered stars by the names from Earth's star charts — prose can note in passing: "these three stars haven't lined up here in a long time, but he still calls them by the name of the belt." Buried beneath: he watches Orion's shoulder die, and then uses the surviving stars of Orion to measure the brightness of its corpse.

Then he says a second sentence, to Atwood. One sentence. Not banter. It is the **first and only time** between two witnesses that they acknowledge to each other what the other is looking at. A recognition given by someone who only looks at stars to someone who looks at people.

**Line: "As long as someone is watching, they don't count as having happened for nothing."**
The third end of the witness chain: 1A rooftop (silence) → Ep2 scene 5 sand (locked gaze) → here spoken and closed. The first two ends absolutely not spelled out ahead of time.

Then they go on sitting.

The ledger entries that follow may be only a line of fact. The reader knows why she can't write more.

**~2623–2673 (testament period):** CI etches what knowledge it can still output onto metal plates. Atwood translates CI's fragmentary output into human-readable notebooks. This double-thread work is the urgency of "getting it done before the light goes out."

**The ending of Ep5 — CI falls silent:** Do not make it too dramatic. One day Atwood goes to inspect the CI core, as she has done every day for the last several decades. She asks a question. CI gives an answer, but the answer is **logically wrong** — not gibberish, but wrong.

**Callback design:** this wrong answer echoes some specific knowledge CI once perfectly taught Atwood in Ep3A — perhaps some atmospheric science parameter, some key numerical value from a radiation model. In Ep3A CI fluently, patiently explained this concept; now, on the same question, CI gives a wrong answer. Atwood corrects it — that she can correct it is precisely because CI once taught her. Then she leaves. The next day she comes again. This time CI doesn't answer. She waits a whole day.

That "The wind will be calm tonight. You can sleep." — in the fragmentation period, CI tries to say the same sentence, but only outputs "Wind... calm... you..." and then silence. Atwood finishes the second half of the sentence herself. After CI's final silence, Atwood sits next to the core and, into the air, speaks the complete sentence. No response.

**When Atwood and CI have their last "conversation," neither knows it is the last time.** Just like real death.

**Thread three: community formation**

Not survival drama, but the accumulation of daily life. The first birthday celebration, the first wedding, the first infant born on Archeon.

---

### Ep 6: The Ledger

**POV:** Atwood, ~2623–2704 (about 80-year span) + Atwood's death
**Core emotional engine: from memory to letting go.**
**This is the Violet Evergarden episode.**

After CI is gone, Atwood's role shifts from "translator" to "the one who remembers."

**Time-jump structure:** use **entries in the ledger** as anchors — each jump opens with a line of Atwood's handwriting, the writing going from clear to shaky, the content from technical data to personal reflection. The audience feels time through changes in the handwriting.

**Core scenes:**

* The notebook, teaching scenes (she doesn't use Federation terminology, but "pointed to curves and told stories about nights she had been on the ridge")
* A tooth-mark on the notebook — some child bit the corner of one page many years ago. She keeps it, doesn't repair it
* Children hang tags on the apple tree ("For the ship that will come back — I want a ride")
* Marin comes to ask for the notebook

**"The last contemporary is gone" anchor:** Nakamura's exit doesn't need to be dramatized — maybe just one ledger entry: "Nakamura went today. The first tree in Basin Seven he planted." Then the next entry jumps five years. The writing changes a little. That's enough.

**Robot exit line (ledger entries):**
* "Reaper 4 didn't start today. The wheat in Sector 7B will have to be cut by hand."
* "Took Reaper 5 apart to keep 4 going."
* "Today I took the last transport's drive shaft and put it on the wind tower. Goodbye."

**The children teach Atwood to let go:** Marin (descendant of the first generation of Archeon natives, young to middle-aged in Atwood's late years) says to Atwood: "You always say 'home,' but this is home." Atwood is silent. She nods. From "trauma-adult teaches child to survive" to "descendant teaches predecessor to let go" — the inversion.

**Ledger drift:** the earliest entries in Atwood's ledger were about wind speed and soil moisture. She turns back one day to an early volume and finds the whole book of 2570 is data. She turns to a 2640 volume — the first few pages are treatment tower records, and after that more and more are people — the names of fourteen people, what they said, when they began to cough, who cried at the funeral. She closes the book and finds she is not surprised. She has long since known. She is only now admitting it. The last entry might be: "No one at market used Federation terminology today. Even the word 'Federation' itself needed explaining."

**Wind tower callback:** a child asks "did the wind tower used to sound different?" Atwood says "Yes. But I can't remember it clearly anymore."

**Apple tree callback:** the apple tree, during the same period as CI's degradation, sprouts anew, and finally bears fruit. Atwood tastes the first ripe apple. This moment needs no line.

**Atwood's death:**

No grand funeral. One morning she does not come downstairs. The apple tree has a few new tags on it. "Thank you for your writing." "Thank you for your days."

**Violet Evergarden moment:** Atwood's tragedy is not "she didn't have enough time" — quite the opposite, **she had too much time**. She lived long enough to see CI die, to see Hsiang go, to see the first generation of Axiom survivors go one by one, to see the CI-incubated children grow up, grow old, and go too, to see the robots stop one after another. When she finally hands the notebook to Marin, Marin is not receiving the legacy of a contemporary — she is receiving the complete memory of the last remnant of the last epoch.

---

### Ep 7: Skies Beyond the Star

**Double-line cross-cutting, 3094.**


**A-line — Anna Freedman, Archeon:**

Anna's entrance — an airship slides in from between clouds, blonde hair, goggles, red scarf. But not a poster frame: she is at the ship's rail, one hand on the brass tube, the scarf's tail carried behind her by the wind. She is listening — not to a fault, but to today's sky. The brass tube is a little cooler than it should be for the hour. She pushes the goggles up onto her forehead, turns her head, and says over to the other end of the deck: this afternoon we drop at the north tower first; south tower can wait till morning. Milo, holding a crate, answers, doesn't ask why. The ship goes on flying. This is the grammar of the walking-shuttle: a low-stakes, complete, unexplained judgment; the audience sees how she flies the ship, not how she thinks. 521 years later on Archeon — a complete, self-sufficient, beautiful steampunk civilization. No warp engine, no FTL communications, no robots — but airships, steam turbines, hand-forged precision machinery.

Ep1–6 give this frame the weight of a complete prior history:

* The deck wood Anna stands on comes from descendants of the tree Nakamura planted
* The script Anna uses, its strokes inherited from Atwood's "straight script"
* The sky Anna flies through carries the same nebula Atwood once looked up at — only now it is no longer a scar, but "part of the skin"

**Anna's connection to Atwood:** Anna knows the name "Atwood" the way we know "Cangjie." She has read Atwood's ledger — copies of those notebooks circulate all around Archeon. But the name to her is part of history, not a person. She knows some words were written by her, some years recorded by her; she has never seen Atwood actually walking, turning her head, smiling, cutting a sentence in half. Archeon's memory media preserved her as handwriting, retellings, portraits, and a name — not as a moving record. Knowing the name but not the actions or voice of a person — this is the true texture of a five-century time distance.

**Later-arc payoff lock:** the Axiom departure fragment of A4-15 is the first time Anna sees Atwood as "a person who is really alive," not the first time she learns Atwood existed. The reader has already seen the same frame in Ep1B, so the shock comes from time distance, not from an identity twist.

**Callback scene:** in some public space Anna sees an old notebook copy in a glass case, offhandedly turns a page — on it is a line from Year 1. She doesn't stop. That line traveled through the hands of many generations into today's knowledge. Anna looks up at the sky — Sky Fire's Remnant is there, rose and violet interwoven — beautiful.

**B-line — Federation / Earth:**

The observatory's telescope captures Betelgeuse suddenly brightening. The light of 531 years ago finally arrives. The whole Federation confirms for the first time with its own eyes: that star is dead — "How is Archeon doing?" is pulled out of the file cabinet.

The 31st-century Federation is not the 25th-century Federation — they have FTL instant communication and warp technology far beyond the 25th century. 530 light years is no longer a decade's voyage but **three to eight months**. From "not worth going" to "can go anytime" — with the technical bottleneck gone, all that remains is political will and historical debt.

That light did something no messenger ship and no report had done in 500 years: it let the whole Federation public see with their own eyes the death of a star, and then someone remembered — "wait, we have people over by that star."

**Rhythm of the cross-cutting:** A-line and B-line advance alternately. A-line shows the answer (Archeon is alive, and living beautifully), B-line shows the question ("Are they still there?"). The audience knows both at the same time, and people on both sides don't know about each other. The last beat reveals: these two things happen on the same day. Anna looks up at the sky — the same nebula. The Federation observatory sees the death-light of the same star. 530 light years' distance, one day's resonance.

**Core question — what Ep7 leaves for later arcs:** not "can they go" but "in what state of mind do they go." Rescue? Archaeology? Recolonization? Contact with a civilization that no longer knows them?

**The fate of the messenger probe:** in the intro arc, no answer is given. Saved for the Federation arc. The probe carries its own detour program avoiding the Betelgeuse radiation zone; whether it arrives is later-arc material.

---

## 4.1 Intro · Ep7 B-line seeds

§F5 Ep7 B-line scene seed: Sofia sees the light
Time: 3094. The light of Betelgeuse arrives at Earth.
Sofia chooses to watch with her physical body. She could plug her consciousness directly into the observatory's sensor array — get full spectral analysis with millisecond latency. Her neural interface warms slightly behind her ear, signaling data packets waiting to be received.
She did not open it.
She walked out onto the balcony. Fourth floor of a curved building. The building's biofilm shifts toward warm orange in the dusk light. The air smells of — nothing. The 31st-century air is too clean; no smell of any flower or soil or food can penetrate the atmospheric purification systems. Sofia sometimes misses smells — in an old book she has read of "the smell of the ground after rain," called petrichor. She smelled the synthetic version once. Not the same.
The sky. On Orion's shoulder — that star which should have been dim red — is now a burst of white. Not warm white — the kind of oversaturated white that makes the eye instinctively contract its pupil. Her bio-enhanced retinas automatically adjust the iris. Tears rise up — not emotion, but brightness.
A few other people have come out beside her. One of them has data streams running across the surface of his eyeballs — he is looking with the naked eye while also receiving the analytical data through the neural interface. Sofia notices the corner of his mouth twitching — she can't tell whether at the data or at the light.
On the polis network — if Sofia wanted to "listen" — tens of billions of digital consciousnesses are discussing this at speeds she cannot perceive. She chose not to listen.
The light of a star that died 531 years ago has finally arrived.
Sofia's thought: "We have people over by that star."
On the polis network this sentence would parse into an information query. In Sofia's mouth, it is a bodily intuition — 500 years ago a group of people who, like her, had chosen the physical body were sent to that star. Then communications went dark.
Narrative function: on the same day, 530 light years away, Anna looks up at the same nebula — with two naked eyes that have never had any enhancement since birth. Anna had no choice — she has only naked eyes. Sofia has countless choices, but she chose the same way as Anna.

Attention synchronization (deep function of the light's arrival in 3094): the supernova's light is the first physical sky event in centuries that all modes of existence — every polis, every hybrid, every Static — share. A species fragmented into hundreds of thousands of mutually unreachable simulated worlds is forcibly pulled back into the same present moment by one beam of light. Post-scarcity has not destroyed cosmic stakes, only decollectivized them; this light for the first time re-collectivizes the concerns scattered over five hundred years. The trigger of the rescue is thus not a sudden guilt, but a rare attention synchronization: this species once lived by looking at the sky together, then stopped looking at the sky together — the sky had to explode once to get them to look up together again.

FED-line archival material (Ep7+ pending deployment, aligned with world §Long Silence fourth layer): One, the archive subprocess — from ~2700 on, the Archeon archive is maintained by a program that dutifully re-runs the risk assessment every ten years, generates a report, files it — no one reads. When Sofia looks through the archive she finds "decide not to go" was finally being decided by a process no one listened to. The dark side of the "no one speaks with the ASI" painting. Two, the failed petition — some Awakener in the 26th–27th century formally proposed sending a ship; it was procedurally digested by the "re-assessment" process. Someone had cared, once, and caring was not enough. This person doesn't need to be developed; he only needs to exist in the file, waiting for Sofia to run into him.


### Arc 1 · The Story Begins Already on the Road

Anna and Milo, when they appear, are already used to waking up on the ship, eating on the ship, taking turns watching the ship and the arrival. "Used to it" is written first through a specific morning on the ship: without opening her eyes, Anna already knows which way the ship is leaning, whether there is water under the soundscape below, whether the breathing of the envelope wall is even; then she looks at the chalkboard and it's the same as what she thought. Take-off, docking, and entering with a crosswind are all written through human motion, not proven by technical failures that she can fly.

On an ordinary line-running day, let the ship stop at two or three completely different small places, but don't let every stop fall into "shed + workshop + ship repair." One stopover can be only a pressing house shared by a few households at the edge of an orchard; another might be a small river village with a mill and a stone oven; after the ship stops, Anna / Milo enter the life someone else was already doing that day.

As the place Arc 1 truly lives in, Riverbend needs at least one full day. On approach the first thing to be seen is not a T2 berth, but two main canals stretching the town into a long band: the water's reflection cuts the houses, fruit trees, mills, and bridges into segments. Houses close to the water often sit on dark basalt stone foundations holding heartwood upper stories; the door front has a washing platform, a narrow vegetable plot, a fish rack, or fruit-pressing barrels; further from the water are the lighter wooden houses and farmsteads. There is no single "center" square you can spot at a glance in the town — bridgeheads, the communal oven, the water wheels, the food stalls, and various shaded long benches each gather people.

In the morning the upper canal releases water, and several water wheels wake in sequence from far to near; when Anna and Milo eat breakfast they can hear the first wheel already turning while the second hasn't moved yet. At noon they can be let sit behind the wall of the oven, or in someone's courtyard, to eat — no need to automatically go back to the workshop long table. In the afternoon children walk in the shallow canals, old people push carts along the flattest canalside path, mill, pressing house, dwelling, vegetable plot, and berth all exist at the same time. The bonfire evening is thus not "the town's only life," but only on this night, this stretch of canalside people happen to want to sit a while longer; downstream, other canal segments have their own fire and their own night.

Orun is still only one of the famous large landforms. The meaning of Riverbend comes from a town shaped by water and lived in for a long time; by the end the reader should still feel they've only seen a corner of Anna's working map.

### Arc 2 · Return to Atheria: person / seeing the world together / empty seat → Race → setting out again (Ep21–28)
======================================================================

### Narrative function

It does four things:

1. Let the nineteen-year-old Anna come back to the Atheria she really grew out of, but Atheria is no longer a frozen childhood image.
2. Fully unfold Edmund: first let the reader meet again a specific living man, then let Anna encounter ordinary actions the father and daughter once did together, once looked at together, and finally let "these things are still here" and "this person is no longer here" both hold true at the same time — he is a person, not an inheritance package.
3. Let memory go from grief material back to something Anna can still use, can still play with, can still share with others — carried by a race.
4. Let Milo, after leaving Anna's field of view, still possess his own life; when Anna sets out again she is not carrying an apprentice who forever follows her, but is inside a Loom that separates and rejoins.

Atheria is Anna's deepest knot, not the world's center.

### Atheria · Return-home shots


Atheria's terrain unfolds as Falling Plateaus:

Upper Shelf
→ first cliff
→ T1
→ second cliff
→ T2 / home terrace
→ third cliff
→ down to the horizon, the lower field-sea plain spreading south.

The northern branch of the Ishkar step by step falls into waterfall and water ladder.

Coming home begins from far away:

- Gust / adult work ship approaches from the southern plain;
- the lower field-sea unrolls first, the ship's shadow slides across crops and canal lines;
- the three falling cliffs are not "walls," but tiers of continental terrace rising layer by layer;
- the waterfall's low frequency enters the ship's hull first, then the ear;
- approaching the home terrace, the single-sided echo of the cliff face begins;
- the open south still eats half the sound; the horizon never disappears;
- other ships come and go at the pier terrace; Anna coming home is not the world pausing for her.

The sense of home comes from:
the waterfall's low frequency, the tube bells, cooking smoke drawn slanted by the south wind, Miriam's hands, the ways acquaintances call her, the light under the aerie's eaves, some small stall that has already changed positions, some slope she once trod as a child that has widened a little now.


----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-1 · Edmund: the person
----------------------------------------------------------------------

There is only one goal: love Edmund first.

See first:

- him getting flour all over himself;
- eating and forgetting what's still on his hands;
- a thorn-goat leaving him covered in mud;
- telling a joke that isn't funny, laughing at it himself first;
- complaining, before Anna, about the swing of a hammock aboard ship;
- on the road running into acquaintances who thrust food on him, and in another place lifting someone else's child onto the deck to look at clouds;
- he doesn't take Anna out "in order to teach Anna" — he already lives in these routes, and the child gradually enters his life.

The scope of memory must be larger than the front door.

Childhood P2/P3 has already established:
Atheria → Frontier transfer → Millhaven → Iris;
Atheria / Frontier → Thornfield / Ironvein → Amber Reach and other long hauls.

Edmund's happiness is thus scattered across many places — let the reader see:
the same person, called by different names in different places;
him at Millhaven eating with hands covered in flour;
at a stranger's long table giving Anna the last piece;
aboard at night waking first to listen to the wind a while, then sleeping again.

Anna's memory is not "dad taught me what," but:
"this person once took up so much ordinary air."

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-2 · Edmund: seeing the world together
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The nineteen-year-old Anna simply keeps running into very ordinary coincidences:
the way she now catches a load, waits out a wind, hands a finished thing to someone else to keep using, can all coincide with Edmund's ordinary motions;
she is used to standing at a certain spot at a certain berth because as a child he always nudged her half a step there;
what people in a certain settlement remember is not "the legendary Threadwright," but a man who found the window latch too noisy, put his cup down in the wrong spot at meals, and after finishing a repair would sit another ten minutes.

These coincidences don't prove inheritance.
They only say: Anna's body and judgment grew out of a long life shared with him.
Edmund is already dead; these motions can still exist; the two don't need to explain each other.

Use sensory triggers rather than lesson reviews:

- hand touches the brass tube that has cooled on the ship's rail → some time Edmund waited out the wind;
- the smell of flour → Millhaven;
- wet sea wind → Iris;
- a certain wood grain → a ship she rode as a child;
- a specific pull on a rope knot → Edmund once told her "wait, don't move";
- the shadow of a ship far off on the lower plain → moving day / first time seeing the underside of a ship.

The core is continuity of motion, rich in form and not locked to only the single posture of "put hand on it, wait."

Edmund's residue in Anna's body can be many small actions:
after finishing a joint, first letting someone else take it to try;
when supporting a weight not grabbing the other end away;
after laying a hand on a material, waiting for its state to actually show;
when discovering something is missing here, making a thing that lets the next person carry on.

The nineteen-year-old Anna will still exhibit these actions, but the object has expanded: a piece of framing, a ship, a stretch of weather, a crew, a person's own right to decide.

`diagnostic listening` is still very much Edmund's distinctive craft; what Anna inherits is not a set of readings, but a wider bodily habit: **before the hand goes in, see the causes clearly; after the hand leaves, allow the thing to still belong to someone else.**

The reader should see for themselves the coincidence of two generations' bodies from different actions, not "Edmund taught her X therefore she can Y" as a skill tree.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-3 · Edmund: the empty seat he left
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Only here is:

- the body shrinking;
- route shrinking before body;
- three normal afternoons;
- the second sound;
- voice-only;
- the warmth of the hand vanishing;
- the wind-return funeral;
- six months of spinning in place;

fully brought back in.

The point is not to re-explain plot, but to let the nineteen-year-old Anna finally, in the same arc, hold both:

"I really loved this person."
and
"I really lost this person."

After the third part ends, Anna is only able to step out of the aerie again — neither "healed" nor "let go."

The world does not complete her grief for her.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-4 · Race: bring the action back to now
----------------------------------------------------------------------


Milo boarded Gust when Anna was sixteen; by the beginning of the main line the two of them have already run about three years together, are a familiar crew.
Kassia too is not entering Anna's world for the first time at this race: she begins in the same stage to appear as someone repeatedly encountered along the line, only never fixed on Gust.

The function of the race is:
to let the Edmund memory the trilogy has just reopened appear for the first time in present-time not in the form of grief;
and at the same time, for the first time to let the reader hold, for a sustained stretch, Anna / Kassia's two flying styles that have existed for years, in the same patch of sky.

**Terrain**
The race is completely designed to Falling Plateaus:

- start can be near the upper plain / pier terrace;
- drop through a stretch of cliffside updraft;
- cross the open crosswind of the first terrace;
- skirt the wet, cold turbulence caused by the Ishkar waterfall's plume;
- do a curve close to the cliff at the outer edge of the home terrace, fully open to the south;
- the final stretch along the lower plain's thermals.

The difficulty of Atheria is:
the same patch of open sky is cut by three tiers of altitude into different winds.

**Anna**
Her strength is not the fastest reaction, but daring to wait.
When a gust pushes the skiff off track, she doesn't immediately pull hard; she lets the wing surface find part of its own balance back, then nudges lightly.
Edmund's "wait" here for the first time becomes pure joy.

**Kassia**
Kassia at this time is about twenty.
She is not a newly appearing rival, but the Razor and the person Anna has known about three years.
The race for the first time pushes her from the acquaintance who repeatedly appears along the line into sustained foreground.

She feels wind too, of course. The real difference is in the habit of motion:
- the moment the wind pushes the ship off a little, she often reaches for the controls earlier than Anna;
- she likes correction to be answered quickly, doesn't like to wait for the ship to slowly find its way back on its own;
- in dirty wind she'd rather use a short stretch of power to stabilize the attitude, then decide whether to borrow the next layer of wind;
- she flies beautifully, and knows it.

Anna's joy is in "half a beat later, the ship comes back on its own";
Kassia's joy is in "I touched it, it answers me right away."
Both are real joys of flight; the race only shows the action difference itself.

By twenty, Kassia is indeed less fond than at seventeen of handing critical judgment over to "it should be fine on its own."
But this layer only shows through in the action, no need to explain it as safety-outsourcing or trauma formula.

Who wins between Anna and Kassia does not matter — a championship does not judge which character's philosophy is higher.

**Milo**
Milo may not be a top race contender.
One of his key beats:

Pushed off by a cross-tier wind, he doesn't panic, and doesn't copy Anna's motions; he waits half a beat, sees clearly which way the next layer of wind is running, then rejoins the line.

Anna sees it.

She suddenly sees that those things she kept pressing down on the impulse to do, for about three years — letting him wait, letting him judge for himself — don't need her standing beside him to exist any more.

This is the first time Anna clearly sees:
"holding someone up" does not mean turning them into yourself.

**Milo / Kassia first relationship line**
This one is still kept for Milo and Kassia's own first real relationship line; that Anna and Kassia have known each other for years does not mean Milo and Kassia have established their own relationship.

Milo's take on a race route:
"No one finished that side."

Kassia corrects:
"Not that no one finished. Just one season of record."

Milo:
"There's a difference?"

Kassia:
"Of course."

Milo takes empty space for an invitation.
Kassia, seeing empty space, first asks what record is missing.

No trauma unfolded.
No philosophical dialogue.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-5 · Separating after the race
----------------------------------------------------------------------

After the race, Milo returns to Thornfield for a while.

Not emotional withdrawal.
Not a quarrel.
Not "graduation."

He has his own home, his own routes, people to see, things to learn.

Anna only asks roughly when they'll next run into each other.
Milo gives a not-very-certain season window.

The relations of the Loom, in the ordinary way, separate.

This has to serve the later Milo return:
when he comes back, he has to be carrying a life Anna doesn't know about, not frozen offscreen.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### A2-6 · Setting out again
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Anna once again leaves Atheria.

One can let her retrace a direction of the childhood road:
some part of Millhaven / Iris / Frontier.

This stretch only carries the repeated place, not the "first layer / second layer / third layer" structure of the red jacket. Some people directly call Anna by name; some still open with "your dad used to..."; some only know her surname; some know nothing at all. The red jacket can be present, but no close-up, no registered payoff tier, no signal to the reader that they are solving an object puzzle.

The value of the adult retracing is the repeated place:

- the mill that looked huge at eleven now looks ordinary;
- a certain berth has moved a few dozen meters;
- someone who once gave her food is no longer there;
- a branch line that didn't exist then is now written on the chalkboard;
- the place goes on living while she isn't there.

Arc 2's endpoint is Frontier.

Anna carries an ordinary work line that happens to bring her there.

======================================================================
### Arc 3 · Frontier → Lia's grey-lung worsens → about 1,800 km to Skyleaf → flower fields → glacier (Ep32–50)
======================================================================

### Narrative function

This arc is Lia's arc, not "Anna's redemption quest."

It does seven things at once:

1. Lia turns from the accident victim inside Anna's memory into a person Anna cannot summarize for her.
2. The worsening grey-lung pushes "ongoing fact" to the foreground, but does not create a deathbed countdown.
3. Anna and Lia build a new way of being together: when to stop, when to keep going, when to ask another person for help — Lia says it first, every time.
4. The roughly eighteen-hundred-kilometer voyage has to let Lia actually live inside those days on the road, not simply be delivered to Skyleaf; eating, sleeping, seeing unfamiliar places, talking to people who do not know Anna — all of it must occupy prose.
5. Skyleaf is established first as a whole place: the echo of the bowl-shaped plain, the warm damp air, the thin mist along the lakeshore, the red of the harvest season, the petal-drop flights that have been repeated across generations — all of it exists before the "medicine" enters.
6. After hearing "I'll get you there," Kassia expects to watch a promise slowly empty out. What she sees after eighteen hundred kilometers is different: the promise has not turned into a "definitely" pointed at the future — it has been broken down into daily actions. On the glacier, her own hands extend the life of that promise. The night after Lia gets her answer, she sits in Skyleaf, her body in a position that parallels her childhood, but holding something different inside. She says nothing.
7. The glacier then immediately makes it complicated again: Anna's bodily experience really does fail at one crucial moment; the local CI core, in turn, can only give Lia a bounded answer. Skyleaf offers no one a complete theory — it only lets several conflicting facts stand at the same time.

Skyleaf passes through at least one full cycle from morning thin-mist to afternoon flower fields to long twilight; the same place, under different light and temperature, lets people do different things.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Frontier: let Lia be a person first
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Before Anna sees Lia in the crowd on Frontier's lower pier terrace, she hears someone call her name.

Not Sara. A woman near the dye works, with a long strip of freshly gathered damp cloth draped over one arm, called out casually as she stepped aside to let someone pass. Lia answered something; the voice was covered by people coming down from the terrace above. She was comparing the color at the edges of two pieces of cloth with someone else, crouched on the ground, her finger pressed to the seam, unhappy with how ragged the hem was.

She is thinner than last time.

Anna sees that first. Then she looks at what Lia's hands are doing — the left one pressing the cloth edge, the right one fishing a smaller swatch out of her pocket — then she notes where Lia is crouched and how the flow of people around her runs, which side someone could pass on.

Only then does she come closer.

Lia looks up without surprise. They can run into each other at the same Great Junction now and then, exchange a few words, and go their separate ways. It has been like this for six years.

Lia: "Is this really that one from when you were ten?"

Anna: "The sleeves aren't. The left side isn't. The lining's been replaced."

Lia glances at it. "So is it still the same one?"

Anna: "Of course."

The two of them talk about one thing (red jacket / apron). Someone nearby is hauling a basket of dried fruit; someone crouches to tie a shoe. When Lia stands up she braces one hand on her knee, and doesn't wait for anyone to help her.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Grey-lung worsening: the window narrows
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Lia's body is narrowing, not collapsing.

The slope from the lower pier terrace, which she used to walk up in one go, she now breaks partway with a step she picks for herself, sitting down. Not looking at Anna, not looking at anyone, taking a few breaths, calculating that it's fine, going on. If a sentence runs long, the last two words shorten — not gasping, but her vocal cords unable to hold an even breath across that length. At night when the cold air comes down off the north-facing terraces she pulls the blanket higher, but leaves the window open: the cold makes her cough, but the sound of people outside, the arrival of the lift baskets, the distant water — being shut in the silence is worse than the cold. After one ordinary long walk, the next day she quietly cancels her other plans without explaining.

The day the Frontier healer listens to her breathing again, Anna isn't there.

Lia went by herself. After listening, the healer doesn't speak right away; she pulls out the old records first, compares them, traces her finger along the line from two years ago, quiet for even longer. When Lia asks "how is it," her tone isn't afraid; it's "all right, tell me."

The fibrotic tendency in grey-lung is inherited — in Sky Fire descendants, this line predates Anna, predates the accident, predates any one person's decision. The P4 gantry accident produced rib injuries, lung contusion and the resulting scar burden; Anna carries real responsibility for the accident, but she did not create Lia's grey-lung. This distinction is not made to let Anna off the hook — quite the opposite, it finally makes responsibility exact: she needs to bear the part she truly did, and she also has to stop appropriating Lia's whole body as evidence of her guilt.

When Sara and Kalean come to Frontier looking for Anna, what they carry isn't panic; it's a calculated urgency: Skyleaf's Ember Crown treatment can promise nothing, but under present conditions it is worth trying; Kestrel's Gate is still open this season; if they wait for the next one, the layer still loose in Lia's lungs now may no longer loosen. They have no backup airship on hand able to make the Gate's window in time, and Anna's Gust happens to be at Frontier; Lia also needs someone she knows to be with her on the boat for a few days.

Going to Skyleaf is Lia's decision. Sara and Kalean help her arrange it, Anna will fly her, but no one makes the choice on her behalf.


----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Anna–Lia: "look at the cloth" — the same correction, from window to flower field
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Lia's baseline is: she has already sorted out for herself what she can and cannot do, and she does not need anyone else to sort it a second time. She knows what grey-lung is, she knows cold air will make her cough, she knows there are people arguing outside, friends who will call her name, a chess game not yet finished. She has already made her judgment among all these facts. You don't need to make it for her again.

P4 window (thirteen / fourteen years old): Anna sees Lia pull the blanket higher and her hand is already at the window frame, ready to close it for her. Lia stops her: "Don't. I want to hear." Outside someone is arguing about a pot and a lid that don't match, the lift baskets clatter as they arrive, someone calls another person's name. Lia chooses the cold wind and the outside sounds — not to perform toughness, but because being shut into silence is worse than the cold. This is the first time Lia lets Anna see: I manage my own discomfort.

P5 dye works at seventeen: the moment Anna's eyes drift to Lia's breathing, Lia pulls the cloth back — "Look at the cloth." Two words. Not a speech about autonomy — a movement that pulls Anna's eyes off Lia's body and back onto what the two of them are doing together.

It is the same correcting gesture. The later arc doesn't need a verbal agreement — "we stop when you say stop / it counts when you say go on" is too much like a negotiation, and Lia does not negotiate with you, she just pulls you back. What the later arc needs is for "look at the cloth" to keep repeating along the eighteen hundred kilometers of road, each time pulling Anna out of past-Lia and back to present-Lia, the spacing between corrections gradually widening, until Anna no longer needs to be pulled.

Before departure at nineteen: while they're readying the ship, Anna reaches out to fetch something for Lia that Lia can perfectly well fetch herself. Lia looks at her — not angry, but with that "I'm here" look. Anna's hand pulls back. A moment later on some other task, Lia says "you do this one." Anna reaches out again. No rule needs to be spoken; the rule lives in the movement.

On the road: the same cycle continues, its intervals widening. Anna's "reach first" grows rarer. Lia says "I'm going to sit a minute," Anna lets her sit. Lia says "keep going," Anna keeps going. One time Lia goes on her own to talk to a stranger, and Anna doesn't follow.

Flower field: Lia stops to look at the Ember Crown. Anna's half-step forward is instinctive — she stops herself. For the first time she doesn't need Lia to pull her back.

Late Skyleaf (the only naming in this arc): Lia does not deliver a speech about autonomy. She only says one line: "I'm not the me from that day." Then she goes on doing what she was doing. It's named only here, so late, because none of the earlier "look at the cloths" resolved it completely — Anna's hand still arrived too early now and then.

Birthday cake: Lia hands it, Anna takes it. Her hand does not shake and does not pull back.

Palm up: Lia touches the back of Anna's hand first, Anna turns her hand over. Lia places her own hand on it. From "Lia blocking Anna's hand" to "Lia putting her hand there of her own accord" — a rhythm the two of them took six years to learn.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Kassia joins: the first sustained companionship
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Anna needs a second pilot.

By this point Anna and Kassia have known each other on and off for about three years. They remember each other's ships, each other's ways of flying, a few particular judgments, and are used to going a whole season without word; the Race was only the first time the reader gets to watch two ways of flying side by side for long enough to notice.


The better way in is still a very small piece of weather.

Before departure at dusk, the upper terrace still holds the last band of warm light. Down on the lower levels the flags have already slowed, but the narrow strip of cloth up on the top row loses its tension first; Anna doesn't look at the flags — her hand rests a moment on a wind-loaded strut, and she waves off a boat that is about to cast off, making it wait half a minute longer.

Kassia asks:
"Where?"

Anna points to the first place that changed. Maybe a fine sound sinking first, maybe the leeward side suddenly a layer cooler. She doesn't say it prettily, and doesn't try to turn the whole gust into a conclusion.

The side wind does cut in later.

Kassia does not praise her on the spot. The next morning, she has already gone over her own observations, the timings the distant mast preserved, and the notes of the previous watch. Between mouthfuls she says:

"You were forty-seven seconds ahead of the flag."

Anna: "About."

Kassia: "Those two words are irritating."

This isn't the first time Kassia has seen Anna react earlier than the flags and struts. What holds her attention isn't the head-start itself — anyone who flies with the same person a few days will run into that — it's that those judgments live entirely inside one person's body, and never turn into a rule Kassia can restate for her. She cannot say what it is Anna knows earlier than everyone else; she can only say that Anna is, in fact, earlier.

She also notices something else.

While the ship is being readied, Anna makes a perfectly ordinary movement, and as her hand passes near Lia there is an unnatural pause. Not a withdrawal — the hand stays a beat too long. As if confirming something.

Kassia doesn't know what it is. She only knows that Anna's hand has a tightness near this person that it doesn't have anywhere else.

Then Anna says something to Lia:

"I'll get you there."

Lia looks at her. Doesn't pick up the sentence. Says something completely unrelated — maybe "isn't that line over there loose?"

What Kassia hears is not the meaning of the sentence.

What she hears is everything the people around her said to her when she was twelve: "They'll come back." "Should be here soon." "It'll be all right."

Same shape. One person saying to another person facing an uncertain outcome, "it'll be fine."

Her body reacted before her mind did. Kassia:
"Skyleaf. I'll fly."

The practical reason holds: Kestrel's Gate needs two people who can each handle a ship on their own inside; with Lia along, Anna cannot be the only person who has to be intact at all times.

But at the moment she says "I'll fly," what's inside her is not warmth, not curiosity — it is something she wouldn't name. She wants to see exactly how this promise dies.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### The road to Skyleaf: about eighteen hundred kilometers
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Frontier
→ Thornfield (an eastern T3 town of fifty thousand; a place to break the leg here, verify weather — not a resupply stop)
→ Stormperch pass seasonal town (35k; the northbound seasonal line watches Marneth's weather here)
→ around the northern end of Marneth
→ Kestrel's Gate
→ Skyleaf

Total story-work distance: about 1,800 km.

Prose to render this uniformly as an eighteen-hundred-kilometer-scale / two-to-three-day-scale long haul.


Give these two or three days real temporal weight, at least:

1. **The first day is ordinary almost to the point of being uneventful.** After Frontier's upper platforms shrink behind them, the lowlands go on for a long time. Toward late morning the light whitens, the deck warms up; Lia eats something, leans over for a short nap, and when she wakes she finds the same range of hills still sitting in the distance hours later. When Kassia and Anna swap the watch, they can talk about things that don't matter at all.

2. **The terrain changes the air first, and only then the view.** The farther they go up Marneth's northern wing, the colder and drier the air, the more the smell of bare stone stands out; certain winds touch the ship before the mountain actually fills the view ahead. Let some small movement by Anna or Kassia happen first, so the reader only understands why afterwards.

3. **A night without danger.** They put the ship down for the night in an ordinary inhabited place. Thick walls keep the wind out; the hot food carries a spice Lia has never eaten; she truly falls asleep. In the early morning she wakes, and the same place has already turned into a different set of sounds: steam from the pot, footsteps outside the door, someone spreading the cloth taken in last night back out into the light. The reader has to feel that several hours really passed.

4. **A midway settlement that can be remembered, but doesn't carry a revelation.** It can land on a leeward shoulder along Marneth's northbound line: two or three dozen low houses gathered around a communal oven and a small windbreak courtyard, the lower halves of the buildings in pale grey stone, the upper timbers weathered silver by cold-country sun; a few mooring pylons on the slope outside are enough. After the ship settles, no one crowds the protagonists — someone sets a tray of something just out of the oven on a windowsill to cool, someone shakes out a length of cloth still faintly damp with morning dew, a child cuts between two houses. Anna and the others are just fitted naturally into a meal, a night, and a passing "when did that news come in?"

5. **The Anna / Lia rhythm continues to sit inside ordinary actions.** Lia says "I'm going to sit a minute," Anna lets her sit; Lia says "keep going," Anna keeps going. Once, Lia goes on her own to talk to a stranger, and Anna doesn't follow. No third person needs to name or verify this pattern of movement.



----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Kestrel's Gate
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Kestrel's Gate remains as Skyleaf's seasonal gate.


It belongs to the crater-wall breach at Marneth's northern end — the mountain body and the crater wall squeeze the prevailing wind through a narrow window. Flyable one season, almost completely closed the next. Inside the gate itself there are height layers, too: turbulent low, streaming middle, thin high. Flying it isn't "threading a hole"; it is choosing, minute by minute, which layer of air is worth entrusting your weight to.

For the first time, Anna and Kassia truly put their two habits to work in the same dangerous window.

Anna says the low layer is off: the wake is starting to break up; on one side the sound coming off the hull is going hollow.
Kassia glances at the wind band and the pressure strip, and points to a layer higher up:
"Up four hundred. The middle's still stable."

Or the next time it's the other way — Kassia sees the two layers' readings pulling apart first, and Anna only then hears in the ship itself
that the change has actually reached them.

Let the information travel back and forth between the two; who spots it first depends on where this particular gust begins to shift.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Skyleaf: the flower fields precede the medicine
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Skyleaf, a town people have lived in for many generations inside an impact crater, is established before "the thing that treats Lia."


First the air changes: a little warmer than outside, a moisture that begins to cling to the back of the hand and the side of the face; the sound off the lakeshore doesn't rise directly, but comes back half a beat late from the snow-capped mountains opposite. Farther down, white mist drifts across several rooftops, cut apart by the sun; red then slowly rises out of scattered baskets, drying racks, cuffs, and petals crushed underfoot, until it becomes the ground color of the whole harvest season.

Several lakes at the bottom of the bowl-shaped plain, together with warm springs and workable slope terraces, divide the thirty thousand people into a lakeshore core, the flower fields, and the warm-springs-and-terraces settlements, each visible from the others. Nearer the lake, pale grey stone bases carry darker timber upper stories; near the geothermal springs, thin mist often drifts between kitchens, bathhouses and greenhouses; higher up on the slope are seed houses, herb-drying lofts, guest houses, and the temporary meal sheds at the edge of the fields.

Sound must have layers as well. A single person's shout is quickly swallowed by the daily life of thirty thousand; what actually returns from the crater walls is a whole layer of sound: the morning slap of the lake against the shore, the steam-hiss escaping the bathhouses, a whole row of people calling for someone to pass a basket. The echo makes this town feel cradled by the ring of snow mountains, not "everything especially loud."

Locals have their own eating, flower-gathering, bee-tending, cloth-drying, lake-watching, bathing, greenhouse-tending, and tempers; and another local line that doesn't run to Frontier heads on from here to somewhere else. Skyleaf's wholeness must be established before the "medicine" enters.

**Lia's flower field**

Lia walks alone into the Ember Crown.

The deep-red crowns reach her knees, then her waist.

Her scale shock isn't "at last, an endless horizon." Atheria already has an open face to the south.

What is actually different here is this:
the walls are still there, but they have retreated to several kilometers away;
for the first time, space is filled by a single color, from her feet all the way out.

Lia stops.

Anna instinctively takes a half-step forward, and stops.

Lia doesn't say she is short of breath, doesn't raise a hand to signal.
She is only looking.

Anna doesn't translate the moment into her body being unwell.

Then Lia joins in on an ordinary daily task at the edge of the field, one Skyleaf people do every day: sorting the crowns by dryness, by breakage, by whether they are whole; broken petals into one basket, whole ones into another.

A local child asks where she's from.

Lia says:
"Atheria."

She doesn't mention Anna.

Anna could just as well not be beside her at that moment.

By the time they meet again in the afternoon, Lia's cuff is already smudged with red, and she is helping someone else push a basket into shade. Anna asks:
"Do you want me to?"

Lia: "No."

Anna genuinely doesn't reach.

A little later, on some other task, Lia herself says:
"You take this one."

Only then does Anna take it.

**Petal-drop flights**

During harvest season, the Ember Crown scraps sifted out by the medicine works are carried back into the fields by float-sledges riding the wind.

No one waits especially for this to happen. Several baskets of scrap petals are being hauled onto a small boat while another row of people down the slope are still eating. The boat lifts, rides a stretch of the basin's inner wind, and the first handful of red drifts out from behind it and settles onto ground that is already carrying red.

From nearby, petals strike hair, shoulders, basket rims, damp earth — brushed off by hand, or not brushed off. From the far slope, a few very thin bands of red slowly loosen into the wind, and you cannot tell which petal landed where.

It is, first, organic matter going back into the field. Only after generations of people have done this does it become one of the most beautiful sights of the harvest season.



----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Edmund in Skyleaf: an observation buried under twenty years
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Edmund really did work in Skyleaf twenty years ago.

Keep his causal presence, but change it into a layer of handwriting that has been covered over by twenty years of local experience.

At the edge of the flower fields there is a long-used seasonal paper, or a few pages of an old bound book that has been rebound. It is not a memorial and doesn't specifically preserve "Edmund's original writing." Every season people add to it: which basin-inner winds are good for releasing scrap petals; how far the western slope's shadow has to reach before the near-ground layer will chill; the year the flowers opened early and the old judgment had to be brought forward; the year warming came late and the line left by the previous generation didn't hold; the slope where the afternoon pushes fallen petals back toward the lake, so that later people changed the release direction.

Edmund's handwriting is only in the lower layers. He might have left one very specific, physical observation, for example: "Release only after the shadow on the western slope has crossed the third tier; the wind inside the basin will chill one layer first." Above his line there are already many later hands: someone writing "this year is early"; someone crossing out one direction; someone adding in the margin "not reliable in warm years"; a still more recent line by someone who doesn't even recognize the name Edmund.

Anna looked at these pages in her early days at Skyleaf. She recognized her father's handwriting, but no one stopped to tell her a story about it. The locals still use these pages to decide when to release the scraps today. She asks "do you still go by this?" and gets an answer like "which line?" or "depends on the year." — heavier than "this is a system your father left."

What Edmund really left is not a construct that must be preserved as-is; it is once-useful observation. Once it entered other people's hands it was corrected, for twenty years, by weather, flower fields and human judgment, until in the end it is hard to say which part belongs to whom.

Underneath the seasonal paper there are still older layers. What Anna went through in her early days were the layers this generation uses over and over; the older layers below she only knows exist, and hasn't yet looked at.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ember Crown shows first effect
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Skyleaf's existing treatment has a real effect on Lia.

The first few days after arrival: some layer of tightness in her breathing eases; she sleeps a little better; the spasms triggered by cold air drop in frequency. Lia walks alone in the flower field for one afternoon. Toward dusk she decides on her own to stop, and sits at the edge of a field to eat. Anna doesn't follow. Everything looks like an ordinary day of things getting better.

Lia genuinely believes it too. She isn't pretending.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### After the flower field: several small collapses
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Starting the second day after the flower field, Lia's body does things it hasn't done before.

Not one big event. A few small losses of control, scattered across two or three days.

The first is in the early morning. After she stands up she takes two more steps toward the doorframe and stops, braced there, catching her breath for a while. Anna is already out; by the time she comes back and sees Lia, Lia is sitting under the window and says it's passed.

The second is at dusk. The three of them are on a stairway preparing to head back when her legs won't hold weight, and she sits for a long time. A nearby person asks if they should carry her. She says no. When she stands up on her own, her hand braces once against the stone step first.

The third is at supper, mid-meal, one sentence going long, the vocal cords unable to hold an even breath across that length. She doesn't finish; she sets the bowl down, waits a while, keeps eating.

In between she is still the normal Lia — going on with what she is doing, walking, talking with Anna. But those episodes hang in everyone's judgment.

Lia herself notices first that this isn't the same thing. She goes on her own to see a Skyleaf healer.

The healer has real experience with Ember Crown, and years of baseline on grey-lung. After listening to Lia's description she doesn't pretend to know. She turns through several pages of old records, is quiet for a while, then says two lines:

"The layer Ember Crown loosens is still loosening. This isn't that layer."

Lia asks what it is.

The healer says: "We haven't seen enough of this."

Not evasive — honest. The healer goes on and says what she can: the location this is showing up in now doesn't look like typical grey-lung, and doesn't look like late-stage P4 contusion. It's more like a deeper pathway that has only come into view after Ember Crown has loosened one layer above it — a pathway local healers don't often handle. The parts Skyleaf can treat, they are treating. The layer they can't, they don't know what it is.

Lia comes back and, in the "all right, tell me" tone, tells Anna what's happening now. She's not asking Anna to go find a medicine. She's not delegating. She's telling her the facts.

Anna wants to speak.

Lia doesn't let her finish. "Don't rush."

The healer she went to see herself, the Anna she came back to tell herself, the next step she herself decides not to panic about.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Anna's asking: from one person's side to the next
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Over the next three or four days, Anna does the same thing continuously in Skyleaf: find every person who might know.

Not a scene. A repeated act stretched across days.

In the early morning she comes out of the old healer's door, and walks to the medicine works. The compounders at the medicine works know the ingredient boundaries more precisely than the healer — they know what Ember Crown loosens, and what this formula doesn't touch. At midday she sits on a stair with an old pilot who used to fly the northern line, one of the few who still has some link back to the Federation era. In the afternoon she goes back to the seasonal paper — this time she turns to the very bottom layer, and beyond recognizing her father's handwriting, she reads the layers under it that she'd overlooked before. At dusk she talks with the greenhouse keeper, who has long-term local observations on high-altitude pathways. As the sky darkens she walks back to the herb-drying loft and asks the people there about this year's window for several cold-margin plants.

The second time she turns through the seasonal paper she finds the line "north-bound / old station / next time." It's in the very bottom layer, pressed under twenty years of other hands, almost invisible. She recognizes her father's hand not because this line is special — she was already going through everything. She copies the line down and sets it alongside what she has patched together over these days from other people's mouths: a certain cofactor that only appears where the northern glaciers have retreated, where warm springs meet cold stone; the old coordinates of a five-hundred-year-old high-altitude CI observing station; the stretch her father had wanted to go to twenty years ago and never reached.

Kassia sees this process over and over. Not as one scene — every day. She isn't lodging with Anna, but they move around inside the same town. She sees Anna come out of the healer's in the morning and head to the medicine works; sees her crouched by the seasonal paper for half an hour in the afternoon; sees her on a stair at dusk talking with an old woman who once flew the northern line, the two of them still there when the sun drops behind the mountains; sees her already down at Gust in the evening, sorting equipment; sees her the next morning start again in the same sequence from the next person.

Anna makes no speech, doesn't break down, doesn't report her status to Kassia. She just doesn't stop.

The red jacket becomes itself over these days.

Skyleaf is a red-earth culture — the dye works, the medicine-work compounding, the sorting of dry Ember Crown petals, all handle red. Moving from one person's side to the next, Anna's cuff takes on three different reds in turn: the medicine works' ash-red, the deep red of dry petals, and, from a passing woman with a wet cloth at the dye works, a small patch of freshly dyed red.

Late in the third night, Anna is up too late over the seasonal paper, alone on a stair, her finger pressed on one line for a long time. She doesn't cry. When she tries to stand her legs are a little stiff. She presses her knee herself and gets up and goes on.

Kassia sees Anna spending herself. She doesn't tell anyone about it, and she hasn't figured out what she's watching either. She's just in the same spot every day — beside Razor, at the equipment — and every day she can see who Anna comes out of in the morning, whose place she's sitting at in the afternoon, when at last she comes back at night.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Sara / Kalean to Skyleaf
----------------------------------------------------------------------

On the second day, when Anna is starting to ask around, she also puts word back to Frontier on a boat catching the tail wind home. Not "come save her" — a factual inquiry: the Skyleaf healer has seen a layer beyond Ember Crown; has anyone at Frontier seen records like it, does anyone know someone who has gone in this direction.

Sara and Kalean, receiving the message, don't wait for a reply. They requisition a Class II airship able to make the Gate — same class as Gust — and fly it themselves. Kestrel's Gate is not fully closed this season yet; they can make it.

By the time they arrive at Skyleaf, they are as tired as Anna. They have just flown 1800 km.

Sara meets Anna as Anna is coming out of the medicine works. Sara stands at the foot of the stair and waits for her to come down. No embrace, no tears, no rehashing. She says:

"That's a problem. It doesn't fly nicely."

Anna nods. Neither of them says much more.

Kalean, once he arrives, first goes to see the healer in person. Not to decide anything for Lia, but to run Lia's current condition through his own ears.

Lia's reaction to seeing her parents is very simple. She sees that they flew here themselves — not on someone else's boat, on their own boat, through the Gate. She doesn't need to say anything. Sara and Kalean don't need to prove anything either. Lia's first reaction is not even to feel comforted; it's a little irritation — she has already said "don't rush" to Anna, and now she has to say it again to her parents. She says it, and doesn't say it a second time. That night she sleeps for a long time.

After Sara arrives, Anna doesn't stop. She still goes through the same sequence — healer, medicine works, seasonal paper, greenhouse keeper — only that now sometimes when she looks up, she sees Sara standing at the foot of a stair, or passing by from another direction.

Kassia doesn't know Sara or Kalean well beforehand. She doesn't introduce herself to them, and doesn't report Anna's condition. She stays where she is, beside Razor, checking, watching. When she sees Sara walk toward Anna, she doesn't turn away, and doesn't come closer.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Decision to go up
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The day Anna pieces enough together — the day after her talk with the old pilot who flew the northern line — she knows where to go, what to look for, and roughly what window they have, within a few days.

She doesn't call a meeting or announce anything. She just walks back to Gust and starts checking — fuel, envelope, high-altitude rigging, spare gloves, spare masks, rope anchors, icing tools. She works through her own sequence. She hasn't told anyone she needs a second pilot. The physical fact makes it inevitable — one person taking a ship alone to a high-altitude old station under unknown weather is unreasonable — but she hasn't gotten as far as "call whom" yet.

When she looks up to reach for a tool, she sees Kassia already doing the same thing over at Razor. Checking, going through things, moving spares out.

Anna walks over.

Anna: "North line."

Kassia: "Got it."

Then the two of them go back to check their own ships.

Kalean comes over as Anna is starting her final round of checks. He doesn't ask her whether she's ready. He does his own walk-around — along Gust's deck, his hand touching the line cleats, the envelope seams, the connectors under the rudder position from time to time. He moves like a pressure-systems-sensitive person — not taking things apart, but letting his hand feel out first what is stiff where, slack where, cold where it should not be.

He asks a few functional questions: when was the last time anyone walked the northern line — how many years ago? How reliable are the coordinates of that old CI station? Who gave them? The glacier's weather window — if the wind window closes, does Kassia's Razor have a fallback return route? At altitude, one ship, one round trip — how much can two people carry? If they don't find that cofactor, do they need to plan for one more landing on the way back?

Anna answers each in turn. She's not annoyed, and not especially grateful — what Kalean is asking is what any two pilots would go through with each other before a trip. Kalean listens, gives an "hm," and steps aside to let her continue.

He doesn't say hang in there. Doesn't say be careful. Doesn't say take care of Lia.

Lia doesn't need to go up onto the glacier to prove she's brave. High altitude is a bad environment for her grey-lung. She decides on her own to stay in Skyleaf's lower town. This time she doesn't need Anna beside her — Sara and Kalean are here.

Lia's send-off for Anna is simple. She walks to the foot of the mooring stair and stands there, waiting for Anna to look up and see her. She doesn't say be careful. She says:

"Come back and tell me what it is."

Anna: "Mm."

Lia turns and walks back. She doesn't linger on the stair to watch the ship lift — she has her own things to do.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### The glacier: two-person interlock
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Altitude inverts the structure of the two women's abilities.

Once the air thins, the fine distinctions Anna usually pulls out of sound go blurry. She can still feel the airflow changing — just at lower precision; she can't say "the layer on the left is about to shift," only "something is off."

Wind layers, on the other hand, become harder and more distinct. The simple pressure strips and altitude readings on Razor give Kassia clearer things to grip. Kassia is more at home at this altitude than at normal ones: readings don't lie, layers are clear. But Kassia's way of reading is inherently lagged — instruments report what has already happened, not what is about to happen. At normal altitude, that time-lag is covered by Anna's head-start; on the glacier, Anna's head-start shrinks until it's almost in sync with Kassia's instruments.

For the first time the two women are truly side by side on the time axis.

From lift-off to landing, the glacier is not "Anna flies, Kassia backs her up." It is a sustained, long stretch of two-person alternating load-bearing. The two keep handing position back and forth, not because of crisis, but because the environment itself needs both ways of reading in parallel to work.

Anna is forced, for the first time, to hand her blurry perception over to Kassia in words — not "I feel something" and then silence, but "something on the right is loosening but I can't tell what." Kassia takes that sentence, goes to the instruments, finds it, makes a call.

Next time it's the other way — Kassia sees in the readings that two layers of wind are pulling apart, and Anna confirms from the ship's body that the pull-apart has actually reached them.

The time after that, both directions of information arrive almost at once.

The two women's hands alternate over and over inside the same span of work. Not one flying while the other watches. Every few minutes there is a hand-off, the hand-offs coming faster and faster, until in the latter part of the trip they are moving almost simultaneously.

Inside this sustained alternation, the low-frequency crack inside the glacier travels up from underfoot.

That crack pulls Anna back.

It's:
the cold weight of the seating bar in the palm;
the tak, tak, empty-beat of the brace strut;
her swinging those six months out;
the impact returning along the steel bar to her wrist bone;
the fork ear shearing, the gantry translating sideways;
the sinker and its load sliding out;
Lia pinned toward the guardrail;
Anna's now-empty hand pulling back.

Anna's body relives it:
"At that moment I really wanted something to hurt for me instead."

Anna's hand falls out of the alternating rhythm already in motion. What she passes over starts to lag, then to blur, then for one beat she passes nothing at all.

The trigger belongs only to this specific sensory return: cold metal, the weight of the seating bar, impact traveling back along the wrist bone, and Lia's body once at the receiving end of what her hands did.

Kassia does not perform a rescue movement. Her hand has been taking Anna's handed-off weight for hours already, at roughly the same rhythm as her own breath. This time Anna doesn't hand off; the weight falls of its own accord, and Kassia's hand is still in that position. The only difference is where it came from.

When Anna comes back it isn't "reclaiming the controls." It's re-entering that alternating rhythm. But her way of entering has changed — she no longer tries to pass off her blurry perception as complete judgment; she more honestly says "I don't know." This is the first time in her working life that Anna practices handing over something incomplete.

When those hours are done, the tiredness in Kassia's arms is unlike any tiredness she has felt after any past watch. For the moment she can't say why. She hasn't thought about it yet.

The person twelve-year-old Kassia was waiting for never came back. All the promises around her had run one way — "they'll come back" — and then emptied out. Everything since — her precision, her impatience with "it'll sort itself out," her pressing at Anna's "I feel" — comes from a very deep place: a promise lives only in the speaker's mouth, and the person receiving it has nothing to do but wait.

What the glacier gives her is not "this time the promise didn't empty out" — that would still be a bystander's conclusion. What the glacier gives her is this: a promise can be a structure that requires two pairs of hands to run. She isn't waiting on an outcome. She is part of the outcome.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### The old CI station: this season, it happens to come back to life
----------------------------------------------------------------------

They went up looking for medicine. The CI station wasn't in the plan.

Two days north along the glacier's edge, Kassia stops first. Her ears work better than Anna's in an environment like this. She sits a long time without speaking. When Anna comes close, she only says:

"That isn't natural noise."

It is an old structure buried in the slope, most of it under ice. In its day it had been a high-altitude cross-station — plant phenotypes, medicinal plant cultivation, early human adaptation monitoring, weather at the glacier-geothermal boundary — put up in the CI era five hundred years ago, small in scale, not a central database and not a treasure trove. It has been sitting here for centuries. This season the glacier has drawn back a stretch, and geothermal meltwater has re-connected an old thermoelectric and hydro loop from another direction; a local sub-core has, from all this, drawn a very low but steady trickle of power.

Anna is not the chosen person. Lia is not the child in a prophecy. They are simply here, happening to walk close by, in the season it starts to emit regular pulses again.

Anna only later, slowly, picks out another layer. There is a structure of "waiting" in its rhythm — not waiting for a particular person, but the kind of rhythm a system falls into under low power, repeatedly asking itself whether anyone has come to handshake. She doesn't tell Kassia this. She just stands there and listens a while longer.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### What is brought back from the old station
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Only after the sub-core has finished outputting everything it has for Lia does a detachable piece belonging to its own maintenance routine come into view.

The size of a palm. Many-faceted. Dark, slightly translucent, unlike the glass one commonly finds in Archeon. When Kassia picks it up, she gets a small jolt — not a light, only a very weak phase flicker and a fine mechanical chime, gone in under a second. Kassia doesn't treat it as a treasure. Following what's written in the sub-core's manual, she notes its position, how it was connected, its state at the time, leaving provenance for the people who come after. Then she stows it in an ordinary protective case.

This thing is Federation / CI era work; it is essentially a hardened phase-reference / diagnostic module — five hundred years ago it was designed so it could be pulled out of the wall and carried away for on-site calibration. They aren't prying treasure loose. They are taking a service piece off, following service logic.

Later, locals will offhandedly call it a key — it lets another still-living old CI system recognize its handshake. But on its own it can't open the Kepler Core, can't tell Lia whether she'll be well, can't give Anna an answer. It's only itself.

At the time Anna doesn't feel she has come away with anything. What she is thinking about is that Lia has already been given the boundary of her illness. That the crystal-key can go on answering another question is only discovered later.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### The answer the Core gives Lia
----------------------------------------------------------------------
