What the Core pulled out of the old CI is the classification of this hereditary lineage's fibrosis pathway. Combined with the years of baseline the Skyleaf healer already has, they can, for the first time, break Lia's problem into three layers.

When the healer says it, Lia is sitting across from her. Anna stands in the doorframe without coming in. Sara sits by the window; Kalean leans against the wall. Kassia is not there — she is outside, doing her own work on Razor.

The first layer is the hereditary active fibrosis process. This can be held down over the long term. The Ember Crown series already hits the correct pathway; it's only that the local formulation was missing one cold-margin cofactor and a more precise dosing rhythm. The second layer is the currently reversible inflammation, spasms, and partial loss of function. This can improve markedly — Lia's activity window has a chance to widen, and she doesn't have to keep understanding herself under the frame of "every few years, definitely worse." The third layer is the structural scar already formed: the old fibrosis left behind by years of grey-lung, plus the history left by that P4 lung contusion and rib injury. This layer will not go away.

The healer closes with a simple line: it can be stopped. It cannot be run backwards.

The first thing Lia asks is practical. How high can I fly. How often do I need to come back. How different are the cold season and the warm season. What happens if I go off it for a while. She is asking about a life. When she has finished asking, Kalean picks up with one more: when is the next window when the cold-margin cofactor can be harvested? The healer says locally they use only a limited amount; stable supply may need contact with the medicine-works network back at Frontier. Kalean nods and doesn't push further.

The night the full answer is delivered, or over the following two or three days, the healer adds one more layer on a longer time axis:

"It can be stopped. It cannot be run backwards. Fifty years from now, no guarantee."

Sara's hand closes on her knee at hearing this, and opens again. Kalean doesn't look at her.

This is the second round of "this isn't the same thing" from after the flower field. The first round was the blurry signal the body sent; the second, with the full classification in hand, is the precise but still incomplete answer. Between the two rounds of uncertainty sit the glacier, Kassia's hands, the sub-core's old data, and a crystal-key that can be carried away.

Anna, after listening, says nothing. She will later be inclined to press the whole weight of "no guarantee at fifty years" onto herself — appropriating Lia's body as her own responsibility. This is a thing she has been doing all along her arc.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### "I'm not the me from that day"
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Late in Skyleaf, Anna, once again, judges something for Lia before Lia has opened her mouth.

Lia does not deliver a speech about autonomy.

She only says one line:

"I'm not the me from that day."

Then she goes on doing what she is doing.

Sara has just come in from outside, holding a bunch of dry petals. She pauses at the doorway. She had probably been about to walk in and set them down, and say something. After she sets the petals on the counter she doesn't speak. She turns and goes back out.

This is the one time in the whole "look at the cloth" line where Lia names the thing. It's only named here, so late, because none of the earlier correcting gestures — don't close it, look at the cloth, a look, Anna stopping that half-step in the flower field — resolved one thing completely: where Anna's eyes land on Lia, they still, now and then, land first six years ago.

The line isn't there to force Anna to confess. What it does is this: the child who was hurt that day is a real person; the person who now can accept, refuse, take a risk, and take part is also a real person. The person you're looking at isn't here. Come back.

After Lia says it, there is no reconciliation scene. On the next task, Anna's hand pauses half a second before reaching out. Lia doesn't notice, because she is already onto something else.

The next day Anna stands at the mooring stair, waiting for Lia to come collect her things. Lia comes up the slope path from below, one hand carrying her own cloth bag, the other holding a small packet of dry Ember Crown petals — not for Anna, but something she wants to take with her.

When Anna sees her, what she sees first isn't her breathing.

What she sees first is that small packet of petals in her hand, and the way her thumb is pressed against the opening to keep them from spilling.

This is the first time.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Kassia's night at Skyleaf
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Evening. Kassia has finished eating.

She sits somewhere in Skyleaf with a view of the far distance. On the rim of the bowl-shaped plain, the encircling snow mountains have already gone dark, only the highest stretch of ridge still holding a last line of light. A few lakes are below, the last light on the water narrowed to a small patch. The grassland spreads out into the distance; the basin, tens of kilometers across, becomes a quiet bowl in the dusk.

Far off, people are going home. Not one person — the daily life of thirty thousand is being drawn into the night. Someone calls a name; the sound crosses from the other side of the bowl-shaped plain, blocked once by the snow mountains, half a beat behind the original. A lamp goes on. Then another.

Kassia sits.

Twelve-year-old Kassia sat through an evening like this once. Not in this place — but physically very like it. One person, a stretch of quiet, other people's houses somewhere in the distance. She had counted the days; the one the person should have arrived had passed. The sounds outside were the same as tonight: other people going home. Only the person she was waiting for did not come back.

Between that evening and this one lie eighteen hundred kilometers, hours on a glacier of alternating hand-offs of weight with another person, a bounded answer ("it can be stopped, it cannot be run backwards, no guarantee at fifty years"), and one person who reached Skyleaf without ever using the word "definitely."

She doesn't sum any of this up.

The last line of light on the snow mountains finishes going out. Kassia sets her bowl aside and stays sitting a while longer. Someone walks by behind her, on their way to do something. She doesn't turn around.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Farewell: tell me what the wind was like, first
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Second day, dusk. On a low wall at the edge of the flower field, the evening light has just been pulled off the snow mountains.

Lia sits on the wall, feet hanging. On her lap a small handful of dry petals sorted that day; her fingertips are red — color left over from the afternoon in the fields, unwashed. In the distance, a thin mist drifts across from the warm springs and spreads out over the lake. The voices of a group returning late come back, half a beat behind, from the other side of the bowl-shaped plain. Anna leans against the same stretch of wall, one hand resting on stone that has been sunned all day.

The two of them have just finished eating. Nearby a local child is walking a smaller one back toward the houses; no one is saying goodbye.

Anna says, offhandedly, tell her when she gets back.

Lia turns the petals from one hand into the other, looking at their edges.

"Tell me what the wind was like, first."

Anna thinks she's still looking at the petals.

Lia looks up.

"Don't finish the looking for me. Leave some."

Anna gives a short laugh.
"All right."

Lia's hand is still on the petals. Anna's hand is still on the stone. Below, someone calls a name; blocked once by the snow mountains, the echo comes back half a beat behind the original.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Leaving Skyleaf
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Among the things carried away when they leave, the crystal-key is stowed in an ordinary protective case, its position and test conditions briefly noted, and then covered over by other things. Lia's treatment set and the classification of her illness are much more important than it. Anna adds a small note on Edmund's northern line, but that isn't "completing a last wish."

Lia stays in Skyleaf's lower town. Her dosing rhythm and the warm-season window need a few stable weeks here, and the healer also advises her not to move for now. Sara and Kalean stay with her — not to nurse her, but because they themselves need a few days to come down from this flight. Kalean says "come back whenever" and doesn't unfold it any further. Sara only says one line to Anna: "Send word when you're down at Frontier."

Anna and Kassia board. Lia walks to the foot of the mooring stair and stands there until the ship lifts, then turns to walk back down the slope. She doesn't stay on the stair to watch the ship fly out — she has her own things to do.

As the ship lifts, another small boat is rising off another slope, scattering the day's first handful of scrap petals behind it; a little farther off, people are eating.

Skyleaf goes on with its own life.

======================================================================
### Interlude · chasing old talk (Ep51–55)
======================================================================

After Skyleaf, the crystal-key is stowed in an ordinary protective case. It is still, at this moment, only a piece taken off an old wall; Lia's illness boundary must go on covering it.

What has really changed is the position of those old memories in Anna's head. Idle talk she overheard three years ago at a few ordinary stopovers — that a certain sealed structure over near Scar, if you struck it, "didn't sound completely empty"; that people who had gone in a little way had heard a very faint, widely spaced regular sound — she had no reason to chase it at the time. Now the Skyleaf sub-core makes it possible, for the first time, that those pieces of talk belong to the same thing.

What Ep51 to Ep55 chase is where these accounts came from: who saw them with their own eyes, who only heard them, what year, on which stretch. In these five episodes, the crew doesn't go to Axiom. They go back to a few ordinary places and check things off, person by person. At the end of Ep55 they return to Atheria to change ships in preparation for the long haul to Axiom.

On the Archeon side, things happen strictly Ep51 → Ep52 → Ep53 → Ep54 → Ep55; the Federation line can cross-cut in edit, but doesn't disturb the local order here.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep51 · What was said three years ago
----------------------------------------------------------------------

What really triggers Anna isn't a single moment. It's a few days after leaving Skyleaf, under the deck of some ordinary stopover, when for the second time she thinks about the sub-core at Skyleaf and its rhythm of "coming back once every long while."

She remembers three years ago near Coppergate, an old woman saying to her in the afternoon light: over there (she gestured vaguely with a hand to the north), a certain sealed structure — when you strike it, doesn't sound completely empty. Anna didn't pick anything up then — at the time she didn't know what "doesn't sound completely empty" could possibly mean, and had no reason to chase it.

Then she remembers a few other lines. An old pilot who had crossed Blue Crossing said offhandedly at a ship's rail, "when I was young I knew someone who went in a little way," and then changed the subject. Someone else — Anna doesn't even remember who — described the behavior: "not alive, only recognizing an old rhythm."

At the time these lines had nothing to do with anything. Tonight, for the first time, she realizes they might all describe the same class of thing.

The way she tells Kassia is small. At supper:
"Three years ago, near Coppergate — you probably don't remember — there was an old woman who said something to me."

Kassia: "Is she still there?"

Anna: "Don't know."

Kassia: "Then let's go look first."

They decide to go find the people who said things back then. Not immediately to Axiom.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep52 · Back to the old place: the person isn't there
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The first stop is a living town near Coppergate that Anna went to over and over during P5. She remembers which bridge warms in the afternoon, how to walk from the mooring to that eatery without looking at the sign, which row that old woman lived on around Year 518.

Coming back in Year 521, the person has moved away. Just moved away. She went a year or two ago with her daughter to somewhere else — maybe onto the coastal line, maybe only back in another season. The window has been changed; a tree Anna doesn't recognize has been added in the yard; the place tools used to sit is now hung with cloth or piled with another household's daily things.

The new tenant knows the old woman, but the version she knows is more recent than Anna's memory — the granddaughter's name, one thing she did this year. Anna asks where she is now.

"Past year before last, she's mostly gone south."

Just that one line.

On the way back to the deck, Anna looks a while at the new tree. Three years passed in someone else's yard, without waiting for her.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep53 · Waiting for someone who won't arrive on time: Milo comes back
----------------------------------------------------------------------

At Saltmarch port, a familiar-line boat coming from the direction of Coppergate is two days late.

The expected date on the chalkboard has gone stale. Day after day, the sun has passed over it; the chalk's edge has been rubbed white by hand and wind, but no one has erased it.

In the shadow of the terrace, a person has been sitting there from morning into afternoon. He doesn't keep his eyes on the chalkboard or on the sky the whole time; when someone hands him something, he takes it, when someone speaks to him, he answers. It's only that every time a low ship-sound comes from far off, he looks up first.

When the boat does appear, people at the port begin to walk toward the mooring.

Kassia does not look at the boat first.
She looks at the person waiting.

Before the boat has settled fully, that person is already standing. Only when a familiar person actually appears at the rail does he go running.

Kassia looks for two seconds and lowers her head back to what she was doing.

Anna catches only this one glance. No one asks.

Anna has always known that Kassia's sensitivity to dates is precise. Today, for the first time, she knows that dates were once, also, the shape of "some person will probably come back to this place."

The boat has brought Milo down. He has finished another leg, several days behind schedule. He steps off, sets his pack down, first looks at the chalkboard to see who last saw whom, then asks when the current line of news went up. He hasn't brought an Axiom answer.


----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep54 · Blue Crossing · Two kinds of record become one
----------------------------------------------------------------------

After Milo comes back, the crew moves slowly along a long haul that Anna has flown only once and Kassia has flown many times, going to find where the old woman from Ep51 has now settled in the south. This stretch of road doesn't push any clue for a day or two.

They eat a whole day of local food. They see the terrain that had before only been in the far view actually take up daily life — a river bed with several exposed old CI pump foundations, which children use as diving platforms in summer; a wild patch of Ember Crown on a slope somewhere, growing more tangled than in Skyleaf's fields. At night, under a different latitude, they see Sky Fire's Remnant one grid off from where it sits over Frontier.

Milo starts logging twice a day. Once at dawn for weather conditions, once at dusk for the route deviation of the day, closing with a note: "I saw / someone told me / cannot confirm." He isn't proving anything; he's just letting every passage leave something the next passage can check against.

Kassia takes a look at Milo's booklet once. The next morning she writes a line in it herself — not to write for Milo, but because she has a thought of her own to keep. Milo sees, and says nothing.

On one night leg, the two of them record the same stretch of wind separately. Next morning they lay the two accounts side by side. Milo has recorded order: which layer changed first, how many minutes to the next. Kassia has recorded amplitude: how far each layer deviated from its norm. The two don't overlap and don't conflict.

Milo: "Yours can sit next to mine."

Kassia: "Next time just leave that column empty for me."

The two are only leaving this thing behind in a shape closer to how it actually happened.

At the end of this stretch, an old pilot come down from the coast says offhandedly:
"The one you're looking for switched to the coast this season."

They know now which patch of the south to go toward next. Still no specific location — just a rough direction.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep55 · Back to Atheria · Change ships, decide to go to Axiom
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Tracking the old woman takes another two days. She now lives at her daughter's, on the southern line, and in the afternoons she sits by the courtyard wall in the sun. Anna knows her — she knows Anna too, though she has to think a moment before matching the name.

Anna repeats to her what she said three years ago at Coppergate.

The old woman: "I didn't make that up. I heard it from my father when I was a child."

Anna asks where her father heard it from.

"My father went in a little way into Scar with a friend. They didn't go far — too tired, and they were sick for half a month on the way back. He said one stretch inside — not all of it — when he struck it, it didn't sound completely empty. He didn't hear an echo. It was the feel of the striking that was different."

The old woman looks at a low tree in her own yard. "My father wasn't a maker of stories. He wasn't someone who could hold a lot of details either. He only remembered that one stretch."

Anna writes it down. They sit a while longer. The old woman gives her father's name, roughly which year he went in, and with whom. Kassia writes all of it into Milo's booklet — the booklet is now shared between the two of them.

On the way back to the deck, the three of them lay the three years of asking side by side for the first time.

The memories of seven people, spanning about thirty years, all point to some stretch of sealed structure in Scar's inner layer that might not be completely empty. Two or three old ledgers mention one or several entries into Scar's outer edge, and the years line up. The behavior the Skyleaf sub-core showed — "still recognizing an old rhythm" — tells them what to compare.

There is no address. There is no map. There is no key. They only, at last, have reason to think that the crystal from Skyleaf may let something still alive inside Scar recognize it.

Kassia says: "Then it's a trip back to Atheria."

Anna: "Change ships?"

Kassia: "Gust is fine to the edge of Whisperflat. Any further in, we have to add a day of margin."

The trip back to Atheria takes six days. When they arrive home, it's the end of harvest season.

The ship-change work is mostly Kassia's. She flips through the long-haul redundancy tables, brings the fuel window up one layer, adds a third optional landing point to the return route instead of two. She doesn't explain to Anna why she added that third one. She herself knows Anna will, at some position, ask for a half-hour off course to look at something unrelated to the main line.

The new ship is a Class III long-haul type that runs the Atheria–Frontier line year round — bigger than Gust, about twice the envelope volume, an extra deck — coming in this season for its major refit; they borrow the window for two trips. Milo spends a day getting a feel for the new ship's chalkboard and line positions.

The evening before departure, Anna goes on her own to the place where Edmund is buried and stands there a while. She doesn't bring anything, and doesn't tell anyone. She only stops by, because the next leg is the farthest she will have ever gone on her own. She stays no more than a few minutes and comes back.

When the ship lifts the next day, the water sound under the three-step drop terraces of Atheria is quieter than usual — the end-of-harvest-season water volume. From the upper deck Milo reaches to pull a new tie-down. Kassia is already reading the chalkboard.

Anna puts her hand on the copper rail. The copper is cooler than Gust's.

======================================================================
### Arc 4 · Saltmarch → Whisperflat → Axiom → homeward north → twentieth birthday → "listen" (Ep56–69)
======================================================================

### Narrative function

The finale of Act 1 does seven things:

1. Lets Anna / Kassia / Milo form a crew made of mature but different ways of reading;
2. Writes Frontier–Coppergate–Saltmarch–Whisperflat–Axiom as a long line that people really travel and that also loses continuity;
3. Axiom gives Edmund's historical facts, without answering for Anna "how much did he actually know at the time"; the return trip lets an ordinary living person's imprecise memory end her drive to prove;
4. Skyleaf's send-off has already left a private "after you come back" agreement; after the return north, first make good on that gust of wind, then let Anna's twentieth birthday be the private emotional high point of Act 1;
5. Ep69 has Anna, Lia, Milo, and Kassia jointly complete the first two-way response; Anna's change is not that she is better at forcing an answer, but that she is finally able to wait for the other end to change on its own;
6. The Continuity Archive gives only fragments: hands leaving marks → holding steady, passing over, waiting until it's caught before letting go → building things that no longer need a hand → the hand still there in the farewell → Atwood without sound;
7. LOCAL CONTINUATION stays blank, but Act 1 doesn't close on a status page. After everyone has stopped, the Core leaves only one learned response whose meaning cannot be authenticated; then the camera returns to an Archeon that is still going on.

Frontier
→ Coppergate
→ Saltmarch
→ edge of the Whisperflat region
→ Axiom Scar


----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Saltmarch
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Saltmarch has to first prove that human beings have been living on the edge of the Great Ochre arid zone for many generations.

The town spreads out along a group of old wellheads, several condensation fields, and a stretch of harder old lake-shore terrace, with houses low, thick, and set to shade each other: pale salt-grey / geopolymer walls throw heat back off, dark-wood doors and windows recessed into thick walls, roofs nearly flat and used for drying mineral salts, tubers, cloth, and clay blanks. The streets are not densely continuous; between houses are frequently salt pans, condensation frames, small dry-farmed plots, and open spaces where the wind can pass. The truly prominent public buildings aren't workshops but a few wellyards and well-sheds, each block's own shaded communal kitchen, a few salt-chemistry boiling pans, ceramic kilns, and several rows of long guest quarters for travelers passing through; thirty-five thousand people spread along the terraces in several blocks separated from each other by salt pans, not one continuous street.

What the locals do also has to be specific: some watch the wells and the condensation; some dry salt, burn alkali, and make preserving salt; some make aridic-country pottery jars and pale-colored roofing material; some tend thin grass belts and dryland tubers; the wind blows clothes and drying cloth stiff. Water isn't end-of-the-world scarcity — the wells give enough for the town to live — but every street knows which wellyard it belongs to, which jar on a given day comes from which well, and bath water and wash water have their own order, so "measuring" is a long-term climatic habit, not survival panic.

The spaceport and the Axiom line are only one layer beyond the town. On the chalkboard, alongside the same distant destination, different people's handwritten dates can still appear, and the "most recent" one is already long ago; but beside it are simultaneously familiar lines to Coppergate, the Riverbend canal district, the dryland villages, and another salt pan. That way the Axiom route reads as sparse, and Saltmarch itself is not at all sparse.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Whisperflat: three ways of reading
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Anna looks up first.
A thin line slack at the ship's rail has moved only a little, and she already knows the near-ground wind layer is shifting.
A few seconds later, the mineral shell lights up from the distance: a small stretch of cold green sweeps across, breaks off; farther on another line picks it up, the old line not yet dark before the new one has cut in from the other side.

This particular gust is unusually light. When it touches the ship, it's barely enough to shift the line at the rail; the ground draws it out for a long distance.
Anna watches for a while, doesn't name it.

Kassia turns to look at the ship's wind band only on the second flare; from the third on, she starts recording the order.
Milo crouches beside her drawing, and asks whether the last one also came from the same place.

Kassia suddenly asks Anna:
"You heard it before it lit?"

Anna: "Mm."

"Where?"

Anna points her to the line that moved first. Kassia watches a while longer, doesn't press further.

Three years ago she would have chased it to the end — how do you know, when did you start knowing, how can other people also know. This time she looks at the line first, then the hull, then puts her eyes back on that stretch of green on the ground that hasn't yet gone dark.

Anna, here, is not thinking about Lia, not previewing the birthday, not thinking "I need to remember this line." When Lia asks about the wind after they get north, Anna will hand her "only enough to shift one line, and the ground shone for a long way."


Milo writes on the paper:
"Light west wind overnight, we came through the southern edge."

He pauses, then adds:
"Only recording this once."

Kassia glances over:
"Add the low layer in the second half of the night. It wasn't the same direction later."

Milo adds it in.

It isn't her correcting a child.
The two of them are just leaving this night behind in a shape closer to how it actually happened.



----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Axiom Scar
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Scar doesn't begin with "arriving." It begins with "not arriving for a long time."

It appears on the horizon most of a day before arrival: a stretch of low color-difference too straight, mistaken at first for a far mountain's shadow, or a cloud that has settled and won't move. They fly toward it for hours, and it doesn't come much closer, only slowly widens.

The change comes from the air first: drier wind, carrying a mineral smell of things ground by sand; in the afternoon, near-ground heat currents make the far line waver faintly. Truly seeing it as a piece of terrain cut downward has to wait for the light to lower — at dusk, side light pulls the edge shadows out for the first time, and only then do the people on the ship understand this isn't a mountain but plowed ground. Plow scars on the hundred-kilometer scale. Not an ancient ruin field, but a ship writing one failure into the surface of the earth.

The last stretch, in fact, has to be slow: outer-edge fragmented structures appear sparsely on the ground, widely spaced, each smaller than expected. The ship flies along Scar's true edge a long time before finding a stretch worth landing at. People nearby know Scar, and some occasionally enter the outer edge, but it isn't a warehouse Archeon civilization relies on stripping. Five hundred years on, sand, wind, and several drought-tolerant plants have gone on growing around the wound.

Once they're down, Milo's first response isn't "what can we take."

What he asks is:
"Why did this line stop here?"

Kassia answers offhandedly:
"Because the ship stopped."

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Inside Axiom: sound is taken away
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The most important sense here isn't the darkness.

It is that there is no local daily life here. No distant voices, no clap of wind on drying cloth, no one down on the level below calling another person's name, no kitchen heat leaking through a door crack, no line of writing added today.

Only their own breathing, the sound of shoes touching structure, and a metallic echo that returns very late from the depths.

Anna is facing, for the first time, a space with no living social baseline noise. She walks slowly, keeping herself from adding anything extra.

As Kassia passes Anna's side, she raps very lightly with a knuckle on a piece of structure beside her. The sound falls near her feet first; then, from deeper in the corridor, a layer of it slowly returns, later than she expected. She doesn't rap a second time. She stands, and lets that late echo finish.

Inside Axiom there are still a small number of unusually precise things one can read: a departure timestamp, a stretch of final service record, a certain last confirmed date. The numbers themselves aren't wrong — more precise, in fact, than any handwritten record on Archeon.

But after that line, there is nothing.

It isn't a failure of the record. It is that no one continued to live here; precision by itself cannot stand in for a next day happening.

Anyone standing before that time that stopped five hundred years ago will pause a moment.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Axiom gives facts, not emotional answers
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Axiom is a broken old database. What it emits is fragmented, biased, low-bandwidth. Among the frames of the Core's response sequence, mixed in with other things, there may be a stretch of ambient radiation distribution — the residual radiation levels at the Axiom crash zone and the surrounding old CI infrastructure — or a scrap of a crew exposure protocol, with Federation-era exposure thresholds and symptom latency periods for a certain class of contact.

These things are not for Anna. They are technical files left five hundred years ago for the colony's founders. What Anna sees may be only a few numbers and a unit.

Her eyes stop on them.

Edmund worked, for years, on the old CI infrastructure along the line. Hand on a pipe wall, listening to temperature and vibration. Crouching in old shafts to check seals. Some of those places were hot, some just old, but some — if these few frames of data are right — held, at depths invisible from the surface, what was left over from a crash, or from CI decommissioning. She doesn't need to work it out precisely. She only needs to match that latency window against the time Edmund started walking one leg less.

Then her hand moves. Not toward the Core. Her hand goes to her own cuff — to touch the spot she just patched last month. Under the cloth she knows where the stitching is with her eyes closed, a single stitch off-center that she never picked out.

Only that.

She doesn't speak. Doesn't cry. Doesn't take the jacket off to look at it. Her hand touches the stitching once, and comes back, and goes on with what she is doing. Milo may have seen. Kassia is looking at her own readings.

The next round of Core response comes. Anna takes her hand away from the cuff. Goes on listening.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Kepler Core: broken, but not all broken
----------------------------------------------------------------------

To get to the deck Kepler Core is on, you walk a stretch of old service passage — wide enough for two people abreast, its walls carrying maintenance markings left five hundred years ago, the writing barely faded in the cold.

The core itself doesn't actively give off light, doesn't hum — only on a small old display panel nearby there's a very faint intermittent brightness, as if something is still maintaining an extremely low heartbeat.

It's very badly damaged. The vast majority of the indices, the language layer, the deep data can't be read out by Archeon's current means; what can be extracted must be fragmented, biased, low-bandwidth. It isn't a Federation encyclopedia; it won't automatically reconstruct high technology, and it won't define Archeon as a failed copy of the Federation.

But it has preserved one small screened-through piece of content: what human beings once did in leaving marks, migrating, building, gathering, and separating again. Plus a small piece of Axiom-the-ship's own record: departure date, crew list, mission cache. Plus a colony-continuation area intentionally left blank by design.

That blank is not damage. Five hundred years ago it was left blank on purpose — the intent being that the colony fill it in itself; once the mother civilization had delivered them here, it would no longer write for them. Sky Fire and the Axiom crash meant this handover never properly happened.

When Kassia brings the key close to the core, the old display panel beside it briefly lights up. A few frames. Extremely fragmentary. Color-shifted. Then out.

This isn't a response — only something an old cache spits out when approached. They note the timestamp and don't make anything of it.

The reason to take it isn't "it's valuable"; it's "it may still be able to answer" — not answer historical facts themselves, but whether it will change its next response for whatever Archeon sends it next. Whether that can hold has to wait until they're back at Atheria to see.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Homeward north: Anna and Kassia in sync
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The return trip doesn't need a new crisis.

Back at normal altitude, Anna's perception is restored. The fine distinctions in sound come back, changes in airflow are earlier than the instruments again. She no longer needs Kassia's readings to fill in her blurriness.

The two-person interlock on the glacier was forced by the environment — two ways of reading, at that altitude, had equal structural necessity. Back at normal altitude, that equality is gone. Anna once again becomes the one who knows first. Kassia is still the one who touches the controls earlier. The gap in ability between the two is real, and normal altitude doesn't erase it.

But what the glacier leaves behind is not "we are now, forever, an equal partnership." It is another thing.

When Anna moves, she now hands over an extra sentence. Not a complete judgment — sometimes only a direction or a position. On the glacier she practiced, for the first time, "handing over an incomplete perception for another person to finish," and that habit has stayed with her at normal altitude. She no longer just gives someone else "I feel" and falls silent; she points out the specific place she heard something first.

Kassia, receiving that sentence, knows where it comes from. Before the glacier, she would have automatically taken Anna's "wait, don't move" as unproven flourish. Not anymore. Not because she was talked around — because on the glacier she verified those blurry judgments with her own hands, and she knows they, however unclearly stated, do point to real changes.

A side wind comes in; Anna's hand has just eased down on one side, and Kassia has already taken back a countering amount on the other; neither of them looks up.

Another time it's Kassia dropping power first. Anna doesn't even ask, and just puts the ship into the next layer of updraft.

The time after that, both of them do nothing, and let the ship carry itself for a few seconds.

Sync isn't the two of them becoming the same kind of pilot. It isn't a low-intensity continuation of the glacier's interlock either. It is a new trust: each knows roughly where the other's next hand will land, so a few of the extra confirmations drop away.

What the glacier gives isn't permanent equality. It's that both of them now know under what conditions the other's way is useful, and under what conditions it isn't enough. Harder than equality, and steadier.



----------------------------------------------------------------------
### An old place on the way back: a living person still remembers Edmund
----------------------------------------------------------------------

After Axiom, on the way back, they stop for a night — for ordinary reasons — at a place Edmund used to come to in life and that Anna, as a child, never came to: waiting for wind, eating, or maybe this familiar line simply stops here anyway.

This place carries no "red jacket third-tier arrival."

A living person recognizes the surname Freedman, or looks at Anna a while before it comes back.

What that person remembers about Edmund also doesn't matter:

that he always swapped his cup with someone else's;
that once, waiting for wind, he fell asleep, and when he woke his first question was whether there was any food left in the pot;
that he didn't like how the afternoon light here hit his eyes just so, and always moved to the other side of the table to eat;
then once, offhandedly, he asked:

"That kid of mine in the red jacket — is she able to run all the way over here on her own by now?"

The person can't say what year it was.
May have the season wrong.

Anna could have asked:
"When did he say this?"

She doesn't.

Not because she has suddenly cracked her father's secret, but because at this moment there is nothing that needs to be proven.

What is preserved here is only a living person's imprecise but real memory of another living person. It has no obligation to prove whether Edmund knew his own time was ending, and it doesn't need to piece the red jacket, the stranger at the funeral, and the late-arriving question back into a blueprint.

Anna pushes the cup back, keeps eating.
The ship goes on afterwards.

An ordinary, even slightly misremembered, memory carries more weight than a complete explanation.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Homeward north · landing at Atheria
----------------------------------------------------------------------

As the new ship rounds in from the upper-plateau side, the low bass of the waterfall reaches the deck first. The wind is colder than when they left; the sound of water beneath Atheria's three-step drop terraces has never stopped.

Anna's port-landing movements are familiar: slow, drop low, look up at the stair to see whether someone is taking in cloth. She had expected Lia would still be at Skyleaf — the healer had advised her dosing rhythm to stabilize there a few weeks, and Sara and Kalean said they'd stay.

Once the ship has settled, the crew begins to unload. On Kassia's side she passes down the water bags and dry rations left from the long haul; Milo puts away the paper clip and tie-downs. Anna herself takes from the cabin the ordinary protective case holding the crystal-key and the fragments brought back from Axiom. It isn't big or heavy; she carries it out through the hatch with both hands.

There is someone on the stair.

Not Sara. Lia, in a pale outer garment Anna doesn't recognize, standing at the corner where the pier stair turns toward the houses, hands inside her sleeves. She doesn't step forward. She's waiting for Anna to set the case down safely.

Anna walks down the last two steps and sets the case on a flat patch at the edge of the stair. When she puts it down she doesn't look at it again.

Then she looks at Lia.

Her breathing looks a little steadier than when she left.

Lia: "You're back."

Anna: "You're back too."

Lia: "Last week."

Kassia takes the case over from behind. Anna doesn't turn around. She trusts the case in Kassia's hands.

The two of them walk together toward the houses.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Lia after the north return: bring the wind back first
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Lia hasn't put her life on pause to give Anna a few days to come around. She eats as usual, mixes colors, sees friends, arranges her energy along the new treatment rhythm. Anna doesn't push her on why she came back from Skyleaf early either — if asked, Lia would probably only say "it was time to come back."

After an ordinary daily interval — not the same day, and not the next — Lia suddenly asks:

"What about the wind?"

Anna doesn't catch it at first.
"What?"

"You said you'd tell me about it when you got back."

Anna thinks a moment:
"Very light. Only enough to shift a single line at the rail — and the ground was lit up far out."

After hearing this, Lia doesn't ask about Axiom, doesn't ask Anna to finish telling Whisperflat. She only lets Anna finish this one thing.

When Lia is doing something and hands one end to Anna, she only says "you take this." Anna takes it; the end Lia hasn't opened up, she doesn't touch.

A few days later. The two of them are making colors.

Lia is mixing a bowl of mineral powder; Anna is beside her, grinding another kind. The passage from coarse grit to powder in the mortar is slow, and Anna's wrist has already begun to ache.

After a while grinding, Anna, without looking up:

"When I was over there I saw some pictures."

Lia doesn't stop. Doesn't ask "over where."

Anna keeps grinding. The words and the work go out together, unhurried, unarranged. All of them are impressions left behind by a few seconds of poor image — she isn't sure herself how clearly she saw them.

"People crouched around a fire. Several of them, in a circle. Palms open, facing it — not doing anything, just there."

Pause a moment.

"And a group of people walking. Across an open stretch. Some carrying things, some empty-handed. There was a gap in the middle, but no one falling behind. One person in front looked back once."

Lia goes on mixing what's in the bowl. This one doesn't get a reaction from her. Every day, someone is walking.

"Someone pulling something very long — like a rope, like a line. One hand kept on it, the other waiting."

Lia's hand stops. She's just at that moment pulling a dye line straight in front of her — halfway through pulling it, hearing what Anna said, she looks down at her own fingers, then at Anna. She says nothing. Finishes pulling the line.

"And several people together handling something very heavy. One person moved, and another one's foot moved with it. No one talked. They just knew."

Lia doesn't react to this one either. On the pier stair, every day someone works together like that.

The last one, Anna says more slowly.

"There's one that's different. A very big place. Something moving — very large — but no one touching it. People stand beside it. Their hands are empty."

Lia has stopped grinding.

"So what are they doing?"

Anna sets the pestle down.

"Don't know."

The two of them are quiet a while. Lia starts again first. Anna follows.

The fragments stop there.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Anna's twentieth birthday
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Placed in Year 521, late, after the northern return. Anna is nineteen at the main-era start point; here she turns twenty, no need to push the calendar to Year 522.

The birthday begins with a whole afternoon with no task.

Someone pulls Anna outright away:
"You're having a birthday. Come on."

The afternoon finds a stretch of grass on Atheria's upper plateau. In the distance, a line of windmills turns slowly; below, the waterfall's low bass never stops; ships go in and out as usual.


They toss a common local thick-rimmed disk. Anna's throws aren't pretty, but they're steady. One disk is caught crosswise by a side wind; she chases after it and slides in the grass at last. She laughs first, and only then do the people around her laugh.

Hot bread, cheese, seasonal things, fermented drink; no menu. Anna isn't at the head of the table; she is just naturally pressed to the middle by the group. Some are still arguing over who's to blame for the missed disk; at the other end the bread has already been broken apart.


From two or three people it starts: string, clapping, offhand beats slowly draw more people in. Don't turn it into an instrument showcase.


A very small child is determined to ride Anna's shoulders. When the shadow of the windmill blade sweeps over the ground, the child reaches for it; can't reach, and keeps laughing.

**Lia's cake**
Late in the afternoon, Lia at last brings out a small round cake: one side high, one side collapsed, the jam pattern also crooked.

Anna: "What is this?"

Lia glances at it:
"I meant to draw your ship."
Glances again.
"Doesn't really look like it now."

People nearby laugh. Anna laughs too.

Lia hands her the cake / the first piece.
Anna takes it.

Her hand doesn't shake.
And doesn't pull back first.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Birthday night: palm up
----------------------------------------------------------------------

After dark you can hold a very loose stretch of fireside / night talk. Someone repeats a story already told, someone is half asleep, Sky Fire's Remnant is up above.

Anna looks up. When she flies, she looks at the sky as judgment — this time she isn't. She only looks at it a while.

Lia touches the back of Anna's hand first.
Anna turns her hand over, palm up.
Lia places her own hand on it.
Anna doesn't close her hand; she only holds it up.

Whether Lia stays or not is her decision. Anna's hand is only there.

The birthday ends here. An ordinary day sits between the private high point and Ep69.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
### Ep69: the finale
----------------------------------------------------------------------

The core Anna brought back from Axiom, plus the crystal from Skyleaf, plus something Lia and a few local makers put together that they themselves call a "seat" or "frame" — the three connected together for the first time in a small room in Atheria.

No English voice.
No holographic encyclopedia.
No "WELCOME, ANNA."

Only one very faint response.

The seat gives the crystal a local rhythm.
The crystal turns it into something the old system can recognize.
The core returns something — the frequency offset by half a beat.
Then stops.

Everyone can, at first, take it as automatic echo.

------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Anna
------------------------------------------------------------------
**Does nothing. Waits.**

Her breathing slows.
Her heartbeat slows.
Her fingers loosen — not "mysterious opening," only more contact area, so low-frequency vibration travels more easily through the metacarpals / forearm to be sensed by the body.

On the left is a very faint low frequency.
On the right, another one.

Her body isn't a conducting line between the two ends.
The two sides truly connecting is done by the seat-frame and the connections the old system left behind.
Anna is only, for the first time, able to hear the two ends simultaneously as two independent sounds, and sense where they meet and where they are offset.

**She is not the person who links two civilizations; she is the first person to hear a linking happening.**

Whenever she used to grow tense, she would narrow her hand, add force, correct.
This time she doesn't.

One side emits a very low hum.
The other side answers on a different frequency.
Not the same frequency.
Harmonic compatible.

The two tones stack.

For the first time in five hundred years, it changes its next response for the sake of a rhythm coming from outside, one not belonging to the old standard.

It isn't the first time it's had power, and isn't the first time it's made mechanical noise. Some component inside it, silent for a long time, is truly activated by this change, and only then gives off a low ringing that a person can hear.

The first sound in five hundred years — not the first time there was electricity, but the first time there was a conversation.

------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Four people each complete a proof
------------------------------------------------------------------

**Anna**
Hears first that the interval of the second pulse isn't a copy of the first.
She only states the fact, doesn't say "it's alive":
"Wait. The next one's different."

She does only one thing: hears "not noise."

**Kassia**

After Anna's "wait, the next one's different" lands, Kassia doesn't first ask if she heard wrong.

She doesn't move to act immediately either. She used to move the moment a reading was in her hand — this time she lets herself wait a beat, doesn't cover the sound they just had.

Then she lays the timings of the last two rounds side by side. She has Lia's side change only one very small thing, and waits again.

The core's next response is offset a little again. The direction of the offset follows the change they just made.

Kassia sets down two very small marks on paper.

"It changed with it."

Or she says only:
"It's not playback."

**Milo**
Records the order of each emit, return, and stop.
He is first to see this isn't a single tone but a string; and every round carries its origin.

He does this one thing: turns "this time we heard it" into something the next time can verify.

**Lia**
Lia is not the technical key.
She makes precise small parts, and is used to distinguishing "looks the same" from "this time it really is different." After two rounds of response, she only points out a very small difference:

"This one isn't the same meaning as the last one."

Or she may only point out that the pause / echo tail came a little earlier, without naming the judgment.

------------------------------------------------------------------
#### What "linking two civilizations" means
------------------------------------------------------------------

Act 1 does only one thing: both sides heard each other. The renewed contact between living Federation people is left to what comes after.

Archeon emits a rhythm not belonging to the old standard.
The core does not refuse, and does not only send an old recording back.
It changes its next response.

Then this side changes again.
The core changes again.

One turn, and back.

Two civilizations that branched five hundred years ago,
for the first time it is not one reading the other's leftover,
but the two adjusting to each other.

This is "listening."

Not decoding a treasure.
Not restoring an old civilization.
Not Anna becoming the chosen one.

**Both sides can still hear each other.**

Once both sides confirm they can hear each other, a piece of old metal panel on the wall begins to have light on it.

Not brightness — a wavering. The color is off, frames drop, the picture is like looking into another world through water. No sound.

Pictures come out.


The first is very short.

Rock face. Dark. Shapes attached to it — Anna at first takes it for mineral veins. Then she sees the fingers. Five of them, spread, pressed to stone. All around has been colored over with something, and only the hand's outline is left.

Anna doesn't know the year. Doesn't know why that person wanted to press their hand there.

She only looks at the angle those five fingers are spread at — very close to the position of her own palm against the core's surface at this moment.


More fragments. Unordered. All soundless.

Fire. Not a stove — fire directly on the ground. Several people around it. Someone crouches down, palms open, facing the heat. Not doing anything else.

Anna briefly saw one frame of this picture inside Axiom. Now it is complete: not one person but a ring of people. They stopped here. The fire is the point at which they stopped.

She knows that posture. Cold-season bread ovens have back walls that always have someone leaning against them, sitting, even people they don't know. Hand held out in front, waiting for the back to warm first.


Picture changes.

Open ground. A group of people walking.

Some carrying things, some empty-handed. The gaps in the line are uneven — those in front faster, those behind slower, a gap opened in the middle. But no one falls off. The person in front turns to look back at the ones behind.

Anna has seen the same shape from an airship. A very thin line moving slowly across the ground. It reaches a place and spreads, then after a while gathers again into a line and goes on. She doesn't know where the people in the picture are going. But that shape — "walk together, spread out, walk together again" — she knows; her whole world grew out of it.


Someone is pulling something long — a rope or a line or something else. One hand stays on it, the fingertips don't leave. The other hand is waiting. What it is waiting for Anna can say: waiting for it to arrive. "Arrive" isn't a scale mark — it's the moment the fingertip feels it.


Several people together handling a big thing.

One of them shifts a hand's position. In the picture, another person's foot is already moving — not because anyone called out, but because the body knows which way the weight is going to go. One person braces first, another is at that side before the weight has truly gone over. The first pair of hands, having confirmed the thing has somewhere to land, then lets go.

Anna has seen the same thing at a pier stair. The instant before a cable snaps taut, everyone drops their center of gravity together. No one taught them. Gravity hasn't changed.

These people are trying to bring this thing to another place. One person can't carry it. So there is that understanding, without a word spoken, of where the next hand will land.


Then the picture changes.

Vast space. Light even in a way that isn't fire, isn't a window. A structure is moving — very large, slowly and precisely closing together.

No one is doing it with their hands.

People stand behind something transparent. Watching. Hands empty. The connection completes itself.

In Anna's world, for a thing to be made there must be hands. She doesn't know that there was ever a stage where human beings no longer needed to do this. She has no word for "automation." Archeon has never needed one.

She can only think: why don't they go help?

Someone nearby says, very softly:

"Maybe they don't need to."

That is as far as it goes.


When the picture changes again, human hands come back.

But not to make things.

Waving. Embraces. A pair of hands pressed to an isolation panel — on the other side, another pair. Someone lifts a child up. Someone is wiping a face. Someone puts their palm on another person's shoulder one last time.

A corridor. Lights on. No sand, no rust, no time.

Anna recognizes how the structure is joined — her fingers have just, inside Axiom, felt the same seam in the same metal panels. The same ship. She has just walked through its silence five hundred years later; now she is seeing the sound of its setting out still there.

Someone passes through the corridor. A living back.

Then a woman walks in from the edge of the picture. Someone off-frame calls her; she turns. Smiles a little. Lifts a hand in a very ordinary gesture — "all right."

Her mouth moves.

Says something.

No sound.


All the pictures before have been silent — the fire, the walking, the pulling of the line, the carrying — and Anna has just been watching.

Until this woman's mouth moves.

Only then does Anna notice she wants to hear.

She even instinctively presses her hand a little more firmly. The way she was just now, waiting for the core to give the next response.

Nothing.

Milo checks the record. Kassia looks over the numbers of a moment ago. It wasn't a bad adjustment. The sound of this footage was never there — lost, or never stored in the first place.

Anna's hands can make the core and the crystal talk. She can break the silence technology has left.

But a sentence a person alive five hundred years ago has already finished saying, she cannot make her say it again.

Some things had to be heard while they were still alive.
Some silences are not waiting for the correct method. They have already passed.


The picture only runs a very short stretch further. The crowd grows smaller. A huge glowing thing separates from the structure — extremely slowly. Then a flash. Dark.

The core's low ring is still there.

Then it does one thing.

It gives its last bandwidth to an empty stretch.

Not damaged emptiness — the fragments before all carried the look of damage: color-shifted, frames dropped, picture shaking. This stretch is different. It is clean. Quiet. As if preserved intact — blank.

It's the space left for the colony to keep filling in themselves. The formal handover never happened. Five hundred years, and no one has written in it.

But the core has sent it out.

Not sending an error message, not sending a piece of broken data. It is delivering "this is left for you," itself, intact, to the other side.

Blank is blank.


------------------------------------------------------------------
### Atwood / Anna: the closing loop across distance
------------------------------------------------------------------

Atwood's private wish in Ep1 was:
she wanted to be present when those children became themselves.

She did not get that ending.
She died too early, several centuries too early.

The truly beautiful closing loop of the later arc is the inverse:

Atwood spent a lifetime recording a civilization in the process of growing into itself;
later she was herself pressed by that civilization into a name, a few kinds of handwriting, a lot of misattribution;
five hundred years later, an Archeon person who has already fully become herself at last sees her, inside a broken piece of footage.

**Atwood did not live to see the answer. The answer lived long enough to turn and see her.**

This makes "witness" and "listener" the two ends across five hundred years:
- Atwood's ethic is: if you cannot change it, at least see it and leave it behind;
- Anna's ethic finally becomes: stop only extracting answers from the object; leave the other side room to answer.

Atwood leaves records.
Anna learns to receive.
The five hundred years between are filled by countless people with no names.

It is two ways of attention finally meeting — neither a bloodline loop nor a chosen-one loop.

------------------------------------------------------------------
### Act 1 close
----------------------------------------------------------------------

Atwood's footage ends; the status layer also fades.
Everything worth trying has been tried: the seat-frame no longer sends new rhythms, the key goes quiet, the core returns to a nearly soundless low vibration.

Anna lifts her two hands away.
There is simply no next step that needs her.

Leave a stretch of true silence.

Then, after no one gives it anything more, the Core, on its own, returns something once more.

Only once.
Not a same-as-old repetition left over by the old Federation, and not any of the automatic echoes from the just-past rounds; what it leaves is a rhythm that only grew out of the one back-and-forth, a rhythm belonging only to Archeon's side.
No second one follows.


In engineering it can be explained as residual state, a delayed response; in emotion it can be read as recognition. The work does not adjudicate.

Then the sound of the current world returns:
- the waterfall / mountain wind is still there;
- at some mooring, someone shouts about something completely unrelated;
- to the south, a ship no one recognizes is coming in to dock;
- large stretches of Milo's paper are still blank;
- Kassia's second Whisperflat entry is still there;
- one slice of Lia's crooked cake is still left.

The world widens again here.

The world did not, because the Core answered once, become a sequel to the Federation.
It is still Archeon.

The end point of Act 1: a civilization that has already been alive on its own for five hundred years stops asking, and the old system leaves one response whose meaning cannot be authenticated; then life goes on.

=
